--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page serves as the title page for Johannes Kepler’s seminal 1606 work on the supernova of 1604. It follows a series of blank or introductory leaves in the bound volume.</meta>

<margin>Monastery <unclear>of...</unclear></margin>
<margin>286.</margin>
<margin>8° Duplicate A 1026</margin> <note>This is a library shelfmark indicating the book's size (octavo) and its cataloging location.</note>
<margin>1.</margin>

# JOHANNES KEPLER
## Mathematician to His Sacred Imperial Majesty
# CONCERNING THE <margin>Astronomy P 220.</margin>
# NEW STAR
### IN THE FOOT OF THE SERPENT-BEARER, AND
### CONCERNING THE FIERY TRIGON
### WHICH BEGAN ANEW AT THE TIME OF ITS EMERGENCE.
<note>The "Serpent-Bearer" is the constellation Ophiuchus. The "Fiery Trigon" refers to an astrological phenomenon where the conjunctions of Jupiter and Saturn occur in the "fire" signs (Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius)—an event Kepler linked to the appearance of the new star (Supernova 1604).</note>

A LITTLE BOOK full of Astronomical, Physical, Metaphysical, Meteorological, and Astrological Disputations, both <term>accepted <note>original Greek: ἐνδόξοις (endoxois); referring to reputable or commonly held scholarly opinions.</note></term> and <term>paradoxical <note>original Greek: παραδόξοις (paradoxois); referring to unexpected or unconventional views that challenge common sense.</note></term>.

TO WHICH ARE ADDED
I. CONCERNING THE UNKNOWN STAR IN CYGNUS:
An Astronomical Narration.
II. CONCERNING THE TRUE YEAR OF THE BIRTH OF JESUS CHRIST OUR SAVIOR,
a very recent consideration of the opinion of LAURENCE SUSLYGA of Poland, who finds a four-year discrepancy in the common era.
<note>Kepler used his astronomical calculations of planetary conjunctions to argue that the birth of Christ occurred earlier than the traditional calendar suggested.</note>

With the Privilege of His Sacred Imperial Majesty for 15 years.

-><note>A rectangular woodcut emblem depicts a hen scratching in a dung heap with several chicks. Text within the frame reads: "SHE GIVES GRAIN FROM THE DUNG TO THOSE WHO SEARCH." This emblem serves as Kepler’s personal motto, suggesting that scientific truths must often be sifted out from "messy" or observational data.</note><-

PRAGUE
Printed by PAUL SESSUS, at the AUTHOR’S expense.
IN THE YEAR 1606.

<note>Stamp of the Bavarian State Library</note>
<margin>10. B</margin>

<summary>This is the title page of Johannes Kepler’s "De Stella Nova," a multi-disciplinary study of the 1604 supernova, including its astrological significance and chronological implications for the birth of Christ.</summary>

->TO LORD RUDOLPH II,<-
->EMPEROR OF THE ROMANS,<-
->EVER AUGUST.<-
->PIOUS, FORTUNATE, FATHER OF THE FATHERLAND.<- <note>original: "P. F. P. P." (Pius Felix Pater Patriae), traditional honorific titles used for Roman Emperors since antiquity.</note>
->KING OF GERMANY, HUNGARY,<-
->BOHEMIA, etc.<-
->*ARCHDUKE OF AUSTRIA,*<-
->*etc.*<-

<note>A large woodcut initial "C" is ornately decorated with flowing foliage and leaves, serving as the first letter of the opening word "Cur" (Why).</note> **W**HY I should have dedicated this little book as well, MOST AUGUST CAESAR, <note>A formal address to the Holy Roman Emperor, positioning him as the successor to the ancient Roman emperors.</note> which I have written concerning the **NEW STAR**, most especially to Your Sacred Majesty’s Name: I do not think the reason for that matter needs to be explained to Your Majesty at any great length.

For besides the fact that to Your Sacred Imperial Majesty
<margin>)( 2 <note>This is a "signature mark" used by early modern printers to ensure the pages were folded and bound in the correct sequence.</note></margin> from the stu- <note>The text breaks off here mid-sentence and mid-word; "studiis" or "studies" likely follows on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of Kepler's treatise to Holy Roman Emperor Rudolph II, listing his many titles and beginning the justification for the work.</summary>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Emperor Rudolph II. It completes the sentence started on the previous page regarding Kepler's official duties and explains the delay in publishing the findings on the supernova of 1604.</meta>

->E P I S T L E<-

I serve in mathematical studies, and on this account I enjoy a generous salary; there was also added Your Majesty's most merciful command, that I should devote all effort, care, and diligence to observing and describing the circumstances of this astounding miracle. [flourish]

What, therefore, was better for me to do than to bring this little book—even if bashfully and trembling with an awareness of my own insignificance—yet as becomes a faithful and honest servant, into Your Sacred Imperial Majesty’s sight, [flourish] to hear whether Your Majesty judges that what was ordered has been correctly accomplished. [flourish]

Rather, I must explain the cause of so long a delay: why, since this star shone forth in the month of October in the year 1604, this description now finally comes into public view after nearly two whole years have passed? [flourish] For the explanation of this matter may seem to contain a new argument as to why this little book should be offered to Your Sacred Imperial Majesty, to one mortal alone, and to no one else besides.

I could recount the facts as they are: that the end of the star <note>The "end" refers to when the supernova finally faded from naked-eye visibility.</note> was not certain before February of this year; [flourish] and that those things written and discussed in the first part of this book regarding it—astronomically, physically, and metaphysically—were ready in such a way [flourish] that they could have appeared at the past Frankfurt spring fair, <note>The Frankfurt Book Fair (Frankfurter Buchmesse) was already the most important marketing event for books in Europe in the 17th century.</note> had not the hope of an arriving printer failed me.

Deſigni- <note>This is a catchword, a fragment of the first word on the following page, intended to help the binder assemble the book correctly.</note>

<summary>Kepler justifies his dedication to the Emperor by citing his royal commission to study the 1604 supernova and explains that the two-year publication delay was due to waiting for the star to fade and printer availability.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Rudolph II, mathematical studies, 1604 supernova, Frankfurt Book Fair, astronomical observation, imperial patronage</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Emperor Rudolph II from the previous page. Kepler explains his original structural plan for the book and how the delay in printing led him to reorganize the material into more distinct sections.</meta>

->E P I S T L E<-

-gned <note>original: *Designaveram*; This completes the word from the previous page's catchword.</note> for myself in this matter a specific method of writing: namely, that I should first narrate what I myself saw, then what others observed, and finally what I thought concerning the nature of this star. [flourish] Furthermore, I intended to answer the letters of many individuals who, throughout these two years, have spurred me on with their inquiries. [flourish]

But when I saw that this material had grown into a substantial volume, and because the delay of the printer <note>Kepler frequently struggled with the logistics of 17th-century printing, including finding reliable typesetters and securing the necessary paper.</note> had already deprived me of the proper time, I changed my plan. I decided that the first part of this work, which contains the historical account and the astronomical observations, should be published first, so that the public's long-standing expectation might finally be satisfied.

<margin>Division of the work</margin>
For I have distinguished between the **Astronomical** facts, which concern the position and motion of the star; the **Physical** questions, which investigate its substance and origin; and finally the **Astrological** or metaphysical arguments, which look for the meaning of such a miracle in the theater of the world.

<column-break/>

This star appeared in the so-called "Fiery Trigon" <note>The "Fiery Trigon" refers to the three zodiac signs associated with fire: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. A "Great Conjunction" of Jupiter and Saturn in these signs was considered a rare and momentous omen.</note>, a coincidence that has not occurred for eight hundred years. Truly, Your Sacred Imperial Majesty, it is as if the heavens themselves wished to provide a spectacle worthy of your reign. [flourish]

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut border featuring celestial instruments like quadrants and globes.</image-desc>
<note>The page is framed with a decorative woodcut border. It features various scientific instruments of the age, such as the quadrant used for measuring the height of stars and the celestial globe, symbolizing the Emperor's patronage of the sciences.</note>

Therefore, I ask that You receive this work with the same kindness with which You have always supported my studies. For though the star has faded from our sight, the record of its appearance shall remain as a monument to this extraordinary age. [flourish]

->The most humble servant of Your Sacred Majesty,<-

->JOHANNES KEPLER<-

<summary>Kepler explains his decision to divide the book into astronomical, physical, and astrological sections to speed up publication following printer delays. He highlights the rarity of the supernova appearing in the "Fiery Trigon."</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Rudolph II, Fiery Trigon, supernova 1604, Great Conjunction, Ophiuchus, astronomical methodology, 17th-century printing</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s dedication to Emperor Rudolf II. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing the rare celestial alignment in the "Fiery Trigon." This page links the timing of the star's appearance to the political turmoil in Hungary and clarifies Kepler's stance on astrology and coincidence.</meta>

->E P I S T L E<-

Majesty, <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "Truly, Your [Majesty]..."</note> this star has birthed a new labor in Astronomy, and a slow one at that, due to the long duration of the star itself; and by which the investigation of Planetary motions has been greatly hindered until now. Furthermore, at the very time Your **Sacred Imperial Majesty** <note>original: *S.C. Mtas Va*, an abbreviation for *Sacra Caesarea Maiestas Vestra*.</note> devoted effort to the settlement of Hungarian affairs <note>This refers to the "Long Turkish War" and the various uprisings in Hungary that occupied Emperor Rudolf II’s attention between 1593 and 1606.</note>, having arranged and weighed the means beneficial to this matter: almost in that same stretch of time, Astronomers sweated over the description of the Star, and I was most occupied in preparing and printing this little Book. So many details have aligned thus far; that almost as often as turbulent rumors were brought to us from Hungary, so often some new delay <note>original: *remora*; literally a "sucking-fish" believed to slow ships, used here to mean a professional or technical hindrance.</note> would intervene for this little edition: but when those matters were pacified, these things also proceeded: finally, there was a frequent allusion to the persons and the quality of events.

Not that I hold my mind tainted by this superstition, as to believe that these things cohere by some fatal law; for I do not even concede that the events of human affairs are annexed to the eternal courses of the heavens: but because, in some unknown way—just as we look upon certain rough sketches of perfect figures in fossils <note>original: *fossilibus*; in the 17th century, this term referred to anything dug out of the earth, including minerals and "stones" that appeared to have natural carvings.</note> with great joy, keep them among our treasures, and assist them with the chisel and the brush <note>original: *caeloque & penicillo*; tools used to enhance natural patterns in stones to make them look more like the objects they resemble.</note> so they might more perfectly express that which they resemble from afar—so in these things as well, we uniquely love and praise whatever beauty and harmony chance provides.

<summary>Kepler compares the timing of his astronomical work on the 1604 supernova with the Emperor's political efforts in Hungary, noting a poetic coincidence between the two. He clarifies that while he finds these parallels aesthetically pleasing, he does not believe human history is dictated by the stars.</summary>
<keywords>Supernova 1604, Rudolf II, Hungary, superstition, astrology vs astronomy, fossils, coincidence, printing delays</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s dedication to Emperor Rudolf II. The previous page discussed the coincidental timing between political upheaval in Hungary and the appearance of the New Star in 1604. Kepler argued that while stars do not cause human events, there is a poetic harmony between the "celestial theater" and the "earthly theater."</meta>

DEDICATION.

Although, if the order of nature dictates that the prosperity and adversity of Princes be shared with their ministers and the cultivators of letters and arts—and that the fates of these men be, in a sense, governed by those of the Princes—it should be no disgrace to record these things and to speak of them openly. For such an alignment <note>original: *conspiratio*; Kepler uses this in the literal Latin sense of "breathing together" or harmony, rather than a secret plot.</note> of events in both good and bad times, the more precise it is, the more certain a testimony it provides of the subject's loyalty.

Therefore, O great Omen of Peace and of the Arts which peace nourishes, **Most August Emperor**, receive favorably this little book, this final account of the **New Star** <note>The "New Star" or *Stella Nova* refers to the supernova of 1604, now known as Kepler's Supernova.</note>, by which I finally release myself from this extraordinary labor. Receive it, though late, yet at an opportune moment; specifically at this time when, like a most merciful Father embracing your **Hungary** <note>Kepler refers to the political reconciliation in Hungary, specifically the Peace of Vienna (1606), which Rudolf II was pressured to sign to end the uprising led by István Bocskai.</note>, you admit her to the opened doors of your grace, filling the hearts and prayers of all with hope.

I shall humbly pray to **ALMIGHTY GOD** that He might prolong for as long as possible the life, health, and happiness of Your **Sacred Imperial Majesty** <note>original: *S. C. Mti Vræ*, an abbreviation for *Sacrae Caesareae Maiestati Vestrae*.</note>, and that He might paternally direct and prosper all Your actions and decrees for the glory of His Name, the salvation of the human race, and the defense of the Christian Commonwealth. May this prosperity be such that Astronomers may once again dare to hope for the success of their own studies; and finally, when Your **Sacred Imperial Majesty** [has concluded] this long war

<note>A printer's ornament appears here: )( </note> 4 with

<summary>Kepler concludes his dedication to Emperor Rudolf II, linking the publication of his book on the 1604 supernova to the recent political peace in Hungary. He prays for the Emperor's health and the continued prosperity of the sciences under his patronage.</summary>
<keywords>Dedication, Rudolf II, New Star (Supernova 1604), Hungary, Peace of Vienna, Imperial patronage, loyalty</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Dedicatory Epistle to Emperor Rudolf II. In the preceding text, Kepler drew parallels between the appearance of the New Star and the political peace being brokered in Hungary, presenting his scientific work as a mirror to the Emperor's governing triumphs.</meta>

**DEDICATORY EPISTLE.**

...when he <note>Referring to the Emperor or the personified "Peace" mentioned on the previous page.</note> has successfully concluded matters with the infidels <note>This refers to the Ottoman Empire, with whom the Habsburgs were often at war; a peace treaty was being negotiated around this time.</note>; I myself might also finally be able to triumph in my astronomical labor—to which I was appointed by Your Sacred Imperial Majesty, namely the restoration of the Planets <note>original: *Planetarum restitutione*; Kepler refers to his monumental task of calculating new, accurate planetary tables (the *Rudolphine Tables*) based on the observations of Tycho Brahe.</note>—having been aided by the support of Your same Majesty.

I commend myself most submissively to Your Sacred Imperial Majesty.

PRAGUE, the day after the Nones of September <note>September 6th.</note>, in the year since the birth of **CHRIST** (according to the common reckoning) one thousand six hundred and six.

->Your Sacred Imperial Majesty’s<-

->most submissive Mathematician,<-

->**JOHANNES KEPLER.**<-

**INDEX**

<summary>In this concluding segment of the dedication, Kepler expresses his hope that his ongoing astronomical research, funded by the Emperor, will reach a successful conclusion similar to the Emperor's political victories. He specifically highlights his work on the "restoration of the planets," which would eventually become the famous Rudolphine Tables.</summary>

<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Emperor Rudolf II, Prague, planetary orbits, Rudolphine Tables, astronomical patronage, 1606</keywords>

| II. *Concerning the Fiery Trigon <note>A "Trigon" is a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element; the Fiery Trigon consists of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note>, and initially on the first meaning of the word* **T R I G O N**. | 10. |

| VII. *What natural cause joins the signs of the Zodiac into one Triplicity by a distance of one-third of a circle? Including the order of the periods of the Zodiac.* | 27. |
| VIII. *On the natural powers of the Fiery Triplicity; and first, on the efficacy of Conjunctions, against Giovanni Pico, Count of Mirandola <note>Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494) was a famous philosopher who wrote a massive polemic against divinatory astrology, which Kepler addresses here.</note>.* | 30. |
| IX. *On the efficacy of the other Aspects <note>In astrology, an "aspect" is the angle planets make with each other; Kepler sought to explain these through his theory of musical and geometric harmony.</note>, and likewise of the Trigon, against the same author; based on the doctrine of harmonics, the foundations of which are here laid.* | 37. |
| X. *To what extent a certain significance is added to places on the zodiac by the meetings of Planets that occur within them: including more on the formative faculty of sublunary <note>Referring to the Earthly realm below the sphere of the Moon, which was thought to be subject to change and decay, unlike the "perfect" heavens.</note> things.* | 42. |

| XXVI. *Whether this star coincided by chance with the time and place of the Great Conjunction <note>The meeting of Jupiter and Saturn, which in 1604 occurred very close to where the New Star appeared.</note>, or whether it happened by some design; four modern opinions.* | 129. |
| XXVII. *An examination of the stated opinions; concerning material necessity and Chance—what it is capable of and what it is not—and concerning the Spirits born with the World and natural to it.* | 136. |

### THE AUTHOR’S EPIGRAM ON THE RISING OF THE NEW STAR.

While I bring forth the orbits of **RED MARS**:
Behold, Nature brings forth a rival **REDNESS**. <note>Kepler was famously working on his "Commentaries on Mars" (Astronomia Nova) when the New Star of 1604 appeared, which was initially noted for its fiery red color.</note>
While I bring forth the threats of the **RED** star:
Behold, my wife brings forth a **GOLDEN** boy. <note>Kepler’s son, Friedrich, was born on December 3, 1604, during the height of the supernova's visibility.</note>
Depart, you sad stars of **RED** light,
Pregnant with the tumult of **WAR**;
Greetings to you, my **DARK** <note>original: "Nigella." This likely refers to the dark eyes of the newborn infant.</note> but
**GENTLE** stars of the **MILKY** <note>original: "Lactei." Referring to the "milk-fed" or infant state of his son.</note> child.
Though this radiance was born, let it in turn pass away:
May the goodness of the **Thunderer** <note>original: "Tonantis." A classical epithet for God, common in Renaissance Latin poetry.</note> preserve you for me;
Until you might imprint upon your mind the **Egg-shaped** Orbits
Of the **God of War**, which I now reveal. <note>original: "Oviformes... Orbes." A major scientific milestone; Kepler here references his discovery that planetary orbits are elliptical (egg-shaped) rather than perfect circles.</note>

December 3, the year 1604.

J. K. <note>Johannes Kepler.</note>

# HISTORY OF THE NEW STAR, WHICH IN THE YEARS 1604 AND 1605 SHONE IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. <note>The "Serpent-bearer" refers to the constellation Ophiuchus.</note>

## CHAPTER ONE.

**A**S THE YEAR 1604 of the Christian era began—the start of which modern chronologists dispute as being several years later than the birth of our Lord JESUS CHRIST, God and Man, from the Virgin Mary:

<margin>
Circumstance
of time.
</margin>

In the first year of an eight-hundred-year astrological period, rising from the Fiery Trigon <note>The "Fiery Trigon" or "Fiery Triplicity" refers to the three zodiac signs associated with the element of fire: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. A "Great Conjunction" of Jupiter and Saturn occurs in these signs roughly every 800 years, an event of immense perceived importance to Kepler’s contemporaries.</note>, the eighth since the creation of the world: which the meeting of Saturn and Jupiter at the eighth degree of Sagittarius had already begun on December 7/17 <note>Kepler uses double dating for the Julian and Gregorian calendars, which were both in use across Europe at the time.</note> of the previous year, 1603, though Mars was then absent:

Since there had already existed for many years various predictions by astrologers concerning the returning fiery trigon, and the great omens and political upheavals expected to follow from this year 1604; and since, for that reason, the minds of all were alert for new events:

And now also with Mars having passed Saturn at the 10th degree of Sagittarius on September 16/26, and from then on gradually hastening in the following days toward Jupiter (who had already moved a few degrees beyond Saturn); if Mars should reach it, it would complete the "Great Conjunction" and fulfill its rightful title according to the doctrine of the astrologers:

Since the eyes of all astronomers who diligently pursue their profession were focused intently on this spectacle of nature during the evenings of the passing days—some for the sake of correcting planetary motions; others for pleasure; others for the solemnity of an event returning after the interval of so many centuries; others out of a zeal for predictions; and some even to test the reliability of their **prognostications** <note>original: "προγνωσιῶν" (prognōsiōn) — Greek for foreknowledge or predictions.</note>, as those who, following the teachings of the Arabs, had predicted in explicit terms that a comet would arise at this time:

When I, being in Prague on September 17/27, in clear weather during the departure of Mars from
A <note>This "A" at the bottom of the page is a "catchword," a common feature in early printing to help the bookbinder ensure the pages were in the correct order by previewing the first word of the following page.</note>                                                                                                Saturn,

...to the feast of Michael the Archangel, or September 29 by the Julian style, and October 10 <note>original: "9. Octob. Gregoriano" - Note: Kepler is calculating the 10-day difference between the Julian and Gregorian calendars used at the time.</note> by the Gregorian:

Nor had any of these [observers] seen anything new in the vicinity of the three superior planets <note>The "superior planets" are those with orbits further from the Sun than Earth's: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> up to this point.

But the conjunction of Jupiter and Mars in longitude <note>A coordinate measuring the position of a celestial body along the ecliptic, the Sun's apparent path.</note>, which was the completion of the Great Conjunction, had fallen upon this final day, September 29 or October 9:

Immediately on the following Sunday, September 30 (Julian) or October 10 (Gregorian), which was the first day after the final conjunction; when the clouds opened for a moment after sunset, the aforementioned Johannes Brunowski <note>A student and assistant to Tycho Brahe and Kepler, who is often credited with the first confirmed sighting of the New Star.</note>, a Bohemian, [using] meteoroscopic <note>Instruments used for observing and measuring the positions of celestial or atmospheric phenomena.</note> [instruments]...

...on September 17/27 first of all: that is impossible, as is the claim made by Antonio Lorenzini of Polenza <note>original: "Antonius Laurentinus Politianus"</note> in his book published in the Italian language <note>original: "idiomatis Italici"</note>, where he asserts: that [the star] was observed on October 8 around the 18th degree of Sagittarius, in which sign, as if on that very day, it happened that Jupiter was joined to Mars, with Saturn not far off, and the Moon...

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT [BEARER]<-                                 3

...[meteoro]scopy <note>This word completes "meteoroscopiae" from the previous page, referring to the study of atmospheric and celestial phenomena.</note>, to which he was uniquely devoted; he who long ago first gathered his knowledge of the celestial constellations from navigators <note>original: "Naucleris" — shipmasters or pilots</note> in the Mediterranean Sea, and from there has increased it daily through the use of books and practice. This man, I say, while intending to observe the position of the planets following the conjunction <note>original: "congressu" — the meeting or alignment of planets</note> of the previous day, noticed three very bright stars in the exact location of the conjunction, instead of two; at that same moment, clouds again snatched the place from his sight. 

However, being certain that he had seen something new, he approached me the following morning and asked whether I had seen anything new; and when I replied that the clouds had not granted me any view of the sky at all, I cautioned him not to be foolishly hasty in his belief—as he was somewhat inexperienced—lest he ridiculously invent a "New Star" out of the bright star of Jupiter <note>Jupiter was exceptionally brilliant during this period, and Kepler feared his assistant had simply mistaken the known planet for a new phenomenon.</note>; advising him to look again and again. He then said that he would spread no rumors until the clearest experience itself should provide a verdict. From that point, through six continuous days of cloudiness, even the memory of this report was extinguished from my mind; Brunowski <note>This name at the bottom of the page is a catchword, indicating the first word of the following page.</note>

4 ->OF JOHANNES KEPLER<-

Röslin <note>Helisaeus Röslin (1545–1616), a German physician and astrologer.</note> in Alsace, while riding; when he had obtained that first clear evening after long-lasting rains.
On the day of October 7/17 <note>During this period, both the Julian (old style) and Gregorian (new style) calendars were in use; the double date represents both.</note>, it was noticed everywhere in Württemberg.
On the same October 7/17, David Fabricius in Frisia both saw it, recognized it, and observed it on this and the following day with his accurate instruments, constructed according to Tychonic laws <note>Referring to the rigorous standards for astronomical instruments established by Tycho Brahe.</note>.
On the day of October 8/18, it presented a flash of light through the clouds to Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin, Kepler’s former professor and a renowned astronomer.</note>; but the novelty of it was not yet manifest to him because of the frequency of the clouds.
Finally here in Prague, on the day of October 8/18, the clouds were driven away, but only slightly; when Brunowski and my student Schuler were present—the former intent on confirming his report, the latter intent on refuting it, should the opportunity arise. Having been warned therefore by Schuler, while I was climbing to a suitable location, that part of the sky had already passed from my view.
On the same October 8/18, it was clearly seen by Maestlin at Tübingen.
On the following day, October 9/19, with the sky most clear, I observed it according to the opportunity of the place and instruments.
From that point, everyone everywhere was alerted by letters: and Bartholomew Crestinus, Mathematician to the Prince of Savoy, reports that by the day of November 7/17, letters written from Spain to his Prince had already arrived, in which the rising of the new star was celebrated.
On the day of October 11/21, it was observed by Jost Bürgi <note>A famous clockmaker and mathematician.</note> through an instrument prepared by himself, and on the same day by Franz Tengnagel and me, through the Tychonic Sextant in the Imperial Garden <note>The *Viridarium Caesarianum* in Prague, where Kepler served as Imperial Mathematician.</note>.

<margin>
**Venus**
**mistaken**
**for the**
**new star.**
</margin>

Meanwhile, while the common people were being excited everywhere by the rumor of the new star, the matter having been communicated through frequent letters: the morning rising of Venus happened conveniently; which, since it was very close to the earth—being distant from it by barely a fourth part of the distance of the Sun, and a sixth or seventh of that distance which it maintains at its evening rising—it was also consistent that it should be so many times brighter and larger in appearance. Therefore, it even cast a shadow from objects, and could be seen for a large part of the day in a clear sky; even by me, who possess weaker vision.

**this year,**
**1606, in the**

**April with**
**Venus; in**
**June with**
**Mars at**

**new star**

**appeared.**

While, therefore, Astronomers and those knowledgeable of such things look back toward the West in the evening; the common crowd of men—both the busy and those curious about celestial things, and finally the "wise"—dragging one another along, flock together everywhere into the marketplaces in the morning hours, especially in Vienna; they point with their fingers at the star before the Sun in the high ether, astonished; and the foolish keep their minds and eyes occupied with the "foolish" Venus <note>Kepler uses "stulta Venere" (foolish Venus) to mock the public's lack of astronomical discernment.</note>. This matter even crept into many ordinary newsletters; and since it had occasionally happened through the diligence of the common people, **the new star having already disappeared long ago.** <note>This final bolded line concludes the thought from the marginal note regarding the public's persistent confusion between planets and the supernova.</note>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler's detailed description of the appearance and visual characteristics of the New Star of 1604 (now known as Kepler's Supernova), comparing it to other celestial phenomena like comets and planets.</meta>

# ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT [HOLDER]. 5

...it happened that this brightest star, like that of Venus, was seen emerging more and more from the rays of the Sun from the first sunrise, until it set a few hours before the Sun (for they were setting in a sign of short descents). This was carefully noted and celebrated as a primary marvel.

Furthermore, among astronomers, I know of no one who saw this new star during the day. For it did not equal Venus in magnitude. Nevertheless, some grave men <note>Dignified or serious witnesses.</note> told me of a certain wonderful thing like a "beam" <note>Latin: *trabis*. In traditional meteorology, this referred to a long, beam-shaped atmospheric fire or meteor.</note> seen in the sky during the day, which after some hours, following the daily motion, descended behind the mountains.

<margin>The appearance</margin> Concerning the appearance of the star, all who watched its first rising agree: it was exactly <margin>of the star.</margin> round, with no "hair" <note>The fuzzy envelope or tail of a comet.</note>, no "beard" <note>A type of comet tail that appears to precede the head.</note>, or "train" <note>Latin: *syrmate*, referring to a trailing robe or long tail.</note> projected in any direction. For this reason, it was not to be numbered among any species of "hairy stars," neither the "bearded stars" <note>Greek/Latin: *pogoniae*.</note> nor the comets. Instead, it was most similar to the fixed stars, with rays flashing out on all sides just as the fixed stars do. Its twinkling was most bright, its flashing or vibration so rapid that some claimed they had never seen anything in the heavens in their lifetime with such "swift motion," as they put it. Various people expressed this with different comparisons; for since its color was all the more reddish the closer it was to the setting [horizon] and the deeper it was in the atmospheric vapors, they said it looked like a distant fire rising. Others compared it more beautifully to a torch tossed by the wind; because, just as the continuity of flames is interrupted in a torch by the gusts of wind—where as soon as the first flames are driven off or extinguished, new ones follow—so also this star stretched its rays out very far and, faster than can be said, sucked them back in again, while simultaneously expanding with a new start in a restless back-and-forth. Our Austrians, in an even more elegant local idiom <note>Latin: *idiotismo*, referring to common speech or dialect.</note>, said that it was "laboring" <note>original: *laborare*</note> most vehemently. This word alludes to a feverish pulse, to the struggle of one fighting and now being overwhelmed, or to someone falling into water, or drawing their last breath. For just as these people usually twitch their arms and legs, so this star also shook its trembling point of light.

<margin>The colors</margin> To me, who looked primarily at the variety of rainbow colors it cast out according to the changing <margin>of the star.</margin> state of its twinkling, it was pleasing to use the example of a multi-faceted diamond <note>original: *adamantis multanguli*</note> which, while being turned, reflects the sun’s rays back to the eyes of the spectators with a variable brilliance. At the same time, I wanted to allude to the cause of twinkling that I presented in my *Optics* <note>Kepler refers here to his work *Ad Vitellionem paralipomena* (1604), where he discussed the atmospheric causes of stellar scintillation.</note>.

Concerning the colors, Maestlin is entirely in agreement with me: that they vary from moment to moment—from yellow soon to saffron, immediately to purple and red, and for the most part appearing white when it is raised a little higher out of the vapors.

A 3     In

<summary>Kepler describes the visual characteristics of the New Star, noting its intense twinkling and shifting colors (red, purple, yellow, white). He distinguishes it from comets by its lack of a tail or "hair," comparing its brilliance to a multifaceted diamond or a wind-blown torch.</summary>

...it remained. Therefore, the vanity of Crabbe <note>Paul Crabbe (Crabbius), a contemporary observer whose reports Kepler frequently disputed.</note> must be enormous; he, based on the most faulty observations arranged in a fraudulent order, dared to attribute a very slow motion to our star—a motion which, throughout the entire period of its duration, did not complete as great an arc as Crabbe claimed it did on the very first day, the 4th of October <note>original: "1/4 Octobris". Kepler is likely referencing Crabbe’s disputed claim of seeing the star earlier than other astronomers, or a specific fractional day measurement.</note>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s sharp dismissal of Paul Crabbe’s observations, transitioning into a technical argument about parallax and providing a chronological record of the star's fading visibility as it approached the Sun in late 1604.</meta>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT [FOOT]. 7**

...strayed in his observation, reporting it at 17° 0' of Sagittarius, whereas Fabricius <note>David Fabricius (1564–1617), a German astronomer and correspondent of Kepler.</note> in East Frisia, on the preceding day, found it at 17° 37' or 17° 40' of Sagittarius. If he had communicated only his observations to us, however imperfect, he would have earned some gratitude. But because he commends them only for their certainty, so that with such confidence he might attribute even the slightest motion to the star, he shall rightly be criticized as a deceiver. For what do you think would become of Astronomy if—may God forbid—a wretched posterity were to place that same faith in his observations regarding the Planets which he so affects? Therefore, I pity Herlicius <note>David Herlicius (1557–1636), a physician and mathematician who published popular astrological calendars.</note>, who even now, while the author is still living, has allowed himself to be imposed upon, believing that this was a mobile Comet. If he built his <term>prognostic</term> <note>A formal prediction or astrological forecast common in 17th-century almanacs.</note> upon this motion, he has wasted both his oil and his labor <note>original: "oleum & operam perdidit" - a Latin idiom meaning to waste time and effort.</note>, and he owes very little thanks to Crabbe.

When motion is removed, <term>parallax</term> <note>The apparent shift in position of an object when viewed from different locations; the lack of it proved the star was much farther away than the Moon.</note> is also taken away, which, as the name indicates, is a certain apparent motion or apparent translation of a star that is inherently immobile. And with parallax removed, no place is left for the star among the Planets—certainly none below the Moon, Venus, Mercury, the Sun, or Mars.

As for the parallaxes of Jupiter and Saturn, even though they are imperceptible, it is proved by other arguments that the star did not even stand near these planets.

It was seen throughout the entire month of October with almost equal magnitude, except that as it entered the rays of the Sun—with Jupiter withdrawing somewhat and fleeing the Sun—the estimation of its size decreased when compared to Jupiter. In the month of November, it became known to many more people. Indeed, on the 30th of October / 9th of November <note>Kepler provides dates in both the "Old Style" Julian calendar and "New Style" Gregorian calendar.</note>, a quarter-hour before five o’clock, I saw the new star after sunset; I could not yet find Jupiter, so bright was the star even then, although it was much closer to the rays of the Sun than Jupiter.

On the 7th/17th day of November, I saw it for the last time in the evening. For the following days were cloudy; however, Brengger <note>Johann Georg Brengger, a physician and astronomer who provided Kepler with observations from Kaufbeuren.</note> observed it from Jupiter as late as the 8th/18th of November.

On the 12th/22nd of November, although the star's location was noted by the presence of the Moon, we could by no means see the star itself. Yet the Moon had not covered it, as the Moon's visible latitude was three degrees and more, while the star did not have a latitude of even two degrees.

In Prague, therefore, between the 7th/17th and the 12th/22nd of November, it <term>set heliacally</term> <note>The "heliacal setting" occurs when a star becomes lost in the glare of the Sun as the Sun approaches it in the sky.</note>. <margin>The heliacal / setting of / the star.</margin> However, Crestinus <note>Giovanni Battista Crestina.</note> affirms that in Turin in Savoy, where the <term>pole elevation</term> <note>The latitude of the observer; a lower latitude allows stars to stay visible longer before the Sun overtakes them.</note> is lower, it could still be seen on the 13th/23rd of November by those who possessed especially sharp eyesight.

From the beginning of the 26th of November / 6th of December until the 8th/18th day of December, we observed Mercury; certainly, on the 7th/17th, it was clearly visible. For its rising was further to the left,

<summary>Kepler continues his critique of Crabbe's "fraudulent" observations and argues that the star's lack of parallax places it far beyond the planets. He also provides a precise log of its fading visibility and heliacal setting in late 1604.</summary>

<keywords>parallax, heliacal setting, Paul Crabbe, David Fabricius, David Herlicius, Jupiter, Saturn, astronomical dating, Prague, Turin</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s chronological record of observations from late 1604 into 1605, documenting the gradual fading of the New Star as it moved from its initial brilliance toward invisibility, compared against known planets and stars in the constellation Ophiuchus.</meta>

8 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**JOHANNES KEPLER**

...later than the Sun; but within half an hour of its rising, it hid itself in the light of the <term>twilight</term> <note>original: "crepusculi." In the early 17th century, observations were often hindered by the "glare" of the sun during dawn or dusk.</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the day of **December 8/18**, it is uncertain whether I saw Mercury or the new star; except that if it had been that new one, it certainly would have been more evident than Mercury, and not far distant, and it would have been natural for both to meet the eyes at once; and because a new star could hardly rise so close to the Sun, and because the usual <term>scintillation</term> <note>The rapid "twinkling" or sparkling of a star, which Kepler used to distinguish stars from the steadier light of planets.</note> of new stars gave me no warning. Whichever it was, it chose a point of rising further to the right than Mercury did on the 7/17th. After that, the days were cloudy until the Eve of the Nativity of Christ, **December 14/24**: when, while Arcturus was still visible, yet with the twilight already quite advanced, I noticed a sparkling star on that vertical line which marked the rising point of the Sun on the 7/17th. It was several degrees in altitude, and though for the most part hidden in the light of the twilight, the flashes of its sparkling nevertheless broke through from time to time and struck the sharp gaze of my eyes repeatedly, so much so that from Venus I could imagine a line through it which seemed to lead to that place on the Horizon where that doubtful star was seen to rise on the 8/18th.

<margin>*The heliacal rising of the star.*</margin> <note>A <term>heliacal rising</term> occurs when a star first becomes visible in the eastern sky at dawn, just before being obscured by the sun's light.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In the year **1605**, on **December 24 or January 3**, after a long period of darkness and the clouds having been driven away, the new star undoubtedly appeared clearly again, sparkling most rapidly, but much diminished from that former magnitude: yet it was larger than the <term>Heart of the Scorpion</term> <note>The star Antares, the brightest star in the constellation Scorpius.</note> of the first magnitude, even though it was itself overwhelmed by the rays of the Sun more than the Heart of the Scorpion was. However, Arcturus—I do not know if it was because it was outside the Sun's rays—seemed much larger. Yet on the day of **January 3/13**, it was thought to be larger than Arcturus and Saturn, with no trace of twilight yet present. Saturn had then risen heliacally for three days. For ten days before, it had not yet come forth.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From that time on, it was diminished more and more. For when I was intent on observation on the **20th of March**, the star seemed much smaller than Saturn, nor did it much exceed the magnitude of the stars in the knees of <term>Ophiuchus</term> <note>The constellation of the "Serpent Bearer." Kepler’s New Star appeared in the foot of this figure.</note>, which are of the third <term>honor</term> <note>Kepler uses the term "honor" to refer to stellar magnitude or rank of brightness.</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the **21st of April**, it seemed to equal the bright star in the right knee of Ophiuchus. From then on, this decrease of its light remained somewhat steady through May and June.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the **12th and 14th of August**, having carefully contemplated the surrounding stars, I judged the New Star to be equal to the star in the shin [tibia] of Ophiuchus, which is closest; which itself is also considered to be of the third honor: yet it is smaller than the star in the knee.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Even then, the New Star sparkled more clearly than the others.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the **29th of August**, I indeed pronounced the New Star greater than the shin...

## **ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 9**

...I was not able to observe <note>original: "tiare," the suffix of "observare" or "notare" from the previous page</note>, on account of the brightness of the summer nights; nevertheless, it could be seen better than the star in the **Shin** <note>The star Eta Ophiuchi, located in the leg or "tibia" of the constellation Ophiuchus.</note>, aided by its sparkling <term>scintillation</term> <note>The rapid "twinkling" effect caused by atmospheric turbulence, which Kepler used to distinguish stars from the steady light of planets.</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the **13th of September**, it appeared smaller than that star in the Shin.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the **8th of October**, a full year now having passed since its first appearance, with the sky being very clear, it could be perceived by sight only with difficulty; nevertheless, it was seen better than the star in the Shin, though not always, but only when it flashed out. For already that region was whitening with the light of the evening twilight, as the Sun approached the location. From this time it again set **heliacally** <note>original: "occidit heliacè." A heliacal setting occurs when a star becomes lost in the glare of the setting sun as the sun's position moves closer to it in the sky.</note>, forty days sooner than it had the year before: from which it is given to be understood how great its brightness and magnitude had been in the beginning.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In the year **1606**, in the month of **January**, there was rarely an opportunity for a clear morning sky, especially near the horizon, although the winter was excellent and the frost was continuous.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Nevertheless, on **Saturday, January 4/14** <note>original: "Die ♄ Januarij." Kepler uses the astrological symbol for Saturn (♄) to denote Saturday.</note>, it happened that I saw the location: the **right knee** appeared, as did the bend of the **serpentine coil**, and the bright star in the **palm of the right foot** of Ophiuchus <note>These are specific stars in the constellations of Ophiuchus and Serpens used as celestial landmarks.</note>; the former two had stood closest above the new star, while the latter stood closest below it. However, I was in no way able to see either the New Star or the star in the Shin, which is next to the new one on the right; at that same moment, the light of the morning twilight was kindled. **Saturn** had risen heliacally.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the morning of **January 25 or February 4**, I saw the location of the New Star, but nothing of the star itself, though the Moon was present, not yet joined with Saturn.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On **January 27 or February 6**, uncertain whether I had seen a flashing trace of the new star, I departed from the observatory. Thus it appeared to have become so small that it could not be seen in this brightness of the dawn, even if it still remained. In the month of **March**, however, after the location had been sufficiently cleared of the Sun's presence so that, with the <margin>Disappearance of the star.</margin> twilight not yet risen, it was raised high enough above the horizon, no trace of the star remained that could strike the eyes. It is uncertain, therefore, on which day between October of the year 1605 and February of the year 1606 it was extinguished.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It is memorable that it was approached by all the **Planets**. <margin>Configuration with the Planets.</margin>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;At its first rising, it was joined as closely as possible to **Jupiter** and **Mars**, which were lower toward the south, in the form of an obtuse triangle; on the **12th and 13th of October**, through the motion of the Planets, this became a straight line. On **October 25**, the **Moon** passed over it, and this was repeated in each of the following months. Never, however, did it cover the location of the star. For at this time the **ascending node** <note>The point where the Moon's orbit crosses the ecliptic (the sun's path) moving north.</note> was in **Libra**, receding more and more from the location of the star, so that each subsequent month showed the Moon further north in **Sagittarius**. The days of the conjunctions are these: In the year **1604**: **Oct. 25**, **Nov. 22**, **Dec. 20**. In the year **1605**:

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But let us also look at the other planets. The **Sun**, therefore, comes to this location annually on the **10th of December** (or November 30th in the <note>original: "stylo Juliano"</note> **Julian style** <note>The old calendar system used across Europe before the adoption of the Gregorian reform.</note>). Thus, in the year **1604**, it found **Saturn** there; in the year **1605**, it found the **Moon** in that place; and so the **New Moon** of December 1605 was remarkable, because it fell upon the location of the star.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**Saturn**, on **December 11, 1604**, touched very close to the star, passing just slightly below it: which was evident to the eye on the following **January 13**. During the months of July, August, and September of the year **1605**, retracing its previous steps, it completed its **station** <note>A point where a planet appears to stand still in the sky before changing from direct to retrograde motion.</note> very near the star. It was distant from the New Star toward the left by almost the same amount as the **Shin of the Serpent-Bearer** <note>The star Eta Ophiuchi.</note> was toward the right in the same straight line. This was intersected crosswise by another straight line, drawn from above from the **Coil of the Serpent**, which passed behind the **Right Knee of the Serpent-Bearer**, almost through the New Star, and into the **Palm of the Foot of the Serpent-Bearer**. With these five stars, therefore, a **cross** was fashioned, of which the longer beam, or base, was turned upward: so that the New Star was almost at the joining of the lines, or in the center of the cross.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;After Saturn, **Mercury** passed through this place on **December 13, 1604**, and exactly a year later on **December 17**.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The last was **Venus**, which on **January 21, 1605**, passed above the New Star; and since Saturn was also caught in the same place, it offered a beautiful spectacle to observers. In the year **1605**, on **November 12**, it returned to the same spot, but then it passed below.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This, then, is the true and certain history of this most celebrated star, for the confirmation and illustration of which I shall subjoin the following few chapters.

**ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 11**

...explanation of the history already presented regarding the new star, let me begin by recounting the astrological traditions.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You may see a praiseworthy example of this in the most learned of Latin philosophers, **Gaius Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), author of the "Natural History."</note>; who, the more he is free from astrological superstition, the more he draws from the records of the astrologers throughout his work.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For I must state from the start what sort of wonder this **Fiery Trigon** is, whose conjunction with the new star is celebrated everywhere as such a great miracle. This must be derived from the decrees of the astrologers, so that the political men <note>Referencing statesmen and scholars who used astrology to understand world events.</note> who read this will not complain of being left in the dark. It must also be examined by the standard of truth, so that trifles may be separated from the nature of things. Let the six hundred astrologers who have written about the Fiery Trigon over these last four years—specifically that **Herlicius** <note>David Herlicius (1557–1636), a prominent German physician and astrologer of Kepler's era.</note>, who is at once the most eloquent and most famous professor of astrology—let them have, I say, what one person who cannot be accused of ignorance of the craft thinks of their writings.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For if it is for the common people to judge who is the better astrologer, let them know I stand in excellent favor; but if they choose the judgment of the learned, the others have already failed. Whether they stand with me before the public, or I lie with them before the learned: on both sides I am of their order, I am their equal, and I should not be rejected—certainly not by Herlicius, who has praised me publicly. However, the greatest consideration here must be for the most learned men who, not being so well-versed in **Mathematics**, frequently ask: What is the Fiery Trigon by nature, or is it **something conventional**? <note>original: "θετικόν τι" (thetikon ti) — a Greek philosophical term meaning a concept established by human agreement or tradition rather than being a physical law of nature.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;"**Trigon**" in Greek is "**Triangle**" in Latin; it is a geometrical term signifying a figure very well known to everyone. In astrology, this word has two or three other meanings. First, when the rays of two **Planets** meet on Earth in such a way that they intercept exactly one-third of a circle described from the point of their meeting; this arrangement of two rays is one of the types of **aspects** <note>The angular distance between celestial bodies as seen from Earth; a "trine" aspect is 120 degrees.</note>, and it is called a **Trigon** or a **trine aspect**. The reason for the name is that if a third planet were added at the same interval from the rays of the other two, and it also cast its ray toward the common intersection, and the points of the circle divided by the three rays were connected by straight lines, a genuine **equilateral triangle** would be created, in the geometrical sense.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Here indeed, astrologers—so that they would not be forced to always draw a circle from the meeting point of the rays—used a shortcut: they used the center of the world and the zodiac itself instead of the meeting point; and for the circle to be described, they used the **zodiac** already described in the heavens, under which all the planets travel,

<note>A marginal diagram shows a circle with an equilateral triangle inscribed inside it. A dot marks the center of the circle, representing the Earth. A vertical line connects the top point of the triangle to a small star outside the circle, illustrating the relationship between the triangle's points and celestial positions.</note>

**B 2** **choose...** <note>original: "elege-" — a catchword for the next page, likely "elegerunt."</note>

<summary>Kepler introduces the astrological concept of the "Fiery Trigon" and its connection to the New Star of 1604. He defines the term "Trigon" geometrically as an equilateral triangle formed by planets at 120-degree intervals (the trine aspect) within the zodiac.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler's defense of a reformed astrology based on geometry. The opening word "chosen" completes the sentence from the previous page regarding how people select their authorities.</meta>

->12 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

...have chosen. And so, although the thing itself—namely, the **Trigon** <note>original: *Trigonus*; also known as the "Trine" aspect, where two planets are separated by 120 degrees, forming one side of an equilateral triangle inscribed in the zodiac.</note>—is located at the point where the rays intersect at the center of the earth, its measurement is nonetheless carried out in the highest reaches of the heavens. It is as if all the planets were passing through that uppermost circle with their actual bodies, just as they appear to do to our eyes.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Because the positions of two planets in the zodiac have taken the name **Trigon** (that is, the measurement of the thing itself), the term **aspect** seems to have been born from this. This term is a figure of **personification** <note>original: *prosopopoeia*; a rhetorical device where an abstract thing is given human qualities.</note>, where mutual eyes and a shared gaze are attributed to parts of the zodiac separated by equal distances around the circle. This occurs as if the planets themselves were lifted by our imagination—guided by a deceptive sense of sight—from their lower orbits into these parts of the zodiac.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This type of phenomenon—namely the **aspect** of two stars, where either both or at least one is in motion—is what I believe should be almost the only thing retained in astrology. I defend this philosophically and through the **doctrine of harmonics** <note>Kepler's signature theory that the universe is structured according to the same geometric ratios that produce musical harmony.</note> (which is almost entirely misunderstood) against those philosophers who are completely ignorant of the art. I do this with as much conviction as the confidence with which I judge almost all the rest of the astrologers' equipment should be cast out; and I protest this unceasingly in all my astrological writings.

***

### On the second meaning of the word Trigon: or on the Triangulation of the Signs.
### CHAPTER THREE.

<note>A faint diagram in the left margin depicts a circle with an inscribed triangle and a vertical line above it, representing the geometric division of the celestial circle into thirds.</note> <margin>A diagram of the Trigon</margin>

From this astrological notion of the word **Triangle**, which is its first origin, the others have flowed. For in the second place, this word was transferred from specific points in the zodiac to its entire sections, which are separated by that same interval. For since astrologers divide the zodiac into twelve equal parts—beginning from the intersection of the ecliptic line <note>original: *media linea*; the path of the sun.</note> with the celestial equator <note>original: *linea inter polos mundi media*; the line halfway between the poles.</note>, which the Sun crosses in the springtime—the names of these parts are:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, Virgo,**
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**Libra, Scorpio, Sagittarius** <note>original: *Arcitenens*; literally "The Bow-Bearer."</note>, **Capricorn** <note>original: *Caper*; "The Goat."</note>, **Aquarius** <note>original: *Amphora*; "The Jar."</note>, **Pisces**.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Therefore, when these twelve are divided by three (since three are required to form a triangle), the zodiac is said to have four **Trigons**, which they also call **Triplicities**.

<summary>Kepler defines the "aspect" as a geometric relationship rather than a mystical force and argues that only these harmonic aspects should be preserved in astrology. He then introduces the "Triplicities," which group the twelve zodiac signs into four triangular sets.</summary>

<keywords>Trigon, Trine, Aspect, Prosopopoeia, Harmonics, Zodiac Signs, Triplicity, Astrology Reform, Geometry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s discussion on the geometric foundations of astrology. Having defined the "Trigon" (or Trine aspect) on the previous page as a 120-degree separation, he now explains how these geometric relationships organize the twelve signs of the zodiac into four groups based on the classical elements.</meta>

**ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 13**

The first consists of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius;
The second of Taurus, Virgo, and Capricorn;
The third of Gemini, Libra, and Aquarius;
The fourth of Cancer, Scorpio, and Pisces.
Where you see that between Aries and Leo there are three signs intervening: Taurus, Gemini, and Cancer; between Leo and Sagittarius likewise three: Virgo, Libra, and Scorpio; and finally just as many between Sagittarius and Aries, namely Capricorn, Aquarius, and Pisces—and so it is for all of them. Therefore, if these signs are arranged in a circle and connected by straight lines, a perfect triangle will be created.

From the names of the individual parts, the name "sign" arose, a term common to all twelve parts. Therefore that circle, the circle of signs, is called the **zodiac** in Greek <note>original: *zōdiakos*</note>. Moreover, they distinguish the four **triplicities** <note>Groups of three signs belonging to the same element, forming an equilateral triangle within the 360-degree circle.</note> by the names of the four elements. They call the first **fiery**; the second, **earthy**; the third, **airy**; and the fourth, **watery**.

This entire system of division and naming should be retained in **mathematics** <note>original: *Mathesi*. In Kepler's time, this referred to the "mathematical sciences," which included astronomy, music theory, and optics alongside geometry.</note> not only because it is ancient, but also because it is necessary for memory and very convenient in practice. For even if these names were completely uprooted from the human mind along with astronomy itself, those who came after—if they wished to restore astronomy among men—would be forced to use some similar method of distinguishing the parts of the sky by names.

Therefore, some people act with reckless foolishness and foolish recklessness when they abuse their eloquence to call the public out under the open sky to see for themselves whether they can find actual animals, or fire, or water in the heavens with their own eyes. They wish to claim that astronomers and astrologers alike are all insane to a man, making no distinction between the harmful and the innocent, or between necessary use and superstition.

But when the question is asked whether a natural power matching these names is actually distributed among these parts of the zodiac—or whether nature or human choice invented these distinctions—here the Chaldean, Indian, Egyptian, Arabian, Greek, and Latin astrologers say "yes." But the philosophers of all ages and times say "no," and I am one with them. Among the most powerful reasons why I say "no" is that those who affirm it rely solely on the persuasion of antiquity; so that when they find no logical reasons, they retreat to the claim that it is unbelievable that this naming system lacks a natural foundation, since it is so ancient and has been celebrated as "natural" in every age. Therefore, as for the reason why I say "no," and the caution regarding how far I say "no,"

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;B 3 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; in con-

<summary>Kepler defines the four elemental "triplicities" of the zodiac and argues that while the signs are essential mathematical conventions for mapping the sky, they possess no inherent physical or "natural" power.</summary>

should be in plain view; since this contributes greatly to the evaluation of our miracle <note>Kepler likely refers here to the "New Star" or Supernova of 1604, the primary subject of this treatise.</note>, the entire matter must be unraveled from its first origin.

->**Whether the division of the Zodiac into twelve equal parts is Natural.**<-

***

->### CHAPTER FOUR.###<-

**I**n the beginning, I shall speak of the cause of the twelve-fold division: here I do not deny that several occasions were provided by the nature of things, by which the judgment of the Discoverers <note>The ancient astronomers and observers who first codified the zodiacal system.</note> was prompted to introduce this precise twelve-fold division. I find three primary occasions for this.

The first is supplied by the proportion of the Sun's motion to the motion of the Moon. For while the Sun traverses the zodiac once, the Moon returns to it almost twelve times; yet in such a way that by the third revolution of the Sun, the Moon adds nearly a thirty-seventh return <note>Kepler is describing the discrepancy between the solar year and lunar months; 12 lunar months do not perfectly equal one solar year, but 37 lunar months are very close to three solar years.</note>.

And so, by the twelve meetings <note>conjunctions, or New Moons</note> of the Moon with the Sun, the zodiac is divided as closely as possible into twelve equal parts.

Almost the same occasion led to dividing each sign into thirty degrees: for between two meetings of the Moon with the Sun—which, as already stated, are distant by almost one sign—nearly thirty days pass by.

Since, therefore, in ancient times after the dispersal of the human race across the world, few were learned in letters, and they thus lacked the convenience of calendars <note>original: *diariorum*. Kepler notes that printed almanacs were a relatively recent luxury in the early 17th century.</note> which today—especially for the last hundred years—even the lowest common folk use; and since holy days were not always announced from the pulpit, by whose annual recurrence they might be reminded of seasonal farm work (as has been the practice in the Church for these last thousand years), because they lived scattered about and met rarely: since men lacked these conveniences, it is believable that knowledge of the number of days in a natural year belonged to very few people. And even though all nations have always had their own holidays and solemnities, for the most part these were movable, like our **Easter**, and were shifted back and forth along with the lunations <note>The cycles of the moon's phases.</note>; thus they were not sufficient for planning annual agricultural labor.

For these reasons, farmers were necessarily driven to observe the rising of the fixed stars; and there existed among them precepts of this discipline handed down by hand <note>Meaning oral tradition or practical apprenticeship.</note>, long before they were recorded in literature by **Hesiod** <note>An ancient Greek poet whose work *Works and Days* functioned as an agricultural calendar based on the stars.</note> and others.

From this rustic discipline, the reason why the number of signs arose [is] easily

<summary>Kepler explains that the twelve-fold division of the zodiac and the thirty-degree division of signs originated from the natural cycles of the Sun and Moon. He argues that ancient farmers relied on the stars for agricultural timing because they lacked modern calendars and fixed religious feast days.</summary>
<keywords>zodiac division, lunar cycles, solar year, history of astronomy, agricultural calendars, Hesiod, synodic month, ancient observations</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s discussion on the origins of the twelve-fold zodiacal division. Having previously mentioned the relationship between solar and lunar cycles, he now explores the specific visual observations of the Moon’s quarters and the geometric-harmonic justifications provided by medieval astronomers for the division of the sky.</meta>

**ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 15**

is easy to consider. For whether they looked to the full Moon, or the waxing, or the waning, or finally to the quarters—which is the more efficient method—it was easy to see how, once a start was made (for example) from the horns of Aries, the quarter of the second month stood near the horns or the eye of Taurus; the third near the stars of Gemini; the fourth near the dim stars of Cancer; and the rest stood near the remaining constellations. Thus, the entire circuit of the zodiac was marked in twelve places by the presence of the quarters within the space of a year.

Since, however, it was evident that this recurrence of quarters did not precisely establish twelve parts, but that as the year revolved, other locations for the quarters became the signs of a new division, the precise twelve-fold division was nevertheless retained, and its beginning remained the same and constant perpetually.

Alfraganus <note>Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Kathir al-Farghani, a 9th-century Persian astronomer whose works were foundational to European medieval astronomy.</note> and Alcabitius <note>Al-Qabisi, a 10th-century Arab astrologer known for his introduction to the science of judicial astrology.</note> recount this cause for this fact, which I mention as the second. The Sun, they say, distinguishes the circuit of the zodiac into four parts by as many points or notable boundaries, and indeed perpetually the same ones. For there is the solstitial point, in which the Sun becomes highest; the winter point <note>original: *brumale*</note> in which it is lowest; and there are two equinoctials, in which it becomes midway between the poles of the world. In the former it makes the longest or shortest days; in the latter it makes them equal to the nights. In these four points, it brings back the four great <term>decumanas</term> <note>From the Latin for "tenth" or "large," referring here to the major, primary boundaries of the year.</note> seasons of the year: Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter. All of these are natural, not depending on human choice.

As to why each of these four quadrants of the zodiac should be further divided into three signs, so that the signs become twelve in number, Alfraganus gives this reason: that it is consistent with nature and accepted by usage to distinguish any quantity into a **beginning**, a **middle**, and an **end**.

The third cause or occasion for the precise twelve-fold division is taken from the comparison of the **Trine** aspect <note>An astrological aspect where two planets are 120 degrees apart, or one-third of the circle.</note> with the **Square**.

For experience (which indicates the fact) and harmonic ratio (which indicates the cause of the fact) agree on this: that when two planets are distant to the sight by a third part of the zodiacal circle (of which we spoke above in Chapter 2), or by a fourth or sixth part, a great commotion of sublunary nature <note>The Earth and its atmosphere, which ancient and early modern science believed were influenced by celestial harmonies.</note> exists. Therefore, assuming Saturn is at the beginning of Aries, as if immobile, when the Sun has receded from it so far that it is distant first by a sixth, then a fourth, and finally a third part of the circle: in these three locations, it will intercept the twelfth parts of the circle. For the difference between the sixth and fourth part of the whole, and likewise between the fourth and third, is on both sides a twelfth part of the whole. For in Gemini, the Sun will make a **sextile** <note>60 degrees</note> with Saturn; in Cancer, a **square** <note>90 degrees</note>; in Leo, a **trine** <note>120 degrees</note>. Thus, the two aspects, as stated, provide the occasion for dividing the zodiac into twelve parts; however, they show no certain beginning point.

<note>A geometric diagram illustrates a circle containing an inscribed equilateral triangle and a square. The intersections of these shapes with the circle's circumference demonstrate how the primary harmonic aspects—the trine (1/3) and the square (1/4)—mathematically define the twelve segments of the zodiac.</note>

**The Fourth**

<summary>Kepler explains the twelve-fold division of the zodiac through three lenses: the observation of lunar quarters, the four natural seasons marked by the Sun, and the geometric harmony of planetary aspects.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s critique of the traditional justifications for the twelve-fold division of the zodiac. Having discussed the solar and lunar cycles, he now addresses and refutes the arguments of medieval astrologers like Albumasar regarding the number of constellations and the mathematical precision of astrological signs.</meta>

16 **BY JOHANNES KEPLER**

Albumasar <note>Abu Ma'shar, a 9th-century Persian astrologer whose works were highly influential in Medieval Europe.</note> alleges a fourth, but futile, reason: that the Ancients found forty-eight figures in the heavens, and because twelve of these fall within the zodiac, just as many parts were made of it. For, to begin my refutation from the final cause, in ancient times only eleven figures fell into the zodiac: for those stars we now attribute to **Libra** <note>the Scales</note> were counted as the **Claws** <note>original: *Chelis*</note> of the **Scorpion**. Therefore, let Albumasar hear this: the twelve parts of the zodiac were not established because twelve images of constellations were found; on the contrary, the stars in the vicinity of the zodiac were distributed into a count of precisely twelve because the twelve-fold division of the zodiac had been accepted for other reasons. Add to this that the image of a constellation does not constitute a "sign." For some are extended beyond a twelfth part of the circuit, while others are contracted. 

Truly, the **Signs** of the Astrologers are measured so minutely into twelve sections, that **Jupiter** at 29 degrees, 59 minutes, and 59 seconds of **Gemini**, is considered to be in its detriment <note>a state of astrological weakness</note>, having received five "black stones" <note>original: *atros calculos*; a metaphor for negative points or tallies in a scoring system</note>; yet after a minute of time, once the transition into 0 degrees, 0 minutes, and 1 second of **Cancer** is made, it is now lucky, now in its exaltation <note>a state of astrological strength</note>, and enjoys four "white stones," if it pleases the gods. So much for this fourth reason of Albumasar’s. 

Let us speak also of the three previous reasons, so it may be clear that they do not result in twelve signs in the zodiac being distinguished by true and natural boundaries, or by various and different qualities. Regarding the first and third reasons, it is not difficult to prove that they do not establish constant starting points for this division. Thus, if for example the 19th degree of **Sagittarius** received a power of "dryness" from the conjunction of the luminaries <note>the Sun and Moon</note>: after some months have passed, a full moon falling on that same 19th degree of Sagittarius will instead pour into it a power of "moistening." The same can be said of the aspects of the **Planets**, which they carry with their own bodies through the entire circuit. 

Since, however, the zodiac signs of the astrologers are said to have constant starting points, these reasons mean nothing to them—except for this one thing: that they invited the first astronomers from afar toward the precise number of twelve. This number, along with the thirty-fold subdivision <note>dividing each of the 12 signs into 30 degrees to make 360</note>, they fully embraced—following not the nature of the motions (which differed somewhat), but their own judgment and the capacity of the number 360, which arises from **Geometry**, by a hidden instinct and manifest reason. 

Indeed, what I said about the inconstant boundary of division also applies to the second reason; for even the Sun transposes this starting point of its—namely the first point of **Aries**—under the sphere of the fixed stars, which is the natural one <note>Kepler is referring to the precession of the equinoxes, where the "tropical" zodiac used by astrologers moves against the background of the actual "sidereal" stars</note>. Thus, Astrologers do not divide the precise circuit itself, but something a little more than the zodiac itself into twelve: just as those quarters mentioned above do, dividing something shorter than the whole...

<summary>Kepler refutes the idea that the zodiac has twelve signs because of the constellations, pointing out that constellations vary in size while astrological signs are arbitrary 30-degree mathematical constructs. He argues that the 360-degree system stems from geometric preference rather than the physical reality of planetary motion.</summary>
