->### ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 17<-

...the whole Zodiac, into twelve parts. However, that can be said against all three reasons: even if we were to grant that all lunations return to exactly the same spot after a completed year; likewise all solstices and all aspects; it would still be incredible that force is infused into the location of the zodiac itself by that which is beneath the zodiac. Suppose the 19th degree of **Sagittarius** is "dry" when the New Moon is under it; or the 4th of **Capricorn** "humid" when the Full Moon is under it. Let **Capricorn** be wintry when it holds the Sun; **Cancer**, truly, summer-like from the presence of the same star. I grant even something more: let **Cancer** be even more prolific when it hosts **Jupiter** as a guest than **Capricorn** is, by the benefit of that same **Jupiter**; so that not only do the stars themselves strike more strongly from on high than from a declivity <note>A reference to the "altitude" or house position of a planet affecting its strength.</note>, but even sublunary nature <note>The physical world below the moon, subject to change and decay.</note> itself, out of a certain reverence, shows itself more manageable and more subject to **Cancer**'s influence. Finally, let there be an effective conjunction <note>original: *copula*</note> at 0 degrees of **Aries** and 0 degrees of **Cancer** when **Saturn** is in the former and the Sun in the latter. But when these depart from those places, what remains to the parts of the zodiac from these "guests," by which they might be understood as naturally distinct from one another—whether by boundaries, or by the qualities of the parts lying between those boundaries? Or if something remains, is it not abolished when another of a contrary quality succeeds it? For it must not be entirely denied that, for example, the eighth part of **Sagittarius** might retain some force for a little while from the meeting of **Jupiter** and **Saturn** which occurred in that degree in the year 1603 <note>Kepler refers to the "Great Conjunction" of 1603, a major astronomical event that led to the appearance of a "New Star" or supernova in 1604.</note>; but this must be explained from a most abstruse harmonic reasoning, drawn by the mind from the heavens and led into the earth. By this reasoning, absolutely no natural quality is left to the sign of **Sagittarius** itself: and even this influence vanishes again over time.

I recognize the cardinal points <note>The solstices and equinoxes.</note> of the Sun as natural boundaries, and I confess that the power of the Sun is exerted while lingering in them; but by a reason far different from what pertains to this matter. For Astrologers believe that at the beginning of **Capricorn**, the "hot" parts of the zodiac are suddenly exchanged for "cold" ones, and precisely at the point of division. The Sun, however, decreases summer heat—as far as it is within itself (for what the elements contribute here is not the question)—up to this point, and from this peak begins to increase it again; thus at the point itself, it performs most slowly. Thus, what is nature's turning point <note>original: *metropolis*; used here to mean a central or pivotal point of power.</note> becomes for Astrologers the limits of two hostile regions.

That argumentation concerning the beginning, middle, and end is also not aptly transferred to the natural qualities of the parts. Every quantified thing, every arc, has a beginning and an end: but those boundaries are points; they are not parts of the thing. Truly, according to natural qualities, which themselves undergo dimensions, parts certainly differ from parts, the extremes from the mid-
                                        C                                        from the mid-

<summary>Kepler critiques the astrological belief that specific signs or degrees of the zodiac possess inherent, permanent physical qualities, arguing instead that any observed effects are due to planetary positions or "harmonic" interactions rather than the zodiac itself.</summary>

He concludes Chapter 4 by challenging astrologers to find better physical justifications for their systems and begins Chapter 5, which investigates whether the names of the zodiac signs (like Aries or Leo) reflect the actual nature of the stars or are merely human inventions.</meta>

from the middle; but not by the forms of hostile qualities, but by degrees of the same quality. In this way, if they began the summer quadrant from 15 degrees of **Taurus**, the autumnal from 15 degrees of **Leo**, and the rest likewise; they would indeed philosophize with better justification regarding the three parts of the quadrant—the heat being relaxed at the extremes and intense in the middle; but they would still be bound by the previous arguments.
      Therefore, to conclude, these causes provided occasions for human choice to embrace this form of division; they were not taken from the nature of the thing divided, nor do they express a natural division, but a purely geometric or arithmetic one. Go, Astrologers, seek other reasons.

***

Whether the names of the signs are arbitrary, or truly natural and meaningful.
CHAPTER FIVE.

I shall speak, therefore, in the second place, about the origin of the names with which the stars are marked. And there are indeed great men who think that the names of the constellations were taken from a hidden property in which these stars excel over others, and which were discovered for the purpose of examining human horoscopes <note>original: *geneseibus*—the casting of birth charts or nativities</note>. Those who feel this way may also call upon those many thousands of years of the Indians, Chaldeans, and Egyptians to support this opinion, so as to allow enough time for the ancients to investigate these things. Although I employ this argument in a different way than Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous critic of astrology whose work Kepler frequently engaged with</note>, not intending to strip from the Astrologers all experience of all dogmas as he does, but to show that these specific names did not come to the stars from experience, unless it is established that—with experience as a guide, as in other arts—some things were also corrected and changed for the better over the course of centuries. It is well established from Cicero, Ptolemy, Manilius <note>A Roman poet who wrote 'Astronomica'</note>, and Firmicus <note>Julius Firmicus Maternus, a 4th-century author of a major astrological compendium</note> that this nomenclature of the signs was not merely imported into the Roman Empire under the Emperors Augustus and Tiberius, but was received from antiquity along with sound knowledge. It is also clear enough from the Prophet Isaiah that under the Babylonian Monarchs, when eclipses began to be recorded, Astrology was practiced even to the point of public madness, and stood as the mother of the chief idolatry; while men served the fictitious spirits of the Planets under the pretext of "elections" <note>The astrological practice of choosing the most auspicious time to start an event</note>. What shall I say of Moses? Some who read that he was learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians say he excelled in Astrology, which is confirmed by Lucian. And he was not more than 800 years distant from the deluge <note>The Great Flood of Noah</note>. And yet also, shortly...

<summary>Kepler concludes that zodiac divisions are mathematical rather than physical, then begins investigating the origins of constellation names, weighing traditional claims of ancient Egyptian and Babylonian wisdom against the lack of historical evidence for "empirical" updates to these names.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 5 of Kepler's treatise. Having previously argued that zodiac divisions are mathematical, he now argues that the names of constellations were invented by ancient humans—specifically farmers and sailors—for practical time-keeping and navigation, rather than being derived from the intrinsic nature of the stars themselves.</meta>

ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 19

just a little while before, in the time of Holy Job <note>Kepler refers to the biblical Book of Job, which mentions constellations like Orion and the Pleiades, to show that star names were already established in antiquity.</note>, the names of certain stars had become well known. Therefore, the time available for establishing this science of the effects of the signs, and for deriving the figures of the constellations from that science, is quite brief; conversely, the time during which the ancient method of naming the stars has endured until now without any change is rendered quite long. For what novelty did the diligence of the Arabs introduce into this art that cannot be reduced to the ancient principles known from Ptolemy and Manilius <note>Marcus Manilius, author of the 1st-century astrological poem *Astronomica*.</note>, and derived from them? Thus, by reasoning from old principles and the names of the stars, rather than by experimenting with new ones, they expanded the discipline.

      But let us hear on this matter from a man of that Arabic era, barely imbued with the astronomy reborn from Arabic manuscripts.

      Human conviction, says Johannes de Sacrobosco <note>Johannes de Sacrobosco (c. 1195–1256) was a scholar whose textbook *De Sphaera Mundi* was the standard astronomical manual for centuries.</note>, gave numbers and names to the stars, not from their own nature, but for a distinction suited to their significance; whence Bernard Silvestris <note>A 12th-century Platonic philosopher and poet.</note> says:

            *Lest general things wander without a common voice,*
            *The names that the stars now have, man created.*
            <note>original: *Communi ne voce res generalis oberrent, / Quæ modò sunt stellis nomina, fecit homo.*</note>

Likewise Virgil in the first book of the *Georgics*:

      *While the sailor gave numbers and names to the stars:*
            *The Pleiades, the Hyades, and the bright Bear of Lycaon.*
            <note>original: *Navita dum stellis numeros & nomina fecit: / Pleiades Hyades, Claramq; Lycaonis Arcton.* "The Bear of Lycaon" refers to Ursa Major.</note>

      Nay, let us hear the more sensible of the Arabs themselves, especially Abraham Ibn Ezra <note>A 12th-century Jewish polymath whose works on astrology were highly influential in the Renaissance.</note>, whose words saying this very thing you may see in Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, in his famous critique of astrology.</note>, Book 8, chapter 15.

      It is altogether to be believed that there were two types of men who organized the stars into certain forms: Farmers and Sailors. Regarding the farmers, who seem more ancient, I have spoken above. For how could they understand that the Sun, having completed its period, stands in its former place, and the seasons return to places near the former ones, unless a distinction were made between the stars and names imposed? Nature led the way for those who would necessarily do this; the stars were scattered through the ultimate sphere with no perpetual order—that is, confusedly—lest men be deceived by a similarity of arrangement in different places. From the farmers, therefore, come **Aries** (the Ram), **Taurus** (the Bull), **Virgo** the ear-bearer <note>The star Spica in the constellation Virgo represents an ear of grain, signaling harvest time.</note>, **Caper** (the Goat), **Boötes** or the driver, **Heniochus** (the Charioteer), **Capella** (the little goat), the **Kids**, the **Wagon** or the **Bear** (names already known to Homer), and the like. From the sailors come the **Pleiades**, the **Ship** <note>The constellation Argo Navis.</note>, a constellation near the southern horizon—that is, touching the mountains or the sea whenever it is seen—and the like. From both, the **Hyades**. If any others seem to depart further from things familiar to both types of men, they were certainly taken from common daily use, from things striking the eyes: such as the **Hunter** (**Orion**), the **Beast**, the **Hare**, the **Dog**, the **Serpent**.

<margin>The reasons for the naming of the stars.</margin>
<margin>The arrangement of the stars.</margin>
If one may indulge in conjecture, there were two primary reasons why a selection of images and names—of which many more could have been imposed upon a single constellation based on the nature of things—were chosen: the arrangement of the stars, and the time of year suited to the works of either sailors or farmers. Indeed, anyone who looks at the stars of the Bear, the Serpent-Bearer <note>The constellation Ophiuchus.</note>, the Serpent, the Hydra, Orion, the Hare, the Dog, the Bull, the Twins, the Crab, the Lion, the wings in the Virgin, the Scorpion, the Arrow and the bow, the Hen <note>The constellation Cygnus, often called the Hen in early modern astronomy.</note>, the Raven, the Crown, the Triangle, and many other figures, sees a certain similarity in their arrangement to the limbs of the animals and things from which they take their names. This is especially true of those stars which have been added to the southern sky in our own memory through the diligence of the Spanish and the Dutch. One sees this more correctly in the sky than on a globe, because today these images are considered corrupted, having been shaped according to the whims of painters and sculptors, and they differ greatly from the stars to which they gave names and from the minds of the ancient authors.

Hear the verses of Aratus <note>Aratus of Soli (c. 310–240 BC), a Greek poet whose work *Phaenomena* described the constellations and was the standard astronomical reference in antiquity.</note> concerning the Bull:

<note>original Greek: "Τὰ δὲ οἱ μάλ’ ἐοικότα σήματα κεῖται / Τοίη ἡ κεφαλὴ διακέκριται : οὐδέ τις ἄλλῳ / Σήματι τεκμήραιτο κάρη βοός. οἷά μιν αὐτὸ / Ἀστέρες ἀμφοτέρωθεν ἑλισσόμενοι τυπόωσι."</note>
The signs lie there, looking very much like him;
Thus is his head distinguished: nor could anyone
By any other sign identify the head of a bull.
Such do the stars on both sides, revolving, shape it.

So indeed spoke Albumasar <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886), a leading Persian astrologer and astronomer of the Abbasid court in Baghdad.</note> earlier, repeating the cause of the images not from the inner secrets of nature, but from the authority of Aratus.

In turn, when one hears the names Pleiades, the Ear of Grain <note>The star Spica in the constellation Virgo.</note>, or the Grape-Gatherer <note>The star Vindemiatrix in Virgo.</note>,
<margin>The time of year.</margin>
and simultaneously considers that their rising served as a signal for the labors of sailing, harvesting, and gathering the vintage; what more hidden philosophy, I ask, does he seek in the reasoning behind these names? For the cause of the name is plain to the eye, and therefore not even the arrangement of the stars need be sought to establish the name.

Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), an Italian Renaissance philosopher who wrote a famous critique of astrology.</note> extends this cause to all images, though with a conjecture about human thoughts no less ridiculous than those thoughts of the astrologers regarding the natures of the signs, which he so greatly mocks; for he says it was their intention to express the seasons of the year through the twelve images of the Zodiac.

Let there also be a third cause, mixed with the first, also mentioned by Pico,
<margin>Myths and histories.</margin>
which brought it about that several constellations representing a human figure earned the names of certain individuals—namely, from histories whether true or fabled. In this category are Cepheus, Andromeda, Cassiopeia, Perseus, the Dragon, and others. Nor is it unlike the truth that, conversely, because the common folk of the farmers assigned the names of animals to certain stars,

### **ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 21**

...wrote that fables arose through poetic invention; it is to this that the Hydra, the Bowl <note>The constellation Crater.</note>, the Raven, the Bear, the Serpent-Bearer with the serpent, and similar figures seem to refer.

Lucian, laughing most skillfully at this human meddling in his little book *On Astrology*, makes it so that one can hardly discern whether he is playing games or being serious, unless one knows the man. Whatever animals he found in the various rites and religions of nations, he brought into the heavens; and whatever of this sort wanders in the sky, he dragged down to human superstitions.

Therefore, once all the constellations have been examined through these causes, and it appears that none are left for us without a cause <note>original Greek: ἀναίτιον (anaition), meaning "causeless" or "without a reason."</note>, will it not be clearly evident that these figures possess no inherent power of signifying? Unless, perhaps, there can be many causes for a single celestial figure; causes that conspire by chance and accident toward the same effect so many times and with such a marvelous consensus. Anyone who easily believes this suffers from a weakness of judgment and is worthy of breaking his brain over the Kabbalah of the Jews. For they, in exactly the same way—having neglected the most evident causes of derivations and origins—claim that their own language belongs to divine providence. They are persuaded that it was the purpose of God, the author of language, that the nature of things be depicted through the transposition of letters, and that certain unknown mysteries be hidden within complete sentences. But sober men, having confessed the power of divine providence with due veneration, understand languages used by either God or men just as they sound; they leave to human will its own place among causes, and its own genuine effects. These same men also embrace the obvious causes for the figures in the sky as arising from human will, and they do not search for loftier things concerning the hidden properties of figures which might have been discovered through the contemplation of horoscopes <note>The Latin *genesis* here refers to the casting of nativities or birth charts.</note>.

But the students of this "painted philosophy" <note>Kepler’s term for the belief that the literal pictures of constellations hold secret wisdom.</note> will object that it is credible that <margin>Whether and...</margin> God the Creator chose living creatures that share the same properties He bestowed upon the constellations. <margin>Creation.</margin> They argue He expressed their shapes through the arrangement of the stars so that the figure, by reminding a man of a certain creature, might simultaneously remind him of that animal’s properties, which are shared by these stars.

Whoever affirms this uses both confidence and temerity. For not all names come from the arrangement of the stars; on the contrary, different names have often arisen from the same arrangement. Even if this were not so, another person could just as easily deny this theology as someone of this sort has affirmed it.

Come now, I will even grant something more; let it be that the whole sublunary nature... <margin>Whether and...</margin>

[margin: Sympathy with sublunary <note>Meaning the Earthly realm, "below the moon."</note> things.]
...although it <note>Referring to the "generative nature" or the Earth's vital force discussed on the previous page.</note> does not use reasoning, it might nevertheless, for the same reasons as the human imagination, imagine the same thing about these stars as a human does. For this true dogma concerning the color of stars (against which Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), who wrote a famous critique of astrology.</note> achieved nothing) I will not simply deny regarding their shape; nor will I examine it more accurately at present. Yet what does this have to do with the star itself in the heavens? Or what new property can really be joined to the star itself from the imaginations of men and of the generative nature?

[margin: The stars have left their places.]
What shall we say of the most perplexing dispute being waged among modern astrologers over this business of shapes? For the stars have now entirely departed from the twelve divisions <note>The twelve "signs" of the zodiac.</note> to which they gave their names of shapes, two thousand years having passed since the equinoxes began to recede <note>This refers to the "precession of the equinoxes," where the positions of the stars slowly shift relative to the Earth's seasonal calendar.</note>. It is from this point that the beginning of the zodiac’s twelve divisions is taken. To take an example from our own chart: which sign of the zodiac shall we say provided a home for the meeting of Saturn and Jupiter, and for the New Star <note>The supernova of 1604, now called "Kepler's Supernova."</note>? Sagittarius or Scorpio? If you look at the equinoxes, the place of the conjunction is the eighth degree of Sagittarius, and the place of the new star is the eighteenth degree of the same sign. But if you look at the stars themselves, the stars of Sagittarius—which gave the name "Sagittarius" to the third sign from the equinox—have now moved into the adjacent sign, which begins from the winter solstice (named after Capricorn). Conversely, not far from the place of the meeting, the star called the Heart of Scorpio <note>The star Antares.</note> is found. Therefore, if we look at the fixed stars, this conjunction of the higher planets and this new star fell within the constellation of Scorpio.

[margin: From where the Zodiac is named.]
But enough on the origin of names. Let us conclude this topic with an explanation of the word *Zodiac*. The Latins translated it most appropriately as *Signifer* <note>meaning "The Sign-Bearer."</note>; the Germans unfortunately as *den Thierkreyss* <note>original: "Thierkreyss"; modern German: "Tierkreis," meaning "Circle of Animals."</note>, but more genuinely as *den Bilderkreyss* <note>original: "Bilderkreyss"; modern: "Bilderkreis," meaning "Circle of Images."</note>. The Greek word *Zodion* <note>original Greek: Ζώδιον</note> is not "a little animal" here, but any sort of image <note>original: "Idolum"</note> of a man, whether painted, sculpted, or cast. In this sense *ta Zodia* <note>original Greek: τὰ Ζώδια, meaning "the figures."</note> is used by Hero in his work on automatons <note>original: "spiritalibus," referring to Hero of Alexandria’s *Pneumatica*, which describes mechanical devices.</note>; in this sense also Cicero and other Latin writers use the word *Signa* <note>meaning "Signs" or "Statues."</note>. To Aratus <note>A Greek poet who wrote the *Phaenomena*, a famous description of the constellations.</note>, the arrangements of the fixed stars are "signs of limbs" <note>original Greek: σήματα (sēmata), meaning marks or signs.</note> in animals; but it is not likely that the person who first said "celestial Signs" was looking toward the word *sēma* rather than the word *Zodion*. But whoever translated the twelve signs into German as *die zwölff Zeichen* <note>The twelve signs.</note> either did not attend to the genuine meaning of the word *Signum* (which in this context means the same as *Zodion*, or in German, *Bild* <note>Image or picture.</note>), or the word *Zeichen* was once broader than it is today, also encompassing the meaning of the word *Bild*. For today the word *Zeichen* signifi-

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s linguistic and physical analysis of the Zodiac. On the previous page, he began discussing the word "sign" (signum) in relation to the constellation Sagittarius. Here, he explores the various definitions of "sign" to show that its astrological use is often more metaphorical or arbitrary than physical.</meta>

ON THE FIERY TRIGON <note>In astrology, a "Trigon" (or triplicity) is a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element. The "Fiery Trigon" consists of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note>. 23

signifies generally either an indication of any kind—such as the swellings and rashes of the plague, or the sexual genitals—or any kind of written mark, such as the written symbols of the Planets and signs; or any sign placed for the sake of memory, whether in writing, such as obelisks <note>original: "obelisci"; these were marks (often †) used by scholars to indicate doubtful text in manuscripts.</note>, asterisks, hands <note>referring to "manicules," the small pointing hand symbols drawn in margins.</note>, indexes, or the letters N.B. <note>Nota Bene, meaning "Note Well."</note>; or outside of writing, such as heaps of stones, crosses, knots in the branches of trees, brands, incisions, and similar things: in which sense the word by no means fits the celestial signs. But specifically, to Germans, the word *Zeychen* <note>original: "Zeychen," a contemporary spelling of the German "Zeichen" (sign).</note> sounds like a celestial omen, a portent, or a miracle; again, this is not quite properly used regarding the signs of the Zodiac; yet it is somewhat more tolerable insofar as we Astrologers have fashioned more portentous monsters in the sky.

these lower things do not heat up, except by accident, unless a certain degree of heating has already occurred.
Much less do they cool things down by their own nature. To the extent that anything provides heat,
to that same extent they are themselves hot; but I assert that the Sun is the most burning of all: hence, as far as I am concerned, there is no distinction of signs based on these qualities.

But why are three adjacent signs—Gemini, Cancer, and Leo—not all "fiery" since they are the 
summer signs? Or why are the autumn signs not "earthy," the winter signs "watery," and the spring signs "airy"?

The reason why this was omitted depends on the accepted number of the division.
For since they had made twelve parts of the zodiac, it followed that two 
adjacent signs would be divided by contrary qualities, unless they wished for those signs 
to be considered as a single part—signs which they, certainly with great reason, had divided into three.

Why then are individual signs assigned to these specific qualities held by 
Astrologers, and not to their opposites?

Two reasons are brought forward: the first is from the nature of the images themselves. For Cancer,
Scorpio, and Pisces are aquatic animals; therefore, these signs were considered watery.
Conversely, Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius (the latter being a horse in its lower part) are animals
of a fiery nature; for which reason these signs are also fiery. Likewise, since these three,
as well as Gemini and Aquarius, have a masculine appearance—and since males possess more 
abundant heat—Gemini, Libra, and Aquarius share in that heat.
However, this cause is not universal: for why is Aquarius not "moist," and why are Taurus and 
Cancer not "fiery"? And why are Taurus and Capricorn "feminine" signs? Add to this the fact that the actual constellations <note>original: "imagines"; referring to the physical stars of the constellations.</note> have moved away from their [assigned] signs; yet the qualities remain according to the astrologers' own dogma <note>Kepler is referring to the precession of the equinoxes, where the physical stars no longer align with the 30-degree "signs" used in tropical astrology.</note>.

Let us therefore hear what Albumasar <note>Albumasar (787–886 AD) was a famous Persian astrologer whose works were highly influential in Medieval and Renaissance Europe.</note> has to say. First, he places a less-than-trustworthy foundation for this distribution among the elements: the division of the 
signs into masculine and feminine. Why are they alternately masculine
and feminine, starting from Aries? It pleased the authors, as 
Ptolemy says, to take the opportunity of the perpetual alternation between day and 
night, so that the signs—which were twelve in number—would be distributed 
into six "marriages," where each male would be joined to his own female. For perhaps 
they would not even seem to be twelve unless they were distinguished by the sex of those adjacent to them. Truly, I find no sounder reason than this.

Here, then, are twelve signs, alternately masculine and feminine: and there are
four elements, each of which, once the distribution is made equally, 
claims three signs for itself. Thus, either three adjacent signs must be "hot," or—because this 
is not possible (lest adjacent signs merge into one)—as a compensation, the three 
must be arranged in a triangular shape <note>a "Trigon" or "Triplicity".</note> throughout the zodiac. Since Aries 
is the first and most important sign of the zodiac, and Fire is the primary element, 
endowed with heat—which is the active quality for life—it is therefore

# ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 25

...masculine. Therefore, Aries, and the signs forming a triangle with it—Leo and Sagittarius—are fiery. In the first quadrant, Aries is the beginning; in the second, Leo is the middle; in the third, Sagittarius is the end. The fourth winter quadrant lacks a fiery sign.

<note>A circular astrological diagram illustrates the "Fiery Trigon." A triangle connects the signs of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. At the vertices of the triangle, specific years are listed for the "Great Conjunctions"—rare alignments of the planets Jupiter (♃) and Saturn (♄) that occur every 20 years and were thought to signal major historical changes. The years listed include 1583, 1643, 1703, 1763 at the top; 1623, 1683, 1743 at the bottom left; and 1599, 1659, 1719 at the bottom right.</note>

But why are the three succeeding signs—Taurus, Virgo, and Capricorn—earthy rather than airy, since air immediately follows fire <note>In the traditional Aristotelian hierarchy of elements, the order is usually Fire, Air, Water, Earth.</note>? Albumasar <note>original: "Albumasar." This refers to Abu Ma'shar al-Balkhi (787–886), a Persian astrologer whose works were the foundation of medieval European astrology.</note> offers this reason: that heat has the power of giving life, while cold likewise has a power, but one of mortifying and corrupting. Every active quality, he argues, is nobler than a passive one. Therefore, Taurus, which immediately follows Aries, was assigned the other active quality—namely cold—and among the elements, Earth, which is the seat of cold. Thus, whatever proportion exists between life and death, day and night, or male and female, this same proportion seemed to exist between heat and cold. For even the female performs some action in the act of generation (for we must help the miserably struggling Albumasar here), and she does not behave in a purely passive manner.

Albumasar has stated why he thinks a fiery sign should be followed by a cold one; let him also state why it should be dry rather than moist.

First, he says, if Taurus had been established as moist, Cancer would have had to be dry. But it was better for Taurus to be dry so that two paired signs might agree in one passive and material quality: Aries and Taurus in dryness; Gemini and Cancer in moisture; Leo and Virgo in dryness; and so on.

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s critique of traditional astrology, specifically the arbitrary way elements (fire, earth, air, water) were assigned to the zodiac signs. On the previous page, he began questioning the logic of the "Fiery Trigon" and the influence of the medieval Persian astrologer Albumasar.</meta>

26 ->OF JOHANNES KEPLER<-

	It seems that here Albumasar is revealing some treasures from the Kabbalah <note>original: "Cabala." Kepler refers to Jewish mystical traditions, often used in the Renaissance to imply secret or esoteric knowledge.</note>. For he explains the nature of things almost as if playing a game. It is altogether necessary for a male and female to agree in species so that they may propagate that species, yet they must be distinguished from one another by sex. Therefore, sketching this out according to Albumasar’s opinion, those ancients said that two contiguous signs, which they coupled in "marriage," agree in their material quality <note>In Aristotelian physics, "material" qualities are the passive ones: dryness and moisture.</note> but are distinguished by active qualities <note>The "active" qualities are heat and cold, which were thought to drive biological or physical change.</note>, acting as certain reproductive organs. And since there were only two passive qualities—the dry and the moist—they established two types of "marriages," which were assigned alternating places in the zodiac. Just as in the nature of things there is one type of coupling between a male and female human, and another between cattle. Perhaps they even alluded to the mutual successions of summer and winter, and in both, the succession of day and night.
	However these things may be, although I am rightly delighted by this ingenious imitation of nature and the distribution of qualities into the signs, I do not value it more than is proper. If the first authors borrowed a certain law from the nature of things for their own arbitrary reasonings, it does not mean that the entire remaining nature of the heavens has, in turn, subjected itself to these reasonings of theirs. It is like a gardener who, looking at the number five—which is common in flowers—decides to build pentagonal garden beds; he does not, for that reason, cause the roses to grow more successfully in them. This is because the nature of the flower, although it reminds the reasoning gardener of the pentagon, does not receive a reciprocal benefit from the gardener’s actual pentagon.
	Indeed, it appears more likely from a careful weighing of these causes that the signs of the zodiac were named after the elements by the mere whim of their inventors; in reality, however, they are connected by no close kinship with those elements from which each is named.
	Finally, these reasons for the naming conventions are such that the names "Fiery," "Airy," "Watery," and "Earthy" remain the same even as the fixed stars—or the images of the constellations—move away from them <note>This refers to the "Precession of the Equinoxes." Because the Earth wobbles on its axis, the actual stars (constellations) slowly shift out of alignment with the 30-degree divisions of the zodiac (signs).</note>.
	For if the person who first deliberated which signs should be named after which elements had looked at the fixed stars, he would have called Taurus "hot and moist." He would have called it "hot" because of the fiery brightness of Aldebaran <note>original: "Palilicij." An ancient name for the bright star Aldebaran in the constellation Taurus.</note>, and "moist" because of the Pleiades and Hyades, which were believed to generate rains.
	In the same way, he would have called Scorpio "fiery" because of the fiery star Antares <note>original: "Antareos." The bright red star at the heart of Scorpio.</note>, a star of the first magnitude, which was very close to the recent conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter <note>Jupiter and Saturn meet roughly every 20 years. Kepler is likely referring to the great conjunction of 1603/1604, which he famously observed.</note>.
	Thus you see that these names, taken from the elements, did not come to the signs out of respect for the fixed stars; therefore, they are not extinguished when the fixed stars depart from those signs.

He notes that these assignments are based on the abstract divisions of the zodiac rather than the actual stars, which have shifted due to precession.</summary>

->What natural cause joins the signs of the Zodiac, separated by a third of a circle, into a single Triplicity?<-

## CHAPTER SEVEN.

So far, then, we have discussed what the **Fiery Trigon** means in an astrological sense: namely, that there are three twelfth-parts of the zodiac, arranged in a triangle across the zodiac, and named after fire. However, since I have maintained that both the division of the zodiac into twelve parts and the naming of those parts—whether after sexes, animals, or elements—arise from mere human choice rather than the nature of things, you might rightly ask further: is there no other natural reason available by which these three signs are joined in the form of a triangle?

Therefore, just as I said above regarding the division of the zodiac itself—that nature does not precisely divide it into twelve parts on its own, yet it provides the *occasions* for adopting such a division (since the Moon meets with the Sun twelve times in any given year)—so too, even if the nature of celestial movements does not describe these **Trigons** to the letter as they are defined by the authors, it nonetheless provides the opportunities for observing them. For Saturn and Jupiter, the highest planets, arrange their two closest successive meetings in such a way that they are separated by nearly a third of the zodiac. By this reasoning, it happens that in any given age, three signs of the zodiac (which the authors have grouped under one trigon) obtain a special power from the conjunctions <note>The "conjunction" is when two planets appear in the same spot in the sky. For Saturn and Jupiter, this was called the "Great Conjunction."</note> of these higher planets to stir up (I do not say to compel) the nature of sublunary things. This part of astrology has not yet been snatched away from me by **Giovanni Pico della Mirandola** <note>original: "Joan: Picus Mirandulanus." Pico (1463–1494) was a famous philosopher who wrote a massive critique against astrology. Kepler agreed with many of his points but felt the physical movements of the planets still held some significance.</note>; although I subscribe to most of what he argued in his twelve books against astrologers, provided they are understood soberly and according to the weight of the arguments used.

The movement of the Moon, compared with the movement of the Sun, suggests something similar. Indeed, if the Moon holds Aries, after one year has returned it occupies Leo, and in the second year Sagittarius, or the beginning of Capricorn.

Now, therefore, it is time for me to explain the second meaning of the phrase **Fiery Trigon**, which is the proper focus of this section. <margin>What the Fiery Triplicity is.</margin> For astrologers use the term **Fiery Trigon** to refer to a period of nearly two hundred years; within this time, the meetings of the higher planets, Saturn and Jupiter, occur frequently in only the signs of the **Fiery Triplicity**: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.
                                       D 2             for us

<summary>Kepler argues that while the twelve zodiac signs are human inventions, the "Fiery Trigon" has a basis in the physical movements of Saturn and Jupiter, which meet in a triangular pattern over long cycles of time. He defends this physical astrology while still respecting the skepticism of critics like Pico della Mirandola.</summary>

<keywords>Fiery Trigon, Triplicity, Great Conjunction, Saturn, Jupiter, Pico della Mirandola, Zodiac, Astrological History</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s mathematical demonstration of how the movements of the planets Saturn and Jupiter naturally create the triangular groupings of the zodiac signs known as the Trigons, which he introduced on the previous page.</meta>

28 JOANNES KEPLER

appear to us indeed dwelling on the earth: where it usually happens that before those slow ones <note>The "slow ones" refer to the superior planets, Saturn and Jupiter, which take the longest time to orbit the Sun.</note> depart from these signs, all the remaining planets follow and are joined with those slow ones within the borders of these signs.

How it could happen, however, that the meetings of the superior planets fall only into the signs of one **Triplicity** <note>A Triplicity, or Trigon, is a group of three zodiac signs associated with one of the four elements. The Fiery Trigon consists of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note> will be revealed as follows: Saturn traverses the zodiac in 30 years. Jupiter in 12 years. Therefore, the annual journey of Saturn is a thirtieth, and of Jupiter a twelfth of the whole path; subtract a thirtieth from a twelfth, and a twentieth remains. Jupiter therefore overtakes Saturn annually by a twentieth of the whole path: thus in twenty years, it catches up to Saturn again. But Jupiter himself measures out individual signs each year, that is, twelfths of the zodiac; therefore in twenty years, he travels twenty signs—which is to say, a complete circle plus eight additional signs.

Suppose a conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter occurs in Sagittarius in the year 1603. After 12 years have passed, Jupiter returns to Sagittarius, but Saturn remains in Taurus; after 8 more years, Saturn is moved from Taurus into Leo, and Jupiter likewise from Sagittarius into Leo, and there <margin>See the diagram on page 25.</margin> he overtakes Saturn. After another 20 years, the same happens in Aries; where you see that between Sagittarius, Leo, and Aries, a triangle exists.

By the same reasoning, it will also become clear how it happens that these meetings pass from one **Trigon** into another after 200 years. For those things already said regarding the thirtieth and <margin>The duration of the Fiery Trigon.</margin> twelfth part of the path do not hold exactly so, nor does Jupiter precisely finish a sign within a year. Thus it happens that two meetings are not distant by exactly a third part of the circle, but by about three degrees less. Therefore, three degrees multiplied ten times complete one sign; thus ten meetings, starting from the beginning of a sign, slide toward the end of the sign, and on the eleventh repetition they pass to the beginning of the following sign, which begins a new **Triplicity**.

Thus the four **Triplicities**, in which the whole zodiac is contained, extended over two hundred years each, create a period of a little less than eight hundred years; in which space of time the entire zodiac is divided by forty meetings into nearly as many equal parts: and when that time has passed, it returns to the beginning. Therefore I can say that, in place of the conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter which was in the year 1544 at 30 degrees of Scorpio, the closest in both time and place is this one, which occurred in December of the year 1603 at 8 degrees of Sagittarius, such that within eight hundred years none will approach closer to 30 degrees of Scorpio.

<margin>Which return of the Fiery Trigon this is.</margin> From this place, therefore, once a comparison is made with the age of the world, the succession of the trigons and the return of the fiery one becomes clear. For since roughly 5,600 years are numbered from the founding of the world, these divided by 800 constitute seven great periods and returns of the **Fiery Trigon**.

<margin>The usefulness of the teaching of the Trigons in the history of the world's ages.</margin>

It is truly remarkable that the most significant <term>epochs</term> <note>Kepler refers to major historical turning points or eras that define the "ages" of the world.</note> fall almost exactly at the joints of these periods. For this reason, I am greatly delighted by this series of Trigons; they serve as a kind of summary of time, most useful for aiding the memory. I also believe this will be most welcome to many in this current age, because our own time falls within one of these specific joints.

The fiery group leads the family of periods, both for the reason I just mentioned regarding the epochs, and also because it shares its starting point with the beginning of Aries, which is the most prominent of the <term>cardinal points</term> <note>The four points of the zodiac—the equinoxes and solstices—that mark the change of seasons. Aries marks the vernal equinox.</note>.

Accept in this table, using rounded and not strictly precise numbers, the centuries in which the Fiery Trigon began.

<margin>Where the beginning of the fiery triplicity is to be taken from.</margin>

Having progressed this far, a doubt arises concerning the genuine beginning of this fiery Trigon. For if we look at the <term>mean motions</term> <note>A calculation of planetary positions based on an average speed, rather than their actual, variable speeds.</note>, the "mean" or theoretical conjunction is placed by Cardano <note>Gerolamo Cardano (1501–1576), an influential Italian polymath who wrote extensively on mathematics and astrology.</note> in the year 1583, at the very point of Aries. It is indeed a beautiful thing to give the Trigon a beginning from a conjunction that is near a cardinal point—for one such point exists within every <term>triplicity</term> <note>The four groups of three zodiac signs, each associated with an element: Fire, Earth, Air, and Water.</note>.

30 JOHANNES KEPLER

However, the mean motions <note>The "mean motion" is a mathematical average of a planet's speed, assuming a perfectly circular orbit, used as a baseline for calculations.</note> according to the Prutenic calculation <note>original: "calculo Prutenico." The Prutenic Tables were astronomical tables published by Erasmus Reinhold in 1551, based on Copernicus's heliocentric theory.</note> suggest otherwise, showing a conjunction at 28 degrees and 14 minutes of Pisces, rather than at the very beginning of Aries. The true conjunction, however, falls even further back, specifically at the 21st degree of Pisces.

The conjunction that occurred in the year 1603 fell at 1 degree and 7 minutes of Sagittarius in terms of mean motions, but at 8 degrees of Sagittarius in terms of true motions; by either measure, it was the first to occur in the fiery signs <note>The "fiery signs" are Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. This event marked the beginning of a new 200-year cycle.</note>.

Yet even so, not all dispute is removed. For it is said that this period of time is claimed for the Fiery Trigon <note>A "Trigon" is a group of three zodiac signs forming a triangle; the "Fiery Trigon" consists of the signs associated with the element of fire.</note>, during which no meeting of the superior planets <note>The "superior planets" are those further from the Sun than Earth—in Kepler’s time, Saturn and Jupiter.</note> occurs except in fiery signs. And yet, although this holds true for the years 1603 and 1623, it changes in the year 1643. For then the mean conjunction is in Aries, but the true one is at 26 degrees of Pisces, as <term>retrogradation</term> <note>The apparent backward motion of a planet in the sky caused by the Earth passing it in orbit.</note> pulls the planets forward; although, by themselves, due to physical and optical causes of motion (which the ancients expressed by the terms <term>Equant</term> <note>An imaginary mathematical point used in old astronomy to explain why planets seem to speed up or slow down.</note> and <term>Eccentric</term> <note>An orbital path where the center of the circle is offset from the Earth or Sun.</note>), Saturn is slow in its motion from Sagittarius into Pisces, and Jupiter from Libra into Pisces, and they have subtractive <term>equations</term> <note>In historical astronomy, an "equation" was the difference between the observed position of a planet and its calculated "mean" or average position.</note>. Therefore, all these things must be understood in a broad sense. For if the apparent truth of the motions is observed, there will not even be a triangle <note>A "Trine" aspect, where planets are 120 degrees apart.</note> between the two most recent meetings. In the year 1583, the conjunction was at 21 degrees of Pisces; in the year 1603, it was at 8 degrees of Sagittarius. The interval, therefore, is not 120 degrees (which is a perfect Trigon), nor 117 (which is ordinary between mean conjunctions), but 102 degrees—closer to a <term>square</term> <note>An aspect where planets are 90 degrees apart, usually considered a "harsh" or difficult alignment in traditional astrology.</note> than to a Trigon, as you can see in the diagram.

->**On the natural powers of the Fiery Triplicity: and first, on the efficacy of Conjunctions, against Giovanni Pico della Mirandola.**<-

### CHAPTER EIGHT.

Thus far, I have said that neither the distribution of the zodiac into twelve signs is "natural," nor is this distinction into four triplicities—and the connection of three distant points—produced by the motions of the superior planets, entirely perpetual or accurate. It has also been said that neither do the signs possess any properties of those animals <note>Kepler argues that the sign "Aries" (the Ram) does not actually possess the physical or spiritual nature of a ram.</note>, nor do the Triplicities of signs contain the qualities of those elements (Fire, Earth, Air, Water) from which they are named. <margin>Book 6, chapter 4.</margin> Since that famous Giovanni Pico of Mirandola <note>Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494) was a philosopher whose "Disputations Against Divinatory Astrology" was the era's most famous attack on the practice.</note> <margin>Book 6, chapter 15.</margin> taught all these things more than a hundred years before me...

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the timing and movement of the "Fiery Trigon." Kepler now transitions into a philosophical defense of his theories against the famous skeptical arguments of Giovanni Pico della Mirandola.</meta>

**ON THE FIERY TRIGON.** 31

...Pico, <note>The OCR begins with "cus," the end of "Picus" (Pico della Mirandola), whose name was split across the page break.</note> Count of Mirandola: I might seem to agree entirely with his opinion regarding the vanity of astrology.

And not to be too long-winded, that author in Book 5, Chapter 5, rejected the effects of the great conjunctions <note>A "Great Conjunction" is the meeting of Saturn and Jupiter, occurring roughly every 20 years.</note> entirely; in Book 6, Chapters 5, 6, and 7, he rejected all astrological aspects <note>An "aspect" is the specific geometric angle between two planets, such as a square (90°) or trine (120°).</note> in general; and in Book 6, Chapter 15, he denied that there was any power inherent in the Trigons <note>The "Trigons" are groupings of zodiac signs by element: Fire, Earth, Air, and Water.</note>, to which point certain arguments from Book 6, Chapter 4, can also be referred. Since I hold a different view from the author on these chapters, among others, I shall weigh his reasons—but only after I have explained my own view, for doing so will serve our present purpose.

Pico asks in Book 5, Chapter 5: why should one believe that Saturn and Jupiter accomplish more when they are together than when they are separate? <margin>The source of the effects that experience attributes</margin> I shall answer according to my own view, not that of the astrologers: the work that we ascribe to the planets when they are joined, which does not occur when they are separate, is by no means a result of the planets themselves (aside from their simple light and heat). Rather, it is the work of sublunary nature <term>sublunary nature: in Kepler’s philosophy, the Earth itself possesses a "soul" or animating faculty that perceives and reacts to the geometric patterns formed by the planets</term> itself. For if this work were attributed to the planets themselves, <margin>to the conjunctions of the planets.</margin> one might respond with the principle that "united virtue is stronger." But as it stands, although they do indeed move sublunary nature, they do not move it as "natural agents" that pour out some physical power because of the presence of a material thing; instead, they move nature just as objects move the senses—as light moves the eyes, sound the hearing, and heat the touch. For none of these sensations is weakened simply because the senses of many people are open to receive them; rather, a certain natural and immaterial outflow of the quality they possess is granted to all these things. This quality—the "species" <note>In medieval and Renaissance physics, a "species" was an immaterial image or form transmitted from an object to the observer.</note> of the object—strikes the senses until they are exhausted, yet without exhausting its own source.

Here some philosopher might expect me to apply what I have said in these examples regarding the various qualities of things to the light of the stars. Thus, he will claim to know in advance what I am about to conclude: namely, that Pico's reasoning still holds against me, since the conjunction of two planets creates a light no greater than the sum of both, because they contribute only as much to the meeting as they possessed when separate.

However, I am by no means speaking of light at present—which certainly possesses heat and brightness according to the nature of the body, and not according to the mutual proximity of two planets (one of which is not the cause of light for the other).

Indeed, I have used the comparison of light and other sensible things to explain an obscure matter. For in the conjunction of two planets, it is no longer the light, but this very relationship—this very position <margin>The conjunctions of the planets themselves,</margin> which we call a "conjunction"—that acts as the object. As is the object, so is the "sense" within sublunary nature. The object belongs to the category of "relations"; therefore—

<summary>Kepler begins a rebuttal of Pico della Mirandola’s skepticism toward astrology, arguing that celestial influence is not a physical force from the planets but a reaction by the Earth’s own "nature" to geometric relationships.</summary>
<keywords>Pico della Mirandola, Great Conjunction, sublunary nature, celestial influence, astrology, planetary aspects, light and sense, geometry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s rebuttal of Pico della Mirandola. Having argued that the "sublunary nature" (the soul of the Earth or individual organisms) is what perceives and reacts to planetary alignments, Kepler now uses examples from biology to illustrate how an internal "formative faculty" responds to external visual patterns.</meta>

...colored drops exist in a flower, while the rest of the flower is imbued with another color? Indeed, why is the colored sap not digested so that, stripped of its own properties, it might pass into the body of the plant—as you see happen with other properties of nourishment? Therefore, you must admit that some **impression** exists upon a certain spiritual **formative faculty**, <note>Latin: *facultas formatrix*. Kepler identifies this as the internal "architect" or vital force within a living thing (or the Earth) that translates patterns and "blueprints" into physical reality.</note> which others call the **seminal reason**. <term>seminal reason: a translation of *ratio seminaria* (or Greek *logos spermatikos*), a philosophical concept describing the underlying rational "seeds" or principles that govern the growth and form of all natural things.</term>

But why do I, a humble man, creep among the plants? Look at the pregnant sheep of the Patriarch Jacob. <note>A reference to the biblical story in Genesis 30:37–39, where Jacob influenced the coloring of his flocks by placing striped wooden rods in their drinking troughs.</note> Having gazed upon the variegated rods under the clear waters, she transferred that appearance into her offspring. How this **appearance** <note>original: *species*. In the science of Kepler’s day, a *species* was a technical term for the "likeness" or "form" of an object that traveled through the air to reach the eye.</note> traveled from the rod to the eye, I do not ask; that is common knowledge. But how it traveled from the eye to the womb, into the formative faculty, and into the fetus—how, I say, from this rod to that corner—this requires explanation; it requires, I say, a certain impression that is more than the usual kind.

In this same way, I say that both the colors of the Planets (to answer Pico in passing regarding the objections he raises elsewhere) and their very meetings <note>conjunctions</note> and configurations <note>astrological aspects</note> are **impressed** upon the natures or faculties of sublunary things; and by these objects, those faculties are deeply moved, both to form and to move the physical body, for the moving of which...

<summary>Kepler uses the "formative faculty" in plants and the biblical example of Jacob's sheep to argue that nature possesses an internal ability to perceive and manifest external patterns. This serves as his model for how the "soul of the Earth" perceives and responds to the geometric alignments of the planets.</summary>

He transitions from discussing the "formative faculty" of plants and animals to the collective experience of humanity regarding the "Wanderers" (planets) and the nature of scientific discovery.</meta>

->**CONCERNING THE FIERY TRIGON. 33**<-
<note>The "Fiery Trigon" refers to the grouping of the three zodiac signs associated with fire: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. Great Conjunctions of Jupiter and Saturn occur in these signs roughly every 800 years, an event of immense significance to Renaissance astronomers.</note>

experience (as much as can be hoped for in natural things) has taught us about the commotion of sublunary natures <margin>of the astrologers.</margin> under the conjunctions and aspects of the **Wanderers**, <note>original: *Erronum*. A traditional term for the planets, derived from the Greek *planētai*, meaning "those who wander" against the fixed stars.</note> and it has overcome the skeptic.

But perhaps this stands in my way of gaining your belief: that none of the astrologers have brought forward this reasoning; only I—(but what kind of man am I, coming from where, born when?)—do I teach Philosophy to the Astrologers?

Indeed, I appeal to philosophers and fair judges of things: should not all the discoveries of recent men, and all changes for the better, be excluded from Philosophy in this way, as if by a bolted door? I do not deny to Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, whose famous 15th-century critique of astrology, *Disputationes adversus astrologiam divinatricem*, Kepler is systematically refuting.</note> that there is great vanity in the "experience" boasted of by Astrologers, even regarding this chapter; but I do not therefore concede that there has been no real experience at all. <margin>To what extent experience in Astrology is valid; to what extent it is in error.</margin>

Thus, as usually happens in obscure matters, great errors have arisen from the variety of minds thinking about causes. Let the greatest question of all be our example: Who, unless completely insane and corrupted in the school of **Epicurus**, <note>Epicurus was often unfairly characterized in this era as an atheist or materialist who denied divine providence.</note> will deny that God exists? Since, to use the words of St. Paul, every race of men "reaches out for Him." <margin>An example regarding God.</margin> And yet, how many falsehoods daily mix themselves into this experience, while everyone makes a God for themselves based on where they felt some relief? Hence so many types of impiety. But just because infinite myriads of gentiles were deceived, Christians, Turks, and Jews are not also deceived today, worshipping one God and **feeling after Him**. <note>original: *ψηλαφῶντες* (psēlaphōntes). A Greek term meaning to grope or feel around in the dark, used in Acts 17:27 to describe humanity's search for God.</note>

It can therefore happen in so complex a matter that everyone is deceived in part; yet in general, they experience something true. Concerning the **Sun**, <margin>Concerning the Sun.</margin> one person believes that the day is carried around by it like a garment spread most widely; another that waters are drawn from the earth by it; another that grapes are colored by its light; another that nature is made fertile by its entry into Aries; another that the stars are driven from the sky at its coming. A philosopher, weighing each point, finds that they are all in error regarding these particulars, and that the Sun is not sufficient on its own to cause them. However, having weighed the matter, when he sees all these things happen with the Sun's approach and diminish with its retreat, he gathers something general from it: that the Sun is the author of only light and heat through itself. From these two things, combined with the nature of **sublunary things**, <term>sublunary: literally "below the moon," referring to the Earthly realm where things are subject to change and decay, unlike the supposedly eternal heavens.</term> all the rest follow by natural consequence—and if some cause below the Moon is lacking, they can be hindered.

Thus it was once with the **Magnet**, <margin>Concerning the Magnet.</margin> when a certain James of Florence <note>Likely referring to a historical figure associated with the early development of the mariner’s compass.</note> noticed in it a pole which strove toward the pole of the world, and which attracted iron, while the opposite part repelled iron. Immediately there arose those who gave hope of wonderful devices: **perpetual motions** by means of iron nails in a wheel; a sphere moving in sync with the heavens without rollers or weights; or an **alphabet** by which someone might communicate to another at a set time beyond a hundred...

<summary>Kepler argues that while astrologers are often wrong in their specific claims, the underlying phenomenon of planetary influence is real, comparing it to how people have different but partially true ideas about God, the Sun, or magnetism.</summary>

34 JOHANNES KEPLER

...a distance of a hundred miles, whatever he wished, he might signify: <note>Kepler is likely referring to early methods of long-distance signaling or "telegraphy" discussed in medieval texts.</note> which were excerpted from the treatise of **Master Henry of Hesse**, <note>Heinrich von Langenstein (c. 1325–1397), a German theologian and scientist who wrote against superstitious astrology but studied natural phenomena.</note> as shown by a small book, a manuscript from nearly 200 years ago which I possess. When these writings came into the hands of **Jean Taisnier of Hainaut**, <note>Jean Taisnier (1508–1562) was a Belgian author later accused of plagiarizing the works of Petrus Peregrinus and Henry of Hesse.</note> they were published by him again under the title of a new invention, using mostly the same words and with the diagrams expressed exactly to the point.

<margin>
That there is experience of the effectiveness of conjunctions.
</margin>

In the same way, I say it has been observed by the ancients, and is observed today, that the greatest force of the planetary conjunctions lies in stirring the faculties of **sublunary things**. <note>The "sublunary" realm refers to the Earth and its atmosphere, which, in the Aristotelian worldview, was subject to change and decay, unlike the "unchanging" heavens.</note> But when one person after another tried to build upon this universal principle, and all of them strove toward making particular predictions, they fashioned a great variety of **Aphorisms** <term>Aphorisms: short, authoritative rules used by astrologers to interpret specific planetary positions.</term> so that their rules might respond to the variety of events. Afterward, each one would allege "experience" on his own behalf; they were deceived, blinded by the desire for predicting, so that they stumbled into so many superstitions.

But I return to **Pico**. <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), the Italian Renaissance philosopher whose "Disputations Against Divinatory Astrology" is the target of Kepler's critique.</note> He thinks the works of the Planets are hindered by the presence of contrary Planets; and thus, that their effects become lesser than if each were acting separately. First, I deny that one is hindered by another, for they are mixed, just as the rays of light-bearing bodies are. Next, I recall Pico to my earlier response: we are not considering here what the Planets do by themselves, but what sublunary natures *suffer* <note>original: *patiantur*. In this context, it means to "undergo" or "be affected by."</note> from their conjunction, as if from an object. In this, experience precedes reasoning. For unless I show to the eye that the force of sublunary things is incited by the joined Planets, I do not ask that my offered reasons be believed. On the contrary, if the fact of the matter is established, a contrary reasoning will be able to achieve nothing; instead, by the introduction of this dogma concerning the **sensitive impression of the species** <note>Kepler suggests that the "soul" or "formative faculty" of the Earth perceives the "species" (the visual or geometric form) of the planetary alignment and reacts to it.</note> of the conjunction into the faculties of sublunary things, the objection will be rightly dissolved.

The man of Mirandola <note>Pico della Mirandola.</note> continues: "Why should the conjunctions of the Sun and Moon not be more powerful than those of Saturn and Jupiter in administering these great changes of the world?" I answer according to my own opinion, not that of the Astrologers: **No change of the world is effected or pre-signified by any position of the stars.** For it is not the same thing for the Entirety of sublunary Natures (and thus also the minds of men, insofar as they are natural) to be most vehemently stirred, and for the state of this World or the human race to be transformed from one form into another. Next, for this inciting of natures, the conjunctions and eclipses of the Sun and Moon are indeed powerful, but by a far different reckoning. For insofar as they are simply joined, that is a monthly occurrence; thus the conjunction does not last long, since the Moon is very swift. Therefore, this stimulus is neither sharp nor unusual, and for that reason, no great commotion follows it. Furthermore, what does Pico have as to why...

<summary>Kepler argues that planetary conjunctions physically "stir" the Earthly realm and human minds, but refutes the idea that they "cause" or "predict" specific historical shifts or world-ending changes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s point-by-point rebuttal of Giovanni Pico della Mirandola’s "Disputationes adversus astrologiam divinatricem." Kepler is moving from a general defense of planetary influence to specific arguments about the "Great Conjunctions" of Saturn and Jupiter and how they affect the natural world.</meta>

### ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 35

<note>original: "DE TRIGONO IGNEO." The Fiery Trigon refers to the group of three zodiac signs—Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius—associated with the element of fire. In traditional astrology, the "Great Conjunctions" of Saturn and Jupiter shift through these trigons over centuries.</note>

...why does he <note>Pico della Mirandola</note> make the Sun and Moon more universal in their effects than Saturn and Jupiter? They say the sky is everywhere above; therefore, it is not only the Sun that is above, but also Saturn. Or did he perhaps mean that the powers of the "luminaries" <note>The Sun and Moon.</note> are greater than those of the other **wanderers**? <note>original: "erronum," a literal translation of the Greek word "planetes."</note> I admit this, but look, here is Pico speaking again about the "proper work" of the stars, which each star obtains according to the measure of its physical body and its light. But we are no longer speaking of this. For I have long since confessed that this individual work of theirs is not strengthened by conjunctions. Since our discourse concerns the **objective commotion of nature**; we must deny that which is conceded regarding the individual work of the stars. Therefore: the greater the conjunction, the greater the commotion of nature. If the conjunction is long-lasting, if it is rare, the commotion will also be great and unusual; and consequently, a greater commotion arises from the conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter than from that of the Sun and Moon.

Indeed, Pico says that the doctrine of conjunctions does not belong to all astrologers, nor to the principal ones; therefore, he claims it is false. I say that regarding conjunctions in general, no astrologer has remained silent. As for the fact that **Ptolemy** <note>Claude Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the most influential astronomer/astrologer of antiquity.</note> omitted this series—and, as it were, this **Governance** <note>original: "Politiam."</note>—of the great conjunctions in his *Prognostics of Universal Matters*, and his Arab successors adopted it: let those astrologers fight amongst themselves who boast they can predict the changes of human affairs in the world from the stars. For me, it is not the "change of affairs"—which involves many specific human details—but only the **commotion of natures**, which is universal (and even then, not every kind), which is sought through the heavens.

Pico argues against **Masha'allah**, <note>Masha'allah ibn Athari (c. 740–815 AD), a Persian Jewish astrologer and astronomer who wrote extensively on the "conjunctions of the planets" and their role in history.</note> because Masha'allah said that great things proceed from those planets that have slower motion. Pico himself thinks that speed is a sign of a star's nobility; and that efficacy accompanies nobility, such that those which are faster are more powerful. I say that—speaking for my own view, not Masha'allah's—Pico is **binding together straw**. <note>original: "connectere paleas," an idiom meaning to make a flimsy or worthless argument.</note> For efficacy has nothing in common with speed here, at least not in itself. For as to those things which result from a kind of accidental composition, this is not the place to speak of them. In a runner, speed is noble; in a King, rest and stability. What will you say to **Copernicus**, Pico, who teaches that the Sun stands still, even though it is the most noble of the planets? <note>Kepler, a committed Copernican, points out the flaw in Pico's logic: if speed equals power, the Sun (the center of the system) would be the weakest, which is absurd.</note> Nor is the reasoning sound of those who make Saturn "faster" than Jupiter simply because it covers a larger circuit. For true astronomy makes Saturn truly slower and more sluggish in proportion to how much wider and longer its path is than Jupiter's. What Pico discusses regarding the 50th aphorism of the **Centiloquium**, <note>A collection of 100 astrological aphorisms formerly attributed to Ptolemy, but now considered pseudo-Ptolemaic.</note> that is nothing to us, since Pico himself deduces only as much from it as we retain and defend here.

In Book 5, Chapter 6, Pico objects to astrologers who look at the "middle conjunctions," that...

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s rebuttal of Giovanni Pico della Mirandola. Kepler is addressing Pico’s criticism of "mean conjunctions"—theoretical calculations of planetary meetings—versus "apparent" or true conjunctions. He admits that using astrology to predict the rise and fall of empires is superstitious, but maintains that the physical alignment of planets still triggers a significant "commotion" in the natural world.</meta>

36 JOHANNES KEPLER

...that these <note>the "mean" or average conjunctions used by older astrologers</note> are fictitious: for he <note>Pico</note> argues that only the apparent ones ought to have been considered. I confess, and not only this, but this entire art of determining the cycles of religions and empires from conjunctions, I also, along with Pico, condemn as ineptitude and superstition. But it is not for that reason that the experience of astrologers regarding conjunctions is worthless. <margin>As you see in the diagram on page 25.</margin> For what does it matter if a **true conjunction** differs from a **mean conjunction**? <note>A "mean" conjunction is a theoretical calculation based on average speeds, whereas a "true" conjunction is the actual observed meeting in the sky.</note> Since any true one comes very close to the mean one. For even if in the year 1583, which according to mean motion stood at the beginning of Aries, yet by true motion it fell in the 21st degree of Pisces, and only in the year 1703 will one first occur in Aries; nevertheless, there was an apparent conjunction in that year in which the mean one was shown. Therefore, if anything happened in human affairs that was attributed to a conjunction in Aries, the observer might indeed have been mistaken about the **sign**; but not about the **conjunction** itself, which is what we are discussing here.

I do not say these things to defend the inductions of astrologers down to the prediction of specifics; but rather to maintain that, around the time of great conjunctions, there have been such great commotions of Nature, and of the natural impulses in humans, that astrologers were deceived by them, thinking that the events themselves which were carried out through those commotions proceeded from this celestial principle.

->On the efficacy of the other aspects, and likewise of the Trigon, against Giovanni Pico della Mirandola.<-

->### CHAPTER NINE.<-

In Book 6, Chapter 5, Pico first attacks the idea that some **aspects** <note>The specific angular distances between planets, such as 90 or 180 degrees.</note> are lucky by their very nature, and others unlucky and evil, such as the square <note>A 90-degree angle.</note> or the opposition <note>A 180-degree angle.</note>. On this point, I agree with Pico: for I affirm that aspects are distinguished only by their intensity, and that all are equally powerful in inciting the nature of sublunary things <note>Things "under the moon," meaning on Earth.</note>. The reason why the square is considered "evil" is often attributed to a "diversity of gender," because one of the signs at a square distance is masculine and the other feminine; these are the sorts of things I have said I consider to be empty fantasies. 

But when he asks whether the aspects are thought to exist between the bodies of the planets themselves, or in respect to the effects that follow here on Earth, I again approve of his opinion: that the planets in the heavens suffer nothing from one another. Nor could they have any knowledge of when two of them meet in a mutual aspect relative to the Earth, even if you were to grant them rational souls. In short, these aspects exist between...

<summary>Kepler distinguishes between superstitious "predictive" astrology and the physical reality of planetary conjunctions, arguing that while specific predictions are often wrong, the physical alignment of planets causes a genuine "commotion" in nature. He also begins a critique of the traditional labeling of certain planetary angles as "lucky" or "unlucky."</summary>

<meta>Continuing the rebuttal of Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, Kepler moves from discussing the timing of conjunctions to his unique theory of how planets actually influence the Earth. He argues that the Earth itself possesses a soul-like "faculty" that recognizes geometric patterns in the sky.</meta>

## ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 37

between the rays descending to the earth, rather than between the bodies themselves of the Planets: All these things, I say, I accept along with Pico. Therefore, this very thing which experience witnesses—that sublunary nature <note>The "sublunary" realm refers to the Earth and its atmosphere, which ancient science believed was subject to change, unlike the "unchanging" heavens.</note> is moved by the **aspects**, since an aspect results from the position of the earth in relation to two Planets—this very thing, I say, showed me the way to that truth concerning the method [of influence]. Specifically, that the **sovereignty of the cause resides in the earth**, not in the Planets themselves, and that this commotion of nature happens from within itself, because of the impression of the aspects; in the same way that an animal endowed with senses is moved to perceive external things.

For regarding what Pico insists upon from Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was a major Neoplatonic philosopher.</note>—that rays departing from the heavens cannot be mixed on earth so that a new power or form might arise—I indeed held that view previously. I also held that it seemed rays could be mixed more rightly and closely when they emanate from things joined together; but then, that rays are always being mixed, even if not always in a **Triangular** or **Hexagonal** figure. Finally, I considered that in the natural body itself where the rays are mixed, as the rays converge at a point, every geometric figure vanishes. I weighed all these things carefully. 

<margin>How the reason for the efficacy of the aspects was discovered.</margin>

Yet, these many reasons could not refute the clearest experience, which testified that all kinds of **meteors** <note>In Kepler’s time, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomenon, including rain, wind, and lightning, not just falling stars.</note> exist most especially when the Planets are configured in aspects; and conversely, that there is mostly a stillness in the air when there are no aspects or conjunctions. This happens not only if the planets are near in their bodies, but even if they approach **hexagonal** <note>A "sextile" aspect, or 60 degrees apart.</note> or **square** <note>A "quadrate" aspect, or 90 degrees apart.</note> distances, and it happens suddenly at the very moments of the aspects; whereas on the days immediately before and after, the effects cease. 

Jofranco Offusio <note>A 16th-century German physician and astrologer who attempted to apply mathematical precision to weather prediction.</note>, a most bitter enemy of the aspects, experienced the same thing regarding the square aspect, besides the conjunction and opposition which he thought he had attributed to the causes of "reverberations." But he confessed the weakness of his own little theory, yielding to experience regarding the square aspect. Because of these things, it had to be said that neither the stars, nor their rays by themselves, nor even the configurations themselves (being merely relationships), produce these effects in any other way than as an **object** [of perception]. 

Instead, there must be some **faculty** <note>Kepler uses the term "facultas" to describe a natural, almost instinctive power or soul-like ability of the Earth to "sense" the geometry of the heavens.</note> presiding over the things capable of being acted upon (such as the fluids in the globe of the Earth). This faculty is able to perceive and estimate the figures of the radiations from the sky, and from this, it can shake its own body with a certain impulse—either by moving it, if it were a motive faculty, or by warming it and sublimating the fluids, if it had been assigned this duty. By finding this explanation, all the arguments that Pico drew from Philosophy against the aspects, which we have mentioned, are resolved.

But the nature of this faculty, or faculties, will become more apparent from what follows. For Pico, raising a controversy against Astrologers regarding the number of aspects, also directs his arguments against the thing itself. First, he refutes the reasons...
