<summary>Kepler argues that planetary influence is not a direct physical force, but rather that the Earth possesses a "faculty" that perceives geometric alignments (aspects) and responds by generating weather and "commotions." He uses this theory to reconcile observed astrological effects with the philosophical criticisms of Pico della Mirandola.</summary>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>Continuing the rebuttal of Giovanni Pico della Mirandola from page 37, Kepler transitions from acknowledging the physical limitations of planetary rays to proposing his revolutionary theory: that the Earth itself is a living being capable of "hearing" the geometry of the heavens.</meta>

## ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 38

difficulties, which might have seemed to block the path to discovering the reason for the efficacy of the **Aspects**, did not prevent me—supported by certain convictions—from finally concluding this: that the Earth, this massive bulk, and indeed the other bodies of the world, possess certain minds or faculties akin to the Divine Mind <note>Kepler believed that God used geometry as a blueprint for creation; therefore, the Earth’s "soul" must also be geometric in nature to recognize God's patterns.</note>, through whose operation this symphony of the aspects is perceived.

<margin>The Earth is endowed with a soul.</margin>

For since it is agreed that the aspects work, and since it is also agreed (as Pico argues) that the rays themselves have no power to "mix" or act upon each other in empty space, it follows that the cause must be sought in the subject which receives them. Just as the ear perceives the harmony of sounds, and the eye the harmony of colors, so the **Soul of the Earth** perceives the harmony of the angles formed by the rays of the planets. 

<margin>How the Earth "hears" the stars.</margin>

This is not a conscious, reasoning thought, but a natural faculty—what I call a **formative power** <term>formative power: an innate biological or spiritual "instinct" that allows a being to fulfill its natural function without conscious effort.</term>—similar to the way a pregnant woman’s soul forms the body of an infant without her "thinking" about the placement of every bone. When the planets reach a harmonic configuration, such as a **Trine** <note>original: "Trigonus" — an aspect where planets are 120 degrees apart.</note> or a **Square** <note>original: "Quadratus" — an aspect where planets are 90 degrees apart.</note>, the Earthly soul recognizes this geometric perfection. 

<margin>The signal and the response.</margin>

It is then stirred into action, just as a soldier is stirred by the blast of a trumpet. The trumpet does not provide the strength for the battle, nor does it force the soldier to move; rather, it serves as the signal that calls forth the soldier's own internal vigor. In the same way, the **Aspects** do not "force" the weather or the tides through physical pressure, but rather they "invite" the Soul of the Earth to exert its own natural powers and stir the sublunary matter.

<summary>Kepler argues that the Earth possesses a soul-like faculty that recognizes geometric planetary alignments, acting as the bridge between celestial patterns and terrestrial effects.</summary>
<keywords>Soul of the Earth, planetary aspects, Divine Mind, formative power, geometric harmony, Pico della Mirandola, celestial influence</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 38, Kepler is further dismantling the traditional, illogical explanations for astrology criticized by Pico della Mirandola, leading into his own theory that the efficacy of the "Aspects" (the angles between planets) is rooted in the geometric blueprint of creation itself.</meta>

## ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 39

...achieve <note>The text begins mid-word, completing a thought from the previous page about how the soul perceives the harmony of the stars.</note> it, and may delight in a wonderful manner through the sweetness of knowledge. For as Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a Renaissance philosopher who wrote a famous attack on traditional astrology, arguing it had no rational or physical basis.</note> indeed recounted it, the theory is held miserably and is rightly rejected by him. For first, what does a harmony of voices have in common with the rays of the Planets? Then, if two Planets are said to harmonize by the same reasoning that certain signs of the Zodiac are called "mute" <note>In traditional astrology, certain constellations (like Cancer or Scorpio) were labeled "mute" because the animals they represent do not make sounds. Kepler agrees with Pico that these are arbitrary human inventions.</note>, then one futility is merely confirmed by another. Finally, if the zodiac signs are equated to musical voices by their number, then the entire logic is destroyed; the similarity will not fit, nor will the reason for the comparison be clear.

<margin>Explanation of where the efficacy of the aspects comes from.</margin>

Therefore, it is true of both conjunctions and aspects that they rely on reason—not on physical bodies, but on spiritual faculties—in the most natural way. It is admitted among the leading philosophers that the footprints of **Geometry** are expressed in the World, so much so that Geometry is, as it were, a kind of **archetype** <term>archetype: an original model or "blueprint" that God used to design the universe.</term> of the World. This holds true whether one says, with the Christians, that the World was founded at a specific beginning of time, or whether one follows the Platonists in making creation eternal. Furthermore, this is also true: that in all living creatures—just as life itself, the growth of the body, and the procreation of the species are a kind of "play" of creation—so too the architect of this work <note>By "architect," Kepler refers to the "soul" or formative power within a living being that builds its body according to a plan.</note> bears a certain likeness to **GOD the Creator**. Therefore, those things which are most common to us in use...

40 JOHANNES KEPLER

kindred things, as from light and the celestial luminaries; and whenever the rays of two planets meet in such a way that a sketch of a suitable figure is represented—whether to extend a plane or to enclose a solid figure within itself—then they themselves, having captured the impulse, briskly restore that image of their creation (that is, the motion of their bodies). And that which they indeed do perpetually, they do most diligently at those moments, and with a notable excess, being excited by a certain fervor.

[Marginal note:]
<margin>Definition of Harmonic ratios.</margin>

However, they do not love all **form-giving** <note>original: *σωματοποιητικὰς* (somatopoietikas) – literally "body-making" or "solid-forming" figures, referring to the geometric shapes that can be constructed with a compass and straightedge.</note> figures indiscriminately. Rather, they have this preference among them: that by continuously subtracting the smaller number from the larger, and the difference from the previous number, all results down to unity must be the markers of **form-giving** figures. For example, the ratio of 5 to 8 is harmonic because 5 subtracted from 8 leaves 3; 3 subtracted from 5 leaves 2; 2 subtracted from 3 leaves 1. And 1, 2, 3, 5, and 8 are all denominations of suitable figures, or markers of a rational part of a circle to be designated geometrically. For 1 signifies the whole, and 2 signifies the half-circle. From this circumscription, eight ratios arise, to which none can be missing and none can be added; any of these, when transferred to the circle, prescribes a limit to a single **aspect** <term>aspect: the specific angular distance between two planets as seen from Earth, which astrologers believe creates a "harmony" or "discord."</term> of the radiations.

If there is anyone who cannot believe that these ratios are approved by the nature of things, let him turn to Music with these ratios so formed. He will not be testing the radiations by means of voices, but rather he will be testing truly harmonic ratios—which he may perceive less clearly in the obscure example of radiations—in the much clearer example of Music. Indeed, a voice has nothing in common with light; but the **Calculation**, as well as the **Geometrical** and **World-making** <note>original: *Cosmopoëtica* – Kepler’s term for the divine geometry used by God to "construct" the universe.</note> origin, is common to both the consonances of voices and the aspects of celestial lights.

He will find in Music that the proportions of the strings are exactly the same, down to the fingernail, whenever the sounds harmonize well. But then, let him not simply count for me the voices of one **octave** <note>original: *διὰ πασῶν* (dia pason) – the Greek term for the musical interval of an octave.</note> to the signs of the zodiac. For what does it matter whether someone counts seven or ten intervals in one system of the octave? Instead, let him do this: let him take the space over which a string is stretched—representing the zodiac divided by the seven aspects and extended in length—and let him divide it into signs, not deviating a hair's breadth from this Geometrical section. Then, let him apply the **bridge** <term>bridge: (original: *Magadium* or *Ephippium*) the movable piece on a monochord (a single-stringed instrument) used to change the vibrating length of the string to produce different pitches.</term> to each of the marks of the divisions. Let him first pluck the open string, then each part of the string resting on the bridge over that mark of division. It will then appear that just as many pairs or trios of consonant voices are established as my definition shows harmonic ratios—which is also the same number of aspects in the sky, with the conjunction added. For with the first voice itself being taken, then seven other pairs [emerge], such as...

-># ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 43<-
<note>The "Fiery Trigon" refers to the three zodiac signs associated with the element of fire: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. In astrology, great conjunctions of Jupiter and Saturn occur in the same "trigon" for approximately 200 years.</note>

...frequently, from which I am also persuaded by analogy regarding this matter. It is this: that for children who are born, especially the firstborn, the Planets—likewise the **Rising** <term>Rising: Also called the Ascendant, the point of the zodiac rising over the eastern horizon at the moment of birth.</term> and the **Midheaven**—are often positioned in those locations of the zodiac, or in the **squares** and **oppositions** <term>squares and oppositions: Aspects of 90 and 180 degrees respectively, which were traditionally considered "hard" or challenging tensions between planets.</term> of those locations, which were significant in the nativity of the father, and especially indeed the mother; and aspects either the same or similar occur, as much as can happen within the space of 14 days near the birth, through the natural speed of the stars. I will give an example, even if the exact hour is unknown, regarding Frederick II, Emperor of the Romans, and his grandson Conradin: The grandson was born in the year 1252, on the 8th day before the Kalends of April <note>original: *8. Cal. Apriles* — March 25th.</note>: at that time the **Sun** was with **Jupiter** and **Venus** at 13° of Aries; **Mercury** at 15° of Aries; **Mars** at 20° of Cancer; the **Moon** at 18° of Capricorn; **Saturn** at 28° of Sagittarius. But the grandfather is said to have been born in the year 1193, on December 26th. At that time, the **Sun** was at 13° of Capricorn; **Mercury** and the **Moon** at 17° of Capricorn; **Mars** at 18° of Libra; **Saturn** at 30° of Sagittarius. You see a wonderful congruence through squares, oppositions, and identical positions.

In this same way was I born, when 40 degrees remained for the Moon to reach the opposition of the Sun: for my firstborn, the Moon held just as much toward the body of the Sun <note>original: *corpus Solis* — meaning a conjunction, or 0 degrees apart.</note>; for my second, the Moon exceeded the Sun’s opposition by just as much, for the Moon was 38 degrees away from the Sun’s opposition; for the third, it was not much different, for the Moon was 40 degrees away from the Sun’s opposition, plus one further daily motion of the Moon—that is to say, on the day before, it was meditating its departure. I remain silent on the other congruencies.

I do not say these things because I would pronounce all people who have the same or similar constellations to be relatives; but because, for the most part, relatives share the same constellations. This is so true of the entire nativity of a human being, but it is most evident in **revolutions** and **transits** <term>revolutions and transits: "Revolutions" refers to the planet returning to its birth position; "transits" refers to the ongoing movement of planets over the points in a birth chart.</term>. For as often as the Planets come to those places which were either rising at the person’s birth, or had received the Sun or Moon as guests: the nature of the person is excited, so that they apply themselves more eagerly to all works which are at hand for the person according to the condition of the places and times. And in general, a nativity is not easy and fortunate unless the rays and qualities of the Planets agree aptly and indeed geometrically.

However, these things cannot happen in any other way than through the impression of the **character** <note>original: *character* — here used in the sense of a "seal" or "imprint," like a stamp on wax.</note> of the entire position of the heavens upon the human faculty itself—the generative, nutritive, formative, sensitive, and vital faculties; for the body is too coarse to receive such a subtle character of a thing that is not palpable, but rational. By this reasoning, therefore, I believe it happens that, while the sky runs on into all other positions, that "character" of its position remains, which existed at the time when human life was ignited through birth and, as it were, was freshly poured out. That which therefore happens

44 ->JOANNIS KEPPLERI<-

in individual human beings, it is credible that it also occurs within that faculty with which this globe of the Earth is endowed. Thus, it is not merely the conjunction of two planets in the heavens themselves that matters, but that the heavens are imprinted upon our very sublunary <note>sublunary: Literally "below the moon," referring to the Earthly realm of change and decay, as opposed to the eternal celestial spheres.</note> nature—and along with the heavens, the very location of the Great Conjunction is stamped there and lingers for a time. A physician might compare this to the example of memory, that faculty by which past events are preserved as if they were perpetually present. There is a great similarity here, and a great wonder: the difference lies only in this—that the things which shine in our memory were either received from the outside through the five senses, or offered to the imagination from within by active reasoning. In contrast, these **species** <note>original: *species* — In the Aristotelian sense, these are the formal "likenesses" or "images" of things that the mind perceives.</note> of celestial things are imprinted within by some hidden means of perception. Therefore, just as the contents of memory stir our reason rather than our physical nature, these celestial **characters** <note>character: A mark or impression, like a seal pressed into wax.</note>, conversely, do not incite our reason but our nature itself. Consequently, what the character of a thing seen, heard, or thought is to the memory, the character of the heavens—which flowed in at the moment of birth—is to this faculty of the soul. Conversely, what the act of reasoning or a new sensation is to the process of remembering (which prompts us to recall a prior event), the new movement of the heavens and the **transit** <term>transit: The passage of a planet over a specific point in the zodiac, often compared to its position at the moment of one's birth.</term> of planets through the positions they held at birth is to this hidden impression. However, as this is a very difficult and slippery subject, I shall not argue with absolute certainty; I am ready to hear the opinions of other philosophers on this matter.

I have now explained, to the best of my ability, the nature of the **Fiery Triplicity** <term>Fiery Triplicity: The group of three zodiac signs—Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius—associated with the element of fire.</term> and the **Great Conjunction** <term>Great Conjunction: The rare meeting of Jupiter and Saturn, which astrologers believed signaled major historical and religious shifts.</term> using both mathematical and physical science. It now remains for me to demonstrate the actual day of this Great Conjunction.

**CONCERNING THE FIERY TRIGON.** [Page number] **45**

<note>Kepler is discussing the "Fiery Trigon," a rare astrological alignment where the conjunctions of Jupiter and Saturn occur in the fire signs—Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius—roughly every 800 years.</note>

...miserable and deplored; Cyprian Leovitius <note>Cyprián Karásek ze Lvovic (1524–1574), a Bohemian astronomer</note> refers it to the noon of December 18/28; Magini <note>Giovanni Paolo Magini (1555–1617), an Italian astronomer</note>, using the Prutenic Tables <note>Astronomical tables based on the Copernican model</note>, to the noon of December 14/24. Tycho Brahe’s calculation—as much as it could be corrected in passing by the living author—points to December 7/17. In my own forecast for the year 1603, after considering Brahe’s observations and my own, I placed it on December 6/16, 7/17, or 8/18. <note>The double dates represent the Julian "Old Style" and Gregorian "New Style" calendars, which were both in use during Kepler's time.</note> Behold the vanity of the rule that commands us to choose one single degree, and the true one at that, from among the degrees of the zodiac (of which 361 revolve in one day), while meanwhile the astronomers, from whom the science of movements must be sought, do not agree within thirteen whole days! And would anyone believe this canon of the astrologers, as if it were established by experience, when astronomy has never been better than it is today? Add to this that not even the sense of sight itself (if it happens that the conjunction is witnessed by the eyes) can pronounce a judgment more precisely than within a single day. For the daily motion of Saturn was 7 minutes, and of Jupiter 13 minutes. Therefore, daily, Jupiter overtook Saturn by no more than 6 minutes; yet the body of Jupiter equals about 3 ½ minutes, and Saturn's about 2 ½ minutes. Thus, for an entire day, Saturn and Jupiter will appear to stick together, their bodies joined in conjunction.

However, listen to what I have achieved through observation, and judge by them the observations of that fellow Krabbe <note>original: "Crabbi"; likely referring to Paul Krabbe, a contemporary rival or critic</note>, which he brings forth as if from the Tripod of Apollo <note>A classical reference to the Delphic Oracle, implying Krabbe speaks as if his words are divine truth without providing proof</note>, without adding the circumstances of either the instruments or the calculation. It is as if the matter were so easy as to exclude all deceptions in observing, or as if it were enough to establish the authority of an observation simply by asserting it boldly once you have usurped the name of an "Astronomer."

When the Sun approached Jupiter on November 17/27 in the year 1603, and Saturn on November 18/28, it happened that for several succeeding days the planets lay hidden, concealed beneath the Sun's rays.

I first saw Jupiter with Mercury after two days of long rains on the morning of December 7/17, in a very clear dawn, which overpowered the thinner light of Saturn so that it could not be seen. Mercury stood above Jupiter, declining slightly to the right from Jupiter's vertical line; the distance from Jupiter to Mercury was approximately 5 or 6 degrees by visual estimation.

From that point a massive <term>decumanus<gloss>Literally "of the tenth," used here to mean an unusually large or powerful wind, similar to the "tenth wave" in Latin literature known for its size</gloss></term> wind arose, which lasted for two whole days with great force. At the same time, it snowed heavily; there followed fog, warmth, and again heavy rain until December 14/24. The public daily reports of events record that on December 7/17, 8/18, and 9/19, across the coasts of Portugal <note>original: "Lusitaniæ"</note> and Saxony, the stirred-up Ocean brought many losses and destruction to several hundred people.

Finally, on December 15/25, during the Christmas holidays themselves, as a morning clarity shone forth, the opportunity of the heavens was granted to me; a description of which I provide to the reader in the letter [on signature] F 3...

46 **JOHANNES KEPLER**

In the following letter, which I wrote out on that same day, I shall offer the matter to be read. This, then, is the letter:

I KNOW that you are not usually interrupted by the solemnities of this day; yet the heavens recognize no holidays. Throughout these days, as the requirements of my office suggested, I have been attentive to the sight of the great conjunction. For I had written that it would appear eight days earlier than was thought. Truly, until this day, I have seen no clear morning.

It happened, therefore, by a beautiful chance, that on this very morning, upon which the sacred day of Christ's birth dawned, three planets—Saturn, Jupiter, and Mercury—arranged in the shape of a triangle, appeared in the light of the clear dawn. Therefore, if any happened to look toward the sky, this appearance undoubtedly gave them strong faith in the beginning of the Fiery Trigon. <note>The "Fiery Trigon" is an astrological cycle where the conjunctions of Saturn and Jupiter occur in the "fire" signs of the zodiac (Aries, Leo, Sagittarius). While astronomers see this as a mathematical cycle, Kepler notes the public's excitement at seeing a literal triangle of planets.</note> For otherwise, the common folk are persuaded that the Fiery Trigon, of which there is so much talk today, is a celestial portent, ignited, in the shape of a triangle.

Thus, if it pleases you to behold this Triangle with your own eyes, tomorrow morning you will still have the planets joined, although the Triangle tomorrow, on account of Mercury's daily motion, will not retain the same beauty, but will degenerate into an obtuse angle.

The arrangement of the planets this morning was as follows:

<note>An illustration depicts a landscape with a church and houses beneath a morning sky. In the sky, three celestial bodies are marked with astronomical symbols: ♄ (Saturn), ♃ (Jupiter), and ☿ (Mercury), connected by dotted lines to form a triangle. A diagonal line is labeled "Eclipt:" <note>original: *Ecliptica*, the path of the sun through the sky.</note></note>

Therefore, regarding the day of the conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter—which was attributed by other astronomers to yesterday, but was placed by me on the 16th, 17th, or 18th of December—it is evident to the eye from this observation that this day and the preceding one far anticipated it. I shall prove this by the following calculation.

At an azimuth <term>azimuth|An angular measurement used to determine the direction of a celestial body along the horizon.</term> of 45 1/2°, Saturn and Jupiter coincided in the same vertical circle, and the difference in altitude between Jupiter and Saturn—that is, the true distance between Saturn and Jupiter, as far as the dawn allowed—was 1° 32', with Jupiter being lower.

Therefore, the right ascension of Jupiter is 24° 8'. **OF THE FIERY TRIGON. 47**

The distance of the heavens <note>original: "dist cæli"; this refers to the Right Ascension of the Midheaven, a coordinate used to determine the positions of the stars relative to the observer's local time.</note> was 196° 46', and that of the Sun was 273° 23'. The difference is 76° 37', which equals 5 hours and 6 minutes. The time, therefore, was 6:54 in the morning, with 17° 40' of Sagittarius rising, so that the <term>Nonagesimal</term> <note>The Nonagesimal is the point on the ecliptic (the Sun's path) that is exactly 90 degrees from where the ecliptic meets the horizon; it is the highest point of the zodiac above the horizon at any given moment.</note> was at 17° 40' of Virgo. From this, according to the 24th problem of the 11th chapter of my *Optical Part of Astronomy* <note>original: "Astr. partis Optices"; Kepler refers to his book *Ad Vitellionem paralipomena, quibus astronomiae pars optica traditur* (1604), where he established the foundations of modern optics.</note>, the distance of the Nonagesimal from the zenith was 51° 5 1/2', and the distance of the Nonagesimal from Jupiter was approximately 82° 20'.

Therefore, the vertical angle (in which Saturn and Jupiter were located) with the Ecliptic was 50° 46 1/2'. Wherefore, if the observed distance of 1° 32' is broken down into longitude and latitude, there results 1° 11' for the latitude, and 58' for the longitude, by which Jupiter surpassed Saturn. But since the excess of Jupiter's daily motion—by which it moves faster than the daily motion of Saturn—is at most 6 minutes of a degree, and often less: therefore, by dividing the 58 minutes of longitude by those 6 minutes, the result shows that at least 10 days, or even more, had passed since they were aligned. Consequently, this conjunction actually took place not on the 24th <insert>of December</insert>...

<summary>Kepler uses precise trigonometric calculations based on his Christmas morning observation to prove that the "true" conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter had actually occurred at least ten days earlier than some contemporary tables suggested.</summary>

There remain 17 days at 17 0/5/6 hours in the morning, with Saturn elevated above Jupiter. <note>Kepler calculates that the conjunction occurred early in the morning on December 18th.</note> The day is quite certain, which I was also able to predict in my *Prognostic* <note>Kepler produced annual "Prognostications," which were astronomical and astrological calendars for the public.</note> from the Tychonic <note>Referring to the observations of his predecessor, Tycho Brahe.</note> and my own observations, that it would be the 16th, 17th, or 18th of December. But the pronouncement of the exact hour is most uncertain. For as was said above, even if these two Planets touched each other in their bodies, with no difference in latitude—which for us was 58 1/3 minutes—it could hardly be discerned within the space of one day which one was ahead or behind the other.

This observation, such as it is, is also moderately confirmed by the calculation of Saturn and Jupiter which I completed since that time from Tycho's observations and a few of my own. For through it, at noon on the 17/27 day of December, in the year 1603 <note>original: "17/27 Decembris"; Kepler provides dates for both the Julian and Gregorian calendars, which were both in use in Europe at the time.</note>, Saturn is recorded at 8° 8' 38'' of Sagittarius with 1° 41' 5'' of Northern latitude. Jupiter was at 8° 5' 32'' of Sagittarius with 0° 36' 5'' of Northern latitude. Therefore, at noon, Jupiter would have stood three minutes before Saturn. The calculation differs from the observation by 23 hours, and nothing more. The difference in latitudes was 1° 5', which after 8 days seemed to me to be 58 1/3', a discrepancy of 6 2/3 minutes, which can very easily have arisen from that; if Saturn was not precisely in the same vertical as Jupiter. For when the latitudes of the Planets are observed with dedicated effort, the matter is not committed to such slippery <note>original: "lubricis"; Kepler uses this to describe the difficulty and potential for error in naked-eye measurements of celestial bodies.</note> methods. Therefore the reader, by this proximity, ought to be con-

->ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 49<-

...to be content. So far, therefore, we have only sought the time of the great conjunction, neglecting its true position in the zodiac.

Come now, let us consult other circumstances by which we may be made certain concerning the true positions of the planets in longitude <note>The position of a planet measured along the Ecliptic.</note> and latitude <note>The distance of a planet north or south of the Ecliptic.</note>. First, let us deal with Mercury from the night of December 22nd to the 23rd. For when I had brought the sights <term>pinnacidia<gloss>the small sighting vanes or plates on an astronomical instrument used to align the eye with a star</gloss></term> of my instrument to a proximity of one degree and 59 minutes (I could not force them any closer together), it appeared that the distance between Jupiter and Mercury was even smaller. They were, therefore, less distant than 1° 59', and lay in a line that was almost parallel to the Horizon; however, Mercury was slightly more elevated. It formed a scalene triangle <note>A triangle where all three sides are of different lengths.</note>, whose smallest angle was at Mercury, the largest at Jupiter, and the medium angle at Saturn. The sides were in the proportion of these numbers: 10, 13, 14, or 10, 13, 15. We shall first compare these with one another. For we know from what has been said before that the distance between Saturn and Jupiter was 1° 15' 20''. Truly, in the units where that distance has 10 parts, the distance of Jupiter and Mercury had about 13 parts. If, therefore, 10 parts become 1° 15' 20'' (or 75 1/3 minutes), then 13 parts will become 1° 38', which is certainly less than 1° 59', as was expected.

And since the vertical angle through Jupiter with the Ecliptic and its parallel was previously found to be 50° 46', the angle of that same vertical with the Horizon and its nearby parallels will be 39° 14'. But because Mercury, which was to the left, was raised a little more than Jupiter, let the line passing through the planets be inclined to the Horizon by an angle of 10 degrees. Therefore, between the Ecliptic and this line there will be an angle of 49° 14'. When this is sought in the margin of our parallactic table, it shows under column 49 (which is half of 1° 38', the distance between Jupiter and Mercury) half of the value by which Mercury is further north than Jupiter: specifically 37' 6''. Therefore, the difference in latitudes is 1° 14' 12''. Likewise, the complement of that angle, which is 40° 46', shows from the same column half the difference in longitude, namely 32'. Thus, Mercury is further ahead in the sequence of the zodiac <note>original: "in consequentia" — moving forward through the signs of the zodiac (Aries to Taurus, etc.).</note> by 1° 4', a value we can safely announce with a risk of only a very few seconds <term>scrupulorum<gloss>literally "scruples," referring to the smallest units of angular measurement, or arc-seconds</gloss></term>. 

And since Magini <note>Giovanni Antonio Magini (1555–1617), an Italian astronomer whose planetary tables Kepler frequently corrected.</note> makes the daily motion of Mercury 55 minutes from the preceding day, and that of Jupiter 13 minutes, the daily excess is therefore 42 minutes. Thus, the 36 1/2 hours that passed absorbed the distance of 64 minutes. Consequently, on the evening of December 22nd/23rd at 6:30, there was <margin>The time of the true conjunction of Jupiter and Mercury.</margin> the true conjunction of Jupiter and Mercury in longitude, with a difference in latitudes of 1° 14', at a time when the planets were below the Horizon.

And since Saturn on the 25th was at a distance of 48 minutes behind Jupiter, by adding the 1° 4', the difference in longitudes between Saturn and Mercury was...

50          OF JOHANNES KEPLER

...was 1° 52'. But the three-day motion of Mercury is 2° 35': that of Saturn is 21', according to Magini <note>Giovanni Paolo Magini (1555–1617), an Italian astronomer whose planetary tables were widely used before Kepler’s own Rudolphine Tables.</note>. Therefore, the distance covered in three days is 2° 14'. Thus, 2 days and 12 hours earlier—namely, on the evening of December $\frac{12}{22}$ at 7 o'clock—the conjunction of Saturn and Mercury occurred, with a very slight difference in latitude. 

<margin>Time of the true conjunction of Saturn and Mercury.</margin>

For Saturn was also more than one degree north of Jupiter, just as Mercury was. Behold the cause of the heavy rain that occurred on the 22nd, and why clarity was restored from the evening of the 23rd, when Mercury began to separate from Jupiter.

And since Mercury is otherwise very powerful in stirring up storms (the cause of which must be explained to diligent investigators of nature), the reason is partly clear why the first part of winter was so mild. For on October $\frac{3}{13}$ Mercury first came to Jupiter, and on November 5th first to Saturn. Then, on November $\frac{12}{22}$, with its course reversed <note>This refers to "apparent retrograde motion," where a planet seems to move backward in the sky from the perspective of Earth.</note> (which itself disturbs the air on its own: not because the planet truly becomes retrograde, but because the nature of sublunary things <note>Things beneath the moon, i.e., the Earth’s atmosphere and environment.</note> imagines this for itself due to the position of the earth, just as the eyes of men do; so that I may respond here in passing to Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, who famously attacked the foundations of astrology. Kepler is arguing that even "imaginary" or perspective-based movements have physical effects on Earth’s weather.</note> from the principles emphasized above), at the beginning of December it came to the Sun, then to Venus, and immediately on November 25th or December 5th it came a second time to Saturn; on November 27th or December 7th it came a second time to Jupiter; because a few days later it was found to be 5 or 6 degrees distant from Jupiter, while the tables barely indicate 3, as was said above. Toward December $\frac{3}{13}$, with its course reversed again, on the $\frac{6}{16}$ and $\frac{7}{17}$ of December—on which days that horrible wind blew—it struck the sextile <note>An astrological aspect where two planets are 60 degrees apart.</note> of Mars; and so finally on December $\frac{12}{22}$ it reached Saturn for the third time, and on December $\frac{13}{23}$ it reached Jupiter for the third time: to say nothing now of the very frequent meetings and configurations of the other planets.

To confirm the position of Mercury, we began on December $\frac{18}{28}$ to measure its distance from Spica <note>The brightest star in the constellation Virgo.</note> at 55° 53', corrected by the observation of other sufficiently known distances. And since this distance is transverse to the Ecliptic <note>The path of the Sun across the sky.</note>—because Spica is in the South and Mercury is in the North—it will correspond in longitude to nearly 55° 47': which, added to the position of Spica at 18° 19' of Libra, produces a longitude for Mercury of 14° 6' of Sagittarius, and a little beyond, because Mercury was being raised toward Spica by refraction <note>The bending of light through the atmosphere, which makes objects near the horizon appear higher than they are.</note>. Now, take the position of Jupiter earlier on December $\frac{18}{28}$ as 9° 45' of Sagittarius from calculation, and add the distance of Mercury found on that day, 1° 4': so that the position of Mercury is 10° 49' of Sagittarius. Thus, the three-day motion is 3° 17': not much different in Magini.

On the same day, December $\frac{18}{28}$, we found the distance of Jupiter from the Heart of the Scorpion <note>The star Antares.</note> to be 7° 25': though not without doubt, because of a reversed sighting vane <term>pinnacidio<gloss>the small sighting plate on an astronomical instrument like a sextant or quadrant</gloss></term>, as the note records. And because the altitude was nearly the same, there is no danger from refraction. Now truly, unless we assume something already known, nothing...

ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 51
<note>The "Fiery Trigon" refers to the grouping of the zodiac signs Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius, which are associated with the element of fire. Great conjunctions of Jupiter and Saturn occur in these signs for approximately 200 years at a time, an event of immense historical and astrological significance in Kepler's era.</note>

...we shall construct nothing. Let the known northern latitude of Jupiter be taken from calculation as 0° 36'. For it changes little from the 7th/17th day of December <note>Kepler uses double dates to denote the Julian (old style) and Gregorian (new style) calendars, which were both in use across Europe at this time.</note>. Since, therefore, the latitude of the Heart of the Scorpion <note>original: "cordis Scorpij." This is the bright red star Antares.</note> is 4° 27' South, and its longitude is 4° 16' 30" of Sagittarius. Therefore, since the sum of the latitudes is 5° 3', the difference of longitudes will be about 5° 2 1/3'. Jupiter, therefore, is in 9° 44' of Sagittarius: a difference of no more than one minute from the calculation, which is surprising given the imperfect sighting-vane <note>original: "pinnacidio." The sighting apparatus on an astronomical instrument like a quadrant or sextant.</note> and such brightness of the dawn. But from this, the calculation of the position of Jupiter, deduced above for the noon of December 7th, is also confirmed.

But the position of Saturn is obtained from Jupiter; because it was proven above that Saturn was ahead of Jupiter by 48'. Thus, very nearly in 8° 57 1/2' of Sagittarius, at 1° 40' North latitude. However, calculation refuted that quantity of 48' somewhat. Therefore, Saturn is more truly in 9° 1' of Sagittarius.

It has been shown thus far on which days Saturn and Jupiter, and on which Mercury, met with both. Because, however, according to the teaching of Cyprianus Leovitius <note>Cyprian von Leowitz (1524–1574), a Bohemian astronomer and astrologer famous for his predictions regarding the "Great Conjunctions" of Saturn and Jupiter.</note>, the approach of Mars completes the proper measure of a "great conjunction": come, let us also add the observations of this planet. Therefore, at the time when Saturn and Jupiter met, Mars was lingering in Libra, having entered that sign on the 8th of December; thus, near the beginning of the year 1604, it proceeded through the sextiles <note>An aspect where planets are 60 degrees apart, considered harmonious in astrology.</note> of both planets. Meanwhile, the Sun, having passed the trine <note>original: "triangulo." An aspect where planets are 120 degrees apart.</note> of Mars, showed it as stationary near the beginning of March, when it had reached the final minutes of the 27th degree of Libra. Therefore, after retracing its steps from there <note>This refers to the planet's apparent retrograde motion, where it appears to move backward in the sky.</note>, in the month of April it hurried into opposition with the Sun, near Spica in the Virgin: in the month of May, having completed its station in 8 2/3° of Libra; finally, by direct path, it hurried toward the superior planets <note>The "superior planets" are those further from the sun than Earth: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note>: and on the 15th/25th day of July, it finally exited Libra, after having stayed in it for more than seven months. On the 7th/17th day of September, it entered Sagittarius: along with much rain and cold winds because of the aspects <note>The geometric angles between planets, which Kepler believed influenced the Earth's weather.</note>, which you may see in the Ephemerides <note>A table showing the daily positions of celestial bodies.</note>, not neglecting the quintiles. Particularly on the 18th/28th, 21st/31st, and 22nd/1st of September, the sky was unstable and rainy, with cold winds: for both the Sun was in quintile and Mercury was in sextile with Mars. The 2nd/12th day dawned clear: and so in the evening I kept watch over the motions of the planets: But since the Sun had entered Libra, and because this sign along with the following sign of Scorpio are of "short descent," <note>Signs of short descent (or short ascension) set at a steep angle to the horizon, meaning they disappear from view more quickly after sunset than other signs.</note> not much time could pass between the end of twilight and the setting of the planets.

Therefore, for the latitudes of the planets, I measured the distance of Jupiter from the head of Ophiuchus <note>The star Rasalhague (Alpha Ophiuchi).</note> as 35° 38'; and that of Saturn from the same, once as 35° 5 1/2' and again as 35° 9', where the distance between Lyra <note>The star Vega.</note> and Aquila <note>The star Altair.</note> was 34° 17': (for I use much cheaper instruments than those of Tycho <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), Kepler’s predecessor, known for having the most precise and expensive astronomical instruments in the world.</note>, and which...

...continually require proof), yet I could neither observe the star of Mars, nor compare the superior planets <note>The "superior planets" are those further from the Sun than Earth: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> to the stars in the zodiac for their longitude; for the planets set earlier. And in the following days, most heavy rains followed, with winds and great cold; this was because Venus and Mercury had met in Libra, and Mars and Saturn in Sagittarius, both in each other's sextiles <note>A "sextile" is an astrological aspect where celestial bodies are 60 degrees apart. Kepler famously sought to prove that these alignments physically influenced Earth's weather.</note>; nor was Jupiter much further away. Whosoever among the philosophers is hostile toward me because of the new dogmas by which I establish the Aspects and Conjunctions, I ask him to endure the tedium of just one year, comparing the celestial aspects with the storms: or, being persuaded that he is dealing with an honest man, let him diligently consider this single year, day by day, just as it was recorded by me. I know that if he is eager to learn and a lover of truth, he will yield.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the day 16/26 of September <note>Kepler uses double dates to represent the Julian and Gregorian calendars. 16/26 September 1604.</note>, the sky burned <note>original: "coelum arsit." Saturn, Jupiter, and the Moon were in the same straight line; Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn formed a scalene triangle <note>A triangle with three sides of unequal length.</note>. There was a very short distance between Mars and Saturn, then between the Moon and Saturn, then Jupiter and Mars, then Jupiter and Saturn, and finally Jupiter and the Moon. Mars set first, then the Moon, then Saturn, and Jupiter last. It appeared to the eye that the conjunction of Mars and Saturn had already passed. I believe it is clear from this record that on the day 17/27 of September, the new star <note>This "new star" refers to the supernova of 1604 (SN 1604), often called Kepler’s Star. Here, he provides vital evidence that it had not yet appeared on this date.</note> had not yet been ignited; before they set, we measured these positions with a Sextant:

**CONCERNING THE FIERY TRIGON.** 53
<note>original: "De Trigono Igneo." In astrology, the "Fiery Trigon" or "Triplicity" refers to the three zodiac signs associated with fire: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. Kepler is particularly interested in the Great Conjunction of Jupiter and Saturn occurring in these signs.</note>

Between the last star of the tail of the Great Bear and the Crown <note>The stars Eta Ursae Majoris (Alkaid) and Alpha Coronae Borealis (Alphecca).</note>: 30° 32'.
It should have been 30° 36 ¾'.
Therefore, the sextant <term>Sextans<gloss>An astronomical instrument used to measure the angular distance between two celestial objects.</gloss></term> erred by about 4 minutes, due to the inverted placement of the sight <term>pinnacidium<gloss>A small plate with a hole or slit used as a sighting mechanism on early astronomical instruments.</gloss></term>: And from this, I correct the remaining distances.

Here, you may seek elsewhere for the rules of calculating the positions of the planets that Tycho Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601) was Kepler's predecessor and the most famous observational astronomer of the era; Kepler inherited his massive trove of data.</note> or others might hand down: For my part, I do not now ask how elaborately I might do the same as they, but how briefly; provided it is done safely; because life is short, art is long <note>original: "vita brevis, ars longa." A famous aphorism attributed to Hippocrates, suggesting that the time it takes to master a craft exceeds a human lifetime.</note>.

The Head of Ophiuchus <note>The star Rasalhague (Alpha Ophiuchi).</note> is at this time at 16° 53 ½' of Sagittarius. <margin>A simplified method for solving the two triangles familiar in observations.</margin>
The Shoulder of Sagittarius is at 6° 54 ½' of Capricorn.
From here I subtract the distance of 27° 21', as if the entire distance was accounted for in longitude; there remains a fictitious position for Saturn, at approximately 9° 33' of Sagittarius: thus the difference in longitudes between the head of Ophiuchus and Saturn is about 7°.

## 54 JOHANNES KEPLER

appeared here, due to refraction <note>Refraction is the bending of light as it passes through the Earth's atmosphere, which makes celestial objects appear higher in the sky than they actually are.</note>: in longitude, refractions disturb the position not at all or very little, because the fixed star was at nearly the same altitude as Saturn.

### For the position of Jupiter.
The bright star of the quadrilateral of Sagittarius <note>Likely referring to a star in the "Teapot" asterism, possibly Sigma Sagittarii (Nunki).</note> is at 6° 54 ½' of Capricorn.
The distance of Jupiter from it: 19° 44'.
Therefore, Jupiter is roughly at 17° 10 ½' of Sagittarius.
But the Head of Ophiuchus <note>The star Rasalhague.</note> is at 16° 53 ½'.
The difference is 0° 17'. Secant <term>Secant<gloss>A trigonometric function used here to calculate coordinates on the celestial sphere; Kepler uses a scale where the radius is 100,000.</gloss></term>: 100001.

This value, used as a divisor, does not change the distance from the Head of Ophiuchus, 35° 38'. Therefore, subtract this from the latitude of Ophiuchus, 35° 57'; there remains 19 minutes for the northern latitude of Jupiter. And these 19 minutes, added to 3° 31', constitute 3° 50'. The secant is 100224: divide the secant of 19° 44' (which is 106239) by this. The result is 19° 22' 24", which you subtract from 6° 54' of Capricorn; there remains 17° 32' of Sagittarius as the true position of Jupiter—unless refraction disturbed anything, which is little. However, there will certainly be something not to be ignored in the latitude.

And these observations agree reasonably well with those from four days ago. For on that day, the distance between the Lyre <note>The star Vega.</note> and the Eagle <note>The star Altair.</note> proved an excess in the instrument of 4 minutes. Therefore, when the distance between Saturn and the Head of Ophiuchus was observed a little earlier as 35° 9', it was in excess by that same amount; but when it was previously 35° 5 ½', it was correct. It seems the sighting vane <term>pinnacidium<gloss>The small metal plate with a sighting hole or slit used to align the instrument with a star.</gloss></term> was moved from its place in the intervening time by a bump, as often happens. Therefore, the distance between Jupiter and the Head of Ophiuchus is truly 35° 38', which, when subtracted from the latitude of Ophiuchus, leaves a latitude for Jupiter of 19', since the longitude is the same. 

Truly, the latitude of Saturn (by taking the difference in longitude from the Head of Ophiuchus as 7° 13 ½' and the distance as 35° 5 ½', thus making the difference in latitudes 34° 26') is calculated to be 1° 31' North—three minutes less than four days later, because on the 22nd/27th of September <note>Kepler provides dates in both the Julian (Old Style) and Gregorian (New Style) calendars, which were both in use across Europe at the time.</note> it was high and more free from refractions.

### For the position of Mars on September 22/27.
At noon on that day, the calculation—which was not yet fully corrected in every respect—showed the position of Mars at 10° 38' of Sagittarius, with a latitude of 1° 36' South. Since Mars then stood at a distance of 53° 8' from the Eagle <note>The star Altair.</note>, and that fixed star is at 26° 12 ½' of Capricorn with a latitude of 29° 21 ½': assuming Mars's latitude to be 1° 30 ½' South, its position comes out to 10° 55' of Sagittarius. And since its distance from the shoulder of Sagittarius <note>Possibly the star Sigma or Phi Sagittarii.</note> was 26° 5', which is at 6° 54 ½' of Capricorn with a latitude of 3° 31' South: using that same assumed latitude for Mars, its position comes out to 10° 53' of Sagittarius. Because these do not agree, I examine another latitude for Mars, 1° 24', which gives me a position there of 10° 50'.

He compares his current observations with data from four days prior to ensure accuracy despite a minor mechanical shift in his sextant.</summary>

**ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 55**

10. 10° 50' Sagittarius; here 10° 54' Sagittarius. Wherefore I understand through these two observations that the latitude is established at 1° 29' South and the location at 10° 53 ½' Sagittarius. But the same altitude did not remain for it in both distances, and the method of wanting to define the location of Mars in longitude and latitude through two such stars, both standing in the same region of the zodiac, is very slippery <note>Kepler means the calculation is prone to error or "unstable" because of the geometric arrangement of the stars used for reference.</note>. Nor could I observe the planet otherwise, as it was near its heliacal setting <note>A planet's heliacal setting occurs when it moves too close to the Sun to be seen in the evening sky.</note>. Therefore, I also set aside that very long operation which Tycho Brahe used in his *Introduction to the New Astronomy* <note>original: "Progymnasmata." This was Tycho Brahe's massive astronomical work which Kepler edited and published.</note>, as it would have sought precision in vain within a coarse observation. It agrees well enough with the calculation, however: for if at noon its location was 10° 38' and its daily motion 42', then for 8 hours, 14' are owed. Thus, the location of Mars at our hour [8:00 PM] is 10° 52' Sagittarius. The fact that the latitude is 7 minutes <gloss>scrupulis: small divisions of a degree, or minutes of arc</gloss> smaller, and Mars is that much higher and closer to the Ecliptic than in the calculation, is due to refraction; because Mars was observed near the horizon.

<margin>The true time of the conjunction of Saturn and Mars.</margin> From these observations, the true day of the conjunction of Saturn and Mars is also evident. For if Saturn on this day, September 17/27 <note>Kepler provides both the Julian (17th) and Gregorian (27th) calendar dates.</note>, at the 8th hour [8:00 PM], was at 10° 1' Sagittarius, and Mars was at 10° 38' Sagittarius, then on the preceding day, September 16/26, in the evening after the 8th hour, they were closest to each other in longitude at nearly 10° Sagittarius. This was very close to the location of the meeting of Saturn and Jupiter which occurred the previous December; they were at nearly equal distances from the Ecliptic in opposite directions, and a little more than three degrees from each other, with Saturn being the higher one.

<margin>The true day of the conjunction of Jupiter and Mars.</margin> Finally, the true day of the conjunction of Jupiter and Mars, by which the Great Conjunction <note>The "Great Conjunction" usually refers to Jupiter and Saturn meeting, but here Kepler describes the sequence where Mars joins them, completing the astronomical event.</note> is at last completed, is obtained from these observations. For a stormy night held the day and location in a cloud; although on the days immediately before and after, the sky could be seen through gaps in the clouds, and whether the conjunction was past or imminent could easily be discerned.

On September 29 or October 9, at noon, the position of Jupiter—from a sufficiently reliable analogy of the previous observation and the daily motion—was at 19° 12' Sagittarius. At the same noon, the calculation (which you have just now seen confirmed by observation) shows the position of Mars at 19° 14' Sagittarius, with a Southern latitude of 1° 36'. During this intermediate time, the latitude was, so to speak, stationary, and at this turn, it was at its maximum. Therefore, one hour before noon, there had been a full conjunction of Jupiter and Mars at 19° 12' Sagittarius, with Jupiter being more northerly than Mars by nearly two degrees; since immediately the following day, at 17° 40' Sagittarius—a degree and a half earlier—the new star <note>This "new star" is the famous Supernova of 1604, now known as Kepler's Supernova.</note> first appeared.

<margin>Comparison of the new star.</margin> Just as in the world-system Saturn is the highest, Jupiter middle, and Mars the lowest of the three, so in this visible Great Conjunction of the three superior planets, Saturn was also the highest to the eye in the north,

He moves from the specific coordinates of Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars to the broader significance of their meeting in the Fiery Trigon—an event he believes occurs only every 800 years.</meta>

56          JOHANNES KEPLER

...north, so that Jupiter passed beneath it: so in turn, Jupiter was to the north, so that Mars, being to the south, passed beneath both of them. However, the New Star shone higher than all three: both in terms of its distance from the Earth <note>original: "mundi diametrum." Kepler is referring to the star's lack of parallax, proving it was far beyond the planets in the celestial sphere.</note> and its position relative to the Ecliptic <note>The Ecliptic is the apparent path of the Sun across the celestial sphere; Kepler is noting the star's celestial latitude.</note>.

<margin>Conclusion of the description of this Fiery Trigon.</margin>

          And this is that most celebrated Great Conjunction of the three superior planets <note>The "superior planets" are those further from the Sun than Earth: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note>, which, now that seven great revolutions of eight hundred years have been completed since the creation of the world, occurred for the first time again in the sign of Sagittarius. This is the third sign of the Fiery Triplicity <note>In astrology, the twelve zodiac signs are divided into four "triplicities" or trigons based on the elements. The Fiery Trigon consists of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note>. I pass down this astronomical description—adorned with as much care as could be managed—to be read by those who will be born during these next eight hundred years: if indeed these writings remain worthy and those future people remain capable; and provided that the world has not perished, nor some universal pestilence (the likes of which we read of in histories) extinguished the greater part of the human race and driven the survivors from their books back to the plow from which they were first taken. For then it would be said: EARTH THOU ART <gloss>that is, a farmer you are</gloss> AND TO THE EARTH <gloss>to agriculture</gloss> YOU SHALL RETURN <note>Kepler is playfully and darkly punning on the Latin Vulgate of Genesis 3:19, "Dust thou art and unto dust thou shalt return," replacing "dust" with "earth/soil" to signify a collapse of civilization into primitive farming.</note>. Or, perhaps, if some wild flood of barbarian nations should inundate Europe—now made effeminate by luxury and dissolved by excessive liberty—and utterly root out all the arts and this prodigious abundance of books:

          *Just as when a storm cloud, from a broken star, moves toward the lands,*
          *It travels through the midst of the sea: alas, from afar the hearts*
          *Of the wretched farmers shudder with foreboding; it will bring ruin*
          *To the trees and destruction to the crops, it will overwhelm everything far and wide.*
          *The winds fly ahead, carrying the sound to the shores.*
               *And now, the dikes broken, the foaming river*
          *Has rushed out, and overcome the opposing barriers with its whirlpool;*

                                                                           It is said

<note>original: "Fertur in arva furens cumulo, camposq́; per omnes, Cum stabulis armenta trahit." This is a quote from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (Book II), where the poet describes a flooding river or a ruinous force. Kepler uses it here to evoke the overwhelming power of the celestial events.</note>

->Observations of this new star.<-
## CHAPTER XII.

It is a memorable thing, and should be added to what came before, that in all the places
from which news of the new star reached me, the sky—throughout this
conjunction of Jupiter and Mars and the very rising of the star—was
rainy for several continuous days. On the 7/17 day of October, <note>Kepler provides dates in both the Julian (old style) and Gregorian (new style) calendars, which were both in use across Europe at the time.</note> clarity
having been restored, I observed these things with an iron sextant <note>An astronomical instrument used to measure the angular distance between two objects in the sky.</note>, which in the following days
was found to be off by several minutes.
After sunset, while it was still clear day, we saw the star at a sufficient height before
either Jupiter or any other star could be discerned.

Between the New Star &
*   Jupiter: 3° 28 1/2′
*   Mars: 8° 31′
*   Saturn: 6° 12′ or 6° 14′
*   Shoulder of Sagittarius: 20° 0′
*   Right knee of Ophiuchus: 7° 39′ or 7° 35′ (a difficult observation)

These were taken from the time when the New Star, Jupiter, and Mars were at an altitude
of 9 degrees, until this decreased to an altitude of 4 degrees.
Saturn, the New Star, and the "femur" <note>likely referring to a star in the thigh of the constellation Ophiuchus</note> formed an acute-angled scalene triangle. At
first, the New Star and Mars were in equilibrium on the horizon, and a little later the New Star and
Jupiter. The New Star, Mars, and the rear star in the "trapezium" of
Sagittarius were in the same straight line, with Jupiter lower, who nevertheless was several minutes to the North. Therefore, it was argued that the star was north of the Ecliptic. <note>The Ecliptic is the sun's apparent path; "North" here refers to celestial latitude.</note>

->The 8/18 day of October.<-
Between the New Star &
*   Jupiter: 3° 40′ (measured twice)
*   Shoulder of Sagittarius: 20° 1′ and 20° 2 1/2′ (measured twice)
*   Right knee of Ophiuchus: 7° 31′ and 7° 32′ (a difficult observation, due to the inconvenient positions of the star and the instrument).

Then with the sextant <note>A precision instrument used to measure the angular distance between two celestial objects.</note> we measured the distance between the wing of Pegasus and the following shoulder of Aquarius as 35° 12½'; it ought to have been 35° 9½'. Likewise, between the preceding shoulder of Aquarius and the lower star in the horn of Capricorn, the distance was 19° 42½'; it ought to have been 19° 37½'. Therefore, the sextant is in excess by about 4 minutes because of a displacement <note>original: "luxationem"</note> of the sight <note><term>Pinnacidium</term>: The sighting vane or small plate on the arm of an astronomical instrument used to align the eye with the star.</note>.

On October 11/21, in the Emperor’s garden <note>The royal gardens of Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II in Prague, where Kepler served as Imperial Mathematician.</note> where the Brahean instruments <note>The world-renowned astronomical instruments designed by Tycho Brahe, which were moved to Prague after his death in 1601.</note> were kept, Tengnagel <note>Frans Gansneb Tengnagel, Tycho Brahe's son-in-law, who inherited Tycho’s observations and often restricted Kepler's access to them.</note>, the son-in-law of Tycho, along with some students and in my presence, observed the following:

->### ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 59<-

To test the sextant, we took the distance between the Heart of the Lion <note>original: "cor Leonis"; the star Regulus.</note> and the Ear of Wheat of the Virgin <note>original: "spicam Virginis"; the star Spica.</note> twice: it measured 54° 2'; it ought to have been 54° 2'. 
And between the Ear of Wheat of the Virgin and the Southern Scale <note>original: "lancem Austrinam"; the star Zubenelgenubi in the constellation Libra.</note>, 21° 24'; it ought to have been 21° 22'. 
To be brief, in the months of March and April, I measured the meridian altitude <note>The highest point an object reaches in the sky as it crosses the observer's north-south line.</note> of the New Star generally as 18° 47' or 18° 48', compared to 30° 9' for the left knee [of Ophiuchus] and 24° 48' for the right. I omit the specific details to avoid being tedious. It reached its peak with a very slight difference before or after the small star in the heel of the right foot of Ophiuchus.

It will be beneficial to add to these observations of ours those which David Fabricius <margin>David Fabricius, a diligent astronomer.</note> made in East Frisia <note>A region in northwest Germany.</note>: he is truly a man of such standing in astronomical matters that, since the diligence of observing the heavens has expired along with the author Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe, Kepler's predecessor as Imperial Mathematician, who died in 1601.</note>, all authority in observation rests with him. I very willingly yield that prize to him, for my part, as I am much hindered by blurred vision <note>Kepler suffered from permanent eye damage and double vision due to a childhood case of smallpox.</note>. Add also his most sagacious talent in scrutinizing the motions of the planets, and his tireless zeal in contemplation. 

As far as astrology is concerned, I confess that the man sometimes yields to the authority of the ancients and the desire for predictions where these two coincide, and is carried away by a certain enthusiasm beyond reason; but he shares these things in common with a vast crowd of learned men. For this reason alone he deserves pardon. Why do you snarl, you fastidious philosopher, <margin>An excuse for astrology.</margin> if the foolish daughter (as astrology seems to you) supports and nourishes her most wise but poor mother, Astronomy, with her trifles <note>original: "naeniis"; often translated as "charms" or "silly songs." This is Kepler's famous defense of astrology's role in funding astronomical research.</note>? If she could not otherwise find her proper place among the crowds of far more foolish men, except through the intercession of this simplicity? For unless someone had previously been so credulous as to hope he might predict future events from the heavens, you would never have become so wise as to think that astronomy (which would have been unknown) ought to be learned for its own sake. If we are not led to philosophy except through wisdom, we shall never be led to it. In all wonder and all desire, while it is raw, there is much vanity; yet this vanity, at the crossroads where one goes toward philosophy, directs those who encounter it toward a beautiful place. 

But putting these matters aside for now and leaving them to their proper place, we will now excerpt the observations of Fabricius himself from his German booklet, printed in Hamburg, and from letters sent to me. He says the following:

->Investigation of the true location of the new star under the zodiac, based on these observations.<-

### CHAPTER XIII.

Because the star was observed relative to three planets, and Mars in fact varies its daily motions <note>original: "diurnos metus"; likely a printer's error for "motus" (motions).</note> quickly, one can investigate its position from the observation of October 11/21 <note>Kepler provides both the Julian (Old Style) and Gregorian (New Style) dates, which differed by 10 days at this time.</note> using that method which Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe, the famous Danish astronomer whose data Kepler used.</note> taught in his *Progymnasmata*. This method consists of six operations using sines <note>Trigonometric functions used to calculate angles and distances on a sphere.</note> and, in addition, many additions and subtractions of sines and arcs. When the distances are examined by this method, they show the position of Mars at 28° 15 1/2' of Sagittarius with a southern <term>latitude</term> <note>The distance of a celestial body north or south of the Ecliptic (the sun's apparent path).</note> of 1° 20 1/2'. The calculation for the preceding noon shows its position at 28° 2' of Sagittarius. Therefore, since the observation was made around the 7th and 8th hour, and its daily motion is 45', its position at that hour would have been 28° 17' of Sagittarius—a beautiful agreement. The latitude according to the calculation should have been 1° 34 1/2' South, but it appeared to be 1° 23 1/2'. Atmospheric <term>refraction</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes through the Earth's atmosphere, which makes celestial objects appear slightly higher in the sky than they actually are.</note> took away the remaining 11 minutes from us, as it lifted Mars toward the North and moved it closer to the <term>Ecliptic</term> <note>The path the sun follows across the sky; planets are usually found near this line.</note>, as it was in the South.

Having premised this, let us now proceed as we did in the previous sections. First, because the star appeared to the eye to be in the North, just as Saturn was, I assume the position of Saturn from the observations of the preceding days and its daily motion to be at 11° 39' of Sagittarius. I add the corrected distance of the new star, 6° 9', which yields a nearly true position of 17° 48' of Sagittarius. Now, the head of the Serpent-Bearer <note>The star Rasalhague.</note> is at 16° 53 1/2' of Sagittarius. Thus, the difference in longitudes is about 54 1/2', the <term>secant</term> <note>A trigonometric ratio used here to calculate the diagonal distance between two points on the celestial sphere.</note> of which is 100012 1/2. However, the secant of the distance from the head of the Serpent-Bearer, 34° 2', is 120669. In this region, the secants increase by 24 units for every single minute, whereas in the former secant they exceed the length of the diameter by only 12 1/2 units. Therefore, the difference in latitudes is about half a minute less than the discovered distance. Since the head of the Serpent-Bearer has a latitude of 35° 57' [North]:

I subtract the difference: 34° 2'.
The remaining latitude of the New Star is: 1° 55'. From October 21.
Or:

This latitude <note>original: "latitudo"; in astronomy, the distance of a celestial body north or south of the ecliptic, the sun's apparent path.</note> of the New Star is the most certain, as far as visual observation is concerned. Therefore, we shall now use this as a fixed value in defining its position according to longitude <note>original: "longitudinem"; the position of a celestial body measured East along the ecliptic.</note>.

The very first distance measured from Jupiter was on October 17/27 <note>Kepler provides both the Julian (17th) and Gregorian (27th) calendar dates.</note>, which was 3° 24 1/2' correctly [measured], but in very bright twilight, when Jupiter (based on the pattern of preceding days) was at 20° 35' of Sagittarius, with a northern latitude of 21 minutes North; or rather, twenty days later, it was a little less, about 13', for it was decreasing for two reasons <note>Likely referring to the planet's orbital motion combined with the change in Earth's position, which alters the observed angle.</note>. <margin>From the observations of Fabricius, the location of the new [star].</margin>

The location of the Heart <note>The star Antares, also known as Cor Scorpionis, the Heart of the Scorpion.</note> is added: 4° 16' 20'' Sagittarius.
The location of the New [Star] results in: 17° 43' 35'' Sagittarius.
Therefore, this location differs from the previous one by 3 minutes <note>original: "scrupulis", used here as minutes of arc.</note>. But it is clear that there was an error in the observation itself from Fabricius’s data, which makes this distance 14° 50 1/2'.

Now we shall compute the location of the star from all of Fabricius’s data:
First, because along with Bürgi <note>Jost Bürgi, a Swiss mathematician and clockmaker.</note> he gives the distance of the star from the head of the Serpent-bearer <note>The star Rasalhague (Alpha Ophiuchi).</note> as 34° 0': Therefore, the latitude of the star for him is 1° 57' North <note>original: "Sept." (Septentrionalis).</note>.

II. Because the Fabrician distance from the knee of the Serpent-bearer <note>The star Eta Ophiuchi.</note> is 6 minutes smaller than ours using the Tychonic instrument. Therefore, the location of the star is given as 17° 37' Sagittarius. For Brengger <note>Johann Georg Brengger, a physician and astronomer who corresponded with Kepler.</note> it is 17° 41' or 17° 36'.

III. Because the distance from the Eagle <note>The star Altair (Alpha Aquilae).</note> is 2 minutes greater than mine and Bürgi’s, the location is given as 17° 39' Sagittarius.

IV. The distance from Antares is smaller than mine by 2 1/2 minutes. Thus, the location of the new star is 17° 41' Sagittarius.

Therefore, Fabricius’s observations are nearly the same as ours, defining the location of the star from 17° 36 2/3' Sagittarius to 17° 46 2/3' Sagittarius; atmospheric refractions <note>The bending of light as it passes through the Earth's atmosphere, which makes stars near the horizon appear higher than they actually are.</note> having introduced this variation. In the East, indeed, it seemed to be shifted forward <note>original: "in antecedentia," meaning westward or against the order of the zodiac signs.</note>; in the West, however, it seemed to be shifted backward <note>original: "in consequentia," meaning eastward or following the order of the zodiac signs.</note>.

But it must be noted concerning the right knee of Ophiuchus <note>The constellation of the Serpent-bearer.</note>, which is the star that pushes the location of the New Star too far forward for both me and Fabricius (which is undoubtedly the reason why Fabricius omitted this distance, which was included in his Latin manuscript, from his German one). The issue is that the longitude and latitude in Tycho Brahe's catalog disagree with the right ascension and declination <note>Right ascension and declination are coordinates measured from the celestial equator, while longitude and latitude are measured from the ecliptic (the Sun's path).</note>, which are known earlier and more certainly, due to some calculation error. For Tycho Brahe, in the year 1600, sets the right ascension of the right knee of Ophiuchus at 251° 50' and the declination at 15° 7' South. To this, however, corresponds a longitude of 12° 20 1/2' Sagittarius and a latitude of 7° 17 1/3' North. Yet Brahe records it as 12° 24' Sagittarius and a latitude of 7° 18' North for the year 1600. Thus, by using the right knee, the New Star is placed by Fabricius at 17° 43' Sagittarius; by me, in my earlier observation, at 17° 45 1/2', or in my later observation at 17° 34 1/2' or 17° 35 1/2' Sagittarius, which I have said was caused by the inconvenient positioning of the instrument.

And so much for the longitude and latitude of the New Star with respect to the Ecliptic. It follows that we should establish its right ascension and declination. <margin>Right ascension of the star.</margin> Assuming, therefore, the longitude to be 17° 40' Sagittarius and the latitude 1° 56' North, its Right Ascension results in 256° 47' and its declination 21° 1 1/2' South.

Accordingly, it reached the midheaven <note>The point where a celestial body crosses the observer's local meridian, reaching its highest point in the sky.</note> at 17° 51' Sagittarius; it rose in Prague at 15 1/2' Sagittarius; <margin>Declination of the star.</margin> and it set at 19 1/2' Sagittarius, almost from the beginning alongside Jupiter. And since the altitude of the pole at Prague is 50° 6' and the Equator is 39° 54', if the southern declination is subtracted from this, it yields a meridian altitude for the star of 18° 52 1/2', which I found to be 18° 48' or 49' using the <term>two-cubit quadrant</term> <note>An astronomical instrument for measuring angles, with a radius of approximately 3 feet.</note>, the testing of which was performed by the left knee.

<summary>Kepler compares his calculations with those of David Fabricius, noting that atmospheric refraction and errors in Tycho Brahe's existing star catalog for the "Right Knee of Ophiuchus" explain slight discrepancies in the New Star's position. He concludes by providing the star's coordinates and its observed altitude in Prague relative to Jupiter.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Kepler is reconciling his observational data with that of David Fabricius. He focuses here on the "left knee" of the constellation Ophiuchus to determine the exact declination of the New Star (the 1604 Supernova).</meta>

66 **JOHANNES KEPLER**

This occurred because, while the instrument should have shown an elevation of 30° 14' (since it declines by 9° 40'), it actually showed 30° 9', or 30° 10', or at most 30° 12', depending on how accurately or carelessly the <term>plumb line</term> <note>A weighted string used by astronomers to ensure their instruments were perfectly vertical.</note> was adjusted, in which a residual error remained. But you can proceed without any error in this way:

The altitude of the New Star (18° 48') differed from the altitude of the <term>left knee</term> <note>The star Eta Ophiuchi.</note> (30° 9') by 11° 21'. Since that knee was 9° 40' to the South, by adding 9° 40' to 11° 21', a value of 21° 1' is produced for the declination of the New Star, just as was proven by the mathematical calculation.

It should be noted, however, that due to <term>refractions</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes through the Earth's atmosphere, which makes celestial objects near the horizon appear higher than they actually are.</note>—which at an altitude of 19 degrees are not entirely avoidable—the northern latitude was in reality slightly smaller, and the southern declination slightly larger. At this altitude, the star could have partly shed that refraction which it had when it was closer to the horizon.

<margin>Where it was vertical.</margin> Since the star held this declination, it passed directly overhead (at the <term>zenith</term>) only for the southern-most Africans in the Kingdom of Monomotapa <note>A major historical kingdom in Southern Africa, located in the Zambezi valley.</note>, and in the New World in Brazil and Peru—regions lying closest to the Tropic of Capricorn and inhabited mostly by wild nations. Thus, I might use that saying: *The people sitting in darkness see a light.* <note>original: "Populus sedens in tenebris videt lucem." A reference to the biblical prophecy in Isaiah 9:2 and Matthew 4:16; Kepler likely uses it here to mean that the star shone for people in the "darker" or southern parts of the world.</note> For the rest of its path, its daily circuit was carried across the vast Ocean, leaving all of Asia and almost all the Indian islands to the North.

<margin>To whom it touched the horizon.</margin> It could be seen throughout the entire world, except for the Northern Frigid Zone. For the furthest Laplanders, it merely skimmed the southern horizon.

### Concerning the Constellation of the Serpent-bearer, the Serpent, and the Scorpion.
### *CHAPTER XIV.*

<margin>A description of the Serpent, useful for identifying the fixed stars.</margin> It is easily apparent in this constellation, as I said in Chapter Five, that the names of the stars have come from some slight outline of things familiar to us in the arrangement of neighboring stars. Furthermore, from those names once adopted, new poetic fables arose, or ancient ones were adapted with some effort to fit the names chosen. In the <term>Serpent-bearer</term> <note>The constellation Ophiuchus.</note>, which hosted the New Star—especially when it begins to rise on clear and moonless nights—there appears most clearly a line of twelve notable stars. These are drawn in a circular curve around Ariadne's Crown <note>The constellation Corona Borealis.</note>, as if around a center, between the Crown and the heads of Hercules and Ophiuchus. After being led around by five stars, the line then reflects downward, with stars in pairs...

He then begins a description of the constellation Ophiuchus, explaining how constellations were named after familiar shapes.</summary>
