arranged in almost the same line, it is very remarkable. The first of those twelve is missing from my table, excluded by its brevity; it is referred to by <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the influential Greco-Egyptian astronomer whose star catalog served as the foundation for Kepler's work.</note> as being in the shoulder, the second in the upper arm, and the third in the right elbow of Hercules; this third one is smaller than the others, yet it is equally manifest in the circular sequence of the arrangement. The fourth is eventually assigned to the temples of the Serpent; the fifth to the extension of the neck; the sixth is in the curve of the serpent; the seventh and eighth are near that place (Ptolemy’s ninth and tenth of the Serpent) and are within the serpent itself; the ninth and tenth have come closer together and are reckoned to the left hand of <term>Ophiuchus</term> <note>original: "Serpentarij". In modern astronomy, this constellation is called Ophiuchus, the Serpent-Bearer.</note>; the eleventh and twelfth are in the left and right knees of Ophiuchus. After numbering these twelve, I was accustomed to count the **New Star** in the thirteenth place; and thus, after it began to fade, I happened upon its appearance: for it was slightly further south than the aforementioned line.

By this winding and self-evident line—especially when it rises steeply from the horizon—I believe the ancients were prompted to imagine the likeness of a serpent <margin>The head of Ophiuchus.</margin> reared up high. To this, the care of those who followed conveniently added and discovered the image of a man. For at the top there is a great star, a little lower than the stars of the second rank; beneath which, to the right and left, two pairs of bright stars are placed, creating the appearance of shoulders; <margin>Shoulders.</margin> and again, two other such pairs created the appearance of hands. <margin>Hands.</margin> Indeed, **Aratus** <note>A Greek didactic poet (c. 315–240 BC) whose poem "Phaenomena" described the constellations.</note> indicates this in express words:

<note>original Greek: "Τοῖοι οἱ κεφαλῇ ὑποκείμενοι ἀγλαοὶ ὦμοι / Ἔιδονται. Κεῖνοί γε κὴ ἂν διχόμηνι σελήνῃ / Εὐωποὶ τελέθοιεν: ἀτὰρ χέρες ὐ μάλα ἶσαι / Λεπτή γὰρ τῇ κὴ τῇ ἐξεπιθέδρομεν αἴγλη; / Ἀλλ’ ἔμπης κἀκεῖναι ἐπόπτιοι, ὐ γὰρ ἐλαφραί."</note>
"Such are the bright shoulders situated below the head
that are seen. These would appear beautiful even in the
light of a mid-month moon; but the hands are not quite so equal,
for a thin splendor runs here and there;
nevertheless, they too are visible, for they are not faint."

There was also the very fitting representation of the right foot, resting on its toes: <margin>Foot.</margin> an image formed by six stars, which Ptolemy numbers as 13, 14, 15, 16, 17, and 18, to which the **New Star** was added.

With a picture of a man holding a Serpent in his hands begun by so many members, it is no wonder if it was afterwards rendered more complete at the discretion of astronomers; <margin>The coil of the Serpent. The Tail.</margin> they adopted many more stars for the coil and tail of the Serpent, which were not such obvious patterns in themselves, but which the imagination (once it had accepted the previous stars) seemed to adopt by a certain necessary consequence. Then, of the twelve stars I numbered, the first three were taken away from the Serpent and handed over to <term>Hercules the Kneeler</term> <note>original: "Herculi ingeniculo". Hercules is often depicted in star charts as kneeling.</note>, so that no dispute might arise between him and the Serpent over boundaries, nor should the Serpent be reduced to too narrow a space. The Serpent was able to bear this loss with a calm mind, as a beautiful image of a gaping mouth <margin>The Gaping Mouth or Head.</margin> fell to it in compensation,

<meta>Kepler continues his critique of astronomical visualizations, moving from the general arrangement of stars in Serpens and Ophiuchus to specific errors found in the famous star catalogs and celestial globes of his era, specifically those by Johann Bayer, Gerardus Mercator, and Tycho Brahe.</meta>

68 JOHANNES KEPLER

image, which consists of three stars, above the stars of the temples and the origin of the neck, with which the two outer ones (the higher of which fell outside the limits of my table) form a trapezium, with the third received in the middle. <margin>Errors of this asterism.</margin> And [Johann] <term>Bayer</term> <note>Johann Bayer (1572–1625), author of the famous star atlas *Uranometria* (1603), which introduced the system of naming stars with Greek letters.</note> expresses this arrangement of stars in the head of the Serpent excellently; but the painter, as is often the case, showed off his art in the wrong place, transferring the eye to the position of the jaw, and the forehead to the position of the nostrils. Similar things happened to the globes of <term>Mercator</term> <note>Gerardus Mercator (1512–1594), the renowned cartographer who also produced celestial globes.</note> and Tycho. Added to this in Tycho Brahe's *Progymnasmata* is this: that he names the stars not strictly according to Ptolemy, but according to the illustration by Mercator; and the scribes corrupted some numbers; so that if you were to make the arrangement based on the reliability of his catalog, you would by no means express the arrangement of the gaping mouth which appears in the sky; just as Tycho's globe certainly does not express it.

But even in the Greek text of Ptolemy, the designation of the region for the five stars in the right foot is perverse: for "South" must be substituted for "North." This ancient error also passed into the Arabic translation. Therefore, Johann Bayer retained it, which you might wonder at given the man’s most sharp-eyed diligence. For Mercator recognized this on his globe and transferred these stars to the South: whence Weidner rightly sings:

*Where the orbit of the Sun cuts the right sole of the serpent-bearer.*
<note>original: "Quâ dextram anguiferi plantam ſecat orbita Solis." From Paul Weidner, a contemporary writer or poet referenced by Kepler.</note>

Doubtless, globes that carry the authority of Tycho Brahe will persuade the unwary of many such things; while the mechanical imitators add on their own those stars which Brahe had passed over—either because of their low position near the horizon or other impediments—but they leave the images [of the figures] to the whim of the painters. Hence it happened that these stars are marked everywhere on Tychonic globes and in Bayer's table, by a most perverse <term>articulation</term> <note>original: "διαρθρώσει" (diarthrōsei), a Greek term referring to the anatomical arrangement or jointing of the figure's limbs.</note>, in the right hollow of the knee; stars which the ancient authors intended to be in the sole of the foot, as clearly indicated by the character of the foot.

Bayer’s painter also swapped (certainly to the author's grief) right for left; from which arises much confusion in the comparison of Ptolemy's description with the sky; a matter which was rightly criticized by <term>Brengger</term> <note>Johann Georg Brengger, a physician and astronomer who corresponded frequently with Kepler regarding observations.</note>. I had the same struggle with my own engraver; who, when after various efforts he had finally reconciled the proximity of the feet of my figure to this width of the shoulders, he demanded that the right elbow be yielded to him, and he won through sheer persistence. For that which Ptolemy named from the right elbow is here the fourth star of the Serpent from the end. Other things are very well in accordance with Ptolemy's mind.

But before I pursue everything with tedious heaps of numbers, that...

<meta>This page continues Kepler's discussion on the constellation Ophiuchus (the Serpent-bearer), shifting from astronomical corrections to the различными mythological interpretations attributed to the star pattern.</meta>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT, PAGE 69**

<margin>The myth of the Serpent-bearer is uncertain and varied.</margin>

I shall now conclude what I began to say regarding the myths: namely, that the origin of this celestial **figure** <note>Original: *Idoli*. In this context, it refers to the visual representation or "character" formed by the constellation.</note> arises from the arrangement of the stars. Once it was accepted into the heavens, the poets then began to devise fables for it. **Avienus** <note>Postumius Rufius Festus Avienus (4th century AD), a Roman poet who translated Aratus's astronomical works.</note> imagines this to be **Aesculapius**, who, after restoring **Hippolytus** to life through the medical arts, paid the penalty to Jupiter by being struck by a lightning bolt; yet as a consolation, he received immortality through the translation of his image into the sky. Doubtless, because the people of Epidaurus worshipped Aesculapius in the form of a serpent, it was suggested that a serpent be added to him. Others prefer that he represents the boy **Hercules** squeezing snakes in his hands; these people are indeed forgetful, and perhaps have never ascended to the heavens to learn that Hercules was already there, resting on his knees, pressing his head against the head of Ophiuchus so closely that he seems to butt heads with him. To others he is Triopas, to others Glaucus. Hence **Pontanus**, still undecided, assigns some myths to the East and others to the West in Book III of his *Celestial Things*. <note>Giovanni Pontano (1426–1503), an Italian humanist and astronomer.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*If you seek the Marsi and the tamed snakes, and the charms* <note>The Marsi were an ancient Italian people famed for their snake-charming abilities.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Of the Colchian woman, and the Dragon conquered by spells and sleep;* <note>Refers to Medea of Colchis, who helped Jason lull the dragon guarding the Golden Fleece to sleep.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*The Serpent-bearer gives these to you as the star rises:*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*If by chance you seek Eurydice wandering through the joyful*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Meadows, groaning much from the wound of the snake:*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*The Serpent-bearer will mark her by its ill-fated setting.* &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;These lines are taken almost word-for-word from **Julius Firmicus**. <note>Julius Firmicus Maternus, a 4th-century lawyer and astrologer.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Such is the license of poets that they transform this figure from male to female daily: is it any wonder, then, if he is imagined by some as effeminate? Thus, through Pontanus, one may call him **Apollo**, the slayer of the Python; or **Cadmus**, the slayer of the Boeotian Dragon; or **Jason**, who lulled the Colchian Dragon to sleep; or **Aesacus**, avenging the death of the nymph Egeria <note>Likely a reference to the myth where Aesacus pursues the nymph Hesperia, who is bitten by a snake.</note> upon a serpent; or even, as he himself says, **Eurydice**. But what if you were to call it that **Lesbian Dragon**, turned to stone by Phoebus—the one that gnawed at the head of **Orpheus**? Do you see how, with its neck bent back toward the **Crown** <note>The constellation Corona Borealis.</note> and the **Lyre of Orpheus** which follows it at no great distance, it still seems to seek the head of Orpheus (the master of the lyre) within that crown? If this pleases you less, recognize **Aristaeus** in the Serpent-bearer and **Proteus** in the Serpent, so that the reason for their struggle becomes clear.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Yet nothing is more fitting than the **Laocoön** of **Virgil**, whom the serpents—turning him away from saving his sons—

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Seize and bind in their massive coils: and now...* <note>A famous passage from Virgil's *Aeneid*, describing the Trojan priest Laocoön being strangled by sea serpents.</note>

<summary>Kepler discusses various mythological identities for the constellation Ophiuchus (the Serpent-bearer), ranging from Aesculapius and Hercules to Laocoön, noting how poets have fluidly reinterpreted the star pattern over time.</summary>
<keywords>Ophiuchus, Serpens, mythology, Aesculapius, Hercules, Laocoön, Giovanni Pontano, Avienus, constellation lore, astronomy history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s technical account of his observations of the constellation Ophiuchus (the Serpent-bearer) and the "New Star" of 1604, following his discussion of the mythological origins of the constellation.</meta>

Here, the will was never lacking to supply these defects in **Brahe’s** <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), the famous Danish astronomer whose precise observations Kepler inherited and refined.</note> work, as far as the latitude of **Prague** <note>Kepler was serving as Imperial Mathematician in Prague; the city's northern latitude (approx. 50° N) limited the visibility of stars low in the southern sky.</note> would permit; yet my resources were quite slender. For I am almost alone, and I have **weak eyesight**; the stars, indeed, are small, near the horizon, and lost in the brilliance of the **Milky Way**. Furthermore, I lack **Brahe’s instruments**, and the sky in Bohemia is rarely as clear as one would wish.

Consequently, a proper observation of scarcely one of the omitted stars will be found; however, I have noted the sequence of the others as they cross the meridian, and their midday altitudes, as far as I could achieve with my own sight. I have even determined the positions of some by their conjunction with the planet **Mars**, for which I possess highly accurate calculations. In most instances, I was aided by **Mechanics** <note>Likely referring to the geometric methods or the "Astronomiae Instauratae Mechanica"—the system of instruments and techniques developed by Tycho Brahe.</note> and the noting of straight lines; specifically, whenever three stars coincided in a single straight line, or very nearly so. For this reason, I present to you a **diagram** <note>Original: *typum*. In early modern printing, this refers to a visual figure or illustration.</note> of this constellation.

<margin>The reason for the copperplate diagram.</margin>

For, so that the straight lines of the stars—especially those somewhat further apart—may align with the mechanical [geometrical] method, the diagrams must be prepared a bit differently than...

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s technical explanation of how he mapped the constellation Ophiuchus and the "New Star," specifically addressing the geometric challenges of projecting a curved sky onto a flat map.</meta>

ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 71

designates [the lines] in the projection onto the hemisphere. Regarding the minor circles, namely those parallel to the Equator or the Ecliptic <note>The Ecliptic is the apparent path of the Sun across the sky; the Equator is the projection of Earth’s equator into space.</note>, and others like them: when projected, they become **conic sections**. For every minor circle of the hemisphere, whose circumference is exactly one quadrant <note>90 degrees</note> away from the point of contact, becomes a **parabola** when projected onto a plane. But if any part of the circumference falls into the other non-projectable hemisphere—that is, if it is further than a quadrant from the point of contact—such a projected semicircle (as far as it can be projected) becomes a **hyperbola**. Thus, in our current map, all circles parallel to the Ecliptic, which designate latitude, are hyperbolas. Finally, if no part of the minor circle's circumference is a full quadrant's distance from the point of contact, such a circle projected onto a plane becomes an **ellipse**; or, if its pole is exactly at the point of contact, it is simply a circle. The proof is readily available; for the center [of the sphere], connected on all sides with any of the minor circles, creates a conical surface, which the plane, touching the sphere, cuts. <note>Kepler is describing a gnomonic projection, where the eye is at the center of the sphere and the stars are projected onto a flat tangent plane. In this geometry, all "great circles" (like the horizon or meridian) appear as straight lines, while "small circles" (like parallels of latitude) appear as conic sections.</note>

But although the figures are distorted, especially at the extremities of the map if it were wider—as here near the head of the **Serpent**, where it is necessary for a right angle (by which a circle of latitude is cut by a parallel of latitude on the sphere) to degenerate here on the plane into an obtuse one—nevertheless, those same straight lines remain. The grasp of these is easier, as they are simple, and they are therefore more useful.

->**Receive, therefore, the sequence of stars crossing the meridian with their altitudes.**<-

->**Altitudes measured by a quadrant of one and a half cubits.**<- <note>A quadrant is an instrument for measuring angles up to 90 degrees. A "sesquicubital" quadrant has a radius of about 2.25 feet (approx. 70 cm).</note>

In the front right horse-foot of Sagittarius &emsp; — &emsp; 5. 32'. Etc.

Most of these stars were affected by refraction <note>The bending of light by Earth's atmosphere, which makes stars near the horizon appear higher than they actually are.</note>, and they could not be captured with enough certainty using the sights <note>original: "pinnulis," the small metal plates with holes or slits used to align an instrument with a star.</note> because of their dimness <note>original: "ἀμαυρότητα" (amaurotēta), a Greek term for faintness or obscurity.</note>. Therefore, since their right ascensions <note>The celestial equivalent of longitude, measured along the equator.</note> were not determined, I refrained from the labor of calculation, contenting myself with a mechanical approach; and by applying straight lines upon which three stars fell or were aligned, and other geometric patterns. Those who wish may seek these out from the sky through their own industry, since they are perpetual and do not require instruments; let them evaluate this table against those observations and correct it wherever necessary, leaving aside those positions which the industry of Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe, Kepler's predecessor and the most accurate observer of the pre-telescopic era.</note> has already proven certain.

&emsp; Regarding the bright star of the right foot, because that foot was adorned with this new star, it seemed worthy to me to measure it by whatever means I could. It appeared twice to be at a distance of 11° 53' from the heart of Scorpio <note>The star Antares.</note>, just as the right knee was distant from the middle of the forehead <note>of Scorpio</note> by 17° 55', though they are actually only 17° 53' apart. Therefore, the corrected distance is 11° 51'. Its altitude <note>Height above the horizon.</note> was 15° 26', which, when subtracted from the equator, leaves 24° 28'. Thus, its location is approximately 15° 50' of Sagittarius, with a South latitude of 1° 43'.

&emsp; Mars approached the stars of the left foot in March of the year 1606. For on the 10th day, New Style <note>The Gregorian calendar.</note>, at 5 o'clock in the morning, when Mars was placed by calculation at 1° 42' of Sagittarius with a North latitude of 1° 5', it was seen almost in the middle position between the northernmost [star] of the tri-

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s meticulous astronomical calculations. He is using the observed positions of the planet Mars and the Moon in March and May 1604 to mathematically "triangulate" the exact coordinates of the fixed stars in the feet of Ophiuchus (the Serpent Bearer) and the head of Scorpio. This serves as a celestial grid to precisely locate the New Star.</meta>

# ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER 73

the angle in the forehead of Scorpio and the Ankle, which is closest to the lower part of the shin, separated by half the distance that [divides] the top of the forehead from the double star, and not yet on the line of the shin.
On the 16th of March, when **Mars** was at 3° 51' of Sagittarius with a latitude of 0° 59' North, it stood one Moon’s diameter <note>The "diameter of the Moon" was a common visual measurement for astronomers, roughly equal to half a degree (30 to 32 arcminutes).</note> above the ankle of the left foot, on a line extending from the ankle to above the middle of the shin. On the following 17th of March, when it was at 4° 11' of Sagittarius with a latitude of 0° 58' North, it had already slightly passed the line [extending] from the Heart of Scorpio <note>The "Heart of Scorpio" refers to the bright red star Antares.</note> to the ankle.
Since the Heart of Scorpio is at 4° 17' Sagittarius, the Ankle therefore falls just a little before 4° 11' Sagittarius: specifically, in the year 1604, at 4° 7' Sagittarius. And because it was lower than Mars by one Moon’s diameter (that is, by about 32 minutes), its northern latitude will be 0° 26'. And since on March 17th Mars was with the Ankle at 4° 11' Sagittarius, but on the 10th it was at 1° 42', it therefore moved forward by 2° 29'. Its previous position was distant from the ankle by 1° 32' across the path. And because the northernmost [star] in the triangle of Scorpio’s forehead is at 29° 8' of Scorpio, from there to 1° 42' of Sagittarius is 2° 34', which you see is nearly equal to the previous distance of 2° 32', exactly as noted above. It follows from this that the lower part of the left shin is beyond 1° 42' Sagittarius. Therefore, it is approximately at 1° 48' Sagittarius with a latitude of 1° 38' North.
From this, and from the observations of March 16th, we also derive the position of the middle of the shin, adding that the line of the three [stars] of the shin leaves the left knee slightly to the West, etc.

Again, on the evening of May 25th at 11 o'clock: **Mars**, now in retrograde motion <note>Retrograde motion is an optical illusion where a planet appears to move backward in the sky as Earth passes it in orbit.</note>, had already passed the ankle and the Heart of Scorpio. Indeed, calculation places it at 3° 57' Sagittarius with a latitude of 1° 30' South. You see the agreement [of the data]. There was a nearly isosceles triangle <note>A triangle with two sides of equal length.</note> formed by Mars, the Heart, and the star preceding it, with the side between the Heart and the preceding star being the shortest. Yet Mars was a little closer to the Heart than to the preceding one, which confirms its position. Furthermore, a line from the middle of the forehead, passing through the star under the sole of the foot, passed below Mars. And since the middle of the forehead is at 1° 54' South and Mars is at 1° 30' South, it is understood from this that the latitude of the sole is greater than 1° 30' South.

On the evening of May 28th, the star under the sole of the foot could not be seen due to the proximity of Mars. And since Mars was then at 2° 56' Sagittarius with a latitude of 1° 51' South, this fixed star falls at approximately 3° 0' Sagittarius with a latitude of 1° 50' South. Therefore, the locations of these fixed stars are quite certain. In other matters, if my manifold weighing of figures and lines and various observations of meridian altitudes <note>The height of a star or planet when it crosses the north-south line in the sky.</note> did not lead me to the very smallest fractions, they certainly did not allow me to wander too far astray.

Now, furthermore, I will present a catalog of the longitudes and latitudes of those stars which either have not yet been recorded in the catalog of fixed stars by Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), the famous Danish astronomer whose data Kepler used.</note>, or are seen in these constellations in addition to the Ptolemaic ones.

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s meticulous refinement of star catalogs. 74 **JOHANNES KEPLER**

First, the naming of the first stars in the Serpent <note>The constellation Serpens, which is held by Ophiuchus.</note> is confused. For what is named by Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), the Danish astronomer whose precise data Kepler inherited.</note> is described by Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Greco-Egyptian authority whose star catalog was the standard for over a millennium.</note> as follows:

| By Tycho Brahe | By Ptolemy |
| :--- | :--- |
| I. The preceding star in the mouth. | Above the end of the jaw in the quadrilateral. |
| II. The star in the mouth. | Touching the nose. |
| V. The star at the left eye. | In the middle of the quadrilateral in the gape of the mouth. |
| VI. The star at the nostrils. | Coming out from the head toward the North. |

Regarding these, I consulted the Tychonic distances observed and subjected them to a new calculation. For the first star, the position remains the same as found in Tycho’s work. However, the observations attributed to the second star actually produced the position that the first star holds in Tycho’s records. As for the second star itself, I have not yet found anything certain, for I have not had the opportunity to access the clean copies <note>original: "munda exemplaria," referring to the original, unedited manuscript logs of observations.</note> of the observations. Neither this second star, nor much less the sixth "unformed" star <note>Stars not strictly part of the constellation's main outline were called "informis" or unformed.</note>, could be included within the narrow limits of my table.

As for the fifth star, its distances from:
The Head of Hercules <note>The star Ras Alhague.</note> are 20° 50 1/2' } which argue for a position of 14° 10'
The Tail of the Eagle <note>The star Altair.</note> are 47° 18' }
of Scorpio in the year 1600; not 15° 10': and a latitude of 37° 17' North, not 37° 28'.

Regarding the right knee of Ophiuchus, it was mentioned above in Chapter XIII that it is in excess by 4 minutes <note>original: "scrupulis," a term for minutes of arc (1/60th of a degree).</note>.

A surplus in longitude of ten minutes <note>original: "unius denarij scrupulorum," literally "one ten of minutes."</note> was also noted in the small star of the triangle in the forehead of Scorpio, which is a double star. To the senses, the angle at the bright star of the forehead appears to be a right angle. But if you follow the numbers in the *Progymnasmata* <note>Tycho Brahe's book *Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata* (Introduction to the Renewed Astronomy).</note>, the angle becomes acute. I prove it thus: because it is a right angle, and the triangle, being near the Ecliptic <note>The sun's apparent path across the sky, used as the primary reference line for celestial coordinates.</note>, is almost rectilinear; therefore, as the difference in longitude of the two upper stars of the triangle (87 1/2') is to the difference in latitude (37'), so the difference in latitude between the brightest and the small star (51') is to the difference in their longitude (21 1/2'). Since the brightest star in the year 1600 is at 27° 36' of Scorpio, by adding 21 1/2', this small star will be at 27° 57' of Scorpio—not at 28° 7', as is written in the book.

Refer here to the copperplate illustration marked with the symbol ***, in which **AEQ** denotes the equator; **EC** the Ecliptic; **α** and **ε** Saturn; **η** and **ι** Jupiter; **υ** Mars; and **N** the New Star <note>The supernova SN 1604, which appeared in the constellation Ophiuchus.</note>. They are distinguished thus: **α** and **η**, shown as empty circles, designate the locations of Saturn and Jupiter when they were in conjunction on December 7/17, 1603 <note>Kepler provides the date for both the Julian and Gregorian calendars.</note>. The symbols **ε**, **ι**, and **υ** signify the positions of Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars on September 30 or October 10 <note>1604</note>, on which day the New Star was first seen in Prague. The remaining straight and curved lines were explained a little further above. Indeed, the names of the other stars are evident from the parts of the constellation figures to which they belong.

<meta>This page transitions from the introductory front matter and library markings to the core scientific argument of Kepler’s treatise. It begins Chapter 15, which focuses on determining the distance of the 1604 Supernova from Earth.</meta>

ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT <note>original: "Serpentarius," the historical name for the constellation Ophiuchus, the Serpent Bearer.</note>. 77

### On the position of this star in the diameter of the universe, or on its immense distance from the center of the earth.

### CHAPTER XV.

The more carefully that most eminent Astronomer, **Tycho Brahe** <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601) was Kepler’s mentor and the most precise observational astronomer of the pre-telescopic era.</note>, has examined this subject, the more briefly I shall be able to dispatch my own business here. At the outset, I shall demonstrate that this star was not only located above the orbit of the Moon, but was not even closer to us than the Sun itself.

<margin>
This star
lacked
Parallax.
</margin>

The demonstration is easy. Since the star possessed no "true motion" <note>Meaning it did not move across the sky like a planet, but stayed fixed relative to the other stars.</note> of its own; if it had been closer than the Moon itself, it would have shifted its apparent position against the zodiac more than the Moon does. Therefore, at least to our sight, it would have appeared to move through some small space of the heavens, even if it were actually standing still. For the sake of clarity, let the less experienced reader take an example—even though in my book *Optics*, Chapter 9, I believe I explained the theory of <term>parallaxes<gloss>the apparent displacement of an object when viewed from two different points</gloss></term> so thoroughly that nothing more could be desired. 

Nevertheless, let us use this simple and tangible example: Hold your head still and close your eyes alternately. With one eye open, look at your nose. If you look with the right eye, the nose will seem to be over the left part of a book or the floor; but if you look with the left eye, the nose will hide the right parts of the book. Every time you switch eyes, the nose will seem to "jump," even though it is standing still.

Next, do not look at your nose, but look at your thumb held still, a little further away from your face. By alternating eyes, the thumb will do the same thing the nose did before, but the "jumps" will not be as large. From this, common sense judges that the thumb is further away than the nose, because it creates a smaller parallax—or change in apparent position.

This example applies perfectly to our subject. For what two eyes are to us, two locations in the world are to astronomers; what the nose and thumb are in the example, the Moon and some more distant star are to them; and what the book or floor is here, the <term>Sphere of Fixed Stars<gloss>the outer shell of the universe in the Copernican and Tychonic systems, where the stars were thought to be embedded</gloss></term> is to them. Just as the thumb and nose are still far away from the floor or the book (even though when viewed with one eye they seem to touch and cover them), so the wandering stars <note>The planets.</note> are not in the highest heavens among the fixed stars themselves, but are far below them. They only *seem* to be in the highest heavens; and the Moon, when viewed from different places on Earth, appears to stand next to different fixed stars. These simple explanations will perhaps help those who are a bit slower to understand the business of parallaxes.

K 3 Now... <note>A printer's "signature mark" used to help the bookbinder assemble the pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Kepler begins Chapter 15 by arguing that the New Star (supernova) is incredibly distant from Earth. He uses a simple analogy of looking at one's nose and thumb with alternating eyes to explain the concept of parallax.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, parallax, supernova, Ophiuchus, celestial distance, astronomical observation, optics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 15 of Kepler’s "De Stella Nova," moving from a conceptual explanation of parallax to specific observational data and mathematical proofs regarding the distance of the 1604 Supernova.</meta>

->78 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

Now it must be proven that the new star produced a smaller <term>parallax<gloss>the apparent shift of an object against a distant background when viewed from different positions</gloss></term> than the Moon, and not larger than the Sun: and therefore, without doubt, none at all. I prove this in two ways. First, as follows: the star was observed in the month of October, while it was setting in the West; but in the months of February and March, it was observed while rising in the East, always maintaining the same position within 6 minutes of arc. Such a small discrepancy could easily be attributed to atmospheric refraction or, in my case, a slightly flawed instrument. For on October $\frac{11}{21}$ <note>Kepler provides both the Julian and Gregorian calendar dates, which were 10 days apart at this time.</note> in the year 1604, the position of the New Star was found—using three other stars as references—to be at 17° 39', 17° 40', or 17° 42' of Sagittarius while it was setting. Yet on February $\frac{1}{11}$, when it was observed while rising by the star Aquila <note>the star Altair</note>, it was similarly found at 17° 40½'; and by the heart of the Scorpion <note>original: "cor Scorpij," the star Antares</note> at 17° 44½' of Sagittarius; or more correctly, from **Fabricius’s** <note>David Fabricius (1564–1617), a German astronomer and frequent correspondent of Kepler.</note> observation, at 17° 42' of Sagittarius.

<margin>
If the star had been in the vicinity of the lunar orbit, how great an apparent change of position it would have made.
</margin>

But if it had been no further away than sixty <term>semidiameters<gloss>the distance from the center of the Earth to its surface; one Earth-radius</gloss></term> of the Earth, and had remained stationary under the same spot on the sphere of the fixed stars, it would have produced a sufficiently large parallax. Let us suppose 0° of Sagittarius is setting and 0° of Gemini is rising: the <term>nonagesimal<gloss>the point on the ecliptic that is 90 degrees from the horizon, representing its highest point in the sky</gloss></term> from the rising point is 0° of Pisces, which, according to the tables of **Magini's** "First Mobile" <note>Giovanni Paolo Magini (1555–1617) was an Italian astronomer whose tables were widely used for calculating celestial positions.</note>, would be elevated at nearly 23°, being 67 degrees distant from the <term>zenith<gloss>the point directly overhead</gloss></term>. Therefore, according to the doctrine of parallax delivered in Chapter 9 of my *Optics*, and by the table of parallaxes—assuming a distance of 60½ semidiameters and a total parallax of 57'—the parallax in latitude is shown to be 52' 28'' and the horizontal parallax in longitude to be 22' 16''. And since the position of the star is at 18° of Sagittarius, 72° distant from 0° of Pisces: the parallax of longitude under the values of 22' and 16'' will be 21' 10'' toward the West. Thus, even if the star were truly at 17° 40' of Sagittarius, it would nonetheless appear to be at 18° 1' 10'' of Sagittarius.

Conversely, let 0° of Capricorn rise, so that 0° of Libra becomes the nonagesimal, which is elevated by 33° 20', being 59° 40' distant from the zenith. Therefore, the parallax of latitude (given the same parameters as before) is 47' 37'', which creates a difference in latitude (from the previous 52' 28'') of nearly five minutes. Yet the distance of the star from the head of Ophiuchus <note>original: "capite Ophiuchi," referring to the star Rasalhague</note> was always the same, both when it was setting in 1604 and when it was rising in 1605: and thus the latitude was always the same, between 1° 55' and 1° 57' North. But let us complete the calculation for the parallax of longitude: for here, by the altitude of the nonagesimal at 33° 20', the value for longitude is 31' 35'', under which—at a distance of 18° of Sagittarius from the nonagesimal—the parallax is shown to be 30' 53'' toward the East. Therefore, while the star remains at 17° 40' Sagittarius, it would be seen at 17° 9' 7'' Sagittarius. Consequently, it would have changed its apparent position from 17° 9' 7'' Sagittarius to 18° 1' 10'' Sagittarius—a shift of 52½ minutes.

And yet, it did not change its position at all—certainly not by any amount that could be compared with this quantity.

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER. 79**

<note>A geometric diagram illustrates the principle of parallax. Point **a** represents the center of the Earth, while point **b** represents an observer on the Earth's surface. A celestial arc at the top represents the sphere of the fixed stars in the sign of Sagittarius. Lines of sight are drawn from both the center of the Earth and the observer's position to show how a nearby object would appear to shift against the background of distant stars (points G, H, e, f, c, and d) depending on the observer's perspective.</note>

G and H are two markers at the 18th degree of Sagittarius. G represents the East <note>the rising position</note>, and H represents the West <note>the setting position</note>; so that in both cases, the New Star would remain on the same line drawn from the center of the Earth **A** to the fixed stars. This line is **AG** during its rising in the month of February, and **AH** during its setting in the month of October. It is evident that if the New Star had existed so close to the Earth, it would have been seen from the place of observation **B** along the line **BCE** when rising, and along **BDF** when setting. In the latter case, it would appear at **F**, before the marker **H** (at 18° Sagittarius); in the former case, it would appear at **E**, after the marker **G** (at 18° Sagittarius). Indeed, it could by no means have appeared in the same position among the fixed stars, at **G** or **H**.

And at this point, what should I say—or rather, what should I not say—concerning the wretched state of our age? Amidst such great illumination of the doctrine of <term>parallaxes<gloss>the apparent shift of an object against a distant background when viewed from different positions</gloss></term>, and such great consensus between the Philosophers and the Mathematicians, there still exists a certain individual—not a commoner, but a Philosopher and a physician... <note>original: "medic-", likely "medicum." Kepler is likely beginning a critique of a specific contemporary scholar who disputed the astronomical findings regarding the star's distance.</note>

<summary>Kepler uses a geometric diagram to demonstrate that if the New Star were close to Earth, its position relative to the zodiac would shift between its rising and setting; since no such shift was observed, it must be located among the distant fixed stars.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s Chapter 15 of "De Stella Nova," where he defends the precision of astronomical observation against critics who claimed the 1604 Supernova's position could not be accurately measured.</meta>

80 JOHANNES KEPLER

...so many others? What of Christine of Savoy? What of the other Frenchmen, in whose country another booklet by this Italian was printed, dealing with the same subject in the Latin language? Why do you tolerate such great disgrace with such patience? Or do I truly suspect that you consider these trifles unworthy of public refutation?

But the Philosopher's reasoning is plainly ridiculous. He perhaps heard from mathematicians that in the doctrine of <term>parallaxes<gloss>the apparent shift of an object against a distant background when viewed from different positions</gloss></term>, the center of the Moon and the star come into consideration. Understanding this "center" to be that which is internal and within the physical body, he leaps for joy, thinking he has found an escape: for he denies that this center can be seen. It would be beneath me to answer him here in front of mathematicians; but if anyone else has been driven mad by this blind Master, I tell him that astronomers speak of the *visible center* of a *visible object*, endowed with *visible size*. This center does not need to be seen by itself, but is instead calculated from the observed and measured edges. For this is how astronomers are accustomed to work: first they observe the <term>altitude<gloss>the height of a celestial object above the horizon</gloss></term> of the top edge of the Moon, then the bottom edge. Then they add half of the difference to the smaller value to obtain the altitude of the middle, or the center. For example, if the altitude of the top edge is 50° 44' and the bottom is 50° 12', the difference is 32', and the half is 16'. This, added to 50° 12', makes 50° 28'. This is what they call the altitude of the center, seen through the edges, not in itself.

But why do I linger on these things, when I see that the man is ignorant of what an astronomical observation even is, let alone having ever performed one himself? For he denies that the center of the Sun can be seen except by those with eagle-like eyes. And since he himself lacks these, it follows that the Sun has never been observed by him. It was not fitting for such a man to teach in the assembly at Padua <note>The University of Padua was a prestigious center for learning where Galileo also taught; Kepler is criticizing a specific Aristotelian rival there.</note> the things he dreams up about the New Star and other astronomical matters; rather, he should learn from that most learned assembly what he has remained ignorant of until now.

I can hardly say with what disgust I read his book on celestial matters, written against the mathematicians and printed in Paris. It had to be read, however, for the sake of my patron <note>Kepler served as the Imperial Mathematician to Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II.</note>. O, the remarkable preoccupations of the human race! That one man builds, and another destroys because he never learned how to build; so that, forsooth, some work and space remains for a third person to rebuild. How gladly I exclaim here that line from <term>Persius<gloss>A Roman satirist known for his difficult but moralizing poetry</gloss></term>: *“O, the cares of men! Oh, how much emptiness there is in human affairs!”* <note>original Latin: "O curas hominum, ô quantum est in rebus inane."</note> But enough of this.

For it has been stated and demonstrated, despite the grumbling of this most unskilled Philosopher, that the star did not change its location by any notable portion of the 52 1/2 minutes. Now let us proceed further.

<margin>And how much in</margin> And since 3 minutes of parallax are usually attributed to the Sun, because it is judged to be 1,200 <term>Earth radii<gloss>the distance from the Earth's center to its surface, used by early astronomers as a standard unit of measurement</gloss></term> away; and these 3 minutes are the nineteenth part...

<meta>This page continues Chapter 16 of Kepler’s work on the New Star of 1604. Having previously calculated the star's distance using a traditional stationary-Earth model, Kepler now shifts to the controversial Copernican system to show how much further away the star must be if the Earth is in motion.</meta>

82 JOHANNES KEPLER

<margin>The altitude of the New Star according to Copernican hypotheses.</margin> Truly, those distances which we have considered for the new star thus far are mere trifles, so long as we remain within the common opinion that the earth is at rest. But if we were to open the bottomless depths of the Copernican immensity—good God!—to what height will this star be raised?

Let no one think that this labor is useless: to demonstrate something according to the opinion of Copernicus. Believe me, this opinion is not only spreading, but it is clearly gaining strength among Philosophers. Nor is this surprising, since Mathematics and natural science struggle eagerly on its behalf; nor do the more sacred disciplines <note>Kepler refers here to theology and the study of the Bible.</note> contradict it, provided they are not twisted according to the whim of any private individual, but are valued according to their own custom.

In the diagram provided previously, **C** is the Sun, **HKO** is the orbit in which the earth travels, and **D** is the location of the new star, near the path of Saturn, or even of Saturn itself.

According to the Prutenic Tables <note>original: "Ex Prutenicis"; these were astronomical tables published in 1551 by Erasmus Reinhold, based on Copernicus's theories, used by astronomers to calculate the positions of celestial bodies.</note>, it can be seen that if Saturn stood motionless at the highest point of its orbit, which is **D** (when it is ten times further away than the Sun is from the Earth); and if the Earth moved away from the line **CD** (which connects the Sun to Saturn) from point **K** to the furthest side of its orbit at **O**; then the <term>parallax<gloss>the apparent shift of an object's position when viewed from two different points</gloss></term> of Saturn, or the angle **CDO**, would be about 6 degrees.

However, since the star did not change its position by even a tenth of one degree (which would occupy the angle **CDO**), which is to say, not even a sixtieth of those six degrees; it is necessary that this star be higher than sixty times the distance of Saturn. And because this Saturnian distance is ten times the Solar distance, the star must be higher than six hundred times the Solar distance. Thus, the distance **CO** (from the Sun to the Earth) does not even equal a six-hundredth part of the distance **CD**.

Therefore, if 600 distances of the Sun from the earth—each having 1,200 <term>semidiameters<gloss>the radius of the Earth, the standard unit for astronomical distance at the time</gloss></term> of the earth—which is to say, if seven hundred and twenty thousand semidiameters of the earth were stretched from the earth upward into the heavens; even at that remote location, a parallax of six minutes could still occur. But since the most faithful observations have all agreed within 2 minutes (which is a third part of six), it is certain, according to the opinion of Copernicus, that this star was at least three times higher still. Therefore, twenty-one hundred thousand and sixty thousand <note>2,160,000</note> semidiameters of the earth lay between this star and the earth. 

Why say more? The mind is exhausted wandering in this immensity of the world, in which it finds no rest in counting, no stopping place, and no goal for returning. Therefore, having set aside these overly subtle divisions of numbers, let us aspire toward a geometric contemplation of the whole.

He concludes by expressing awe at the resulting "immensity" of the universe, which exhausts the human mind's ability to calculate.</summary>

ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 83

On the immensity of the Sphere of the fixed stars in the Copernican hypotheses: and on the magnitude of the New Star.
CHAPTER XVI.

**I** am certain that among those who will read this, some will laugh at the madness of Copernicus; others, who embrace his view because of other Astronomical and Physical arguments, will nonetheless be offended by this immensity. They will begin to doubt whether the arguments brought from other sciences to establish the opinion of Copernicus are truly stated, or whether there might be some way to dissolve them and show the occasion by which the Copernicans were led into error.

<margin>What Brahe was accustomed to object against Copernicus.</margin> And Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), the great Danish astronomer and Kepler's predecessor, who proposed a system where the Sun orbits the Earth while other planets orbit the Sun.</note> added weight to this objection, as he sought harmony in a most perfect work; he found it offensive if the vastness of the single sphere of fixed stars <term>Fixed stars: stars that appear to stay in the same position relative to one another, unlike the "wandering" planets.</term> were so absurdly large, while the smallness of all the moving bodies was so contemptible. As he says, it would be a great defect in the human body if a finger or a nose were to exceed the mass of all the rest of the body by many parts. Therefore, to dissolve this objection as the occasion arises, I will say three things: first, that much more incredible things result from the opinion of the ancients and Ptolemy <note>Ptolemy was the 2nd-century astronomer whose Earth-centered model dominated for 1,400 years.</note> regarding the motion of the fixed stars; second, I will establish the inestimable magnitude of this proportion by other examples taken from the world; finally, I will show a mutual compensation by which a certain equality arises from these most unequal things.

<margin>What the measurement of motions is according to Copernicus.</margin> Therefore, regarding the motion of worldly bodies, in the Copernican hypotheses, the globe of Saturn in one hour travels nearly 300 German miles <note>A German mile (milliarium germanicum) was significantly longer than a standard mile, measuring approximately 7.5 kilometers or 4.6 miles.</note>, Jupiter 400, Mars 600, the center of the earth 740, Venus 800, and Mercury 1000. This is a beautiful proportion, where the motion is always faster the closer a body is to the resting Sun, which is the dispenser of all motion. And because the earth is not only moved annually, but also rotates daily: thus in one hour, the parts of the earth situated under the equator travel 240 German miles relative to the center; the rest travel less, as each is nearer to the poles. Therefore, by adding the 240 miles of the surface to the 740 of the center, they amount to nearly a thousand miles—which is the amount of etheric space a city located under the Equator traverses in one hour at midnight. Similarly, by subtracting 240 from 740, there remain 500 miles, the journey of one hour at midday under the Equator through the etheric air. From this, one begins to consider that because the traversal is twice as fast...
L 2

<summary>Kepler defends the vast size of the Copernican universe against Tycho Brahe's criticisms by arguing that the speeds of the planets follow a logical, proportional harmony centered on the Sun.</summary>
<keywords>Copernicus, Tycho Brahe, planetary motion, German miles, sphere of fixed stars, cosmic proportions, solar system, orbital speed</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 16 of Kepler’s "On the New Star," where he defends the Copernican sun-centered model against the charge that its vast distances are "absurd." Kepler shifts the argument from the size of the universe to the speed of the celestial bodies.</meta>

84 JOHANNES KEPLER

<margin>On the cause of the coldness of the nights and the morning time.</margin> ...the path of the lands, the nocturnal compared to the diurnal under the Equator; whether this can contribute anything to the coldness of the nights, as if from a kind of ventilation; and whether for this same reason, the morning time is more pleasant; because the parts of the earth where the Sun is already rising are, at that moment, carried directly into the ethereal breeze <note>Kepler suggests that as the Earth rotates, the side entering daylight "hits" the ether—the substance filling space—creating a cooling breeze, much like sticking one's hand out of a moving carriage window.</note>; whereas the parts where the Sun sets are, at that time, essentially being pulled away from the ethereal breeze. 

There remains for us the Moon, which travels 476 miles in one hour relative to the center of the Earth; this is because it is 60 radii <note>A radius here is the distance from the center of the Earth to its surface.</note> away from the Earth. Add 476 to the 740-mile journey of the Earth's center, and the journey of the **Full Moon** through the ethereal breeze amounts to 1,216 miles. Conversely, subtract 476 from the 740-mile journey of the center, and there remains a journey of 264 miles in one hour for the **New Moon**. You have the proportionate speed of all the stars according to Copernicus. 

<margin>The rest <note>original Greek: ἠρέμησις (eremesis), meaning a state of stillness or rest.</note> of the fixed stars.</margin> For the fixed stars are in place and remain at rest. The Sun acts as the Mover, and maintains an immobile station in the middle of the World; except that I have established, by physical reasoning in my commentaries on the motions of the star Mars <note>This refers to Kepler's *Astronomia Nova* (1609), where he first proposed that the Sun’s rotation drives the planets.</note>, that its body is turned within its own space. 

<margin>The rotation <note>original Greek: δίνησις (dinesis), meaning a whirling or circular motion.</note> of the Sun.</margin> By this rotation, the Sun drives an immaterial species <term>Immaterial species: an invisible force or "influence" that radiates from a body to affect others at a distance, a precursor to the concept of gravity.</term>, a motive force, throughout the breadth of the World, which all the wandering stars <note>The planets.</note> follow; the closer each one is, the faster it moves.

But lest this journey of a thousand miles in a single hour should still seem incredible to you, I bid you consider the proportion of the density of air to the density of the ether, which I demonstrated in my *Optics* <note>Kepler's *Astronomiae Pars Optica* (1604). He argues that space is so thin (low density) that moving through it at high speeds causes no friction or resistance.</note>; from which it follows that a journey of a thousand miles in an hour through the ether is many times—by an incredible factor—calmer than a journey of one mile in an hour through our air.

<margin>The incredible swiftness of movements in Ptolemy and the accepted opinion.</margin> Go now to Ptolemy and the ancient opinion; you will find everything there to be even more incredible. In that system, the radius of the Sphere of fixed stars is twenty thousand times the Earth's radius; the circumference, therefore, will be sixty-three thousand radii. A modest enough number, certainly, when compared to the Copernican scale; but this entire distance is said to be traveled in a single day. Therefore, 2,625 radii are traveled in one hour—and each of these radii contains 860 miles. 

Note here the immense difference: Saturn, which in Ptolemy's system is closest to the fixed stars (so close it nearly touches them), travels 300 miles in one hour according to Copernicus. According to Ptolemy, it travels two million, two hundred and fifty-seven thousand, five hundred miles <note>2,257,500 miles per hour.</note>. Therefore, one must believe that Saturn is seven thousand, five hundred and twenty-five times faster in Ptolemy’s system than in that of Copernicus. Whoever tries to grasp this incredible velocity in their mind will be just as exhausted—and even more violently so—than one who tries to grasp the Copernican immensity.

<summary>Kepler compares the speeds of celestial bodies in the Copernican and Ptolemaic systems, arguing that the high speeds required by a stationary Earth (Ptolemy) are far more "incredible" and physically taxing to imagine than the large distances required by a moving Earth (Copernicus).</summary>
<keywords>celestial mechanics, orbital speed, ether, atmospheric density, Saturn, Moon, solar rotation, motive force, Copernican vs Ptolemaic systems</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 16 of Kepler’s "On the New Star," where he defends the Copernican sun-centered model. Kepler is addressing the common objection that the universe would have to be "absurdly large" in Copernicus's system. He argues that this "absurd" size is no more difficult to believe than the "absurd" speeds required by the older Ptolemaic (Earth-centered) system.</meta>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 85**

...consider this, Philosopher: the lack of proportion between an accident <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a property (like motion or color) that cannot exist without a "subject" (a physical body).</note> and its subject is much more problematic here than anyone can rightly claim the lack of proportion between a Part of the world and another part is in the Copernican system. <margin>Compare</margin> <margin>An incredible comparison between the speed in Ptolemy and the incredible mass in Copernicus.</margin> For accidents do not exist without a suitable subject. It is more credible that there is a large subject without motion, than a massive motion in a tiny subject. Therefore, if I translate the Ptolemaic proportion of motions into the Copernican proportion of bodies, I shall do so with the greater applause of Philosophers. Ptolemy multiplies the motion of Aristarchus <note>Aristarchus of Samos was an ancient Greek astronomer who proposed a sun-centered model long before Copernicus.</note> or Copernicus seven-and-a-half thousand times. I will multiply the Ptolemaic body of the fixed stars (20,000 radii <note>Kepler uses the term "semidiameters."</note>) just as many times, so that they rise to 1,500,000 radii taken a hundredfold—that is, 150,500,000. These, when distributed into sums of 1,200 (which is the measure of the interval between the Sun and the Earth), constitute 125,000 solar distances—that is, 125,417.

Yet we do not need such a vast mass to defend Copernicus as the ancients need speed to defend Ptolemy, as will appear. <margin>From where the proportion of the sphere of fixed stars to the body of the sun should be derived, and its beauty.</margin> Let us grant the World its own harmony of proportion—not the one of magnitude that Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe, the great observational astronomer, rejected the Copernican system largely because he believed it required the stars to be too large and the universe too empty.</note> used to desire, but one of beauty and ratio. The perfection of the World is motion, which is, as it were, its very life. For motion, three things are required: the Mover, the Mobile <note>The "Mobile" refers to the objects being moved—in this case, the planets.</note>, and the Place. The Mover is the Sun; the Mobiles are the planets from Mercury all the way to Saturn; the Place is the outermost sphere of the fixed stars.

If it is permitted to state a physical reality mathematically: the Mobiles are the "proportional mean" <note>A mathematical term where the middle value balances the two extremes (A is to B as B is to C).</note> between the Mover and the Place. For the planets indeed strive for the rest of the Place (or the surrounding body) through resistance and a certain kind of weight—why do you laugh, you "Philosophasters" <note>original: "Philosophrasti," a mocking play on the name Theophrastus, used for pseudo-philosophers who pretend to know nature but do not.</note> inexperienced in heavenly matters, rich in a store of imaginary things but most destitute of the true? It is from this resistance that their specific time periods arise (for as far as the Mover is concerned, he is unique and moves uniformly). On the other hand, they receive their motion from the Mover, so that in the Mobiles, both Motion and Rest are in some way mixed.

But if the Mobiles are physically the proportional mean between the Mover and the Place, what is more likely than that, mathematically as well, the diameter of the planets' region is the proportional mean between the diameter of the Sun (the Mover) and the diameter of the Place (the fixed stars)? This being established, it is easy to investigate the size of the fixed stars. For the Sun's diameter exceeds the Earth's diameter five-and-a-half times; let it exceed it by exactly six. When I say this, I assign not 1,200 but 1,432 radii to the distance between the Sun and the Earth. Yet let this be so; I shall be generous, so that the world may become truly great.

In the following diagram, let **CE** be the radius of the Sun's body, **6**, of which **CK**

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s geometric defense of the Copernican system. Having calculated the vast distances required for the stars to remain motionless while the Earth orbits the Sun, he now addresses the objection that these distances are "monstrous" or "absurd" by demonstrating that such distances would result in a stellar parallax so small that no astronomer of his time could possibly detect it.</meta>

# ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 87

<note>original: "perniciſſimo motu moventur"</note> move with a most destructive and rapid motion; in the Copernican system, they rest immobile, while the seven small bodies of the sun <note>the known planets, including Earth</note> move. 

But perhaps the world, adorned with this proportion I have demonstrated, will become so narrow and so close that the fixed stars <term>fixed stars: stars that do not move relative to each other, unlike "wandering stars" or planets</term> would produce some parallax? That is, will the proportion of the lines CK or CO (by which the Sun and Earth are distant) become sensible relative to the distance CD between the Sun and the fixed stars? Not at all. For just a bit above, a distance twenty-one times greater (which is a bit less than a sixteenth part of this current distance, namely 340 times) could only produce a parallax of two minutes. Therefore, this 340-fold distance—being a bit less than a sixteenth of the previous—will claim a parallax of only about an eighth part of a single minute <note>one minute of arc is 1/60th of a degree</note>. 

Or, more skillfully and briefly, like this: Since the Sun has a size of 30 minutes when viewed from Earth (that is, because the angle CKI is 15 minutes), and indeed Saturn is ten times higher, the Sun viewed from Saturn will have a tenth part of those 30 minutes, which is 3 minutes; clearly, this quantity is double the angle CGI. But it is posited that as the solar radius IC is to the radius of Saturn's orbit CG, so is CG (or CB) to the radius of the fixed stars CD. Therefore, by connecting the points B and D, the triangles BCD and ICG will be similar; for this reason, the angles BCD and IGC are equal. 

Thus, even this orbit of Saturn, CB, if viewed from the Sphere of the fixed stars (or point D), would show an appearance of only 1½ minutes. And since the Earth's path CO is a tenth part of Saturn's diameter CB, the angle ODC will be roughly a tenth part of the angle BDC. In this way, the radius of the Earth’s annual orbit CO would cause a parallax <term>parallax: the apparent shift of an object against a distant background when viewed from two different points</term> of no more than 9 seconds <note>9"</note>, which is a seventh part of a single minute. 

But there is no astronomer, however skilled in observation, who could boast of reaching a seventh or eighth part of a minute in his observations. For example, even if the distance between two stars at sign D is smaller by a seventh part of a minute when the Earth is at point O (when D is seen at a right angle to the Sun, or line OC) than when D is in opposition to the Sun (with the Earth at K), the astronomer still will not discern these distances while observing them. This is because the bodies of the fixed stars themselves usually occupy and span one, two, three, or even four minutes of space <note>Before the invention of the telescope, stars appeared to the naked eye as small, blurry discs rather than points of light, making precise measurements of their centers very difficult.</note>.

Thus it has been shown that no one is well-advised to object to the immensity of the World against Copernicus—at least no one who embraces any of the astronomical hypotheses as true, and who does not wish to see the whole of Astronomy utterly overturned.

Now we must also see by what examples this monstrous proportion <margin>Monstrous proportions [are not...]</margin> might be softened for us. Here I am accustomed to object to Tycho Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe was a famous Danish astronomer who argued against the Copernican system because he believed the required distances were theologically and physically impossible.</note> by using the proportion of a tiny animal—the one that drives burrows under the skin of the hand <note>Kepler likely refers to the *Sarcoptes scabiei* or scabies mite, a microscopic creature well-known in early medicine.</note>—compared to that Great Serpent...

<summary>Kepler uses geometry to prove that the "immense" distances required by the Copernican model are consistent with observation, as the resulting shift in star positions (parallax) would be too small for human instruments to detect. He begins to use biological analogies to argue that vast differences in scale are natural.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument against the "absurdity" of vast cosmic distances. Having shown that a large universe is mathematically consistent with observation, he now addresses the philosophical objection that such vastness makes humanity seem insignificantly small, arguing that physical size is not a measure of spiritual or functional importance.</meta>

88 JOHANNES KEPLER

<margin>that they are rare among creatures.</margin>
...that serpent mentioned by Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist; he described a 120-foot snake killed in Africa during the Punic Wars</note> of 120 feet, whose remains were preserved at Rome, and one even larger seen in the North, as Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe, the great Danish astronomer and Kepler’s predecessor</note> asserted. The proportion of its length to its environment extended to one part in a hundred thousand. But this is a playful example. Take another. How small, I ask, is a man compared to the globe of the Earth?

<margin>The lowly proportion of the human body compared to the globe of the Earth.</margin>
Let us calculate. From the surface to the center is 860 miles <note>Kepler uses "German miles," which are much longer than modern English miles</note>, which you multiply by 5,000 paces. Therefore, there are 4,300,000 paces. This is quintupled for feet, namely 21,500,000 feet. Give a man’s height exactly seven feet, and divide that number by this. Therefore, if you were to arrange thirty-one hundred thousand men <note>3.1 million men</note> in a line, they would reach from the surface of the earth to its center. And a single diameter of the earth would equal more than six hundred myriads <note>one myriad is 10,000; thus 6 million</note> of human heights. Everyone admits this immense proportion of man to the globe of the earth; yet they now call "incredible" the proportion of the Earth to the sphere of Saturn, which is one to twelve thousand, and the "incredible" proportion of the moving spheres to the sphere of the fixed stars <term>fixed stars: stars that appear not to move relative to one another, forming the outermost shell of the universe in older models</term>, which is one to three thousand—both being much smaller than the first proportion. Truly, they do not look at the proportion, but at the magnitude, because they themselves are small. Indeed, the world is not "big" to God, but we are "tiny" to the world.

<margin>How the smallness of creatures is compensated by their nobility.</margin>
Yet, look also at the analogy. Where magnitude dominates, there perfection is lacking; and as mass diminishes, nobility <note>original: "nobilitas," meaning inherent worth, excellence, or complexity</note> takes its place. In the Copernican system, the Sphere of the fixed stars is certainly the most vast; but it is inert, with no motion. Then follows the moving World <note>the solar system</note>. This is as much more divine as it is smaller, because it has received a motion so admirable and so orderly. Yet that region does not consist of a vegetative faculty, nor does it reason, nor does it engage in discourse: what it does (while it moves), it did not learn, but retains as something impressed upon it from the beginning; what it is not, it will never be; what it is, it did not make of itself; it remains the same as it was created. 

Then comes this little pill of ours, our little hut, which we call Earth: the womb of growing things, itself informed within by a certain faculty, the architect of wondrous works. Every day, it kindles from itself the little souls of so many plants, so many fish, so many insects, that it might easily despise the remaining mass of the universe in comparison to its own nobility. Finally, look for me at the little bodies we call animals; what can be imagined more tiny in comparison to the world? Yet there we find senses, voluntary motions, and an infinite architecture of bodies. Look among these at these specks of dust called Men; to whom the Creator gave this: that they are, in a way, born from themselves, they clothe themselves, arm themselves, teach themselves infinite arts, and daily progress toward the better. In them is the image of God; they are, in a way, the masters of the entire mass. And who among us would wish for a body...

<summary>Kepler uses mathematical calculations to show that the relative size of a human to the Earth is far smaller than the Earth is to the stars, arguing that physical vastness does not diminish human importance. He proposes a law of "nobility" where smaller things (like life and human reason) possess greater complexity and divinity than vast, inert cosmic spaces.</summary>

ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 89

...body <note>continuing the sentence from page 88: "is the human body so overwhelmed..."</note> by the vastness of the World, so that it might lack a soul for that reason? Let us learn, therefore, the good pleasure of the creator, who is the author of both the raw mass and the perfection of tiny things; yet He does not boast in mass, but ennobles those things which He willed to be small.

Finally, through these intervals from the Earth to the Sun, from the Sun to Saturn, and from Saturn to the fixed stars, let us learn to gradually ascend to recognize the immensity of divine power.

And I have inserted these things regarding the vastness of the fixed stars—an objection raised against Copernicus—all the more willingly because they pertain to estimating the incredible magnitude of the new star. <margin>The immense mass of the newest star.</margin> For if it occupied only four minutes <note>arcminutes: a unit of angular measurement; there are 60 minutes in one degree</note> (as large as Sirius appears), then by this Copernican hypothesis, it was much larger than the entire machine of the moving spheres; whereas we previously assigned it only three minutes <note>original: "tria solùm minuta"</note>, if someone were to look back at the solar system from the fixed stars. I refrain from expressing this magnitude in numbers, fearing that I have already pushed these matters too far before the common crowd, inviting their mockery.

***

### On other arguments by which it is proved that this New Phenomenon stood among the fixed stars.
### CHAPTER XVII.

<margin>Immobility as a physical argument for its location among the fixed stars.</margin> You have heard the mathematical argument; now hear also two physical ones, one derived from motion, the other from light. Although what is derived from motion also contends mathematically. Throughout the entire time of its duration, it stood in the same place beneath the fixed stars; it did not change its configurations—with the stars of the foot, the shin, and the right knee of the Serpent-Bearer <note>Ophiuchus</note>, and with the Triangle in the coil of the Serpent—not even in the slightest degree that the senses could perceive. Such is not the nature of Comets, which are found throughout the region of the moving planets: for no comet has yet been observed to be immobile for many days, being at such proximity that it would change its apparent place beneath the fixed stars during the course of a day. However many were so near as to admit a daily or annual parallax <note>parallax: the apparent displacement of an object when viewed from two different positions; closer objects show larger parallax</note>, they were all found to move with some proper motion of their own, in addition to this optical shift we call Parallax. Therefore, it is consistent that those new stars which—as Pliny says in Book 2, Chapter 25 <note>Pliny the Elder’s *Natural History*; the OCR "lib. 11" is likely a typo for "lib. II"</note>—move in the manner of wandering stars <note>planets</note>, are also in the region of the wanderers; but those which remain immobile stand among the fixed stars; so that the shared nature of each property also argues for a shared location.

This immobility, which previously provided us in the Copernican hypothesis...

<summary>Kepler argues that the massive scale of the universe reflects divine power rather than human insignificance. He then begins Chapter 17, asserting that the New Star's lack of motion relative to Ophiuchus proves it is a fixed star, unlike comets which move through the planetary spheres.</summary>
<keywords>Copernican system, divine power, scale of the universe, New Star of 1604, Ophiuchus, parallax, comets, fixed stars, Pliny the Elder</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument that the New Star of 1604 must be located among the fixed stars. Having previously used the Copernican system to prove this via the lack of annual parallax, he now demonstrates that even within the geocentric systems of Ptolemy and Tycho Brahe, the star’s lack of independent motion proves it cannot be a local phenomenon or a planet.</meta>

90 JOHANNES KEPLER

<margin>The new star is also placed among the fixed stars in the accepted hypotheses.</margin>

The argument of zero annual parallax <note>The apparent shift of an object's position when viewed from different points in Earth's orbit</note>, which the movement of the Earth would cause, also applies here. Even in the common Ptolemaic hypothesis and the most recent one by Brahe—in which the Earth remains motionless in its place—it is clearly proven, by a way other than annual parallax, that a place below Saturn cannot be assigned to the New Star. For as far as Ptolemy is concerned, and the ancient opinion he follows, he holds that all the spheres are solid, from the lowest Moon up to the outermost Saturn. Therefore, if the star were within the complex of the Saturnian heaven (since by all three types of hypotheses it is most certain to be placed above the Moon), it would then be in one of the solid spheres. And since the penetration of bodies is not possible <note>The Aristotelian principle that two solid bodies cannot occupy the same space</note>, it would clearly be carried along with the spheres; for there is none of them that stands idle and motionless.

<margin>The same applies to the Tychonic system.</margin>

As for Tycho Brahe, he would first argue with a dilemma: For either the solid spheres touch each other, as the Aristotelians wish, and thus the new star could not be among the orbits of the planets without being carried to the parts toward which that sphere was turned which provided a home for the star, since all spheres are assumed to move: or there are no solid spheres, no contact, and no carrying along, as Tycho himself believes. And then Tycho would ask: if this new star was within the region of the moving bodies <note>The planetary region</note>, by what mental agitation could a corruptible and temporary body follow the fixed stars in the feet of the Serpent-bearer <note>original: "Serpentarii"; the constellation Ophiuchus</note> so religiously in their daily motion—in whose region and complex it is not admitted by the opponent—that it did not desert them by so much as a finger’s breadth, despite having revolved so many times around the earth? For the Planets indeed recede from the fixed stars by their daily motion, on account of their own proper motions: which same thing all Comets have done, which were observed to have stood among the Planets; so that the place of the New Star is not among the Planets. Next, Tycho maintains that all Planets follow the Sun in such a way that if you mentally remove the Planet’s proper motion—that is, if it should happen that the Planet was by itself free from its peculiar course—nevertheless, with the Sun circling around, by a certain natural law of sympathy, like some ape, it would circle in the ethereal air in a path clearly similar to the Solar path, and clearly equal in dimensions and time, each in its own place. 

<note>A geometrical diagram illustrates the Tychonic or Ptolemaic planetary theory. The Earth (A) is at the center. Around it is the circular orbit of the Sun (BCDE). A planet (P) is positioned relative to the Sun (B) and the distant fixed stars at the top. Lines and labels (a, q, k, p, d) indicate the angles of observation and the geometric relationships between the Earth, Sun, and the planetary body.</note>

In the attached Diagram, let A be the Earth, and BCDE be the circuit of the Sun. First, let the Sun be at B and the Planet at P, so that the line connects the Sun to the Planet—

He is arguing that if the New Star were located within the planetary spheres, it would be subject to the same physical forces (the "dragging" power of the Sun) that move the planets. Since the star does not move, it must be located further away, in the sphere of the fixed stars.</meta>
