...connecting [them], becomes B P, which when extended beneath the fixed stars falls at F. Therefore, given that a Planet lacks its own proper motion—as this New Star lacked—yet by the law of sympathy <note>original: *sympatheias*; here referring to a shared physical coordination or "feeling together" between the Sun and other bodies in the Tychonic or Copernican systems</note>, the line B P (and the Planet at its end at P) revolves with the Sun itself, remaining parallel to itself, and is positioned at QC, XD, IE. Thus, the Planet, by this external force <note>original: *raptu extraneo*; literally "extraneous snatching," describing the force that "drags" a celestial body along a path not its own</note>, describes the circuit PQXI, which is equal to BCDE; however, it appears among the fixed stars at FKZY. 

Whoever maintains this position, what reason would he offer as to why the New Star would not do the same as all the Planets do, if it were situated in the same place as the Planets? Is it that none of the Planets is strong enough to resist the Sun as it revolves, so that all are overcome and all are dragged along; yet only this New Star has learned a contrary path? Has it acquired such great power that, by some force and along some path, it might retrace with a contrary effort that very journey along which it was being dragged, and thus, having eluded the Sun, remain fixed in its place? 

How incredible these things are! How utterly inconsistent <note>original: *analogōtata*; a Greek term meaning "most disproportionate" or "lacking in any logical analogy"</note>! And yet it is certain, from the apparent immobility of the star, that either it was not moved in such a way as to imitate the annual motion of the Sun; or [the] movement...

<summary>Kepler argues that if the New Star of 1604 were a planet, it would be compelled to move in coordination with the Sun's path; its absolute immobility proves it must be located far beyond the planets among the fixed stars.</summary>

92 JOHANNES KEPLER

is different from the twinkling of the fixed stars; it is surely slow and continues at great intervals, whereas the twinkling of the fixed stars confuses the sight with an abrupt and extremely rapid reciprocity <note>original: *reciprocatione*; referring to the back-and-forth "pulsing" or "vibrating" appearance of a star</note>, one overflowing of rays overwhelming another. Since there is none among these fixed stars which this New Star did not surpass, both in the purity of its light and in the most rapid shooting forth of its twinkles: there is no reason why a physicist, trained in the school of Aristotle <note>The "school of Aristotle" refers to the traditional academic view that the heavens beyond the Moon were perfect and unchanging. Kepler is mocking this view, as the New Star proves the heavens *do* change.</note>, should continue to doubt its common place among the fixed stars.

***

->**On the purity of light, colors, and twinkling of the New Star.**<-

### CHAPTER XVIII. ###

It is pleasing to examine this topic more diligently. For what is it that gained us knowledge of this new star, if not this very light of its, so bright, so admirable? Therefore, it is a great reward for my labor if, in what I am attempting, I draw out the causes of this brilliance from the inner depths of nature here and there.

<margin>Causes of twinkling.</margin>
J. C. Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558), a famous scholar whose work on natural philosophy was widely read in the 17th century.</note>, in his *Exoteric Exercises*, book 63, brings forward five causes concurring to produce twinkling: Magnitude, Clarity, Motion of the star, the Medium of the air, and the Motion of the light in the star. Whoever examines these causes diligently will find the first, second, and fourth among the helping causes; but will hold the third or fifth as the primary cause. For that which I previously argued with Aristotle from twinkling regarding altitude <note>original: *altitudinem*; in this context, it refers to the distance or height of the star above the Earth</note>: I did so to convince the Aristotelians. As far as the matter itself is concerned, altitude can do nothing by itself, unless the other causes are present or are provided through altitude. For it has been said that twinkling somehow belongs even to the Planets themselves; especially to Venus and Mercury; to Mars very obscurely; to the others not at all. And yet these latter are higher, while the former three are low; and Venus is indeed lower than Mars itself. Therefore, I remain in that opinion which I handed down in chapter 6 of my *Optics* <note>Kepler refers to his 1604 work *Astronomiae Pars Optica*, where he laid the foundations for modern optical science.</note>, which Scaliger also defends: that the cause of twinkling resides in the bodies of the stars themselves, whether it be a motion of light or of the body. And first, concerning the motion of light.

<margin>I. Motion of light.</margin>
It pleases Scaliger, and I myself strongly approve, that there is a natural power in the body of a star of spreading its light, not without motion. The explanation of an obscure matter is not very clear: I shall declare it by examples, if perhaps I may grasp the author's meaning while putting forward my own. I think a sufficiently apt example is found in the motion of the heart and the arteries; so that this also might be common to the heart with the Sun, and life with light, besides those things...

ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT [BEARER]. 93

besides those things which I said in chapters 1 and 6 of my *Optics* <note>Kepler refers to his 1604 work *Astronomiae Pars Optica* (The Optical Part of Astronomy), where he laid the foundations for modern optics.</note>, let this be a shared point. Do not draw an analogy from this, as if just as the heart forced out the spirit by compression and pours it into the body, so the body of the star expresses light by a kind of contraction of itself. For the vital spirit <note>original: *spiritus vitalis*; in Renaissance medicine, this was a subtle substance thought to flow through the arteries to sustain life.</note> is something physical; light, being the purest quality, does not require such crude propulsion. But nonetheless, let some analogous motion be understood in the star—not a motion of place, but of alteration, according to that quality by which the star is a luminous body. Indeed, I propose a living example of this alteration in "living coals" [burning embers]; which from this very motion, and as it were an alternating vigor, have acquired the name "living." Although this example, if you imagine a greater speed for it, corresponds more to the twinkling of Venus.
      Look, is this what it means to philosophize, to be reduced to such poverty: that opinions and the causes of things must be begged from the common folk? For by Christ, nothing is more fitting for an example than that which everyone perceives, and which Francesco Patrizi <note>Francesco Patrizi (1529–1597) was a Venetian philosopher who argued that the stars were made of fire, contrary to the Aristotelian view of celestial ether.</note>—who seemed to possess the deepest wisdom, whether he truly thought so or was speaking from "barbaric" philosophy—certainly imitated uniquely; pronouncing the stars to be "little flames." This is precisely how they appear to the public, and they bear an express resemblance to it, which is why they are so named by most, especially the poets: a term we also used above regarding our New Star.
      Is this, then, the cause of twinkling in the fixed stars, which in our torches is the cause of spewing out different rays one after another in an interrupted fashion? I do not know. And indeed, I might more easily say this of our New Star, because it disappeared, and because it suddenly shone forth with great magnitude, growing by no gradual increments. But regarding the fixed stars, which are eternal fires, <margin>Why our flames twinkle.</margin> there is a reason why I cannot affirm this. For if you ask the reason why our fires flash so, as much in the hearth as in candles, and especially in the furnaces of Chemists [Alchemists] while they burn metals; you will find that which ought not to be found in the fixed stars, lest we should eventually extinguish them along with our own fires. Specifically, a battle exists between the surrounding moist air (or the spirit exhaled from the metal) and the very dry and thin fire. Always, the place occupied by the fire, since it is thinned out by the fire through force, draws into itself the surrounding air—the heavy pressing upon the light by its own nature. Always the fire, having acquired new strength from its matter and being carried by the abundance, dispels the air. Therefore, it is both nourished by fuel <note>original: *pabulum*; literally "food" or "fodder," used here to mean the material consumed by a fire.</note> as long as it lasts, and is attacked by the surrounding medium. But how can these things be said of the fixed stars? What is their fuel? What air is their enemy? What is their age? When have we ever missed any one of them in these six thousand years? <note>Kepler is using the common chronological assumption of his time that the universe was approximately 6,000 years old.</note> Therefore, let those be examples not of substance, but of light. Concerning the fixed stars, let the opinion of Scaliger remain: that a power resides in the permanent—and add also transparent—body, for reciprocating light, analogous to...

94      JOHANNES KEPLER

<margin>II. The Whirling <note>original: *Δίνησις* (dinēsis); a Greek term for rotation or circular motion.</note> of the star.</margin>
[...] analogous to the burning out of our own flames. But to our star, we shall attribute either the same body or the same power of light, as will be seen later.
      Let us, however, weigh the second cause of twinkling, derived from motion: not, indeed, a motion of translation <note>In physics, "translation" refers to an object moving from one location to another in space.</note>, which Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558) was a celebrated scholar who wrote extensively on physics and poetics; Kepler frequently engaged with his theories.</note> alleges; but rather a motion of rotation <note>original: *conversionis*</note>, as I have demonstrated in the Diamond. By this motion, the various parts and angles of a polished and transparent body are revealed one after another. In this case, the first cause is not removed (for no amount of rotation provides light to a star), but it is equipped by this second cause as if by a certain instrument. Thus, even while the star remains quietly in its place, it can display both flashes and various colors—provided that such a great variety of motion in the eternal faculty of an eternal body might be accepted by the perhaps too-unyielding opinions of Philosophers. That the motion of translation, which Scaliger had in mind, can do nothing to cause twinkling,

<margin>The motion of translation is not the cause of twinkling.</margin>
is clear both by reason and by experiment. For how would the daily motion <note>The apparent daily rotation of the heavens.</note> interfere with the rays? This is true whether that motion does not belong to the fixed stars at all (according to the opinion of Copernicus) or whether it leaves the fixed stars in a state of tranquility and carries them around as if they were enclosed in a box (according to the old, accepted opinion). For as for the idea that our air is rubbed <note>original: *atteritur*</note> by the heavens through this motion, that occurs below the Moon. Therefore, if this could contribute anything to twinkling, Saturn and Jupiter would also twinkle. Furthermore, what is the logical consequence leading from the friction of the air to twinkling? He seems to be speaking of iron horseshoes when they are worn down against stones or the hardest ice. Moreover, experiments testify that not all fixed stars twinkle equally, even when they are at an equal elevation above the horizon and of equal magnitude. Therefore, since the daily motion is equally common to all, it is not a suitable cause for producing twinkling.
      But that rotation of individual fixed stars, which I have proposed, is preferable on all these counts. For it indicates the manner of both the twinkling and the colors, which are familiar to transparent bodies, as I showed in my *Optics*; and it derives that manner from the very disposition of the bodies of the stars. This is all the more probable because it is necessary that those perpetual colors, which differ from one star to another, reside in this very disposition of the star's body, as I proved in my *Optics*. Therefore, I do not fear that the bodies of stars cannot be eternal simply because they might be angular, or if they are of unequal density inside, as is common in Pearls <note>original: *Uniones*; large, high-quality pearls often noted for their internal layers.</note>,

<margin>The fixed stars, remaining in their own spaces, rotate <note>original: *δινείσθαι* (dineisthai), to whirl or spin.</note>.</margin>
where some parts are more transparent than others. For no enemy is present there to inflict violence; and we have gems here on this Earth which, if you remove external force, are eternal; and yet they are partly spotted, partly cracked, and partly cloudy.
      Furthermore, the rotation of the fixed stars itself rests upon great probability and great examples. But Copernicus alone provided the example: this Earth of ours; which, so that it might enjoy the sight of the Sun from every side, rotates once every single day,

<summary>Kepler argues that the twinkling of stars is caused by their individual rotation on an axis, rather than their movement through space or the friction of the atmosphere. He uses the optical properties of diamonds and pearls as analogies for how a rotating, multifaceted body can produce flashes of light and color.</summary>
<keywords>scintillation, twinkling, rotation, conversion, translation, Scaliger, Copernicus, optics, diamond, pearls, stellar physics</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 94, Kepler is developing his hypothesis that the twinkling of stars is caused by their rotation. He is comparing the stars to the planets and explaining how they interact with the Sun's light.</meta>

ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 95

each star turns itself, as it were, to be warmed at this fire <note>Kepler refers to the Sun as the central "vital fire" of the universe.</note>. It is believable, therefore, that both the Planets and all the fixed stars rotate in their own spaces; lest there be anything in the World that is entirely deprived of the vital rays and most splendid light of the center, that most noble body <note>The Sun</note>. For as to what perhaps occurs to you regarding the Moon—that she always looks back at the earth with the same face while she is carried around it—she, by this very circuit, since she embraces not the Sun but only the earth, ensures by this constant fixing of her face toward the Earth that she enjoys the Sun from every side in the space of one month: from a distance at the full moon, on the part of the face visible to us; at close range during the new moon, on the part turned away from us.

Two objections occur to me. First, that the sight of the Sun seems to have nothing to do with the fixed stars, because it has been said that the entire Sphere of Saturn <note>In the Copernican and Tychonic systems, the sphere of Saturn was the outermost planetary boundary before the stars.</note> occupies an angle of scarcely three minutes <note>A "minute" here is a <term>minute of arc</term>, a unit of angular measurement equal to 1/60th of a degree.</note> when viewed from the fixed stars. The Sun, therefore, which occupies such a tiny portion of the diameter of Saturn's heaven, will vanish entirely from the perspective of the fixed stars; for will it not appear smaller than the seven-hundredth part of one minute? The second objection is that twinklings are exceedingly rapid; it is therefore a wonder if such vast bodies, some of which surpass the sphere of Saturn in size, are turned so quickly. <margin>What can be objected against the rotation of the fixed stars.</margin>

First, if they have many parts of the kind we said serve for twinklings and colors (which it is indeed fair to grant, since they are massive bodies, to anyone who otherwise accepts this cause), then it is not necessary that every time a single flash gleams, one entire rotation must be completed; rather, just as a wheel has many nails, so these bodies may be believed to thrust out many angles and many flashes in a single rotation. And what if these rotations are entirely rapid, perhaps to compensate for the fact that they are denied any movement from place to place <note>Kepler uses the term "translation," meaning movement through space as opposed to spinning in place.</note>, or for this very reason: that the light of the Sun is so weak among them? For the Sun does not vanish from the nature of things simply because it is reduced to the seven-hundredth part of a minute; it vanishes, perhaps, and indeed certainly, only to our eyes if they were positioned there. For it is not fair to judge the power and sharpness of nature by the dullness of our own vision, just as the lowly insignificance of our small bodies creates no prejudice in acknowledging the vastness of the heavens. If God is so powerful in mass, as we weighed above, why then should He not also be so sharp and subtle in dispensing the light of the Sun? I ask you, reader, to forgive me if I have been too minute in arguing about a matter in which you have seen above that we humans, our globe, the heaven of our globe, and even ten times that heaven, are clearly the most minute of things. Let rotation, however, remain in its place as the cause of twinkling. Let us now see the remaining arguments of Scaliger.

Truly—

<margin>III. Clarity of light.</margin> He <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558), a polymath whose works on natural philosophy Kepler frequently engaged with.</note> says most truly in the second place that the weakness of light hinders twinkling, which I interpret as a certain dullness. Therefore, the clarity and purity of light, purified from matter <note>original: "à materia defecati" — referring to light that is "strained" or cleansed of any obscuring physical substance.</note>, will greatly assist in the twinkling. Certainly. For since light is the very source of the sparkles, the purer the light is, the more readily it twinkles. For this reason, the lights of the Planets are indeed strong and evident, especially due to their proximity; but they are thick, full, corporeal, and heavily colored: and thus they twinkle less. The light of the fixed stars, however, is more watery, thin, sharp, glassy, translucent, or diluted. And this is the cause why the twinkling in our New Star <note>The supernova of 1604, which appeared in the constellation Ophiuchus and was studied extensively by Kepler.</note> was so rapid. For nothing purer, nothing clearer, nothing more delightful than that extraordinary light could be, or even be imagined.

<margin>IV. Magnitude</margin> Next follows magnitude, which is placed in the first rank by Scaliger. But again, magnitude could do nothing, if the light from...

...this is represented more clearly; because humors <note>In 17th-century science, "humors" referred to translucent fluids, such as those within the eye, which can bend light.</note> in themselves, or dense transparent media, with the aid of refraction, produce those same colors of the Rainbow, as can be seen in Crystal Prisms. To conclude this point: although it is true that the star was the brightest and purest, and greater than all others, and was mostly seen at its setting near the horizon in very humid air; nevertheless, all these causes combined could have achieved nothing, unless the light itself in the body of the star—either by an internal faculty <note>The "internal faculty" suggests a soul-like power that Kepler believed might reside within celestial bodies to direct their behavior.</note> or by the very frequent motion of the body itself—had found the causes of its twinkling and colors.

***

On the Matter of the New Star.

### CHAPTER XIX.

Every discussion concerning the matter of the star depends on what we have said so far. Since no one has been present in the heavens to see it up close, certainly whatever we do not perceive here with our eyes—to which the lights of the stars descend by a certain propagation of their species <note>original: "speciei propagatione" — In the optical theories of the time, "species" were the invisible forms or images transmitted from an object through a medium to the eye.</note>—we strive in vain to investigate. It follows, therefore, from the discussion of light and twinkling, that it was one of two things: either a flame, or a body consisting of its own matter. If it was a body, it either held that throbbing motion within itself, from whose paroxysms that twinkling flashed out; or, being transparent in itself, it was spun by a most rapid rotation. 

In neither case is the matter accomplished without some semblance of life, and without the service of an informing, moving, or vegetative faculty. I, for my part, favor the first <margin>Whether the star was flaming.</margin> opinion: that it was a flame; because, like a flame, it was consumed, as if its fuel were failing. For then it would not be necessary to assume life to demonstrate the twinkling, except that life which belongs to flame and to coals. But I cannot explain this: why did it scatter its rays in every direction? Since our flames here on Earth grow upwards from a certain and fixed root; nor is it likely that the same battle between moisture and fire exists there, which here among us produces that waving motion of flames; unless someone perhaps wants to carry this humidity of our air all the way up into the highest ether <note>The "ether" was the weightless, fluid element believed to fill the space of the heavens beyond the Earth's atmosphere.</note>—a thing which the discipline of Optics rejects <note>original: "reclamante Optica disciplina"</note>. But if there are rays in every direction, then the nourishment is in the center, and therefore it is a solid body. In this state of uncertainty, let anyone follow what they wish. It is certain that it was either a flame, or life...

N [catchword: life]

...possessed life; unless perhaps the causes that produce these same things (light, colors, twinkling) are not the same under the Moon <note>In the Aristotelian worldview, the "sublunary" realm (everything below the Moon) was subject to change and decay, while the heavens were considered eternal and unchanging.</note> as they are among the fixed stars.
<margin>The kinship of celestial things with sublunary ones.</margin> Anyone who objects to this reproaches us for our entire investigation into the matter. Yet many things—some of which I have even presented in my *Optics*—argue that those celestial things are distinct from these sublunary things not so much by a difference in essence, but by their distances. I will not even dwell on Moses, who separates "these waters" from "those waters" by a single expansion (for this is how those learned in Hebrew understand the word *Raquiah* <note>original: "Raquiah" — Referring to the "firmament" in Genesis 1:6. Kepler notes that the Hebrew implies a stretching out or expansion of space rather than a solid dome.</note>); though they were confused at the very beginning of the World. Nor will I dwell on Copernicus, who gave the Earth itself the right of citizenship in the heavens <note>Kepler refers to the Copernican shift, which moved the Earth from the center of the universe into the heavens as a planet among planets.</note>.

<margin>The star had its own matter.</margin> However it may be, this much is true in general: this star had its own body and matter. For the "Patrician fires" <note>Referring to Francesco Patrizi (1529–1597), a philosopher who argued that stars were immaterial, living lights or "souls" rather than physical bodies.</note>—immaterial and living, as he makes the stars out to be—have long since cooled down and are thus extinguished. Let an example be shown where light, whether standing by itself or depending on the illumination of another, has lacked a material, corporeal subject. Perhaps Patrizi will say that the Sun is such a light? This is "begging the question" <note>A logical fallacy where the premise of an argument assumes the truth of the conclusion.</note>: the dispute concerns those things in heaven that do not offer themselves to be touched; I am asking for an example on Earth. Will he say those celestial things are not similar to these terrestrial ones? Why then does he, a man of the Earth, argue at all—he in whose mind there is nothing that did not first enter through the gates of the terrestrial senses? On the contrary, those very celestial lights descend to us on Earth. Let him show, therefore, in any place, a celestial light—the image <note>original: "speciem" — In Kepler's optics, the "species" is the formal image or likeness of an object transmitted through space.</note> of the Sun, Moon, or a star—where some subject capable of being illuminated is absent. He will find it nowhere, except either as a brilliance in the air, when the air is very thick; or in a cloud, which is already watery; or in a mirror.

<margin>A reflected image, hanging in the air.</margin> For the fact that in concave mirrors images appear to stand between the viewer and the mirror—appearing as they do in the air, and as if without a body—is a deception of sight, as is explained in works on Optics. This is why it takes its name from "imagination"; in which a location in the air is indeed perceived, but the seen form <note>original: "species"</note> itself is nowhere else but in its own proper subject as its source, and in the eye of the viewer, even if turned away from the object; this happens through the illumination reflected from the mirror, as is taught in Optics. But our question here is not about the location, but about the body that represented that brightness of which we speak. Let Patrizi say, if he pleases, that there was some mirror even there, provided that he confesses it was a body, and does not thrust immaterial fires upon us.

<margin>Planets are bodies, not immaterial lights.</margin> Furthermore, he can show no "automatic" fires <note>Fires that move or sustain themselves without physical fuel.</note> without matter. Certainly all the planets—by their roundness, their spots, their colors, their increase in size as they approach, and their parallaxes <note>The apparent displacement of an object when viewed from different positions; Kepler uses this to prove planets have a measurable distance and physical presence.</note> through which we come to knowledge of their certain height—are proven to be bodies, not immaterial fires; and...

<summary>Kepler argues against the philosopher Francesco Patrizi, asserting that stars and planets (including the New Star) must be composed of physical matter rather than being "immaterial fires." He uses optical principles and the Copernican view of Earth as a celestial body to bridge the gap between terrestrial and heavenly substances.</summary>

### ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT, PAGE 99

and some of these properties the fixed stars have in common with the Planets. But in these lands <note>Meaning on Earth, in the sublunary realm where things are subject to change.</note>, who has ever seen a fire that did not have a body? I am speaking of natural things. For I am not concerned here with spirits, nor with divine lights <margin>Divine lights.</margin> which are seen by those who either enjoy secret conversations with the divine or, through a cursed curiosity, delight in dealings with demons. For if this star was such a light, then some "Patrician" <note>A pun on Francesco Patrizi (1529–1597), whose name Kepler associates with the "patrician" or noble class of immaterial lights.</note> who so strongly denies it has matter, would make it out to be a magic trick, or a purely divine light placed among infinite natural circumstances; I would easily allow anyone who wishes to believe that. 

At present, however, I am attempting a path by which I may provide that which appeared among natural things with the necessary natural requirements. Nor did Patrizi achieve anything other than to show those eternal fires of the heavens, which we know to be parts of Nature, as being without matter; which at present I deny him. Let someone, therefore, <margin>The matter of will-o'-the-wisps.</margin> tell me of a fire or light without a subject <note>In the science of the time, a "subject" was the physical substance that "carried" a property, like wood carries the property of burning.</note>. If there is any example, it is the "foolish fires" <note>original: "ignes fatui" — commonly known as will-o'-the-wisps.</note>; but these wander around marshy places and swamps, making it clear that they use an oily exhalation as their subject. 

Shooting stars <margin>The matter of shooting stars.</margin>, a thing most similar to the stars, are proven by Aristotle to consist of their own matter; and experience taught me this once, when a great fire broke out in this city. On that clear night, I saw more shooting stars than in the entire previous decade. Therefore, some either fatty or dry matter, carried upward by heat into the cold air, distributed in bits and compressed into little globes, and thus ignited—or if you prefer, illuminated—exhibited so many types of racing fires. In short, none of these exist without matter. 

Let us conclude, therefore, <margin>What kind of matter the new star had.</margin> with great probability: that the matter provided for the new star was either "fatty," which was ignited by the cold like shooting stars, or "watery," in which either the Sun or an internal light shone forth. Both theories have some probability. For as far as shooting stars are concerned, quite recently on December 8/18, 1605, as cold was setting in after a warm spell, I saw a falling star very similar to the New Star as it shone at the beginning—extremely white, very clear, and sparking several times like the artificial fires they call "rockets" <note>original: "Raketulos" — Kepler uses the vernacular term for fireworks to describe the behavior of the meteor.</note>; Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (23–79 AD), the Roman naturalist.</note> would call them "fireballs" <note>original: "Bolides"</note>. In favor of the "watery globe" theory, however, are the colors of the rainbow and the twinkling, an opinion I also touched upon in my *Optics*.

->**Whether the matter and body of the New Star already existed long ago.**<-
**CHAPTER 20.**

Here the question arises: from where did this star in the highest ether <note>The "ether" was believed to be the pure, unchanging substance that filled the heavens above the Moon.</note> come—this great mass of matter, which was sufficient to ignite such a massive fire? It certainly did not come from our Earth, which produces only smoke and "falling stars" <note>original: "trajectiones" — a term for meteors, then thought to be atmospheric ignitions.</note>; for the Earth is far too small compared to that vastness. But if anyone persists in arguing peevishly—either by dragging matter out of the Earth into the heavens, or by pulling the star down from the heavens to a place below the Moon—I declare to him that I shall walk out of the Peripatetic school <note>The followers of Aristotle, who believed the heavens were perfect and unchanging, and thus argued that any new star must be in the lower atmosphere near Earth.</note> with my ears stopped, leaving that man shouting and raving. For such stubborn defenders are never silenced unless you first ignore their shouts with a firm silence. I shall deal with reasonable men. First, we need not struggle over its size if its location is established; for indeed, to its own region, this star was no larger than some "falling star" is to our air.

<column-break/>

**[Marginal notes:]**
Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, book 3, chapter 2.
  Some of the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of the ancient Greek philosopher Pythagoras.</note> believe a Comet is a star of the sort that is not always visible, but rises at a fixed time during its own revolution.
  Pliny and Aristotle say the same, though Aristotle refutes them because they had said that when comets are not seen, they "set" <note>original: "δύνειν" (dynein) — to sink below the horizon or hide behind the Sun.</note> near the Sun. But Aristotle says many "have disappeared without setting" <note>original: "ἄνευ δύσεως ἠφανίσθησαν" (aneu dyseōs ēphanisthēsan)</note> while in a place clearly visible above the horizon. Pliny translated this passage ridiculously, saying Comets are not visible "in the setting part of the sky." <note>Kepler is mocking Pliny the Elder for a translation error where he confused "setting" (disappearing) with the "west."</note>

<column-break/>

**[Main text:]**
There are, however, two opinions relevant to this matter. The first is held by those who deny that anything new can exist in the heavens after that first Sabbath of creation; they believe instead that bodies of this kind hide in a certain "Storehouse of Nature" from the beginning of things, lacking light until it pleases God to signify something through them. Others, however, believe that not only the light but the bodies themselves in the heavens are sudden and new: whether they are created by that absolute power of Divine Majesty by which God fashioned the entire world in the beginning; or whether they consist of some force of Celestial Nature, in the same way that "thick" or, if you prefer, "dry" exhalations exist in this sublunary air, which, being massed together by the force of cold, are turned into "running stars" <note>Meteors.</note>.

<column-break/>

**[Marginal notes:]**
A brief dispute with David Fabricius. <note>David Fabricius (1564–1617) was a German theologian and astronomer who corresponded frequently with Kepler.</note>

<column-break/>

**[Main text:]**
Theologians especially embrace that former opinion; and my friend Fabricius argues according to its tenor. Being a theologian himself, he is more inclined toward "unaccountable" traditions <note>original: "ἀναιτιολογήτους" (anaitiologētous) — referring to beliefs held by tradition rather than physical cause-and-effect.</note>, which he demonstrates most strongly in matters of Astrology. From him—not being indignant, but rather rejoicing and applauding—

DE STELLA NOVA SERPENT. 101

...biting and provocatively challenging, and doing the same in return, just as I am often accustomed to disagree in frequent exchanges of letters; so he <note>David Fabricius (1564–1617), a German astronomer and theologian who was a long-time correspondent of Kepler.</note> will grant me pardon if I pursue this opinion of his, which has burst out from our private letters into the public, by refuting it in the same way. First, having begun with the theologians, he himself also argues that their reasoning regarding God's rest after the seventh day <note>A reference to the Sabbath in Genesis; some theologians argued that God ceased all "creation" then, meaning no new stars could be made after the first week of the world.</note> is weak; following thus far the thread of my letters. For thus I responded to him, when he proposed this theological objection from the opinion of others: that such fear seems unnecessary—the fear that we might cast God into some new labor, as it were, if He creates something new. But even if those bodies existed from the beginning: yet this much also must be admitted; that things which formerly lacked light are now illuminated. Whoever ascribes this action to God constitutes Him the creator of something new through it. For why? Did God not create the heaven and the earth in six days? What then did He do on the first day? "God said," says the divine Prophet <note>Moses, traditionally regarded as the author of Genesis.</note>, "Let there be light" <note>original: "Fiat lux"</note>: and with its institution God finished the first day. Therefore, to produce light is to create. Unless perhaps He did not create a new light within the star, but removed an external obstacle or screen? As if this were perhaps easier for God than creating something new; or as if this does not pertain to the act of creation? For what, again, did God create on the second day? What else, except that by making the firmament He separated the waters from the waters? Therefore, among the works of the second day were also these: to separate what was joined, and to remove obstacles. Then, moreover, God Himself promises to create "a new heaven and a new earth." All the Orthodox <note>Right-believing Christians holding to established creeds.</note> believe this, as it is recorded among the principal articles of the Christian religion. Add to this that those same theologians, who denied God the power of creating a new star in the heavens, still acknowledge His daily <term>activity</term> <note>original: "ἐνέργειαν" (energeian) — a Greek term for God's ongoing, active power or work in the world.</note> in creating new souls. I had raised this objection to those I was debating. But Fabricius, while raising the same point, weakens it: he leaves the dispute over souls in suspense, which was not necessary when dealing with a specific adversary. And thus far there is agreement between us on the matter itself. Immediately departing from here, he takes up the opinion he previously opposed to modify it: that these stars indeed shine newly, but their bodies are not new. First, he thinks it is an astronomical opinion that the bodies of these stars exist anew. I had denied in my letters that this is an astronomical dogma; for those who are first and foremost guided by light have nothing further to either establish or refute [concerning dark matter]. That this star was suddenly kindled, and from there gradually extinguished—astronomers confirm this using sight as their guide. But whether the bodies existed before the light was received: they leave that to be weighed by Fabricius the theologian and other natural philosophers. I, having long ago entered upon this physical inquiry from the starting point of astronomy...

<meta>Continuing from page 101, Kepler continues his debate with David Fabricius regarding the physical nature of the 1604 Supernova (the "New Star"). 102 JOHANNES KEPLER

investigation; I will examine the four reasons of Fabricius by which he wishes to prove that the body of the star has always existed. First, he says the Moon is often extinguished <note>original: "extingui"; referring to a lunar eclipse where the Moon loses its light but its physical body remains.</note> while its body remains: he believes the same is therefore credible for unusual stars. I deny to you, Fabricius, that a likelihood follows from this example; only the *possibility* of the thing follows, if conjectures were available from elsewhere that might persuade us of the matter itself. You do not, therefore, bring forward an argument, but rather you remove a doubt, should any arguments perhaps follow. Otherwise, it will not even be a doubt; for a doubt presses upon no one, unless he is wearing the shoe <note>A Latin idiom meaning a problem only bothers someone who is directly affected by it, or "the shoe only pinches the one wearing it."</note>.

The second argument he draws from the motion of **Comets**: in which he finds something of nobility, similar to the perennial **Planets**, such as **station**, **retrogression**, and the **speeding up or slowing down** <term>Station and Retrogression: The apparent slowing down and backward movement of a planet in the sky caused by the Earth's own movement in its orbit.</term> of motion. Therefore, he thinks that the bodies of Comets, because of this divinity, are perennial, and are not immediately annihilated when their brightness is extinguished. If Comets are such, he says, it is therefore easy to believe that new stars are such as well.

You strike us with shadows, O Fabricius, to force us frightened cattle into your opinion. For what has the motion of Comets to do with the immobility of this **New** fixed star? If you establish divinity from the motion of Comets, and from this, permanence: then by taking away motion from our star, you have also snatched away the divinity and, along with it, the perpetuity which you had wished to establish. Furthermore, those **retrogressions** you admire in the Planets, and those **stations**, and those inequalities of motion: these have hitherto tormented physicists quite rightly, as things familiar to our sublunary <term>Sublunary: The region below the Moon, traditionally thought in Aristotelian physics to be the realm of change and decay, unlike the eternal heavens.</term> trajectories, but little suited to those divine and perennial bodies of the Planets.

And so **Copernicus** was found, a new version of the very ancient **Aristarchus**, and also **Tycho Brahe**: who attempted to remove those turbulent things from the heavens; a task which succeeded most happily indeed. Therefore, by means of the Planets, we are free to thrust your Comets down below the Moon and subject them to change, using the same argument by which you strive to consecrate them to eternity—unless you also remove from them, with the same dexterity, those fantasies of stations and retrogradations, and find that the motion which remains for the Comets themselves, once stripped of this appearance, is entirely circular.

But I will free you from the labor. I have tried the matter myself as well, as you see me having professed in my **Optics** <note>Kepler’s *Astronomiae Pars Optica* (The Optical Part of Astronomy), published in 1604, where he discussed the physics of light and atmospheric refraction.</note>. Thus, when that motion which was attributed to Comets because of the annual orbit of Tycho and Copernicus was removed, what remained was not circular; but a motion that was for the most part entirely straight and very similar to things thrown. Except that the same can happen through several mediums: hence Brahe and **Maestlin** <note>Michael Maestlin (1550–1631), Kepler’s former professor and a significant astronomer who proved the comet of 1577 was above the Moon.</note> even devised circles for the Comet of the year 1577, though by a very different reasoning. And I, if I did not have other arguments by which I assign the heavens to Comets, would easily, through this argument of yours, place them under the Moon...

<summary>Kepler refutes David Fabricius’s claim that the New Star is eternal by critiquing Fabricius's analogies to the Moon and Comets. Kepler argues that the "divine" circular motions Fabricius sees in comets are actually optical illusions caused by the Earth's movement.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from page 102, Kepler continues to dismantle the arguments of David Fabricius. Fabricius argues that because the Moon and Comets have bodies that persist even when they are not shining, the "New Star" must also have an eternal, dark body. # ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 103

I would allow the Moon to be turned aside. Thus, this second likelihood of yours recoils against you.

Fabricius points out a third conjecture: that a gap <note>original: "hiatus"</note> appeared in the place of the **New Star** which had shone in the year 1572 and was extinguished in 1574, according to the testimony of **Tycho Brahe** on page 795 of his *Progymnasmata* <note>Tycho Brahe's "Introduction to the New Astronomy," his massive work detailing his observations of the 1572 supernova.</note>. Fabricius thinks this gap is the very body of the star, stripped of its light. If Fabricius believed this—that the gap is the body of the star—I confess he had a just cause for founding a new doctrine, though one that is inherently formless. For who would speak against their own eyes? But I hope to snatch that persuasion away from Fabricius, ingenious man that he is. 

First, he himself adds the size of the place, which he says is equal to a half-full Moon. But the star of that year, even with the full display of its rays (which usually seem to the senses to extend beyond the boundaries of their physical bodies), was smaller than **Venus**. I will say nothing of the fact that if the body of the star is seen, it must therefore be seen by means of light; thus the star would not yet be extinguished. Nor do I press the point too strongly that God seems not to have looked after his "treasure" very well—that treasure which he wished to hide until another occasion—since Fabricius and Brahe both still possess it, as if it were not extinguished. 

I bid you to consider carefully that every gap in the **Milky Way** appears as an opening <note>original: "hiatum"</note>, even if no star ever shone there. For physicists know this from Aristotle’s *Meteorology*: if a bright or white border surrounds a dark place lacking light on all sides, a pit <note>original: "foveam"</note> appears in the middle, which the Greeks call a **Chasma** <term>Chasma: A Greek term for a "yawning" or "gaping" hole; in historical astronomy, it referred to dark patches in the sky that looked like physical holes or abysses.</term>. Therefore, Brahe was received with great applause when he applied this gap to confirm his own opinion: that the star of that year was condensed from the matter of the Milky Way, as much as was dispersed through this gap; and once the matter of the star was consumed and, as it were, burnt up, the place was left empty of the "milk." This likelihood of Brahe’s opinion so competes with Fabricius’s that no other arguments are needed to tear his down.

Fabricius derives a fourth conjecture from the location: it stands among the **fixed stars**; therefore, just as the fixed stars are perennial, so too these stars should be seen as perennial. Why did he not also twist his argument toward the perennial nature of their light? Therefore, if the most characteristic quality of stars is to **Shine**; and if this happens to certain New ones—that they shine when they did not shine before—what is the wonder if some also begin to exist among the fixed stars which were not there before, and lose their very bodies once their period is finished, along with their light?

I think it is sufficiently clear that there is no suitable reason why anyone should think those new stars existed before they were seen, or that they are preserved as survivors for a new illumination after they have been extinguished.

# JOHANNES KEPLER

# Whether this star has been withdrawn by motion into the depths of the ether, and whether the Sphere of fixed stars extends into infinity?

## CHAPTER XXI.

**B**UT there is another sect of philosophers—those who (as **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher whose geocentric views Kepler and his contemporaries were in the process of dismantling.</note> unjustly pronounced regarding the opinion of the **Pythagoreans** <note>Ancient followers of Pythagoras who held that numbers were the essence of all things and suggested the Earth might move.</note>, which was later repeated by **Copernicus** <note>Nicolaus Copernicus, the astronomer who famously proposed the Sun-centered model of the universe.</note>) do not derive the starting point of their reasoning from the senses, nor do they adapt the causes of things to experiments. Instead, they abruptly, and as if by a certain *Enthusiasm* <note>In the 17th century, "enthusiasm" often implied a state of being "possessed" by a sudden, ungrounded idea or divine frenzy rather than using rational deduction.</note>, conceive and depict some opinion about the constitution of the World within the walls of their own brains. Once they have embraced this idea tenaciously <note>original: "mordicus," literally "with their teeth."</note>, they thereafter drag those things which fall under experience, and which daily occur and appear, by the hair <note>original: "crinibus pertracta." A Latin idiom meaning to force or distort evidence to fit a preconceived theory.</note> to accommodate them to their own axioms. It pleases these people to believe that this new star, and any others that have been of this kind, come from the innermost bosom of Nature

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>Continuing from Chapter XXI, Kepler is dissecting the arguments of those who believe the New Star of 1572 appeared to grow or shrink because it was moving physically closer to or further from the Earth. ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 105

In what way, then, could nature have provided it with this appearance of magnitude by its approaching? Add to this that it did not take on its increases gradually, as it was seen to decrease gradually, but rather it suddenly shone forth at its greatest.

Thus, there were those who, thirty years ago, explained these things more soberly: that the star of the year 1572 completed its motion along a straight line, extending from the center upward into the ether. But let those people also explain why, having been sent down through that immense space so suddenly (because it suddenly shone forth), it did not fly back suddenly, but gradually, and what the cause of this reversed course was. For this is not a property of natural things. If, however, they say the cause was supernatural, can I not just as easily assert that it was created from nothing by God, as they say it was sent down from on high by Him? Since both are works of the same power. But as I have often done elsewhere, I now break off the argument once again whenever they appeal to the absolute power of God. For it is certain that we can say nothing relevant, nor anything that carries weight in any direction, if we exceed the boundaries of nature. Let us rather say to them that they clothe themselves in that infinity of the fixed stars as if in inexplicable labyrinths.

And so let us snatch away this immensity from them, if it can be done: for then that ascent will fall by itself <margin>of the Fixed Stars</margin>. This idea is brought back from the ancient schools of pagan <margin>Sphere</margin> philosophers, chiefly by this argument: that Aristotle demonstrated that the world is not finite based on motion; but Copernicus, having removed the motion of the sphere of the fixed stars, allows it to be infinite <margin>in height or thickness.</margin>

Thus did that unfortunate Giordano Bruno <note>Giordano Bruno (1548–1600) was an Italian philosopher who was burned at the stake for heresy; he famously proposed that the universe is infinite and contains many worlds.</note> defend it. And William Gilbert <note>An English physician and physicist, famous for his work *De Magnete* (1600), which argued that the Earth is a giant magnet.</note>, in his book *On the Magnet*—otherwise a most excellent work—asserted it not obscurely (though appearing to doubt), yet he demonstrated a religious sentiment in this: he thought the infinite power of God could be understood by nothing more rightly than if He established a world infinite in mass. But Bruno makes the world so infinite that he makes as many worlds as there are fixed stars, and makes this region of ours—the moving planets—one out of innumerable worlds, distinguished by almost no mark from the others surrounding it. So much so that if someone were on the Dog Star <note>original: "stella Canis," referring to Sirius.</note> (I suppose one of Lucian's Dog-Heads <note>Lucian of Samosata was a 2nd-century satirist who wrote a "True History" involving a voyage to the moon and stars populated by strange creatures like "Cynocephali" or Dog-Headed people.</note>), the appearance of the world from there would seem the same to him as it appears to us from here, looking up at the fixed stars from our world. Thus, according to them, this new star would have been some new world. This thought alone carries with it some hidden horror; for one finds oneself wandering in this immensity, in which limits, a center, and therefore certain places, are denied. And it would perhaps be better to put forward the authority of Moses, who shows us finite waters, with the Spirit of the Lord carried above them; and then, on the second day, the waters separated from the waters.

Oh, But

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Kepler is transitioning from a critique of the "star-descent" theory to a direct challenge against the "infinite universe" theory. 106 ->OF JOHANNES KEPLER<-

But because this sect <note>original: "secta"; Kepler refers to thinkers like Giordano Bruno who proposed an infinite universe populated by infinite suns.</note> abuses the authority of the Copernican system, and thus of all Astronomy—claiming that because Copernicus made the fixed stars immobile, and all Astronomy (especially the Copernican) presents them as being at an incredible height—come, let us seek a remedy from Astronomy itself. Just as this insanity of philosophizing was provoked by Astronomy’s indulgence—breaking through the boundaries and barriers to thrust itself out into this immensity—let it be brought back within the limits of the World and its own enclosures by those same arts and by "soothing clucking" <note>original: "blandientibus poppyismis"; a term for the clicking or soothing sounds used to calm a horse. Kepler uses this metaphor to suggest gently leading philosophers back to reason.</note>. Truly, it is not well for one to wander through that infinite void.

I say first, that it can be taught most certainly from Astronomy that the region of the fixed stars is bounded below by an evident limit. It is not true, as they posit, that this lower world—with our Sun—is no different in appearance from any of the fixed stars; that is, that one region does not differ from another region or place.

For, let it be granted among their principles that the fixed stars are extended infinitely outwards; nevertheless, it is true that in this innermost bosom <note>Kepler uses "sinus" (bosom/gulf) to describe the interior space of the universe where the solar system resides.</note>, there is a remarkable cavity. This cavity is distinct from the intervals that exist between the fixed stars by a massive difference in proportion. So much so, that if someone happened to look into this cavity alone—even if they were ignorant of the eight small bodies <note>The Sun, the Moon, and the six known planets: Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> flying around the center of this space in a very narrow interval, or what they were, or how many—he would still conclude from this vacancy alone, compared with the surrounding spherical region packed with stars, that there is some peculiar place here; and thus the principal "heart" of the world.

For let us take, for example, the three stars of the second magnitude in Orion’s Belt, of which any one is distant from its neighbor by 81 minutes <note>81 arc-minutes, or about 1.35 degrees.</note>, while each has a diameter of at least two minutes. Therefore, if they were placed on the same spherical surface of which we are the center, an eye placed on one of those stars would see the neighbor under an angle of almost 2 3/4 degrees. This is a larger area than five of our Suns, placed in a row and touching each other, would occupy for us on Earth. And yet, these are not even the closest fixed stars to each other; for innumerable smaller ones are interspersed between them.

Therefore, if someone were stationed in that Belt of Orion, having our Sun and the center of the world directly over his head, he would see first a certain perpetual sea, as it were, of huge stars, appearing to touch one another. Then, the more he raised his eyes, the smaller he would see the stars, and the less they would touch; they would gradually appear more and more scattered. But around his own zenith <note>The point directly above the observer.</note>, he would see the same stars that we do, but twice as small, and just as much closer to each other as we see them. That is not the face of the sky we see; we who perceive stars everywhere, indeed of various magnitudes; but and

And this distribution is mostly equal in all directions: indeed, around Orion and Gemini, we see many large and crowded stars: the Eye of the Bull <note>Aldebaran</note>, Capella, the heads of the Twins <note>Castor and Pollux</note>, the Dogs <note>Sirius and Procyon</note>, and the shoulders, belt, and foot of Orion. But in the opposite part of the sky there are equally large stars: Lyra <note>Vega</note>, Aquila <note>Altair</note>, the Heart <note>Antares</note> and the brow of the Scorpion, Ophiuchus <note>The Serpent-Bearer</note>, the scales of Libra; and further forward Arcturus and the Ear of Corn of the Virgin <note>Spica</note>; likewise further back, the Last of Aquarius <note>likely Fomalhaut</note> and similar stars.

When I once proposed this theme to certain people—who, to test me, sharply defended the cause of infinity (taken from the aforementioned authors) against me—they thought it would be easy for them, if infinity were granted, to separate any two fixed stars <note>In Kepler's time, "fixed stars" referred to all stars beyond the planets, which appeared not to move relative to one another.</note> from each other by a distance as great as the distance we know ourselves to be from the stars, even though we on Earth judge those two stars to be very close. But this is impossible. 

For let it be granted that you can raise up two fixed stars, equally distant from the center of the moving world <note>The solar system</note>, as far as you please: it would indeed happen, as they say, that they would move as far apart from each other as the center of the moving parts is now distant from both. But let them remember: if they raise the fixed stars further out, this void in the middle and within the circular embrace of the fixed stars is increased at the same time. But they thoughtlessly assumed that this void would remain the same size for us while the fixed stars ascended. 

"What if," they say, "taking two stars of Orion's Belt, one remains in that sphere below which the doctrine of parallaxes <note><term>Parallax</term>: The apparent shift in position of an object when viewed from different locations. Kepler uses it to show that stars must be at a minimum distance because they show no such shift.</note> does not allow a star to be, and the other is said to be higher by infinite intervals? Would we not in this way make it so that they appear as small when seen from one another as both now appear to us? And that the intervals between them, empty of stars, be found equal to the intervals between us and both of them?"

Perhaps, I say, the matter could be helped in this way if there were only two or a few stars, and if they were not scattered and thrown around in a circle. Truly, you will either raise and leave stars in this way alternately, or you will do it for all of them. If you do it alternately, the question is not removed, even if slightly diminished. For regarding those stars that remained close to us, the same assertion still holds: that the two are closer to each other than to the Sun, based on a comparison of the distances between them to their diameters. 

<margin>Whether all fixed stars are of equal body, and at unequal intervals.</margin>
But those stars that are raised higher will indeed be further apart; but they will nonetheless remain in a great proportion <note>Kepler is arguing that even if you move stars further away, the ratio of their size to the distance between them remains problematic for the "infinite" theory.</note>. And this I might easily grant without detriment to my case: that all the fixed stars are of the same actual size; and of these, those which appear large to us are near us, and those which appear small are that much more remote. "Not that they are less bright, but because they recede into greater heights," <note>original: "Non quod clara minus, sed quod magis alta recedant"; Kepler is quoting the Roman poet Manilius's *Astronomica*.</note> as Manilius sings.

And perhaps both are true, following the example of the Planets, some of which are truly larger than others, while others appear larger even though they are smaller in themselves, simply because they are closer to us.

But let us proceed to the other part of the argument, considering what would be achieved if all the stars, every single one of them, were separated from each other by their own distances: in such a way that the star closest to us retains that proximity which Astronomy now sets as the boundary for all stars <note>In Kepler’s era, astronomers attempted to calculate a minimum distance to the "sphere" of the fixed stars; he argues here that there is a clear inner limit to where the stars begin.</note>, admitting none to be closer; and then, from that first star, all the rest are moved further out, each neighbor being placed as much further away as the distance of the lowest star is from us.

<meta>Kepler continues his argument from the previous page, where he began to explain why the Solar System appears to sit in a unique, empty "hollow" within the distribution of stars. **ON THE NEW STAR IN SERPENTARIUS. 109**

<margin>The world has a certain definite center.</margin> ...this provides the reasoning for the arrangement of the fixed stars, with our own sight as the guide: so that this location of ours among those fixed stars possesses something unique, and is distinguished by an evident mark—which is the vast emptiness between us and the fixed stars—from the region through which those stars are scattered.

It is therefore certain that, looking inward toward the Sun and the Planets, the world is finite and, in a sense, hollowed out. Let Metaphysics build upon the rest. For if such a place as ours exists within some infinite body—as some claim—it would necessarily be the center of that entire body; and the surrounding fixed stars would not maintain the same proportions; it would follow that such worlds as ours would be found everywhere. Instead, those stars surround this place in a spherical shape, coming together toward one another. Most especially and most visibly, the Milky Way is sown among them, <margin>The Milky Way is an indicator of the finite sphere of the fixed stars.</margin> which, as an uninterrupted circle, passes through the middle of the orb of fixed stars, holding us in its central embrace. Thus, both the Milky Way and the fixed stars possess the character of an outer limit. For they bound this space of ours, and at their beginning, they are in turn bounded by it. Is it then credible that these stars, being bounded on this side, should extend to infinity on the other? Furthermore, how can a center be found in the infinite, which is "everywhere" in the infinite? For whatever point of the infinite is chosen, it is equally—that is, infinitely—distant from the outer edges, which are themselves infinitely far away. From this it follows that the same point would both be a center and not be a center, along with many other contradictions. A person might most correctly avoid these if, just as they find the heaven of fixed stars to be bounded on the inside, they also bound it on the outside.

Take also this argument drawn from appearances. If the depth of the sphere of fixed stars is infinite—that is, if some fixed stars are infinitely deep in space—they will themselves consist of an infinite mass of bodies. For imagine some star that is seen at a certain angle, say four minutes of a degree; the size of such a body is always one-thousandth of its distance, which is a most certain fact from Geometry <note>original: "ex Geometria". Kepler refers to the small-angle approximation: if an object occupies a specific amount of your field of vision, its physical size must grow larger the further away it is placed.</note>. Therefore, if the distance is infinite, then the diameter of the star is one-thousandth part of infinity. But all proportional parts of infinity are necessarily infinite themselves. Such a star would therefore be infinite in size. Yet at the same time it would be finite, because it has a shape: and every shape is circumscribed by certain boundaries—that is, it is finite or defining. Moreover, we have assigned it a shape by assuming it is visible at a specific angle. But what if, you say, it is too small to be seen? I say, the matter comes to the same result. For the star must occupy some proportional part of the circumference drawn through its own position. But a circumference whose diameter is infinite is itself also infinite. Thus it follows that no star—whether it is visible to us, or whether it vanishes from sight due to its smallness—is at an infinite distance from us.

O 3 Let it be...

<summary>Kepler argues that the universe must be finite, using the Milky Way as evidence of a boundary and showing that if stars were at an infinite distance, their physical size would logically have to be infinite, which is a contradiction.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, cosmology, infinity, geometry, Milky Way, fixed stars, Serpentarius, Ophiuchus, center of the universe</keywords>

<meta>Kepler concludes his geometric argument against an infinite universe, asserting that the very act of placing a star within a space defines a finite distance. He then transitions to Chapter 22 to discuss the physical composition of the "New Star" (the supernova of 1604), arguing it is made of celestial material rather than being a divine miracle or an earthly vapor.</meta>

110 JOHANNES KEPLER

Suppose you extend a starless space to infinity; surely, wherever you place a star within it, the distance to that point is finite, and because of that star, the circumference is also finite. Thus, anyone who speaks of an "infinite sphere" of fixed stars is expressing a contradiction in terms <note>original: "oppositum in adjecto," a logic term for a statement where the adjective contradicts the noun.</note>. Indeed, an infinite body cannot even be grasped by thought. For our mental concepts of the infinite are either about the meaning of the word "Infinite" itself, or about something that exceeds every conceivable measure of number, touch, or sight—which is not immediately and actually infinite, because an infinite measure can never be fully conceived.

***

->### That the Matter of the New Star Was Celestial, and Concerning Its Origin.<-
->### CHAPTER XXII.<-

Therefore, having rejected the opinion of those who believed the matter and body of the star were eternal <note>Kepler is refuting the Aristotelian idea that the heavens are unchanging and that stars cannot be "new" but must have always existed.</note>, it follows that I must weigh the contrary view. If, then, either the matter of this flame or the body of this star began to exist anew, it must have been either created by divine power or born from some force of nature. Before we arrive at "creation"—which is the end of all scientific dispute—I believe every other possibility should be tested. I do this all the more willingly because I see that within the last thirty years, this is now the fourth, or certainly the third, "new star" to appear. Furthermore, Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author who recorded ancient celestial observations.</note> also categorized one type of "immobile" stars among the new stars (which are mostly <term>comata</term> <note>From the Latin for "long-haired," the root of the word "comet."</note> or cometary); from this, you may guess that in ancient times the region of the fixed stars also produced such sights. Nor is there a lack of suspicion regarding this from the histories of earlier ages (see Tycho Brahe's *Progymnasmata*).

But if it was generated by a force of nature, we must ask about both the specific "parent" or agent that caused it, and the matter from which it was made. To deal with the matter first and conclude the discussion I have handled so far: I have already stated that the matter did not come from the Earth. Therefore, the matter was taken from the heavens—provided that the generation of a star can be attributed to the forces of nature in some way (even if it is a "monstrous" birth, like the accidental generation of eels in a human womb <note>A reference to early modern "spontaneous generation" theories, where organisms were thought to grow in unexpected places.</note>). For nature produces nothing without matter. Indeed, if Comets are in the heavens; if, as can be shown by examples, they are carried from the deepest ether toward the Earth and sometimes pass very close to us in a straight line; and if new stars arise among the fixed stars themselves—what prevents us from affirming that they are composed of ethereal essence? 

At this point, mention has already been made of that most beautiful opinion stated by Tycho Brahe: that stars of this kind are procreated from the Milky Way. And he seemed to confirm it.

<summary>Kepler argues that an infinite universe is a logical impossibility and begins investigating whether the New Star of 1604 was formed naturally from celestial matter, specifically referencing Tycho Brahe's theory about the Milky Way.</summary>

And having described the whole circuit of the Zone, he adds: But the Zone, which I spoke of before, with the interruption made (as I said) at the star contained in the Altar, and taking its beginning from there, intercepts three nodes from the body of the Scorpion: but of the three in the body of the Scorpion, it excludes the one that follows, being distant from it by one degree on its western margin. But that which is in the fourth node of the Scorpion occupies the pure air midway between both zones, being distant by almost equal spaces from each, say, a little more than one degree. After these things, however, this preceding zone runs out with a certain bend into the East, in the form of a circular segment, and the preceding side of the "milk" is terminated by the star which is in the right knee of Ophiuchus* <note>the Serpent-Bearer</note>: *the following side indeed by that which is in the same ham* <note>the popliteal or back of the knee</note>. *But the preceding star from the...*

...a portion of the "milk"; in such a way that it is enclosed within that narrow boundary that distinguishes between the two arcs of the Milky Way’s border at that location, like an island between two riverbeds. Therefore, it is consistent to believe that the matter of the new star originated from the Milky Way. Natural contemplation also confirms this. For if the essence of the Milky Way is a denser part of the ether <note>In ancient and Renaissance science, "ether" was the weightless, crystalline substance that filled the heavens, thought to be unchanging.</note> (for some matter must necessarily underlie such brilliant flashes), it will seem probable to everyone that this matter is most suitable for producing new phenomena.

<margin>Whether the matter of the new star comes from the Milky Way.</margin>
However probable these things may be, they are nonetheless undermined by two objections. First: David Fabricius <note>A German astronomer and theologian who was a frequent correspondent of Kepler’s.</note>—whom I have previously praised as trustworthy in his observations—noticed on August 13/23, 1596, a new star. This was around the day the Comet of that year was last seen by Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin, Kepler’s mathematics professor and mentor.</note> (it had by then become nearly stationary in the 4th degree of Virgo). In the morning, Fabricius found a new star of the third magnitude at 25° 45' of Aries, with a southern latitude of 15° 54', which disappeared after October of that same year. That location, however, was very far indeed from the Milky Way. The second objection is this: if the birth of new stars is a natural occurrence (as one suggests who seeks its matter from the Milky Way), then it is believable, as was said above, that they are not so infrequent. Therefore, in the 1400 years that have passed since the time Ptolemy described the Milky Way, it seems that some notable portion of it ought to have vanished, with so many stars having been ignited and fashioned out of it. Or, if such a great accumulation of them has not yet occurred, it seems that eventually the heaven would lose all of this "milk" it possesses—a conclusion not accepted by sound judgment in Philosophy.

<margin>The new star of the year 1595.</margin> <note>Kepler’s marginal note says 1595, though the text identifies the observation as 1596; this refers to the star Mira Ceti, the first discovered periodic variable star.</note>
Thus, I am more inclined to believe that the heaven is everywhere capable of providing the matter for these stars.

***

### That the matter of the heaven is alterable.
## CHAPTER XXIII.

I am not unaware of how hostile this opinion is to Aristotelian Philosophy <note>Aristotle argued that the "Celestial Sphere" was perfect and immutable, meaning nothing new could ever be created or destroyed in the heavens.</note>. But to say it as it is: this view is more opposed to the *sect* than to the leader himself. Give me a revived Aristotle, and—so may my astronomical labor succeed—I would hope to persuade him! It is usually the case that while plaster <note>original: *gypso*</note> is recently poured, you may impress anything upon it; but once that same plaster has hardened, it rejects every mark. Similarly, opinions can be very easily corrected while they flow from the mouths of the Philosophers; but once they have been received by disciples, they harden more than any stone, and they cannot easily be torn away by any amount of reasoning.

<summary>Kepler debates whether new stars are formed from the material of the Milky Way, ultimately concluding that the entire heavens are capable of change. He famously argues that Aristotle himself would have updated his theories in the face of new evidence, unlike his rigid followers.</summary>
<keywords>Milky Way, ether, David Fabricius, Aristotle, Mira Ceti, stellar formation, immutability of the heavens, Michael Maestlin</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>Kepler is transitioning from a discussion of the Milky Way as a possible source of the New Star’s matter to a broader philosophical and physical attack on the traditional Aristotelian view of the heavens as unchanging.</meta>

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT [OPHIUCHUS]. 113<-

...are refuted. Why did Aristotle feel this way, that the heavens are unchangeable? Because (he says) for so many centuries nothing new has been observed. Therefore, if someone were to teach Aristotle that in the succeeding centuries quite a few new things have been observed in the sky, he would most willingly depart from his opinion. But today, the disciples of Aristotle—looking not to reason, but to his bare opinion—dare to speak against experience based on a dogma of the philosopher (which he himself sought from experience), and they object to it by seeking out various diversions. But when the matter is weighed more carefully, <margin>Aristotle’s argumentation.</margin> Aristotle seems to have used induction <note>Induction is a method of reasoning where a general rule is drawn from specific observations; Kepler is arguing Aristotle's data set was simply too small.</note>. It is established from the motions of the stars that the laws have remained the same since the furthest memory of man: and the same planets circle today that existed of old, and the same constellations of fixed stars. Nor can anything different be demonstrated concerning the intermediate space itself (which is not obvious to the eyes, because it is transparent). Therefore, all things in heaven are perpetual, including the very matter of the spheres. This induction is fallacious. <margin>An objection.</margin> We detect the same laws of motion today, which is the one and only "tenth-rate" <note>Kepler uses "decumana," literally "of the tenth wave," a Latin idiom for something massive or primary, here referring to the most fundamental assumption of astronomers.</note> hypothesis of astronomers. But regarding the medium—or the "Raquia" of Moses <note>The Hebrew word *raqia* (often translated as "firmament" or "expanse") appears in Genesis 1:6 to describe the heavens.</note>, the "Tents of Heaven"—our age teaches us something quite different, and not obscurely. And the prior age would have taught the same, if it had occurred to our ancestors to pay attention to these things. Indeed, I will propose here several proofs from which that which I contend can be perceived in some way: so that the new fixed stars, which I claim are born from alterable celestial matter, may be confirmed by other examples of altered celestial matter.

First, I offer to you from my *Optics* <note>Kepler refers to his 1604 book *Ad Vitellionem paralipomena, quibus astronomiae pars optica traditur*, a foundational text in the science of light.</note> folio 259, that example of the <margin>1. The cause of the prodigious fog in the year 1547 was celestial.</margin> prodigious fog of the year 1547: regarding which the Gemmas, father and son <note>Cornelius Gemma and his father Reiner Gemma Frisius, notable Flemish scientists.</margin>, report that the Sun appeared for three days as if drenched in blood, so that even many stars were visible at midday. This happened not only in Belgium, but widely through all of France, Germany, and Britain; as reported by Scaliger <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger, a leading scholar of the time, in his work *On the Emendation of Time*.</note> in Book 5 of *On the Emendation of Time*, and before him by Bünting. History indeed exhibits many examples; among others, that most celebrated one in the year of Caesar’s assassination, which lasted nearly the whole year; and likewise in the year Augustus died. But let us build a demonstration upon our own example. If the cause of the obscured Sun was in the air: then since the Sun retained this pallor from sunrise to sunset—for a full three days, or as Scaliger wishes, four days—it must therefore be that the entire air from east to west was dark. If this were so, how then were the stars seen through the air, through which the Sun could scarcely be seen? For you cannot say that the light of the Sun was diluted by the air in such a way that, with the glare removed, the lights of the stars could reach the eyes. For when our air becomes so thick that the light of the Sun passes through it dull and pale, then indeed by that same...
