usually shines immensely by its action, when the Sun's rays are received into it, like clouds, and reflected in every direction. Thus the air, forcing its clarity into our eyes from all sides and occupying them entirely, leaves no room for the far weaker lights of the stars. Therefore, it is most certain that in the part where the lights of the stars are seen, the air of that place is less illuminated by the Sun, and is therefore thin, and not fit for weakening the light of the Sun as it is brought to that place by its daily motion. Thus, there must be some other cause, besides the air, which weakens the Sun's light so much before it reaches our air, and which accompanies the Sun in its daily revolution.

But they will perhaps say that there was some cause, sublime indeed, but located just below the Moon: and that nothing follows from this regarding the matter of the heavens?

First, it has been demonstrated in Optics that our air is very low <note>Kepler refers to the height of the atmosphere; earlier astronomers often underestimated how thin the atmosphere becomes at high altitudes.</note> (which Aristotle also partly admits); that which follows it is already the ether. This will be even less rejected by anyone who keeps in mind that it was demonstrated by Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), whose precise observations proved that comets and "new stars" were above the moon, shattering the idea of solid celestial spheres.</note> that the heaven consists of no solid spheres. Therefore, the ether is also found immediately below the Moon. If the matter of the darkness <note>original: "materia caliginis," referring to the substance causing the solar dimming.</note> stuck in that place, it therefore stuck in the ether. Next, in what place do you think that matter was, which kept the Sun in paleness for four whole days, yet did not hide the stars at all? For if it were just below the Moon, it would follow that it ought to have also gone around with the sphere of the Moon, carried away by the swiftness of its motion—just as Aristotle wants the element of fire to be carried away. But because the Sun remained hidden for four whole days, during which time the Moon traveled fifty degrees from the Sun, this matter was therefore not near the Moon, but stood entirely above it, in the very heart of the ether accepted by all. Unless, perhaps, one likes to say that a matter fifty degrees long first covered the Sun with its right extremity and last with its left; in which case it also ought to have covered the stars, whichever ones stood around the Sun within a proximity of 50 degrees. Yet we read that the stars were seen at noon: therefore, the stars near the Sun were not covered. Thus, the matter which veiled the Sun was not so wide; and since it stayed in the Sun's place for four days—from which the Moon’s sphere meanwhile departed by 50 degrees—this matter could not have stood near the Moon. It must therefore be sought in the deep ether, very near the Sun, between it and the Earth; 

<margin>II. The celestial matter of Comets.</margin>

indeed, it is to be embraced by us as offered for an argument of the changeable celestial matter which is poured, liquid, around the stars.

The second argument for the generation of ether <note>By "generation," Kepler means the process of things being created or coming into existence in the heavens.</note> shall be the Comets, which it is established by the most certain doctrine of parallaxes <note>Parallax is the measurement of an object's position from two different viewpoints to determine its distance; if an object has no parallax compared to the moon, it must be further away.</note> that they travel through the very field of the ether; and suddenly becoming huge, they gradually weaken, until finally dissipated...

<margin>That matter which sustained the lighting of the Moon’s body was celestial.</margin> But the account from Naples regarding last year <note>referring to the solar eclipse of October 12, 1605</note> says this: The Sun was completely covered, which indeed did not last long; in the middle, where the Moon was, there was a shape like a black cloud; all around was a reddish and flaming splendor, of equal width on all sides, which occupied a good part of the sky. Opposite the Sun, toward the North, the sky was plainly dark, as when it is deep night; yet the stars were not seen. To ensure you have no doubt about the reliability of this history, here is another from Flanders; there, the Sun was not entirely covered; for the uppermost part of the solar circle stood out brightly, the width of one digit <note>A "digit" is an ancient astronomical unit representing one-twelfth of the diameter of the Sun or Moon’s disk.</note> or half a digit (certainly because in Antwerp, a location further toward us, one digit was visible). Yet the globe of the Moon was seen, leaning toward blackness, dark, or as if covered in soot; while the upper circumference of the Moon was entirely white and as if fiery. And so it would be clear that the area of the Moon's disk was seen fully outlined, the account adds that the whole area, in which the view was directed away from the Sun, appeared sooty with a fiery circumference. Therefore, the phenomenon itself could not have been otherwise, since its appearance in the eye was such. Something similar was seen by Jessenius <note>Johannes Jessenius (1566–1621), a physician and astronomer who was a close associate of Kepler and Tycho Brahe.</note> at Torgau in the eclipse of the year 1598. For he saw the Moon plainly surrounded by a glow. See page 299 of my *Optics* <note>original: "Opticorum"; Kepler refers to his 1604 work *Astronomiae Pars Optica*.</note>: where you should take the final word "air" broadly to also mean ethereal substance.

Here I ask: what was that fiery glow surrounding the Moon, which appeared larger than the Sun to the eye because it had hidden the entire Sun? Indeed, what was 그 glow which nevertheless embraced the Moon even at the lower edge, where the Moon exceeded the Sun's width by one digit? "It was the glow of the Sun," you say. True; but that is not what is being asked. Rather, what was the **matter**, what was the **subject** <note>In the physics of this period, a "subject" is the physical material or medium in which a property (like light) exists or is made visible.</note>, in which that glow of the Sun resided? For light itself, having departed from its source, cannot be seen except in a subject; because it stops nowhere and strikes nothing unless it be in some opaque subject. If you say that our air was the subject for this glow, you will be refuted in various ways by the experiences of different locations.

<margin>That glow was not of our air, nor was it [only] at Naples.</margin> In the present diagram <note>Kepler is describing a geometric figure that would have appeared in the printed text.</note>, let **alpha-beta** be the Sun, **delta-zeta** the Moon, and **delta-eta-zeta** the cone of the shadow; let the globe of the Earth be **epsilon-iota**. <note>An astronomical diagram illustrates a solar eclipse. It shows the Sun (top circle, labeled $\alpha, \beta, \gamma$), the Moon (middle circle, labeled $\delta, \zeta, \eta$), and the Earth (bottom circle, labeled $\varepsilon, \iota$). Lines representing light rays (labeled $\sigma, \tau, \rho, \mu, \nu, \lambda$) converge from the Sun past the Moon to form a dark conical shadow (the umbra) whose tip or vertex is labeled $\theta, \varkappa$. ...something remained all around. One might argue that the cone of the Lunar shadow ended exactly at the eye of the observer, or even slightly before it—as if the observer's eye were positioned at point $\varkappa$. Thus, as soon as someone moved a step to the sides of the line $\theta \varkappa$, they would pass out of the Moon's shadow into the Sun's light, and from this, that surrounding splendor would have appeared. Such a thing could be said; but it is argued foolishly, if you weigh the facts well. 

For the Sun was seen totally covered, not by just one person, but by all of Naples and, without a doubt, a wide surrounding area. Indeed, the Moon was several minutes <note>original: "scrupulis"; Kepler uses this term for minutes of arc, units of angular measurement.</note> larger than the Sun, as testified by the English observations and partly by my own, which agree with the problems discussed in my *Optics* <note>Kepler refers to his 1604 work *Ad Vitellionem paralipomena*, where he established the foundations of modern optics.</note>. 

Therefore, imagine the luminaries [the Sun and Moon] were directly at the zenith <note>original: "vertice"; the point in the sky directly above the observer.</note>. At that position, for those traveling one degree away from their original location, the Moon changes its position relative to the stars by one minute, as shown by my table of parallax <note>Parallax is the apparent shift of an object's position when viewed from different locations. Kepler is calculating how the Moon's position "shifts" for observers at different spots on Earth.</note>. Consequently, if the Moon’s diameter had exceeded the Sun’s by two minutes, the shadow would have been two degrees wide—which is 120 Italian miles. 

However, at that time, the Sun was more than 50 degrees away from the zenith. At that location, according to the same parallax index, a journey of three degrees is required for the Moon to shift its position by two minutes. If the Moon exceeded the Sun by three minutes, the shadow $\iota \varepsilon$ would have been more than four degrees wide: specifically 60 German miles, or 240 Italian miles. Therefore, if the shadow began at Naples at point $\varepsilon$, the other end of the shadow would have reached into the middle of Sicily, which we mark here at point $\iota$. Therefore, that place, that matter, in...

<summary>Kepler uses geometry and observations of the 1605 solar eclipse to calculate the physical width of the Moon's shadow on Earth. He argues that the "splendor" (corona) seen around the Moon was visible across a vast area (from Naples to Sicily), proving it was a physical phenomenon in the heavens rather than a local optical illusion.</summary>

<note>An astronomical diagram illustrates a solar eclipse. It shows the Sun at the top (with rays labeled S and π), the Moon in the middle (labeled α, β, γ, δ), and the Earth at the bottom. A dark, conical shadow (the umbra) extends from the Moon toward the Earth. The Earth is marked with various points, including ε (representing Naples) and ι. Lines representing light rays and lines of sight are marked with Greek letters ζ, ο, ν, λ, μ, ρ, and σ. The tip or vertex of the shadow cone is marked with the letters θ and κ.</note>

...something remained all around. For you might say that the cone of the Lunar shadow ended right at the eye of the observer, or even a little before it—as if the eye were positioned at point κ <note>The Greek letter kappa, marking the vertex of the shadow cone in the diagram.</note>. Thus, as soon as anyone moved just a little to the side of the line θκ, they would have emerged from the Moon's shadow into the Sun's light, and from this, that surrounding splendor would have appeared. Such a thing could be said; but it is argued foolishly, if you weigh the facts well.

For the Sun was seen to be totally covered, not just by one person in Naples, but by the entire city and, without a doubt, across a wide surrounding area. Indeed, the Moon was several minutes <note>original: "scrupulis"; Kepler uses this term for minutes of arc, units of angular measurement.</note> larger than the Sun, as the English observations testify, and in part my own, which agree with the optical problems discussed in my *Optics* <note>Referring to Kepler’s 1604 work, *Astronomiae Pars Optica* (The Optical Part of Astronomy), where he laid the foundations for modern optics.</note>...

He is demonstrating that the "splendor" (the corona) observed during the solar eclipse cannot be a local atmospheric effect (refraction or reflection in the Earth's air), because it appeared consistently across vastly different geographic locations and atmospheric conditions.</meta>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER, 119**

<margin>Nor in Flanders</margin>

The same thing will be proven even more clearly from the report from Flanders: that it was not the air near us which placed itself within the Sun's brilliance in a circular shape and surrounded the appearance of the Moon. For in that location, the Sun was not entirely hidden. In the diagram, let υ <note>The Greek letter upsilon, representing an observer in Flanders.</note> be Flanders; when points υ and ζ are connected, the extended line falls on γ. Therefore, at point υ, whatever of the Sun was between γ and β was still shining. Let points υ and δ also be connected, and from the lines υζ and υδ, let the companion lines υοσ and υξρ be drawn; and let δ υ ζ be the appearance of the dark disk. Indeed, let the flaming circle surrounding the dark disk be understood through the lines ζ υ ο and ξ υ δ. Therefore, from this portion of the Sun γ β which remained visible, the entire air—not only that which is around the Moon at ζο, but also the air between the Moon and the observer’s sight in the space ζου—was illuminated equally. By no means, therefore, could a deficient round appearance, with brilliance poured around it, be represented in that portion of air υ λ.

Add to this that the Moon's shadow ιε, which did not encompass Flanders at υ (since the part of the Sun γ β remained visible there), had extended much further on the southern side η, toward the Mediterranean coast of Spain, which should now be understood as being under point ι. Thus, a dark circle of this kind, surrounded by brilliance, clearly would not have encompassed the Sun and Moon; instead, it would have stood entirely to the south. The line of sight, indeed, would have departed from view by a small part of itself, mostly behind the mountains. For the circle ντ, which is encompassed by the shadow and therefore appears dark, is clothed in the brilliance of the surrounding air λ μ that exists outside the shadow. This circle, I say—as you can see—falls entirely below the line υδ, in which the lowest edge of the Moon is seen from υ.

Therefore, there must have been some other matter, much more sublime <note>In this context, "sublime" means "lofty" or "higher up in the heavens," rather than just beautiful.</note> than the Flemish air, in which the appearance of the flame surrounding the dark disk of the Moon, impressed by the Sun, was represented. Indeed, its illumination was so strong that even though this local air υλ was also entirely illuminated by the visible sliver of the Sun γ β in Flanders, that celestial brilliance from ξ δ or ρ π would still overcome the light from the Sun at γ β. Neither confused nor overwhelmed by the illumination of our own air υλ, that light would descend distinctly into the eyes of the observers at υ.

Again, the same thing will be proven if both locations, Naples and Liège <note>original: "Leodium"</note>, are considered together. It is not likely that the thickness of our air is the same in all places. Aristotle attributed a thick and whitish air to the region of the Pontus <note>The Black Sea region; Aristotle often used it as an example of different atmospheric conditions.</note>, the likes of which he did not see in Athens. And what history does not emphasize such differences? But this phenomenon, which one might wish to attribute to the thickness of the air, was the same in the most diverse places. For who would compare the Celtic <note>Referring to the skies of Northern Europe, including Flanders and Germany.</note> sky with the Italian? Therefore, it is not the air poured over us, but some more sublime and clearly celestial matter that cast this brilliance, received from the Sun, around the Moon.

He concludes that a "sublime" or higher celestial matter must be responsible for reflecting the Sun's light.</summary>

<meta>Kepler is continuing his argument from the previous page, using observations of solar eclipses to prove that the "ether" or matter of the heavens is not a fixed, immutable substance (as Aristotle claimed), but is instead a changeable medium that can vary in density and clarity.</meta>

120 JOHANNES KEPLER

To what end, you ask, is all this, or what does it have to do with the subject—this point by which you contend that the matter of heaven is changeable? For what if this splendor of the sky around the Sun or the Moon is constant whenever there is a solar eclipse? Truly, this very point pertains to the completeness of the argument: to now show diverse and plainly contrary examples, so that it may appear that this splendor in eclipses—and therefore this thickness of celestial matter—is not perpetual. Although what need is there for examples, many of which I have recorded in my *Optics* <note>Kepler refers to his 1604 work, *Ad Vitellionem paralipomena, quibus astronomiae pars optica traditur*, where he laid the foundations of modern optics.</note>. Let us remain only with the most recent instance of the year 1560; the eclipse of that year brought an amazing darkness to Portugal. Even though the Moon was at its perigee <note>The point in the Moon's orbit where it is closest to the Earth.</note>, do not think that those shadows were increased solely by astronomical circumstances; rather, you should conclude that the greatest addition to that darkness was made by a lack of celestial matter, which usually carries brilliance far and wide.

Accept what was recently written to me from Trier, from the mouth of a certain trustworthy <note>original: "ἀξιοπίστȣ" (axiopistou)</note> old man, through the effort of a most learned and by no means untrustworthy man: During that eclipse of the Sun in the year 1560, around midday at Trier, there was also such great obscurity of the day that indoors, men standing only a short distance from one another did not recognize each other, and many stars appeared, even though the eclipse in that location was far from total.

But in the past year, 1605, except for one surgeon in Brabant—a sharp-eyed fellow <note>original: "Lynceum," a reference to Lynceus, the Argonaut famous for his superhumanly keen sight.</note>, I believe—I have heard of no one to whom the stars were visible, even though the Moon was near perigee and at Naples the entire Sun was covered. What else is gathered from this, except that the matter of the heaven, poured around the Sun or Moon, behaves differently at different times—now it thickens, and soon again it is purified and dispersed?

<margin>See: The surrounding splendor of celestial matter hides the Moon during new moons.</margin>

Nor do eclipses alone confirm this matter; even apart from a failure of the Sun, the Moon offers us proof of this thing. In the year 1553, as a grave and most learned man wrote to me, the Moon was seen at Seville standing near the Sun in Pisces, in the very middle of the sky. The whole city watched and bore witness. Yet when has anyone seen a similar new moon? Does not the doctrine of the ancients claim the first sight of the Moon for two days or more after the conjunction <note>The "copula" or conjunction is when the Moon is between the Earth and Sun; traditionally, it was thought the Moon could not be seen for a few days before and after this point because of the Sun's glare.</note>? Therefore, this is the fifth testimony of that which we contend: namely, that the matter of heaven, being one thing at one time and another at another, differing greatly from itself in its subtlety, also sometimes drinks in more clarity than at other times. Thus, at some times it obscures the eyes of men more, and at other times less, in seeing the stars by day.

When these arguments have found their place in the Academy <note>The established scholarly community or universities.</note>, then at last I will pray that this also be applied to this New Star, and the one that preceded it in the year 1596—the small one in the Whale <note>The star Mira Ceti, which fluctuates in brightness.</note>—and the one in 1572, the very great one in Cassiopeia; and finally the one in 1600 in the Swan <note>The star P Cygni.</note>, which is seen even today, whatever that might be.

<summary>Kepler argues that the "ether" of space is a changeable substance of varying density, using historical accounts of eclipses and rare lunar sightings to prove that the heavens are not immutable. He connects these atmospheric-like changes in deep space to the appearance of "New Stars" (supernovae and variables).</summary>

# ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 121

...should it be that way (the description of which follows), so that to these New fixed stars, I say, a sixth place of argument may be left. For the present, however, let it suffice for the five prior proofs to have rendered this probable: that the matter of the heavens is alterable. Therefore, nothing absurd follows if someone should say that the matter of the New star was compressed from the Sphere of the fixed stars itself; and, once the star was extinguished, that matter was poured back into the same sphere in turn.

->## Concerning the Efficient Cause of the New Star.<-
### CHAPTER XXIV.
<note>In Aristotelian philosophy, the "efficient cause" is the agent or force that brings something into being. Kepler is looking for the "trigger" that caused the supernova.</note>

IN a doubtful matter, I shall not contend much. Theologians—those who are the opposites of the ones we refuted above—are quick to decide that a miracle was performed by God himself, a new star having been created. For us, the more and more frequent appearance of these stars has stimulated a more careful curiosity. And so, if we had been satisfied with that theological opinion, there would have been no need to speak of matter at all. For it is the work of God alone to create from nothing <note>original: "ex nihilo"</note>. Now, because we have spoken of matter, establishing that it is celestial in nature, let us also add something concerning the efficient cause, or the natural generator <note>original: "gignente naturali"</note>, if any such thing can be found. I hope I shall be received with fair ears, especially by Brengger <note>Christoph Brengger was a physician and astronomer who corresponded with Kepler about the 1604 supernova.</note> and others who have judged that this star cannot be attributed to nature unless we also devise a **New Physics** of the celestial bodies. I shall be the first, therefore, to give my opinion, so that others may have a much more abundant subject for speaking. And I know not what Virgilian strain some Pythagorean might chant to me here:
<note>Kepler quotes Virgil’s *Aeneid* (Book VI, 724-727), a passage often used to describe a "World Soul."</note>
      *In the beginning, the spirit within nourishes the heavens and the earth,*
      *The watery plains, the shining globe of the Moon, and the Titan stars;*
      *And Mind, infused through the limbs, moves the entire mass,*
      *And mingles itself with the great body.*
   Aristotle, however, puts it thus: *Plants and tiny animals arise from the earth just as they do from moisture: because there is moisture in the earth, spirit in the moisture, and in the whole UNIVERSE there is ANIMAL HEAT.* <note>original: "UNIVERSO CALOR ANIMALIS"</note> These are the words by which Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558), a famous scholar who commented on Aristotle's works on nature.</note> believed we are led into the hidden mysteries of nature.
   We said above that our star has this similarity to "Trajections" <note>A "trajection" is an old term for a meteor or shooting star.</note>: it suddenly shone forth with great power, and from there, having been gradually diminished, it finally vanished. If like effects come from like causes, then since these trajections near us consist of a fatty matter <note>Kepler follows the ancient theory that meteors were "fatty" or oily vapors from the Earth that ignited in the upper atmosphere.</note> that is fit for receiving either fire or light—and all such fatness is an excretion—it is produced

He begins exploring the "efficient cause" of the star, looking for a natural explanation rather than a purely miraculous one, citing Virgil and Aristotle regarding a "spirit" or "heat" that permeates the universe.</summary>

<meta>Kepler is continuing his discussion on the "Efficient Cause" (the triggering force) of the New Star. He is transitioning from theological explanations to a naturalistic, biological analogy, suggesting that the universe has a "vital faculty" similar to that found on Earth.</meta>

122 JOHANNES KEPLER

...either through the vital faculty of the Globe of the Earth, or through an external and accidental heat, as seen in fires; therefore, for this new star to be generated in the ether, it also seems to us that a certain fervor is necessary, which is similar to a natural faculty. For all "oiliness" <note>original: "pinguedo"; in the science of Kepler's time, this referred to a fatty or sulfurous substance thought to be the fuel for all fire and light</note>, every flammable thing, and everything changeable into luminous matter, seems to require the work of a certain natural, architectonic faculty. This faculty would be the propagator of native heat within its own body; it would be equipped with that heat to alter and assimilate into itself the matter upon which it rests. Furthermore, this kind of fatty vapor is generated either within the everlasting globes <note>the planets or stars</note>, or in the space between—and if I may transfer a phrase of Virgil’s from the Elements to the heavens—*in the flowing fields* <note>original: "In campis liquentibus"; a reference to Virgil’s Aeneid, describing the liquid nature of the heavens or the sea</note>. If it is generated within the globes, there is no longer any question as to who the parent is: it is well enough established regarding the Earth that it possesses such a faculty within itself. This faculty generates different metals in different places; it fosters heat within; it absorbs and digests the waters of the sea, causing them to evaporate into the heights to provide material for rivers and rains; and it imparts marvelous geometric shapes to fossils <note>Kepler likely refers to crystals or patterned stones here</note>, by no other means than a certain hidden impression, which relies upon a power corresponding to the imagination of animals. I have said more on such things in my *Optics* <note>Kepler’s *Ad Vitellionem Paralipomena* (1604), where he discussed the formative power of nature</note>. Therefore, whatever kind of faculty this Earth (one of the globes) possesses, it is consistent that the other globes are also endowed with such things. The author and the origin of the "exhalation" <note>the vapor that forms the star</note> would thus be clear. However, because this matter is condensed and finally ignited not within the globes of the stars, but *in the flowing fields* of the ether, no matter how much you place natural faculties within the globes, it is now necessary to add some other faculty as well. This one, *infused through all the limbs* <note>original: "totos infusa per artus"; another quote from Virgil’s Aeneid, often used to describe a world-soul or universal spirit</note>, permeates the liquid ether. And to again take examples of celestial things from terrestrial ones: let us consider what performs that task in the ether, just as some faculty—most hidden in name, but most manifest in reality—performs it in this warm air of ours.

This is surely that faculty which, whenever it finds any superfluous matter, converts it into some small living creature that serves the nature of things, whether to help it or to unburden it: it turns the sweat of the head into lice; the sweat of the body (especially a woman's) into fleas; the sap of trees into various kinds of caterpillars, according to the different types of trees; the redundant fats of hides, when abandoned by their own form, into moths; mud into eels; water into frogs, fish, leeches, and infinite and ever-new kinds of sea monsters; and the Earth into various species of plants and trees. In short, from this source come swarms of flies, bees, wasps, hornets, beetles, and huge armies of butterflies. Philosophers call this "putrefaction," <note>In Aristotelian biology, "putrefaction" was not just rotting, but the process by which matter lost one form to gain another through spontaneous generation</note> which in truth is nothing other than a restoration of matter into some new form, occurring as the old form perishes and, as it were, dies; a task performed by this most industrious...

<summary>Kepler argues that the New Star was formed by a "vital faculty" in the ether, using an analogy to spontaneous generation on Earth where "superfluous matter" becomes new life (like lice or eels).</summary>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN SERPENTARIUS. 123**

...most generous architect, whoever that may be. Everyone is free to inquire into this; to me, this very faculty seems to be the one I previously said resides within the globe of the Earth itself; however, with this distinction: just as the internal faculty of the Earth is to the waters and air for producing butterflies and aquatic monsters <note>Kepler refers to the theory of spontaneous generation, where living things were thought to arise from inanimate matter like mud or water</note>, so too is a human’s own natural faculty for the breeding of lice. For both, a certain vapor seems to be the instrument; generated within the body, it surrounds it on the outside once exhaled, filling the more hidden recesses of bodies with its moisture, where the trapped heat can endure. For once this is done, this faculty—the inhabitant of the body—immediately goes out into that vapor (enticed, as it were, by the heat and the outflow of its own vapor) and begins to engineer <term>machinari: to design or construct with mechanical precision</term> these wonders: instructed by a divine instinct received through creation and retained until now, concerning the ordering of individual members toward their final purpose. I am uncertain how this example should be transferred to the great body of the world itself and its architectonic faculty. For either it has no seat <note>or "fixed location"</note> except in the empty fluid <note>original: "liquido inani"; Kepler’s term for the fluid ether that fills the space between celestial bodies</note>: or if it is in the globes of the stars, its seat is not just one: or if it is in the globe of the Sun, its seat is very narrow, while its reach is immense, similar to the infinite. I prefer to say the first...

He questions where this creative force resides, whether in the stars, the Sun, or the fluid space between them.</summary>

to effect; by that instinct by which we said this terrestrial faculty, diffused among animals, produces tiny creatures like butterflies and the like.

<margin>Is it possible for a world to be born from a world?</margin>

    I have heard of some who have gone so far that, relying on this faculty, they are willing to show themselves agreeable if someone wishes to prove that a world can even be born from a world. They are not hindered by the fact that they see the bodies of the world are not physically joined. For neither is the crystalline humor <note>The lens of the eye</note> attached to the tunics of the eye; indeed, it falls out if the eye is barely opened, and there is no need for it to be cut away. Nor is it nourished by veins or grown by them, but rather by vapor. A person who wishes to generate a world from a world might claim something similar regarding the stars flying freely in the ethereal air.
    But this addition builds a false accusation against the truth. In a human, the faculty that produces lice is far inferior to the one that produces a human. And in the Earth, no one will deny the faculty of which I have spoken; yet it would be a monstrous fiction if anyone extended its powers—I will not say to the generation of another earth—but even to its mere growth. Let faculties be judged by their works, through their own bodies; not, conversely, let the works of all things be judged from one or two examples of a faculty. Thus here also, if that faculty presides over maintaining the transparency of the ether, it has its own work by which it defends the name of "faculty," even if it does not generate. I need not mention that its body lacks the organs for generating; Aristotle used a similar argument against the motion of the stars, claiming it could not happen without celestial orbs <term>orbibus: the traditional, solid transparent spheres thought to carry the planets and stars</term>. But those "fine" philosophers <note>Kepler likely refers to the Epicureans or those arguing for an infinite, eternal universe</note> labor so that God may have something to do from eternity; to which end, they link world to world through generation. To them, it might perhaps be appropriately replied that eternity has no parts; therefore, the beginning of time is sought from the whole—a thought that my reading of Saint Augustine seemed to suggest to me.

Comparison of this New Star with the other New one,
which shone in the year 1572 in Cassiopeia,
and with Comets.
CHAPTER XXV.

    I stated in the first chapter how much can be gathered from Tycho Brahe’s book: this recent star was most similar to that prior one of the year 1572 in the clarity of its light, its twinkling, colors, magnitude, its decrease, and its lack of motion.
    Nor was there any difference in duration about which anyone could testify. That one was lit in the month of October or November of the year 1572; this one was lit in October of the year...

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>Kepler is continuing his comparison between the "New Star" of 1604 (now known as Kepler's Supernova) and the famous star of 1572 observed by Tycho Brahe. He is examining their durations, positions, and physical appearances to determine their significance.</meta>

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 125<-

In November of the year 1572, that star appeared. It was extinguished after 16 months, namely in March of the year 1574; our star, seen since October of the year 1604, was still visible in October 1605, after it had already lasted 12 months. For the following four months, it could not be seen due to the presence of the Sun; by February and March of 1606, it had disappeared. They were also very similar in position for two reasons: namely, that both were in the Milky Way, and both were in the highest ether <note>The "highest ether" refers to the region of the fixed stars, proving the stars were not atmospheric phenomena like comets or meteors</note> among the rest of the fixed stars. Indeed, Roeslin <note>Helisaeus Roeslin (1545–1616), a German physician and astronomer who also wrote on the New Star</note> also celebrates this fact: that both stars were joined with Jupiter in terms of longitude, and both, regarding the equinoctial circle, were in "fiery" signs <note>In astrology, the "fiery triplicity" consists of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. The 1604 star appeared in Sagittarius</note>. For that former star reached the meridian when the first point of Aries was at the mid-heaven (which Brahe emphasized diligently), even though the star itself was in Taurus; this star of ours, however, stood in Sagittarius.
      And add what I said above in Chapter 1 concerning the position of Venus, which was the same in both instances.
      Since our star is equal to that previous one in so many ways, see now also in what circumstances it is by far to be preferred. I will say nothing of the fact that certain great men who had seen that earlier star pronounced this one much larger. For it might have seemed so to them because of the star’s proximity to the horizon whenever it appeared, and the humid weather of the year, which was also mentioned above in Chapter 1.
      And Brahe made his star exceedingly large, namely larger than Jupiter at its *acronychal* <note>original: "Acronychio"; this refers to a planet being at "opposition," or directly opposite the sun, when it appears largest and brightest to Earth</note> position, and nearly equal to Venus, which I find surprising. For there is a great difference in magnitude between Jupiter and Venus—especially when Venus is closer to the Earth and shines with a fuller face, as Brahe says. But he also asserts it was seen by day. Our star was neither seen during the day (perhaps due to its low altitude, not for lack of size) nor did it equal Venus. Therefore, let this equality of magnitude remain between them.
      The first difference is in the *scintillation* <note>The rapid "twinkling" or shimmering of a star’s light</note>. While the scintillation in our star was especially admirable and spectacular, exceeding all other fixed stars and filling everyone's eyes and talk, that star of 1572, by contrast, is only modestly celebrated for its scintillation. To be sure, that star closely emulated the light of Jupiter; our star represented a light not so "fat" or steady as Jupiter’s, but subtler and more refined, like that of Mercury or Sirius. Thus, from the very beginning, it threw back the colors of the rainbow in a continuous vibration, whereas that earlier star was merely white at its commencement. In turn, however, that earlier star changed its colors successively; our star was not remarkable in this regard, except that as it faded, that rapid vigor of its twinkling and the projection of its colors died away little by little.
      But the chief and most worthy difference for consideration was in place and time. For that star shone outside the limits of the zodiac, in the constellation of Cassiopeia—an uncommon place in the heavens, ennobled by no visits from the planets—

more favored: this one chose its station very near the Royal Way <term>Royal Way: The Ecliptic, the apparent path of the Sun, Moon, and planets across the celestial sphere.</term> of the Sun, Moon, and the rest of the planets, so that it might be greeted by all the planets: indeed, it was joined to Saturn almost bodily <note>original: "corporaliter jungeretur" — This refers to a very close conjunction where the objects appear nearly on top of one another.</note>. Although that earlier star was surrounded by several bright stars of the second magnitude in Cassiopeia, those were common stars from the general number of fixed stars, notable for no nobility of their own motion. Our star, however, thrust itself into the very midst of the three superior planets <term>Superior Planets: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn, which orbit further from the Sun than the Earth.</term>, using Jupiter and Mars as its advance-guard attendants and Saturn as its following bodyguard. That earlier star occupied the twelfth part of the heavens which takes its name from Taurus, an earthy sign, distinguished at that time by no conjunction of planets above the others; this one blazed forth in the fiery sign of Sagittarius, in which that most celebrated revolution of the Fiery Trigon <term>Fiery Trigon: A major astrological cycle occurring roughly every 800 years when the Great Conjunctions of Jupiter and Saturn shift into the "fire" signs (Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius).</term> had taken its beginning last December—an event that can only occur after intervals of eight hundred years. That earlier star arrived at a common and ignoble time, marked by no particular sign; this one fell precisely into that year which all astrologers had designated with the most diligent warnings as the beginning of the Fiery Trigon and the celestial wonders to come. It fell in the precise month in which the star of Mars also approached the two superior planets, fulfilling a "great conjunction" according to the teaching of Cyprian <note>Cyprian Leowitz (1524–1574), a Bohemian astronomer whose astrological predictions concerning planetary conjunctions were widely discussed.</note>. It fell on the precise day on which Mars, having first overtaken Saturn, had also reached Jupiter. In short, it appeared in the precise location in the heavens toward which the eyes of all astrologers were directed, as they awaited the meeting of Jupiter and Mars.

Therefore, that earlier star came upon a world that had not been forewarned; like an unexpected enemy, having already occupied the city walls, it appeared in the forum before the citizens had even heard a rumor of its advance. Our star, however, commonly expected for a long time, entered on the appointed day with great solemnity and triumphal pomp, in the manner of some most powerful Monarch. When such a King is about to visit the Metropolis of his kingdom, he sends his harbingers <note>original: "Metatoribus" — Roman officials who went ahead of an army or a royal party to scout and mark out a campsite.</note> far in advance to designate locations for his retinue. And now the streets are cleaned, now the common people, stirred by rumors, run to and fro, and the children complain of the delays; then the baggage and the silver tableware arrive. Soon, with the prince’s retinue following, as the first ones pass through the gates, the noise of horses on the paved streets and the blare of trumpets resound. Everyone, fully alert, occupies the windows and fills the streets—boys and tender girls trembling with joy. The Heroes and faithful ministers proceed high on their horses; the common people, eager to gaze upon the King, survey each one with gaping mouths and anxious eyes. Finally, after a long line, the troop of drummers and trumpeters arises; a reverent order of armed men follows, with an elegant company of runners on both sides. In the middle, the King...

ON THE NEW STAR IN THE FOOT OF THE SERPENT-BEARER | 127

...dancing on a noble steed, distinguished by purple and gold: there is no need for the pointing of fingers or for questions; each person, in their own mind, having seen the entourage, says: "Behold, finally, the one you desired." Such, therefore, was the appearance of our star.

But if this matters to the point, the star of the year 1572, through its height and brightness (the former did not allow it to set, while the latter allowed it to be seen even during the day), forced itself first into the eyes of the common people, who spent most of their time outdoors, almost tweaking each person's ear to look at it. This star of ours, however, was accompanied by different circumstances. Since it did not rise very high and followed very closely behind the Sun toward the West, clothed in the brightness of twilight like the evening star <note>original: "hesperus," the personification of the planet Venus as the evening star.</note>, and moved among the planets as if it were one of their number, it could not become known or its novelty made manifest to anyone before the learned—especially those who had studied astronomy and the positions of the planets. Thus, Weidner <note>Paulus Weidner (1525–1585), a physician and academic whose works often touched on theological interpretations of celestial events.</note> beautifully writes:

> *For Jehovah gives so rare and noble a sign,*
> *Primarily to mathematicians, not observable by the common crowd.*

It also seems appropriate at this point to compare our star with comets. For there are some who call it a comet, such as Gunter, Moller, Büttner, Krabbe, and others. And some are found who, for that very reason, deny it should be called a star because it disappeared—for stars, they say, endure forever. Johann Georg Brengger <note>A physician and astronomer with whom Kepler corresponded frequently.</note> beautifully teaches them first their grammar. For "Comet" <margin>This is how the species described by Pliny seem to me to be distinguished. Truly Achilles <note>Referring to Achilles Tatius, an ancient author who wrote on astronomy.</note> says otherwise: "Those who have the light below, and the shimmering rays pointing downward, are called comets." <note>original: Οἱ μὲν οὖν κάτω τὸ φῶς ἔχοντες, καὶ τὰς μαρμαρυγὰς κάτω νεύουσας, καλοῦνται κομῆται.</note></margin> or *Crinita* <note>Literally "hairy," from the Latin "crinis" meaning hair.</note> takes its name from hair. If the hair stands above the star, it is a Comet; if it is below, it is a *Pogonias* <term>Pogonias: From the Greek for "bearded."</term>; if the hair is pointed, it is a *Xiphias* <term>Xiphias: From the Greek for "sword-like."</term>; if it is curved and pointed, it is a *Ceratias* <term>Ceratias: From the Greek for "horned."</term>; but if it is curved and spread wide, it is called an *Hippeus* <term>Hippeus: From the Greek for "horse-like," referring to a comet that resembles a horse's mane.</term>. Thus "Comet" is a name of a type and refers to the appearance, not to the substance.

Indeed, all those types are referred to by writers by the name "star." And whenever they use the word "Comet," they imply "star," as if the word "Comet" were an adjective. They often join them together, as in *hairy star* <note>original: ἀστὴρ κομήτης</note>, hairy star <note>original: "stella crinita"</note>, or falling star; where you see that they have no regard for whether the substance is fleeting.

But let it be that the word for the species, "Comet," has passed into a general term, and all the forms mentioned by Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (Pliny the Elder), whose "Natural History" was a standard source for comet classification.</note> in Book 2, chapter 25, are called comets: to which species then will they refer this star of ours? None of the aforementioned ones fit. But if they call it a *Lampadias* <term>Lampadias: From the Greek for "torch-like," a type of comet that appears like a burning lamp or torch.</term>, they must be referred back to Chapter 18, concerning scintillation. For those scintillations of the star were by no means "hairs" [swiftly...] <note>The Latin text cuts off at "rapi-," likely "rapidly" or "carried away."</note>

128 JOHANNES KEPLER

<margin>
Achilles [Tatius] argues to the contrary, and differently than Pliny: "those that have their light bearded upward are called Lampadias <note>original: "Λαμπάδες" (Lampades), meaning torches.</note>."
</margin>

...is most rapid, but the flash of light is reciprocal. Unless, perhaps, they would also turn the Greater Dog Star <note>Sirius</note> and the other stars into comets, simply because they share their twinkling with this star. Thus, the name *Lampadias* <note>A term for a comet that resembles a burning torch.</note> could be granted to this celestial body, but only in such a way that these "Torches" are not considered a species of "long-haired" comets <note>original: "Crinitarum," from the Latin for hair, referring to the tail of a comet.</note>.

Next follow the final species defined by Pliny: the *disceorum* <note>disk-like</note>, which have very thin hair around their disks; and the *hirtorum* <note>shaggy or rough</note>, which have the appearance of tufts of hair. The author Juba calls these "manes," while more recent writers call them "Roses," to which I also refer the "Night-suns" celebrated by the ancients. But our star is no more to be counted among these species than the Dog Star <note>Sirius</note>, because it did not emit scattered "manes" all around it, but merely twinkled. 

Add to this that in these species, the "Mane" or "Disk" is sometimes transformed into a spear-shape—a phenomenon Pliny records as having happened once. It occurred again in the year of Christ 837 under Louis the Pious, and finally even in our own memory, as observed by Tycho Brahe in the years 1580 and 1585. On these three occasions, the Sun was in opposition to the comets; therefore, since at the beginning the "hairs" <note>tails</note> were turned away from the Sun and from our sight, they showed their heads in the middle of their manes. Later, having left the position opposite the Sun, they gradually unfolded their "hair" from the side from which they were departing. At the same time, the first two were also retrograde <note>appearing to move backward in the sky</note>, exactly like the planets when they are opposed to the Sun; Copernicus most beautifully attributes the cause of this to the motion of the Earth. 

Since our star, therefore, exhibited no appearance of "hair" either at its beginning or its end, it ought by no means to be called a comet. On the contrary, it is not absurd to note that the word "star" <note>stella</note> descends from "to sparkle" <note>original: "στίλβειν" (stilbein), a Greek verb meaning to glisten or twinkle</note>, which is what "scintillating" means. Since this fits our star perfectly, what prevents us from calling it a star?

But what if, they ask, it actually possessed some kind of tail? For our sight did not reach it simply because the whole tail lay hidden behind the head, both when the star was in quadrature <note>at a 90-degree angle to the Sun</note> and when in opposition to the Sun. Indeed, the same arguments can be used to build this case as those used in Chapter XV to show that the star lacked an annual parallax <note>the apparent shift of a star's position due to Earth's orbit</note>. For this reason, look at the diagram on page 86, where the star is depicted with a tail because of this very objection. To be sure, the elongation *CO* of the Earth *O* from the Sun *C* could by no means be great enough to reveal this tail from the side to us at position *D*, owing to the excessive distance of the star. This conjecture was also tolerated by Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin (1550–1631), Kepler’s astronomy professor and mentor</note> regarding the star of 1572, in his booklet on the comet of 1580.

I respond: sight invented the names of stars, not reasoning. Whoever raises this objection is not reporting what he saw, but is divining what might be. Let it be a comet, then, when it is believed by philosophers to project a tail upward away from us. In the meantime, let us other men be allowed to speak according to our sight; for the common people are the masters of the philosophers, and sight is the master of common speech.

Breng- <note>Catchword for the next page, likely referring to the name "Brengger."</note>

<meta>Kepler continues his systematic comparison between the New Star of 1604 and historical comets, focusing on its brightness and lack of motion compared to ancient records.</meta>

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 129<-

Brengger <note>Christoph Brengger, a German astronomer and frequent correspondent of Kepler.</note> also employs another response, denying that comets are typically as bright as what was seen in this star; not only in their tails, but not even in their heads. As Tycho Brahe reported, they are often whitish, bluish-gray <note>original: "livida"</note>, sometimes reddish, and at other times pale; they possess lights that are blunt and dull on both sides—they are more truly **cloudlets** than stars. Indeed, Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the Roman *Natural History*.</note> did establish one species of white comet with silver hair, the brilliance of which might even turn away the gaze of the eyes; however, I know of no such example from the records of history.

I can also use at this point the same argument I used above in Chapter 15 to assign our star's place among the fixed stars: to prove that it also lacked a tail. For "long-haired" stars <note>original: "Crinitæ," the Latin root of "comet."</note> are by their nature mobile, yet our star remained immobile. Pliny seems to tear this reasoning down, as he says that certain species of comets remain fixed and motionless. But this is easily answered based on the experience of our own times.

For it can happen, as with the "stationary" planets, that although they are mobile, they nevertheless appear to stand still for a few days; this occurs if they are carried in the same direction and at the same speed as the Earth. For example, the comet of the year 1585: since it was positioned opposite the Sun, it should have been represented as moving backward <note>original: "retrogradus"</note> by the motion of the Earth (even if the comet itself were at rest). However, due to the speed of its own proper motion in the following signs, it appeared to move "direct" <note>Moving forward along the zodiac.</note>. If you were to subtract just a little from its proper motion, you would make it appear stationary to the sight. And that other comet of the year 1580, when it appeared stationary in the month of December, was still being carried by its own motion along its trajectory toward the end of Libra and the Ecliptic, having traversed nearly equal spaces since its beginning.

Therefore, if such a comet shone in Pliny’s age or before him, and it happened to be rendered stationary at a time when perhaps it had not been seen before or afterward, this could have provided Pliny the occasion to invent a species of immobile comets.

->**Whether this star occurred by chance at the time and place of the great conjunction.**<-

### CHAPTER XXVI.

We now enter a discussion that is the most difficult and tangled of all those concerning the whole nature of things; not only because of the obscurity of the question itself, but also because of the intense disagreements of the entire human race, which is split into completely opposite factions. From what has been stated above, it is clear...

R
</margin>

<summary>Kepler refutes the idea that the New Star was a comet by citing its extreme brightness and lack of motion, explaining that ancient reports of "stationary comets" were likely optical illusions caused by the Earth's orbital movement.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from page 129, where Kepler was discussing how the comet of 1585 appeared stationary because its motion matched the Earth's relative speed for a brief time.</meta>

130

own motion, it stood still for several days, as if it were fixed to the heavens. But that this should happen for an entire year is something that neither history testifies to, nor the nature of things allows. <note>Kepler is refuting the idea that the New Star of 1604 was a comet. He argues that while a comet might appear "stationary" for a few days due to orbital geometry, it could never appear to stay in the exact same spot for a whole year unless it was actually part of the distant, fixed stars.</note>

For if it were a comet or some other wandering body, it would have had to move with a speed and direction perfectly synchronized with the Earth's own movement for a full twelve months to appear motionless to our eyes. This is a physical impossibility. 

<margin>The star shows no parallax</margin>
Furthermore, we must address the lack of **parallax** <term>parallax: the apparent shift in position of an object when viewed from different locations. If the star were close to Earth, it would seem to "jump" against the background stars when seen from different cities.</term>. Had this star been a "meteor" or a comet within our own atmosphere—as those who follow the ancient teachings of Aristotle <note>Aristotle believed the heavens were unchanging; therefore, any "new" stars had to be atmospheric phenomena (like weather) occurring below the Moon.</note> suggest—it would have appeared in different positions to an observer in Prague than to one in Italy. 

However, the records from across the continent are unanimous: the star remained exactly in its place in the foot of the Serpent-bearer <note>The constellation Ophiuchus</note>. It did not drift, and it did not shift. 

<margin>Conclusion on its nature</margin>
Therefore, because it lacked the "long hair" or tail of a comet, because it possessed the twinkling brilliance of a star, and because it remained perfectly immobile for the duration of its appearance, we must conclude it was no comet, but a **New Star** in the highest heavens.

ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER 131

...had arisen from the Great Conjunction. Therefore, while these things coincide with good reason and merit, they were nonetheless joined together by no necessity, but rather with great wonder: for this star arose not only on the day of the Great Conjunction, but even in that very location where the planets met. <note>The "Great Conjunction" refers to the rare meeting of the planets Jupiter and Saturn in the sky, an event astrologers watched with intense scrutiny. In 1604, this happened in the sign of Sagittarius.</note> This contains something in its depths much more sublime than that first opinion of the astrologers. For even if we grant that the star is, according to the way of nature, the genuine offspring of the Great Conjunction; what forced it to stand in that visible location where the conjunction occurred? For there are no such examples from which the astrologers have collected their rule. Therefore, those speculators believe it must be asked—since the astrologers have provided no cause—what the true cause was for such an admirable harmony. For such great beauty and such great order strike the eyes of the mind: it seems not at all a mockery of chance wandering blindly, but the work of a disposing Reason; whether that be the Creatress herself, or a spirit pervading the whole machine of the world, which both saw the appearance of the conjunction on Earth and, according to this, generated this star in the highest **ether** <term>ether: in Kepler's time, the "aether" was the substance believed to fill the heavens beyond the Earth's atmosphere.</term>.

<margin>III. The opinion of the Physicists</margin>
But a third opinion, also held by Physicists, not only rejects the astrologers but also laughs at these speculators, claiming all these things for chance. 

<margin>From the Epicurean sect</margin>
Yet they do not all argue in the same way. Some, for the sake of intellectual exercise, defend the philosophy of Epicurus <note>Epicurus was an ancient Greek philosopher who taught that the universe is made of atoms and governed by chance, rather than divine purpose.</note> with a certain sought-after probability; for them, not only is this harmony accidental, but the star itself flowed together from atoms. Those who are asked for the cause of why the location of the star agrees so beautifully with the time point to the rolling of dice, reminding us that in infinity, all things are possible. I will try to express their meaning: Imagine two dice, one for Time and the other for Place. The first is inscribed with innumerable occasions of time, and among these is also one for the "Great Conjunction" at the start of the **Fiery Trigon** <term>Fiery Trigon: the three zodiac signs associated with fire—Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. The 1604 conjunction was particularly significant because it marked the beginning of a new 800-year cycle in these signs.</term>. The second die is marked with the characters of innumerable places in the sky, among which there is also one for the place of the Great Conjunction. 

Suppose there exist infinite new stars of this kind, each having obtained for itself a time and a place from one throw of these dice. They say, therefore, that it must be granted entirely that if infinite throws of this kind were given—namely, from the ultimate eternity which these disputants presuppose—it would absolutely happen that, among innumerable others, this specific throw would eventually fall, which couples the place of the Great Conjunction with the time of that same conjunction. And so they claim it is no wonder, and there is no suspicion of a governing reason, if among the innumerable new stars succeeding each other in an eternal series (if such were given), some are ignited precisely during the moments of Great Conjunctions; and if among innumerable new stars, at the times of great conjunctions...

<summary>Kepler discusses why the New Star appeared exactly where and when Jupiter and Saturn met. He weighs the idea of a "disposing Reason" (divine order) against the Epicurean view that it was a random collision of atoms, comparable to rolling dice over an infinite amount of time.</summary>

132 JOHANNES KEPLER

...having arisen, certain ones even fall into those beginnings of the eight-hundred-year cycles <note>The Great Conjunction of Jupiter and Saturn returns to the same astrological "triplicity" (set of zodiac signs) roughly every 800 years, an event Kepler and his contemporaries viewed as having massive historical significance.</note> that return infinitely; finally, if among the innumerable new stars which coincide with the beginnings of the Zodiacal Periods, some are also placed in that very location where the boundary of the conjunction period is fixed.

<margin>From the Aristotelian sect.</margin>
Others express more closely the doctrine of Aristotle concerning **fortune** and **chance**, to whom it is pleasing that neither do the planets meet, nor are the new stars ignited, by sheer accident. For there are proper causes for both—for the former in the course of the heavens, for the latter in the inner recesses of nature—causes that are distinct and not linked to one another. From both causes, there arise in their own place and time both the meetings of the planets and the appearances of new stars; nevertheless, it happens by chance <term>chance: here translating the Aristotelian *casus*, which refers to the accidental coincidence of two independent chains of causality.</term> if a conjunction coincides with a new star, whether in time or in place. For it was not the goal proposed by the **Rector** <note>original: *Rectori*; Kepler refers here to the Divine Governor or Creator who steers the universe.</note> of this world to provide these congruencies; rather, while setting the celestial spheres in motion on one hand and the force of nature on the other, spontaneously and beyond any particular intent, by material [force alone]...

# ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 133

...are one hundred and twenty-one. I have said all that is inherent in the matter: for if this condition is removed, as Boethus <note>Boethus was a Stoic philosopher; Kepler likely cites him here via a secondary source to argue against impossible physical scenarios.</note> said in the place cited: *A discourse that speaks of things that are not (which are foreign to the whole nature of the matter, as if someone, having granted the eternity of motion, should finally wish to bring it about that two planets swap their orbits and periodic times), being flawed in itself, waits in vain for the faith which chance might win for it.*

<margin>IV. Of the Theologians.</margin>
There follows the fourth opinion, derived from the sources of the Theologians: for whom, since it is a presupposition that this whole world is contained by divine providence, and that not only is the order of nature preserved, but also that miracles sometimes occur beyond the course of nature in heaven and earth (among the number of which they also hold this star), that men are called to repentance by divine goodness. And although they despise that first opinion of the Astrologers, partly as naked, meager, and too simple, and partly hold it suspect of impiety; they do not, however, deny that, on account of these very rules of the Astrologers, this new miracle of divine omnipotence was placed by divine arrangement both in the time and place of the **Great Conjunction** <note>The rare meeting of Jupiter and Saturn, which occurred in 1604 in the sign of Sagittarius.</note>. 

Not that He transforms nature based on the vain dogmas of men concerning the **Fiery Triplicity** <term>Fiery Triplicity: a group of three zodiac signs—Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius—which astrologers believed shared the elemental nature of fire.</term>, and that Sagittarius therefore receives a fiery quality from God because it pleased men to name it after fire; but because they consider it not absurd that God, with a temporary plan, uses popular human concepts to admonish the multitude of men through that reasoning all the more clearly. 

They set these examples before our eyes. **Pharaoh**, King of Egypt, was devoted to the interpretation of dreams according to the custom of his nation; to him GOD presented a dream, when He was planning the liberation of Joseph and the propagation and housing of the Church in Egypt. And since the Egyptians worshiped the god **Apis**, namely a Bull <note>Apis was a sacred bull of ancient Egyptian religion, representing strength and fertility.</note>; because while other nations used oxen for plowing, they were provided for in the harvest without the labor of plowing, the Nile irrigating their fields: God used either this imagination or knowledge of the King; he depicted for him seven harvests by presenting the appearance of as many oxen.

So **Nebuchadnezzar**, King of the Chaldeans, occupied with the statue of Bel <note>original: *Belis*; Bel was the Babylonian title for 'Lord' and often referred to the god Marduk.</note>, who was the author of the Assyrian kingdom and believed to be its preserver; he sees a statue in a dream, presented to him divinely as an interpreter of things to come, as the Prophet Daniel affirmed. To **Abraham**, to whom they trace the principles of Astronomy, God showed the stars, intending to teach him about the multiplication of his offspring. The **Chaldean Magi** cultivated Astrology, accustomed to conjecture the future from the meeting of stars and rising Comets. These, God [led] toward Christ...

...intended to lead to Christ the Lord, the burning star warns. And almost all circumstances prove that the business of that star <note>The Star of Bethlehem.</note> was very similar to our modern one, except for this star’s immobility and altitude: <note>Kepler notes that the 1604 star appeared fixed like a distant sun, whereas he is debating if the Bethlehem star moved or was lower in the atmosphere.</note> namely, that it also fell at the turning point of the returned **Fiery Triplicity** <note>The "Fiery Triplicity" or Trigon refers to the zodiac signs of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. Great Conjunctions occur in these signs only once every 800 years.</note> and at the time of a conjunction.
      Regarding the **mean motions**; <note>The average or theoretical positions of planets, ignoring the specific wobbles and speeds of their actual "apparent" orbits.</note> Saturn and Jupiter were joined in the Julian year 40, 34 minutes before the beginning of Aries, on the 22nd of January. Immediately, on the 25th of February, the mean conjunction of Saturn and Mars occurred in 3 degrees of Aries; and the conjunction of Jupiter and Mars on the 1st of March in 5 degrees of Aries.
      The **apparent motions**, <note>The positions of the planets as actually seen by an observer on Earth.</note> however, were as follows. In the Julian year 39, around the 22nd of June, with the Sun residing in 27 degrees of Gemini, there was a conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter in 23 degrees of Pisces: both of which became stationary shortly after, so that Jupiter was not separated from Saturn by much. After Jupiter itself turned its course forward, it again encountered Saturn in **retrograde motion** <note>The optical illusion where a planet appears to move backward in the sky.</note> during the following August, around 21 degrees of Pisces; and proceeding forward until November, finally in December, it occupied Saturn for a third time in 17 degrees of Pisces. Mars, following these in the year 40 during February and March, completed a conjunction with the former at the end of Pisces and with the latter at the beginning of Aries.
      Therefore, that **Great Conjunction** <note>A meeting of the two "superior" planets, Jupiter and Saturn.</note> was more powerful than this one of ours, because, according to the condition of their motions, at that time the conjunctions of Saturn and Jupiter could occur three times in a single instance <note>A "triple conjunction," where the planets pass each other three times in a few months due to retrograde motion.</note>—just as today the oppositions of those two can be five, while there is only one conjunction. Conversely, although the mean conjunction touched the principal **Cardinal point** (namely the beginning of Aries) as closely as possible—whither also the true complement of the Great Conjunction, that is, the true conjunction of Jupiter and Mars, fell; and Mars, in its mean motion, coincided as closely as possible with the joined superior planets—nevertheless, the true conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter anticipated its place, just as did the one in our age in the year 1583, which is more similar to it than the most recent one of the year 1603.
      If, therefore, in the Julian year 39, during that very Great Conjunction, that star rose as one, and first shone in the very place of the conjunction (as we believe for the time being): it certainly warned the **Chaldaeans** <note>A term often used for the Magi or astrologer-priests of the East.</note> according to their own rules, which still exist today, of the greatest matters and the universal renewal of the whole world. Let that two-year period now pass, from which time Herod sought out the infants of Bethlehem for slaughter; and after the two years were complete, let the **Magi** come into Judea to the cradle of Christ. It would have been, therefore, in the 
                                                                                                      year...

in the Julian year 41. Go now and read what the Pole Laurentius Suslyga <note>Laurentius Suslyga (1570–1640) was a Polish Jesuit whose chronological research convinced Kepler that Christ was born earlier than the traditional calendar suggested.</note> has argued regarding the calculation of time, published in Graz, Styria, using the most weighty arguments based on historical reliability. (Even if, at the point where Suslyga discusses the year of the Passion, the astronomer Kepler, having thrown aside his book and pen, rushed away from the table in a fit of pique; <note>Kepler describes himself in the third person here, reflecting on his own initial frustration with the complex chronological puzzles of the era.</note>) you will say, being fully persuaded, that Christ the Lord was born not in the Julian year 45, but in 41. Therefore, the star, which had begun to shine two years earlier, occurred at the precise moment of the **greatest conjunction** <term>greatest conjunction: A rare alignment where the three outer planets—Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars—all meet in the same region of the sky.</term> of Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars in the Julian year 39. For this reason, it was—as has been said—most similar to our modern one. And since this star was divinely presented to the Magi, it proves that God Himself adapted to these rules of the Magi for a time, so that He might ignite the star at the very moment the Magi were most expecting a star; perhaps also, as said before, He placed it in that location toward which the eyes of the Magi were most especially directed due to the triple gathering of the Planets; just as was done in the case of this modern star of ours.

      Other arguments from deeper Theology also align with this view: such as that God speaks with men through the Prophets about natural things and about arts in such a way as those men understand them, which appears in the book of Job, the Psalms, and the Prophets. Furthermore, the Holy Spirit set forth the doctrine of salvation not in a new and celestial tongue, but in the languages of the nations, and imbued the Apostles with knowledge of them. Likewise, God appeared to the Patriarchs primarily in human form. Finally, the Son of God took this very nature of ours, whole and entire with all its properties (sin excepted), into the unity of His person from the womb of the Blessed Virgin—including the whole sequence of the inner faculties of the human soul, from which speech, various languages, and all human arts and disciplines flow. It is believable, therefore, that this same Lord and our God, whose delight was so great and whose commerce with the children of men will be so great for eternity, even today does not entirely cease from the public signaling of His care for us. He has so ordered and arranged that signaling, presented in the new star through the description of its time and place, that it could not escape us—especially the learned and the **astrologers** (whose daily almanacs everyone, even the lowest, reads today)—nor fail to move us most profoundly.

Examination of the Proposed Opinions.

CHAPTER XXVII.

I had already begun to say that the first opinion of the astrologers is the least acceptable of all; for two main reasons, which I will now explain.

First, it is easy for them to say that the Great Conjunction of the three planets ignited this star; but perhaps they never considered how difficult this is to explain. If this star had been suspended in our air <note>Kepler refers to the "sublunary" region—the atmosphere below the moon—where many contemporary thinkers believed "new" stars or comets appeared.</note>, I could ask them what those eternal stars have in common with our air, which is separated from them by such a great distance. As it is, this star was almost infinitely further away from the planets in the higher regions than our earth is recessed from those same planets toward the interior of the world. Therefore, I ask with much better right: what do the planets have in common with that new star, which took its place among the boundaries of the fixed stars? Not to mention the axiom of the physicists, which attributes action not from lower things to higher ones, but rather from higher to lower. <note>Aristotelian physics held that influence flowed from the "perfect" celestial spheres down to the "corruptible" Earth; Kepler notes that the planets are "lower" than the fixed stars, so they should not be able to "act" upon them.</note> And what shall I say of the size of the new star? Is it likely to anyone that an elephant could be born from a fly? Unless, perhaps, they are going to say that just as a massive amount of gunpowder <term>gunpowder: Kepler uses the Latin *pyrius pulvis*, literally "fire powder."</term> is suddenly ignited by the smallest spark, so too here nothing prevented the smallness of the bodies of Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars from causing that immense mass to catch sudden light everywhere from them. But this is not to *beget*, but to *ignite*. 

I will ask, then: who else gathered so much matter into that place among the fixed stars? And who made it previously ignited and flammable? Finally, by what hands, by what rod, by what stream, or (to use a German idiom) by what "running fire" <note>original: "currente igne." Kepler is likely translating the German word *Lauffeuer* (wildfire or a trail of gunpowder) into Latin.</note> did the flame pass from the planets through immense spaces to that fixed star? For without the contact of either flame or heat, nothing is ignited by another. Unless perhaps one of the three planets brought along the crystal of Giambattista della Porta, with which he promises to burn things at an infinite distance. <note>Giambattista della Porta (1535–1615) was an Italian scientist whose popular book *Natural Magic* discussed optics and "burning mirrors" that could supposedly set fire to objects from afar.</note> Indeed, amidst such vanity from the many who snatch the name of "Philosopher," it is not unbelievable that some will emerge who wish to compare this ignition to the burning of distant things by mirrors—content to have said something that sounds admirable, unsolvable, and plausible to the common people. But watch out: if the new star burned and blazed, let us hope the planets themselves do not eventually burn up in that same fire they shared with the star!

They will, therefore, take refuge in the theory of "rays," through which they claim something occurs similar to what happens with physical bodies: namely, that once these rays are coupled and mixed into one, they obtain the power to produce something new. <margin>Not even from the joining of rays.</margin> Plotinus, as cited by Pico <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher; Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494) wrote a famous critique of astrology titled *Disputationes adversus astrologiam divinatricem*. Kepler aligns himself with Pico's logical skepticism of physical astrological influence.</note>, has already debunked this reasoning. If something new is born through the binding together of rays, it must be born in the exact place where those rays are joined. However, the rays of any two planets are *always* intersecting somewhere, and one can *always* find a location among the fixed stars where such joined rays might flow.
