<meta>This page continues Chapter 27 of Johannes Kepler's "On the New Star." Kepler is transitioning from a physical refutation of planetary "rays" to a geometric refutation based on the Copernican system, arguing that what we see as a "conjunction" is merely a matter of perspective from Earth.</meta>

->138 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

The Copernican doctrine makes this refutation even more illustrious. For that relationship <note>The "relationship" refers to the geometric alignment or conjunction of the planets.</note> is limited to the Earth (which wanders through the heavens), not to a fixed center of the world. For someone placed at the center of the world would have seen, on the day the star shone forth, Mars already at the end of Capricorn, and Jupiter indeed beyond the place of conjunction, yet within the boundaries of Sagittarius, at a very great distance from Mars. Therefore, this conjunction of Jupiter and Mars that we celebrate was only seen as such from the Earth. Take away this condition—the position of the eye on Earth—and you take away the conjunction. Therefore, what could be generated among the fixed stars by something that only appears to happen from the perspective of Earth? For if someone looked down from the fixed stars into the region of the planets, they would either all vanish, or they would be seen circling in the same sphere joined together in the shortest spaces; certainly, conjunctions would be seen more frequently and closer than they could be seen when the star shone forth.

Let it therefore be held as certain that it is impossible for a conjunction of the planets alone to have contributed to the generation of this new star.

Regarding the predictions of a comet, the physician Johannes Weidner responds with learned verses in this manner:

*But if some lazy "star-juggler"* <note>original: "Astrologaster iners." Kepler uses this derogatory term for practitioners who lacked scientific rigor.</note> *contends with a gaping mouth*
*That the meeting of two planetary stars, Jupiter and Mars,*
*Gave birth to a beardless comet* <note>original: "imberbem... Cometam." In the 17th century, comets were characterized by their "hair" (tails); a "beardless" comet refers to a star-like object without a tail, like the supernova Kepler is studying.</note>
*Which he himself had predicted; we shall oppose him with a hundred*
*Couplings of these wanderers* <note>original: "Erronum," a common term for the planets.</note> *that produced no comet at all:*
*If he speaks the truth, it is by chance, having already spoken a thousand lies.*

Therefore, unless the fourth opinion—that of the theologians—prevails, chance will claim for itself the coincidence of the star with the Great Conjunction.

<margin>II. That the star did not fall by chance into the assembly of the planets.</margin>

But I believe we must no less struggle against the opinion of Epicurus (for why should we not meet ironists with irony? <note>original: "πρὸς τοὺς εἴρωνας ἀντειρωνεύere." Kepler uses this Greek phrase to describe his rhetorical strategy of using the skeptics' own logic against them.</note>). This view, which relies on blind chance and the throwing of dice—perhaps more elegantly than Epicurus himself with his accidental wandering of atoms—nonetheless aims for the same goal: to remove wonder from the minds of men. When wonder is taken away, every fear, every sinister omen, and finally every report of a prodigy is extinguished. This is done so that the brief time of life given to man may be spent in cheerfulness and sweetness, with cares excluded; and the more certainly an evil looms, the more diligently each preceding day is snatched away, as if by stealth, from the cruelty that hangs over us, turning into a triumph for the soul in its bold contempt for its pressing and threatening enemy:

Let us live— <note>The text cuts off mid-word "Viva-" (Vivamus), leading into the next page.</note>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 139**

*Let us live, my Lesbia, and let us love,*
*and let us value the rumors of stern old men*
*at but a single penny.*
*Suns can set and return again:*
*for us, when once our brief light sets,*
*there is one perpetual night to be slept.*
<note>Kepler is quoting the Roman poet Catullus (Poem 5). In this context, he uses these famous lines to represent the Epicurean view that life is short and driven by chance, a view he is about to refute.</note>

But as for me, even if the reasons for this opinion <note>The "opinion" that the star appeared by mere chance or natural accident.</note> were completely binding, my very piety would still have spurred me to untie these knots. For first, it leads to a bad life; and if that life is also a cheerful one, it must be a wicked sort of cheerfulness. Therefore, the discipline that sanctions and protects this cheerfulness, and goads it on with its own flatteries <term>poppysmis</term> <note>original: "poppysmis." This Greek-derived Latin word refers to a stroking or clucking sound used to soothe horses; here, Kepler uses it to mean empty, soothing flattery.</note>, is wicked. Grant a holiness to life, and reason will be sober; even if it wanders from the truth, it will at least be harmless, and indeed even beneficial. For in this way, Christ the God consoles His people, removes the fear of portents, and commands that the anxiety for tomorrow—the kind that eats the heart—be far away. This is not because no evils threaten the nations, but because God intervenes intimately in particular events, stands as a protection for His own, and loves their cheerfulness—yet not that foolish kind which glories in itself, but that which glories in God. For perhaps tomorrow will turn that former glory into smoke; but this latter glory, no one shall take from them.

Next, I am moved by a respect for God. Everyone confesses that the minds of men have been shaken by the sight of this new thing shining in the sky. <margin>It is not safe to minimize this prodigy.</margin> If either chance or nature brought it about that the star was born, then nature also brought it about that it should snatch men away into admiration of itself.

Therefore, he who confesses that God is the author of the World—since he attributes the order of all nature and all its singular works to that same God—should surely also attribute this star, and the admiration of men which follows it, to Him. Indeed, such a man will not fear that God, being established as the author of this prodigy in this way, would be angry at this human admiration. But he who either denies God entirely with Diagoras <note>Diagoras of Melos (5th century BCE), known as "the Atheist."</note>, or makes the World itself God with the ancient Pythagoreans, or removes God's care for our affairs with Epicurus <note>Epicurus taught that the gods exist but are indifferent to human life.</note>—he will have much less reason to discourage men from this admiration of novelty.

Therefore, let it be certain that God is not offended by the celebration of such a great prodigy. But in turn, there is a great fear that perhaps God might be offended by the minimizing of it—the path toward which this opinion <note>The Epicurean view.</note> affects to lead by its "dice-rolls." <note>Kepler compares the theory of atoms colliding by chance to create the star to a game of dice (*tesserarum jactus*).</note> I have not descended from the council of the Gods, nor do I affirm anything as certain; but nevertheless, what if it was not the nature of things that brought it about that this star was born? <meta>This page continues Kepler's philosophical defense of the new star as a divine sign. He is moving from a general theological argument to a specific logical critique of "chance" (Epicurean randomness), arguing that even an infinite amount of time does not guarantee a meaningful event will occur by accident.</meta>

140 JOHANNES KEPLER

If God, by design, out of the ordinary course of nature, and by a specific plan, set this miracle before the eyes of men, did He seek this stunned amazement of theirs on purpose? Namely, so that He might warn them? What then should I say to Him, if I were to diminish what He Himself wanted to appear great—that is, if I were to attribute to chance those things in this star that are to be admired? For although in the previous few chapters I have worked to find a way in which Nature itself might produce stars of this kind, the matter nevertheless returns to God, the author of nature. Even if the rising of the star is attributed to the ordinary patterns of nature, there still remains a place for God in the arrangement of the circumstances of place and time.

<margin>Definition of chance.</margin>
But what is chance? Truly, it is a most detestable idol, and nothing other than an insult to the highest and almighty God, and to the most perfect World which He created. For this idol of chance, blind and rash motion takes the place of a soul, and infinite chaos takes the place of a body. To it are wickedly attributed eternity, omnipotence, and the creation of the World—properties that belong to God alone.

Come then, let us test the reasoning of this opinion. 
<margin>What chance can do.</margin>
Let us grant it eternity, and whatever else it requires for its throws of the dice. Will it therefore have brought about all things? I will not argue here that nothing animate can be made from the inanimate by chance, nor that like cannot possibly be born from like by chance; for these points have nothing to do with our star. I will take those things in which chance is understood to dominate most: geometric figures, innumerable numbers, and many-sided projectiles. This opinion <note>The Epicurean view that the universe is formed by the random collision of atoms over infinite time.</note> says, therefore, that if infinite throws are made from eternity, it is necessary that all chances which are possible must happen.

But I say that this is false. For if fortuitous chance dominates among the throws and among the ways the dice fall, then certainly in any single throw all things could fall; and thus also those things which had fallen before. If those previous results fall again, then others will not fall—specifically, those which have not yet fallen. Thus it can happen, even with infinite throws, that some things which are possible to fall may not fall; therefore, it is not necessary that all things fall. Thus, even if chance is admitted, it will never be certain whether this star fell into the place of the Great Conjunction by chance. For chance could have provided this, and it could also not have provided it. We are left in uncertainty. 

Although Quintus Cicero, in Book 1 of *On Divination*, denies that it can ever happen that chance perfectly expresses the truth. Once in my youth, when I had leisure in abundance, I delighted in that vanity of which many older men are not ashamed—the kind they attribute to the poet Lycophron of Chalcis <note>Lycophron was an ancient Greek poet famous for his wordplay and obscure anagrams.</note>. By rearranging the Greek letters of my name in various ways, I finally produced a definite sentence, so that instead of *Ioannes Keplerus* <note>original: Ιωάννης Κεπλῆρος</note>, it read "Huckster of Sirens" <note>original: Σειρήνων κάπηλος (Seirēnōn kapēlos). Kepler is poking fun at his own role as a "seller" of celestial harmonies or perhaps his astrological work.</note>. And in Latin, *Joannes Keplerus* sounds, through an anagram, like "A serpent in the sting" <note>original: "Serpens in akuleo." This is a pun on the new star appearing in the foot of Ophiuchus, the Serpent-bearer, which Kepler notes is near a "sting-like" part of the constellation.</note>. But since the meaning did not please me, and I desired another, yet it did not succe— <note>The text cuts off mid-word "succederet" (succeed).</note>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s critique of Epicurean chance. He describes a personal experiment with random letters and a conversation with his wife to illustrate why the order found in the new star cannot be accidental.</meta>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN OPHIUCHUS. 141**

<note>original: serpent-bearer, the constellation Ophiuchus</note>

it might succeed: I committed the matter to fortune. I took playing cards and inscribed each one with a single letter, matching the number of letters in a sentence. Then I began to shuffle the cards and look at their sequence again and again, to see if any meaning [fleuron in right margin] would eventually emerge. May all the Epicurean gods and goddesses destroy this "chance," which was never able to show me anything, even from a distance, that hinted at a coherent thought, despite stealing a great deal of my time. And yet, it was among the possible outcomes that the sentence I had devised might come forth: *Retailer of Sirens*. <note>original Greek: Σειρήνων κάπηλος (Seirēnōn kapēlos). This likely refers to a deceptive merchant of seductive but empty ideas.</note> Therefore, I handed my cards over to the Epicurean "Eternity" to be carried away into the "Infinite"; <note>Kepler personifies the Epicurean concept of *Apeiron* (the infinite void) and *Aeternio* (eternity) as places where random atoms wander.</note> and they say that those cards are still fluttering there among the atoms in the greatest confusion, having yet to find any certain meaning.

I will tell my intellectual sparring partners <note>original Greek: γυμνασταῖς (gymnastais), referring to those who "exercise" the mind through debate.</note> an opinion that is not my own, but my wife’s. I do this because, led by their own arguments, I have just now conceded that order *could* be produced by chance—an idea which does not seem right to me. Thus, they may teach me how to defend the opinion they have supplied against this troublesome and serious adversary. Yesterday, when I was exhausted from writing, my mind still dusty from considering those atoms, I was called to dinner. She served me a salad, the very thing I had been thinking of. "So," I said, "if tin plates, lettuce leaves, grains of salt, drops of water, vinegar, and oil, and slices of eggs had been flying through the air for all eternity, would it eventually happen that such a salad would come together by accident?" My beautiful wife responded: "But not with this beauty, nor in this order."

Wretched me! What could I bring forward in defense of my opinion? She seemed to speak the truth. Doubtless, she learned this from Cicero himself. For even he grants to chance some shape not unlike a real one, but certainly not such a shape that you would say it was crafted by Scopas. <note>Scopas was a famous ancient Greek sculptor known for the emotional intensity of his work.</note> The mind has great confidence wherever order appears; the cause of this must be sought from the deepest springs of Geometry. Even if some order were produced by chance, minds would immediately flock to it—this is their delight, this is their life. While they remain sluggish toward a thousand formless accidents, they immediately wake up and stay vigilant for a single ordered one. Therefore, not even if a star fell into the assembly of Planets by chance does it follow that this achieves nothing. On the contrary, all of nature in the world, and whatever is mental anywhere—whether supported by the movement of reason or by eternal instinct—is violently moved by it. Only those philosophers who prefer confusion over order, who have cast this most beautiful world away among unpleasant atoms, have earned this: that they are neither moved by the beauty and harmony of this "chance," as they call it, nor do they allow others to take delight in it.

But we were not doing this to praise the beauty of chance,

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s philosophical rebuttal of the Epicurean view that the universe and the new star were formed by a random collision of atoms. He uses the mathematics of probability and the physics of dice-throwing to argue that "chance" is often just a name for causes we do not yet understand.</meta>

142 JOHANNES KEPLER

[Decorative ornament]

...but rather to overturn the concept of chance itself. Therefore, as I have said, even if we grant the existence of eternity, it is by no means certain that everything that *can* happen *will* happen. This becomes most obvious when the "projectiles" used by these cosmic gamblers are multiplied. With ordinary dice, it is not surprising that every possible combination falls relatively quickly; there are usually only two dice, each with six sides, meaning there are no more than twenty-one possible combinations. <note>In the gambling of Kepler's time, with two six-sided dice, there are 21 unique combinations (e.g., a 1 and a 2 is the same combination as a 2 and a 1).</note> However, when more "projectiles" are added, the variety of outcomes increases immensely. This is especially true in the consideration of natural things, where even time itself—which one might wish to invoke to provide the space for everything to happen—actually increases the number of "projectiles" or variables. Thus, one can never grant enough time for all things to happen by accident; all of eternity is too short for the multitude of possible outcomes. Because the longer eternity lasts, the more these possibilities increase by infinite turns: *Toil brings toil upon toil.* <note>original Greek: Πόνῳ πόνῳ πόνον φέρει (Ponō ponō ponon pherei). This is a quote from Sophocles' "Ajax," suggesting a never-ending cycle of fruitless effort.</note> See what those people do who attribute orderly events to chance: with only a few principles, they achieve nothing—just as in my example, a bowl and lettuce alone do not make a salad. <note>Kepler refers to an anecdote from the previous page where he told his wife that her salad could not have formed by atoms falling randomly into a bowl.</note> But if they multiply their principles, they dodge the very power of their "Eternity," because the infinite multitude of possible accidents outruns eternity itself.

<margin>Even the fall of dice is governed by its own causes.</margin>

Furthermore, if that "chance"—by whose power they think all beautiful and orderly things are made—is compared to a roll of the dice, then those who think these rolls are purely fortuitous (that is, *without cause*) <note>original Greek: ἀναιτίους (anaitious), meaning "causeless" or "without reason."</note> are short-sighted. If they strip their "chance" of every cause, they have yet to find an example of it in the dice. Why did the "Venus" <note><term>Venus</term>: The highest, most favorable throw in ancient Roman dice games.</note> fall in this toss, and the "Dog" <note><term>Dog</term>: Latin *canis*, the lowest or most unlucky throw in dice.</note> in that one? Surely it is because the player gripped the die differently this time, or held it in his hand another way, or shook it with a different motion, or threw it with a different impulse of mind or hand, or the air blew through it differently, or it struck a different spot on the table. Nothing here lacked its own specific cause, if only someone were able to track such subtle details. In this same way, if our stars also leave behind their own causes in nature—explaining why they shone at this specific time and in this specific place, even if those causes are separate from the meeting of the Planets—then these thinkers have already deserted Epicurus for Aristotle. <note>Kepler argues that if you admit there is a physical "cause" for why a star appears, you are no longer an Epicurean (who believes in random atoms) but an Aristotelian (who seeks natural, teleological, or material causes).</note> Therefore, they must be dealt with differently.

<margin>Whether material necessity joined the star to the Planets.</margin>

Up to this point, I have been refuting that part of the "third opinion" which defined chance according to the view of Epicurus. As for the other part, which describes chance not as a lack of all causes, but as a "coincidence of events": indeed, such things were debated in several previous chapters. If we assume the star is a work of nature, I would have subscribed to that opinion in this instance without exception, provided that this new star had only occupied the *time* of the Great Conjunction. <note>The "Great Conjunction" was the rare alignment of Jupiter, Saturn, and Mars in 1604, which occurred exactly when and where this new star appeared.</note> But because it simultaneously occupied the same *place* in our field of vision, I am forced to leave the matter in suspense, as to whether...

<summary>Kepler argues that "chance" is mathematically improbable for creating complex order and suggests that even "random" events like dice rolls have physical causes. He notes that the new star's appearance exactly during and at the location of a rare planetary alignment makes the "pure coincidence" theory difficult to accept.</summary>
<keywords>Epicurus, Aristotle, probability, dice, Venus throw, Dog throw, Great Conjunction, causality, Ophiuchus</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s examination of the origins of the new star of 1604. Having rejected the Epicurean idea of pure chance on the previous page, he now weighs different philosophical and theological explanations for why the star appeared at the exact time and place of a significant planetary conjunction.</meta>

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER 143<-

...whether the nature of the outermost heaven <note>The "outermost heaven" refers to the sphere of the fixed stars in the Aristotelian-Ptolemaic model of the universe.</note> was itself so arranged that it would produce a new star at this particular time and place; or whether, alongside those conjectures which favor the second opinion, we must also investigate a certain cause for the coincidence itself. For the third opinion deduced individual things separately from their own causes: it took the meeting of the planets from Astronomy and the origin of the star from Physics. The second opinion, however, claimed that this very meeting of these diverse things in one place and time was due to some new governing cause. And because the third opinion fails to find a specific cause for why a new star should arise in this place, the 18th degree of Sagittarius, and why it should arise at this time—namely, October 10, 1604—even if some cause might perhaps exist, it remains hidden from us. I am therefore more inclined toward the second opinion. I say we must inquire by what reasoning it happened that this meeting of the planets, which occurred at the same place and time, established this star for us? This is especially so since it could not have happened through the ordinary path of nature (as was said in the refutation of the first opinion). The fourth opinion, meanwhile, provides a *God from the machine* <note>original Greek: Θεὸν ἀπὸ μηχανῆς (*Theon apo mēchanēs*). This refers to the "Deus ex machina," a theatrical device where a god is lowered onto the stage to resolve a seemingly unsolvable problem.</note>; but we should only resort to that when all natural explanations have failed us.

A very sharp conflict between these three opinions thus arises. First, it is doubted whether this coincidence of the new star with the conjunction of the planets is a mere consequence or something intentionally sought after. And because it is likely to be something sought after, it is further doubted: did Nature seek this, or did God himself do so beyond the ordinary order? But then again, because it is difficult to believe that Nature alone has such power, and yet it seems rash to attribute these things to God—because in doing so we would be dragging Him into the laws of Astrology—the mind, placed in an impassable blind alley, returns to the original opinion. That view asserted that this coincidence was not brought about by design, but followed from material necessity. 

<margin>Should this conjunction be attributed to God, or to a deliberating Nature?</margin> We have indeed said much in the preceding pages about the natural causes by which it is consistent to think this star was produced. Whatever those causes may be, the theologians bid us to be careful not to attribute too much to them. And there seem to be arguments to persuade us that it is a higher task to provide that a new star should fall during a meeting of the planets than can be attributed to "Nature"—that is, to that spirit of the whole universe. For first, it would be necessary for this spirit to be most powerful, as one who sits at the helm of celestial nature and can moderate its force in such a way that it brings forth such a part at the desired place and time. Secondly, the most extensive knowledge would be required: namely, to foresee which meetings of the planets, and of what kind, would appear from this Earth of ours, and according to this foresight, to exercise its right over the outermost heaven. Who, then...

<summary>Kepler weighs three theories on the star's origin, debating whether its coincidence with a planetary conjunction was random, a natural law of the "universal spirit," or a deliberate act of God.</summary>
<keywords>1604 Supernova, Sagittarius, planetary conjunction, Providence vs Nature, spirit of the universe, astronomical causes</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s theological and philosophical deduction regarding the New Star of 1604. Having argued that the star’s appearance at the exact time and place of a rare planetary conjunction suggests a deliberate design, he now identifies the author of that design as God and discusses the purpose of the spectacle for humanity.</meta>

144 JOHANNES KEPLER

Who then is He, whose knowledge encompasses the Earth and whose power encompasses the outermost heaven? <note>The "outermost heaven" refers to the sphere of the fixed stars, the furthest boundary of the universe in the cosmology of Kepler's time.</note> Finally, the end toward which this providence strives proves Him to be exceedingly good and a lover of mankind <note>original Greek: φιλάνθρωπον (*philanthrōpon*)</note>. For which creature do you suppose this spectacle was intended? For some heavenly creature? But it was said in the refutation of the first opinion that nowhere in the World, except from the Earth alone, was the star seen associated with the Planets. <note>Kepler’s point is that the "New Star" appeared to be in the same spot as the planets only when viewed from Earth; from any other vantage point in the universe, they would not have appeared aligned.</note> Is it then that this spectacle was intended for some other earthly creature besides man? 
By these arguments, therefore, it seems proven: if this harmony of place and time was sought after, it can be attributed to no one but GOD alone.

On the other hand, those speculative Physicists do not bring this into dispute—that God is the highest and primary cause of all things; but they contend only this: that for the production of this star, He used an ordinary means. They argue that God does not therefore act through that Spirit of His, which moves through the entire World, even if they especially [attribute] to it this knowledge <note>The text cuts off here, but Kepler is beginning to address the "Physicists'" view that God works through natural laws—the "Soul of the World"—rather than through a direct, supernatural miracle.</note>

<summary>Kepler argues that because the new star’s alignment with the planets was only visible from Earth, it must have been a divine sign specifically intended by God for humanity. He then begins to contrast this with the views of "speculative physicists" who seek natural, rather than miraculous, explanations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s examination of the "World Spirit" (Anima Mundi) as a possible secondary cause for the New Star. He has just introduced the idea that while God is the ultimate cause, some philosophers argue that a universal Nature or Spirit, which permeates the cosmos, might have been the immediate agent responsible for manifesting the star during the Great Conjunction.</meta>

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 145<-

of God. Nor do I fear that I shall be refuted by the fact that I provide an occasion for today’s philosophers <note>By "today's philosophers," Kepler likely refers to those influenced by Neoplatonism or Paracelsianism, who might blur the line between the Creator and the created World Soul.</note> to finally make a God out of this faculty, which is a part of the World. Let those men philosophize even unto delirium—those whose predecessors ascribed divine honors to what things? To air, water, the Ocean, the heavens, the visible stars? While in the meantime, some even believed these things to be inanimate. It is certain to Christians that there are innumerable hosts of blessed spirits, who are free from every worldly body, admirable in holiness, and far more excellent in dignity than this spirit which presides over the worldly body (to which it is bound by a certain bond of nature). Among these spirits, there are some higher by distinctions of rank: most glorious princedoms, most powerful potencies. And yet, above all these, they acknowledge a God more sublime, the creator of both Himself and all those others.
I read thus in Prudentius, and I think it ought to be emended:
      *It was ordered by the mouth of Caesar Gallienus:*
      *What the prince worships, let us all worship:*
      I WORSHIP THE ETERNAL PRINCE OF THE GODS,
      *The Creator and Lord of Gallienus.*
<note>original: *ÆTERNUM COLO PRINCIPEM DEORUM, Fictorem Dominumq́; Gallieni.* Kepler is quoting Aurelius Prudentius Clemens, a 4th-century Christian poet. The verse depicts a Christian martyr defying the Emperor Gallienus by asserting that God is the "Prince of Gods" and the one who actually "fashioned" or created the Emperor himself.</note>

He confesses, along with Augustine, that the title of "Gods" is attributed in sacred literature even to the holy Angels; but he acknowledges one eternal and uncreated prince of those created ones, whom Scripture also calls the "God of gods" (for why he should especially celebrate the "Maker of days" <note>original: *dierum factorem*. Kepler notes a potential textual corruption in the copies of Prudentius he was reading, where "days" (*dierum*) might have been mistakenly written for "gods" (*deorum*).</note> here, no reason appears). Nor does he wish to worship what the prince fashions; but rather the highest Prince—him, namely, who was not fashioned by Gallienus, but who fashioned Gallienus; not He who is commanded to us by the order of Lord Gallienus, but He who is the Lord of Lord Gallienus himself.

Someone might, therefore, bring forward these points to defend the cause of Nature; vindicating the coupling of this new star with the Great Conjunction by attributing it to that Spirit which permeates the fields of the entire World.

But so help me Christ, even after examining all these reasonings, I do not satisfy myself. Regarding [Nature's] power and goodness, I am somewhat satisfied; but regarding its knowledge, it is wonderful how much is lacking. For grant that there is within Nature a sense of the conjunction of the Planets: shall we then also attribute to it a knowledge of those things which men have written about the conjunctions of Planets in innumerable books? Shall we also accept this: that Nature wished to speak with us men by this star, as if showing us a written character? Or if this is absurd, tell me then, you physical speculator <note>term: <term>conjectator phyfice</term> (physical speculator) refers to a natural philosopher who attempts to explain celestial phenomena through the laws of physics or natural logic rather than pure theology.</note>, for whose benefit did Nature fit a star in the highest ether to those things which were seen not in the highest...

<summary>Kepler distinguishes his concept of a "World Spirit" from pagan pantheism by subordinating it to the Christian God and his angelic hierarchy. However, he remains skeptical that such a natural spirit could possess the complex astronomical knowledge required to intentionally time a new star with a planetary conjunction for human benefit.</summary>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s philosophical debate regarding whether the New Star was produced by a "World Spirit" (Nature) reacting to the Great Conjunction of planets, or by the direct, providential hand of God. Kepler is wrestling with the idea of whether Nature possesses a form of "reason" that allows it to understand astrological symbols.</meta>

->146 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

in the highest and vastest ether, but on this most humble and narrow Earth? Whoever affirms that it was a destined work—the meeting of the star with the joined Planets, at least in such a way that the joined Planets were the object, moving the hidden sense of Nature—that person will not be able to explain it otherwise than by attributing to that same Nature both an understanding of our astrological writings and a commerce with the hidden language of figures. But in attributing these things, he attributes a discourse of reasoning to it; he would thus make it a rational spirit. Yet, this opinion previously protested that it claimed for Nature a sense of Astronomy not acquired by discourse, but instilled by the law of creation.

<note>Kepler is arguing that if Nature "reacts" to a planetary conjunction by creating a new star, Nature would have to "understand" astrology like a human does. He finds it problematic to grant Nature (the World Soul) human-like reasoning (discourse), suggesting instead it must be an innate instinct or, more likely, direct Divine intervention.</note>

      Whatever, therefore, physical conjecture regarding the machinery of the world may produce from the storehouse of its own discipline: there is still a need for God as the architect, by whose counsel the star should come into the council of the Planets.

      Even granted that what conjecture seeks is true—that Nature is sufficient for this adaptation—who will deny that the moderation of the whole matter nevertheless rests with God himself, who uses Nature, which He established, as a minister? Is there not, in the infinite things happening daily, each from its own natural cause, a place still left for divine providence? This is true in other things, but especially in the exercise of vengeance for crimes; by which it is brought about that what happens is consistent not only with the preceding causes in nature, but also with divine justice. Why was the Jewish Commonwealth <note>original: *Politia Judaica*</note> destroyed by the Romans with such horrendous slaughter, and the city of Jerusalem buried? If you ask Josephus, the writer of *The Jewish War*, he responds to you that the Jews were dragged into the war by the Romans against their will. He tells the truth, if you look at the order of nature; the cause of this event rises from a twin series of circumstances. 

      For on the part of the Romans, there was the license of domination, the insulting nature of injuries, the pride of the Caesars, the peak of the empire, and the ancient custom born with paganism itself of deifying men and dedicating statues, altars, temples, and priests. Add to this the consciousness of their arms, the desire for glory, and the most wicked habit of provoking neighbors, or even subjects, so that the legions would have something to practice on and the Emperors would have material for a triumph. 

      On the part of the Jews, there was trust in God, the author of their laws, a numerous nation, a most fortified city, a most holy temple, the antiquity of the race, stubbornness in defending the rituals and religion of the temple, old and new prophecies, and False Prophets. He who carefully considers both sides and compares them together will confess that it happened by the usual order of things that Jerusalem was overturned by the Romans. 

      But behold God, the avenger of the most innocent death of Christ God, mixed into the middle of the causes of individual things, fitting and colliding the warring customs of the nations; which previously, if not—

<note>Kepler uses the historical example of the fall of Jerusalem (70 AD) to explain his scientific worldview: events have "natural" causes (political tensions, cultural traits), but God coordinates these natural causes to fulfill a higher "Divine" purpose. He applies this same logic to the New Star appearing during the planetary conjunction.</note>

<summary>Kepler argues that while the New Star and historical events like the fall of Jerusalem have natural causes, they are ultimately orchestrated by God's providence. He rejects the idea that Nature has its own "reasoning" power, viewing it instead as a servant to the Divine Architect.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Providence, World Soul, Nature, Great Conjunction, Jerusalem, Josephus, Roman Empire, Jewish War, Astrology, Rational Spirit</keywords>

if they did not rest: yet they lived more mildly. So that He might exercise this vengeance without a great disturbance of natural causes, He waited for the opportunity for forty whole years: and lest it should seem accidental, He predicted forty years beforehand that He would avenge the nation in this way. In the same manner here, however Nature may have brought forth this star, the sum of the matter finally returns to God.

      Though, what need is there for these subtleties? Or what sin is it, if we say God did what Nature made? Have not the holy Prophets of GOD taught us this manner of speaking? What is there of natural things, whether in heaven or on earth, that is not referred to God Himself? Seed, a liquid and white substance, after it falls into the womb: by the formative faculty of the same, which we know to be natural and perpetual, it is curdled: and thus finally an animal is formed. But what does the divine Job say of his own origin? *Did you not milk me like milk, and curdle me like cheese?* <note>original: "Nonne sicut lac mulsisti me, & sicut caseum me coagulasti?" — Job 10:10. Kepler uses this biblical reference to show that even natural biological processes are traditionally attributed to God's direct handiwork.</note> He says that, not to his mother, but to God, the author of both himself and his mother and of all nature. Truly, he does not think that nature deserves to be the subject of much wondering contemplation; rather, he marvels at Him *who spoke and they were made, who commanded and they were created.* <note>Psalm 33:9</note>

      So that I may at last end this so anxious examination of opinions; since the theological sentiment has absorbed the physical conjecture and acquired it all for itself: only two opinions remain. For it must either be said that the place and time of the new star have no commerce with the "Great Conjunction" <term>Great Conjunction|The rare alignment of the planets Saturn and Jupiter. Kepler believed these alignments marked significant historical shifts.</term> of the Planets, but happened by chance (in a sober sense) that the star and the coupling of Planets concurred, each arising from its own cause: Or because we cannot with any probability attribute the place (in the 18th degree of Sagittarius) and the time (October 10, 1601) to an unapparent cause—when the apparent conjunction of the Planets is right at hand, offering itself as the cause—it must therefore be said this other thing, which I pronounce most safely and with full confidence: this new celestial prodigy was associated by the almighty GOD himself with the three Planets, Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars, then joined together, by a certain counsel, directed toward the salvation of men.

      For this is that God, to whom nothing in the World is either large or small, since He alone is the author of all things: who nevertheless prefers the human race, living in these narrow confines of a most contemptible clod of earth, yet bearing His image, to any star—even if that star exceeded the magnitude of the whole world a hundred thousand times. Who, so that he might mark the place and time of the great conjunction of the three superiors <term>three superiors|The planets Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars, which are further from the Sun than Earth.</term> with this, as it were, monument for the perpetual memory of the matter, and to warn the human

might mark human affairs regarding the greatest matters. He required no anxiety, no labor, and no fatigue to create such a thing that could be seen by the inhabitants of the earth in the form of so great a star. Regarding this matter, whether He used Nature as a handmaid, or whether He exerted this as a ray of His extraordinary omnipotence; in either case, this remains true: *He spoke, and they were made; He commanded, and they were created.* <note>original: "Ipse dixit, & facta sunt; ipse mandavit, & creata sunt." — Psalm 33:9.</note> For if He says to Nature, "Beget," Nature—previously dead to begetting—receives the <term>animating faculty</term> <note>original: "facultatem animalem." If I were certain that this wish of mine were ratified by Him, I would not doubt that by His grace I could achieve this very thing, however much I am already like a child in my perception and ignorant of such great mysteries. Thus, I might freely cry out with the Royal Psalmist: *I shall not die, but live, and declare the works of the Lord.* <note>original: "Non moriar, sed vivam, & narrabo opera Domini." — Psalm 118:17.</note> But if He does not look upon me here, nor value that which remains of this labor (which shall come forth at another time, in another place, and—unless my hopes deceive me—at these very same <term>Frankfurt fairs</term> <note>original: "nundinis Francofurto." <meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle to Johannes Friedrich Hoffmann. The "Star of the Swan" refers to the supernova (or nova) of 1600 (P Cygni).</meta>

ON THE STAR OF THE SWAN. 151

[...you supported] me; you instructed me in this mournful event with most prudent words; finally, you provided an **azimuthal Quadrant** and a **Sextant**—the latter made of iron, the former of brass—at your own expense, fashioned after the image of **Brahe’s** instruments, and acquired at no small cost. <note>Tycho Brahe’s instruments were famous for their size and precision. After his death, Kepler struggled to maintain access to them due to disputes with Tycho's heirs; Hoffmann clearly stepped in to provide alternatives.</note> These instruments, after Brahe had died and shortly before we had heard about the star, I possessed for my own use by your **Grace's** permission. <term>Grace</term> <note>original: *G. Tua* for *Generositas Tua*, a formal title for a nobleman of Hoffmann's rank.</note>

Therefore, what I had observed in this star with these instruments of yours, and what needed to be said about it by an **Astronomer**, I had written down that year and shared with a few people; I was thinking of publishing it and dedicating it to your **Name**—a promise you remember I made in person. Not because I hoped that some splendor would be added to your **Illustrious Name** from this very thin piece of writing, but because I thought it most greatly concerned my reputation to publish a public proof of my gratitude toward your **Grace** at the earliest possible moment. For if I were to survey the whole sequence of my fortunes and those of my family in my mind: no part of it, no detail occurred that your **Generosity** alone had not most strongly connected and secured. And to not use many words on a short page: I could then, with some right, be carried away magnificently and exclaim with Virgil’s **Aeolus**:
<note>original: Kepler quotes the *Aeneid* (1.78-80), where Aeolus, god of the winds, thanks Queen Juno for his power and status.</note>
      *You win for me whatever kingdom this is, you win me the scepter and Jove's favor;*
      *you grant me a place at the banquets of the Gods,*
      *and you make me master over the clouds and storms.*

Since these things were so, there was nothing I would rather do than dedicate to your **Name** that little book, the first to be born from me since the death of **Tycho**; so that for those to whom the fruit of your **Foresight** in helping the cause of **Astronomy** should reach, the praises of them might in turn redound to your **Illustrious Name**.

What, then, was the reason why that which had been intended was interrupted? What sufficiently plausible excuse can I offer you?

To be sure, I wanted to wait for the end of the new phenomenon; and meanwhile, I wrote to the leading **Mathematicians** to collect their opinions. However, since the star was not extinguished, nor did the **Mathematicians** respond with the promptness I hoped for, time meanwhile slipped away; until after two years and more, that huge new star, about which in this bo... <note>The text cuts off here, likely referring to the "New Star" of 1604, which is the primary subject of this book.</note>

the star I have discussed in this booklet shone forth in the foot of the **Serpent-bearer** <note>Ophiuchus. It was "new" but less spectacular than the 1604 supernova.</note>—whose novelty was not so openly acknowledged—should yield the first place in the book and its chapters to the one in the Serpent-bearer, just as it was surpassed by it in brightness. If the star in the Swan had anything unique or worthy of knowledge, its explanation would find its place at the very end of the book.
      Even though, therefore, this writing has become meager in this manner, and is almost unworthy of being separated from the rest of the book by a special inscription, nevertheless, your merits toward me have not been rendered less valuable after they have now for some years most joyfully borne fruit for me; nor can a word once spoken by me be recalled or made unsaid, and much less ought it to vanish as if it had never happened.
      Receive therefore, **Illustrious BARON**, you who are most well-versed in all the secrets of Nature, receive, I say, from me this sign of my most just goodwill toward you. Do not judge it with your eyes by the number of pages, but rather by that intricate <note>original: *daedaleâ*, referring to the mythical craftsman Daedalus; meaning skillfully or complexly wrought.</note> stirring of more secret intelligence by which you raise yourself sublime through the inscrutable treasures of divine wisdom; estimate it from the weight of the matter itself of which I treat.
      For it must be proved by **Mathematicians**, shown to **Philosophers**, and noted for the memory of all posterity: whether at this time there is some star in the sky which, though it was not there formerly, has now for at least six years clung there immobile, immutable, and inextinguishable. This is so that—whether it is destined to perish before the consummation of the whole World, or whether it will endure forever with the rest of the fixed stars—men may understand the magnitude of the miracle that lasted so many years. As long as it remains, those to whom the **New Star** is proved by the following arguments should always have before their eyes this most evident proof that the whole **World was created in time** <note>Kepler argues that if the heavens can change (by adding a star), they are not eternal and unchanging as Aristotle claimed, which supports the religious view of a Universe created at a specific moment.</note>. As for those who, because of the star's long duration, stubbornly persist in arguing against the traditions of the **Astronomers** and deny it was recently born: let them not be ignorant of the weight of authority by which they are pressed.
      And this is to be performed by me all the more because **Mathematicians** everywhere look toward the **Brahean observations** <note>The data collected by Tycho Brahe, which was the most accurate in the world at the time.</note>, which they know I have the opportunity to inspect. From these, surely, such arguments are drawn that more weighty evidence in this field could not be hoped for from any other source.

<note>A decorative initial letter A begins the text.</note> **A**T the beginning, since never before this has astronomy been so widely cultivated in the academies; never were there more mariners, or those more observant of the sky, than there are today: yet there is no one in all Europe, indeed in all the world (which today they wander through by strolling as if in private gardens) who has been able to refute the widespread rumor about the novelty of this star from his own experience until now.

154 JOHANNES KEPLER

...given <note>The Latin word *ditus* is the end of *deditus*, meaning 'devoted' or 'given over to.'</note>, I inspected the sky less frequently with care, except for parts of the Zodiac, being mostly occupied with calculations, since Brahe had an abundance of observations and great diligence in observing.

Now that Tycho is dead, this concern indeed took hold of me, lest perhaps something new should emerge in the sky without my knowledge: therefore I looked more frequently at the stars, and at this very Swan. Its appearance and configuration with the others remained fixed in my mind, so that there was no need to compare a celestial globe with the sky. Yet that memory was not so clear that one star, especially at the breast, might not have been added even then without my noticing—a star which had not been there previously while Tycho was alive. Thus, I seemed to see nothing new in that constellation: although I saw this very star of which we speak at that very time, namely in November of the year 1601, as my memory dictates to me without the slightest hesitation. 

For when at that time after sunset the constellation was located in the western part of the sky, stretching its beak toward the horizon, its tail upward, and its wings to the right and left: Indeed, I was thinking that among the stars of the Swan, if some Christian were to begin anew to imagine figures, he would find a most suitable image of the **crucified one**, with head inclined. For the star about which we have doubts was then a little to my right, representing the breast of the crucified; while the one in the breast of the Swan turned to the left, yielding to the head of the crucified. This imagination had preserved the memory of this star for me until now; so that later, when in the month of May of the year 1602 I was first informed by letter of the "new Swan phenomenon," I exclaimed that I had seen nothing new from the previous months. 

For the image of the Cross—known even to farmers, as Bayer <note>Johann Bayer (1572–1625), a German lawyer and uranographer famous for his star atlas, the *Uranometria*.</note> reminds us—I knew I had conceived in my mind long ago; but as for the "inclination of the head" <note>Kepler is using the specific geometry of the stars to justify why he remembered the constellation's shape changing.</note> from the month of November; whether I had seen it formerly as well, I did not remember, nor do I remember now. Thus here I am forsaken by my own memory, as I said: nor from memory alone would I affirm that this is a new star.

What I have said of myself, I can say of almost all modern Professors of Mathematics who are distributed everywhere through the Academies of Europe: who all raise the question of the new Phenomenon; they either suppress their opinion or admit they do not know. Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin, Kepler's former professor.</note> indeed, having confessed that he was in doubt, nevertheless leans toward the view that the longer he examines his memory, the more he thinks it was seen by him even formerly. But immediately he adds the arguments used by those who strive to make it an "ancient" star <note>That is, a permanent star that was simply overlooked previously.</note> through debate; namely, that it has lasted so many years; and that Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), whose *Almagest* was the standard star catalog for over a millennium.</note> omitted many fixed stars. Here, therefore, while he flees to arguments, he admits that he has nothing from his own memory to bring forth against us.

->**O N  T H E  S T A R  O F  T H E  S W A N.** [Page number] **155**<-

What shall I say of the household of Tycho Brahe, in which for twenty years many students of Astronomy lived, and were so practiced in the contemplation of the stars during the coldest nights, that some even departed this life for that very reason <note>Kepler refers to the extreme physical toll of long-term astronomical observation in the cold Danish nights at Tycho's observatory, Uraniborg.</note>. Is there then no one among those survivors who could strike the fame of this new Phenomenon from his own memory, if it were false? Therefore, weighing these matters, Beyer <note>Johann Bayer (1572–1625), author of the famous star atlas *Uranometria*.</note> says: *It is accepted by the silent consent of all as a New Star.*

Indeed, the arguments by which trust in its novelty can be established for one whom his own memory fails are these:

First, this star was omitted by Hipparchus, who dared a thing even <margin>**I.** Hipparchus's plan. Pliny book 2. chap. 26.</margin> <note>original: "Deo improbam"</note> offensive to God: to number the stars for posterity and to lay out the constellations according to a standard, having devised instruments through which he might mark the locations and magnitudes of each, so that from this it could easily be discerned not only whether they perished (mark, I pray, the plan of Hipparchus) or were born; but whether any moved or shifted at all; likewise whether they grew or diminished; leaving the sky as an inheritance to all, if anyone should be found who could grasp that method. <note>Kepler is quoting Pliny the Elder describing Hipparchus’s creation of the first comprehensive star catalog in the 2nd century BCE.</note>

Hipparchus, therefore, who described the fixed stars with this intent—so that it might be discerned if any were born—omitted this one near the breast of the Swan, both in his Catalog and in the second book of his *Commentary on Aratus*, in the <term>simultaneous risings and settings<note>original Greek: συνανατολαῖς κ̀ συγκαταδύσεσι. This refers to stars that rise or set at the same time as other celestial bodies.</note></term>. What else does he do but address us with a living voice—we to whom he left the sky as an inheritance—warning us that now at last, after so many centuries, that which he himself had begun to doubt in his own age is appearing: namely, that new and long-lasting stars can indeed be born in the highest ether.

Lest you think that the constellation of the Hen <note>An old name for the constellation Cygnus, the Swan.</note> <margin>Diligence.</margin> was carelessly surveyed and described by Hipparchus: here are the words from his first book on Aratus: *Again,* he says, *Aratus suffers from ignorance in the following parts, when he writes of the Hen:*

<note>original Greek: Ἀλλ’ ὁ μὲν ἠεροεὶς: τὰ δέ οἱ ἐπιτετρήχυνται / Ἄστρασιν: ἔti λίην μεγάλοις, ἀτὰρ ἐ μὲν ἀφαυροῖς.</note>
*But that one [the Swan] is misty; yet it is roughened with stars, some very large, but none faint.*

*For the Bird has many and splendid stars: indeed, that one which is in the tail is even very splendid, nearly equal to the splendid one in the Lyre.* <note>The "splendid one in the Lyre" is the star Vega.</note> Thus he writes there.

Since he begins the description of the constellation from that line which <margin>Order in the Swan.</margin> extends from the beak through the neck and body of the Swan <note>Latin: *Oloris*</note> to the tail, and recounts each star in this series in order; afterwards turning to the wings, and finally to the feet, as if they were joined to the sides of this principal line: how could he have passed over this Star of ours, occurring in almost that same line and in the same tract, before arriving from the neck to the breast—how, I say, could he have passed it by if it existed in that age? There is absolutely nothing that can be offered as a plausible excuse.

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Kepler is systematically addressing the historical evidence for the novelty of the star in Cygnus (P Cygni). 156          JOANNES KEPLER

<margin>**II.** Ptolemy’s revision.</margin> Next follows the Catalog <note>original: "Abacus"</note> of Ptolemy, which he received from Hipparchus and repeated himself, correcting it in several places and repositioning stars into more appropriate limbs of the Figures <note>The "Figures" refer to the traditional constellations as visual shapes.</note> invented by the ancients. If Hipparchus was "yawning" <note>Kepler uses the term "oscitans" to suggest Hipparchus might have been caught napping or being uncharacteristically careless.</note> regarding this star, why did Ptolemy also omit it? Why did he himself make no mention of it?

<margin>The clarity of the star.</margin> What cause could have been sufficient for such diligent craftsmen to omit a star of the third magnitude, located so near to the one in the chest? They certainly did not think that even stars of the fourth, fifth, or sixth magnitude—and even nebulous ones—should be omitted if they were useful for designing the outlines of the figure, as is seen in this very neck of the Swan <note>original: "Oloris"</note>.

<margin>Evidence of the position.</margin> Did it seem sufficient to them to use only one star to designate the chest? Not at all. For while one could suffice to designate the tail of Capricorn, they mark two: one bright, the other dimmer. In the tail of the Southern Fish, in the shoulder of Sagittarius, and in one horn of Aries, they place two stars. Finally, they sometimes omitted single stars situated in isolation, even if they were large enough, if they occurred far outside the boundaries of the constellations; but from pairs or trios joined together, because of the visual emphasis <note>original: "ἔμφασιν"</note>, I do not know if you will find any omitted. Therefore, for this very reason—because they are seen as a pair—if both had existed in the past, both would have been entered into the number.

Here, Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin (1550–1631), Kepler’s former professor and a renowned astronomer.</note> brings forward the little star seen above the middle of the three in the tail of the Great Bear; farmers call it the Rider or the Charioteer <note>original: "Equitatorem seu Aurigam"</note>, as if it were someone sitting upon the middle horse <note>This refers to the star Alcor, the faint companion to Mizar in the Big Dipper’s handle.</note>. Even though this star is of no negligible magnitude, it was omitted by Ptolemy and Hipparchus. I respond that the cause is manifest: namely, the excessive proximity of this Rider, so small, to its own horse, so large and bright. Because of this, it cannot be discerned except by those with the most perfect vision. There are other examples of this kind of omission. But this cause is of no relevance to this New Star; for although it is near the chest star, it is distant from it in such a way that vision is not confused at all. Not to mention that it is of nearly equal magnitude; thus, both are seen with equal ease.

<margin>Suitability of the image.</margin> But perhaps that which we call the New Star is "unformed" <note>original: "ἀμόρφωτος"; a term for stars near a constellation that are not included in the literal "connect-the-dots" figure of the animal.</note>, falling outside the configuration of the Swan? Far from it. For hardly anyone could paint the Swan through the accustomed traces of the stars without leaving a part of the chest, or at least the base of the neck, in that part of the sky where this star of which we speak is now seen. It is so near to the chest, and the neck of this Swan is so long, stretching from the star assigned to the chest all the way to the tip of the beak. Nor are the efforts of antiquity obscure in extending and twisting the figures of animals so that they might embrace as many notable stars as possible in a single configuration.

Who, therefore, will believe that a star so bright—nearly as bright as the one in the chest itself—positioned in such proximity and so conveniently (lying, as I said, slightly outside the line from the chest to the beak), would have been overlooked and excluded from the constellation's figure?

   Again, Ptolemy, in that chapter where he describes the path of the Milky Way by the tracks of the stars, places only one in the chest. He clearly makes no mention of this star, even though it falls within the Milky Way, and indeed within its most memorable part, where that circle in the constellation of the Swan <note>original: "Gallinæ," literally "the Hen," a common historical name for the constellation Cygnus.</note> splits into two paths and extends a branch toward the shoulder of the Serpent-bearer <note>original: "Ophiuchi," the constellation Ophiuchus.</note>. The strength of this argument will only become apparent once someone has diligently read that chapter and compared it with a celestial globe. <margin>**III.** Position in the Milky Way.</margin>

   From there, he says, *understand that after the Eagle, the Milky Way stretches toward the Swan; and the margin which turns toward the North and West ends at the bend, at the star which is in the southern shoulder of the Swan, and in the one which is below this in the same wing, and in the two above the southern foot. The margin which faces the East and South ends at the star which is at the tip of the southern expansion of the feathers, and it captures two stars outside the figure which are under the same wing and are distant about two degrees from this margin. And these parts of the Milky Way are a little denser around the wing.*

   The following parts are indeed attached to this zone, but they are exceedingly dense, as if beginning from a distinct origin. *They turn toward the ends of the other zone, yet a gap is left between these and those. And from the South, they reach that part of the zone of which we are now speaking, which is very thin at the point of contact; however, after the said gap, they begin their density from the bright star in the tail of the Swan, and from the cloudy nebula which is in the northern knee; afterward, returning somewhat as far as the star in the southern knee, they extend this density, vanishing more and more, as far as Cepheus, etc.* <margin>An error in the Basel edition: <note>original: "τῶν" (of the [plural]), instead of "τὸν" (the [singular])</note>, do not let it disturb you. The grammatical construction, the translation, and the line above refute it.</margin>

   And in the explanation of the other part of the zone, which makes the Milky Way double: From there, he says, *namely from the shoulder of the Serpent-bearer, this zone—besides being thin or rare—is also contracted into a narrow limit near the parts preceding the star in the beak of the Swan, so much so that it presents the appearance of an interruption. The rest of its part from the beak to the star in the chest of the Swan is both more widely spread and quite dense. And the star in the neck of the Swan is in the middle of the dense part; a certain small portion, though thin, turns toward the North and the star of the Chest, as far as the star in the shoulder of the right wing, and to the two contiguous stars at the extremity of the right foot. From which place, as was said before, there is a gap between this and the other zone, extending from the said stars of the Swan as far as the bright star in the Tail.*

                               V 3                      Behold...

<summary>Kepler uses Ptolemy's detailed description of the Milky Way's path through Cygnus to prove that the "new star" must be a recent phenomenon, as Ptolemy meticulously recorded much dimmer stars in that exact region but not this one.</summary>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>Kepler continues his argument from the previous page, using Ptolemy's meticulous records of the Milky Way to prove that the "new star" could not have existed in the 2nd century AD. He is now transitioning to contemporary evidence from Tycho Brahe.</meta>

158 JOHANNES KEPLER

Behold the incredible diligence of Ptolemy, as if he had even then foreseen the importance of this very passage. For Ptolemy did not think it sufficient to note all the notable stars that fall within the Milky Way; rather, he went so far in his diligence—by "most meticulous observation" <note>original: περιεργωτάτη παρατηρήσει (*periergōtatē paratērēsei*). Kepler highlights Ptolemy's use of this Greek term to emphasize the extreme precision of the ancient observations.</note>, as he himself says—that he described the borders of that milky circle using stars either falling on the margin or slightly distant from it, indicating the measure of their distance. If, therefore, this star had appeared in the Milky Way then just as it does today, and indeed so very close to its margin, do you believe it could have happened that Ptolemy would omit it? Unless perhaps those authors described the various spaces and intercepted stars of the Milky Way from a celestial globe rather than from the sky itself—an impossibility which these very words already cited argue against.

### IV.
<margin>**IV.** The authority of Brahe.</margin>
Fourthly, Tycho Brahe in our time took up again—more than twenty years ago—that ancient and most necessary labor of Hipparchus, which had been neglected for so many centuries: [to catalog] all the stars from the ancient...

<summary>Kepler argues that Ptolemy's meticulous mapping of the Milky Way's boundaries would have certainly included the new star had it been visible in antiquity. He then introduces Tycho Brahe as the modern successor to Hipparchus in the essential task of star mapping.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, Milky Way, star catalog, Tycho Brahe, Hipparchus, observational precision, celestial globe</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>Kepler continues his argument that the new star in the breast of Cygnus is indeed new, moving from ancient evidence (Ptolemy) to the modern records of his mentor, Tycho Brahe. He argues that if the star had been visible during Brahe’s extensive observations in Denmark, it would have been recorded.</meta>

**ON THE STAR OF THE SWAN. 159**

Whether for the sake of such certainty, or for some other reason, he would have taken the distances of the surrounding bright stars—of the Eagle <note>original: *Aquilae*</note>, the Lyre <note>original: *Lyrae*</note>, and the tail of the Swan—from the breast of the Swan. Never once does a mention of two stars occur, never of a second star in the breast, nor of a "brighter" one in the breast of the Swan; everywhere the naming of it is simple: "that which is in the breast." Yet, in distinguishing between two stars in the tail of Capricorn and similar cases, he is customarily diligent in using and emphasizing a mark based on greater or lesser brightness, or on a region of North or South, East or West. But if at that time, as there are now, there had been twin stars in the breast of nearly equal magnitude, would he not have confused himself by neglecting the distinction? Would not that very hesitation as to which was the true star of the breast have invited him to inspect Ptolemy's table?

Seventhly, how often did it happen that, because the body of the Swan does not set in Denmark, he observed the transit of these stars through the northern meridian, and measured their altitudes for the sake of [calculating] refractions? <margin>**VII. The advantage of a constellation that does not set.**</margin>
Yet there can be no more convenient position for noticing a New or superfluous star—especially if, like this one of ours, it shines with nearly the same brightness as the neighbors toward which the observer looks.
Therefore, with such a great opportunity of location, and with such frequent and hardly laborious viewing of it (since it was low on the horizon), and since the Braheans <note>The assistants and students of Tycho Brahe who worked at his observatory, Uraniborg.</note> recorded no superfluous star being seen, it is rightly believed that none existed then.

Come now, let us provide evidence for the several preceding arguments from the observation books themselves, by reviewing a few examples that occurred to me while leafing through the Protocol <note>The original logs or "Protocols" of Tycho Brahe's observations.</note>. When these Observations are one day published in full, it will be possible to see much more. <margin>**From the Observations of Brahe.**</margin>
In the year 1582, January 12: the bright star of the left wing, and the tail of the Swan.
February 19: The tail of the Swan in the North. February 18, 23: The Vulture <note>original: *Vultur*. A common name for the star Altair or the constellation Aquila.</note> from the tail and the beak of the Swan. February 24: The same from the beak, the head, and the middle of the three stars in the wing of the Swan.
In the same [year]: *Between the Vulture and the unknown star, which makes a right angle with the tail of the Vulture and the head of the Swan.* You hear that they did not neglect to mention it if they were hesitant about some star. This one, however, is in the region of the Arrow <note>The constellation Sagitta.</note>.
February 26: The distance of the Vulture from the Breast of the Swan. March 1: The head, the middle of the right wing, the breast, the middle of the three outermost stars in the left wing of the Swan, and that unformed star <note>original: *stella illa informis*. "Unformed" stars were those not included within the traditional outline of a constellation figure.</note>. March 5: The head, the middle of the right wing, the breast of the Swan.
March 8: The distance of the Breast of the Swan from the head, from the Lyre, and from the head of the Serpent-bearer <note>The constellation Ophiuchus.</note>, and the distance of the latter from the head of the Swan. March 20: The head and the breast, and their mutual distance, and the distance from the right shoulder of the Serpent-bearer, and of the Lyre from the middle of the three outermost [stars]...

<meta>Kepler continues his meticulous review of Tycho Brahe's observation logs from the late 16th century. **O N  T H E  S T A R  O F  T H E  S W A N.**          161

Here the Ancients count 17 stars and 2 unformed stars <note>Stars not included in the main "connect-the-dots" figure of the constellation were categorized as "unformed."</note>. Brahe therefore omitted stars 7, 8, 9, 11, and 17 on this occasion because they were crowded together as they approached the meridian <note>The imaginary north-south line in the sky. Astronomers measure stars as they cross this line to determine their precise positions.</note>.

And indeed, number 17 is a nebulous cluster <note>Original: *συσροφή* (systrophē), a gathering or mass of stars that appears blurry to the naked eye.</note>; it was therefore omitted on this occasion due to the greater preoccupation with the more obvious stars.

Regarding stars 7, 8, and 9, the reason for the omission is likewise clear: for they come to the meridian first, and when this observation was taken, they had already passed the meridian—both they and the beak—as is clear from the celestial globe. On the globe, the bright star of the wing (which was the first observed on this occasion) culminates after the beak, and after these three stars which are assigned to the description of the Northern wing. For this reason, regarding the beak—which had already escaped the meridian—the observers took only the declination <note>The coordinate equivalent to latitude on the celestial sphere.</note>.

The eleventh star, however, which is in the middle of the southern wing, is smaller than the remaining two from that wing; and since it culminates at nearly the same time as the tenth, being situated between it and the stars of the foot, it is no wonder it was omitted on this occasion.

But as for our star in the breast <note>Kepler refers to the "New Star" of 1600, now known as P Cygni.</note>, since it culminates before the ancient star of the breast and is of almost equal magnitude with it, why would it have been left out? How could it have been missed when it approached the meridian at such a convenient time for observation?

In that very region, while the ancient star of the breast was culminating, the observers had so much leisure that they noted some small unformed star, besides those that the ancients had assigned. <margin>Note.</margin>

What then, you ask, if this small star is the very one we are debating here? Not at all. For that star culminated *after* the ancient star of the breast, whereas ours culminates *before* it. For the culmination of the [ancient] Breast is at 302° 2'. Ours is at 300° 46'. It also differs in altitude. For ours would have been at an altitude of 70° 57', while this one is at only 65° 8'.

But perhaps this star "outside the figure" is one of the two which the Ancients also counted as unformed? Not in the least. For you see that those were observed last, after the twelfth star. Therefore, you can see with your own eyes <margin>Note.</margin> that in this very place around the breast of the Swan, the observers sought more stars than are found in Ptolemy's catalog and even found a certain small one; yet they certainly did not find that extra star of the third magnitude <note>Magnitude refers to brightness; "third magnitude" is quite bright and easily visible to the naked eye.</note> near the breast at that time.

Let us proceed with the observations. September 12 and 22: the tail of the Swan was observed from certain stars of Andromeda. September 18: the beak was observed from certain stars of Pegasus. September 25: the bright star of the southern wing from certain stars of Pegasus. October 10: The middle star of the neck, third in number, from Lyra and Scheat in Pegasus.

Then, between the Northern star in the right foot of the Swan and Scheat in Pegasus: 24° 40'. And it is added: *This seems to be an unformed star.* You see their care and diligence...

<summary>Kepler uses Tycho Brahe's logs to show that astronomers were looking closely at the Swan's breast and even recording tiny, faint stars, yet they never saw the bright "New Star" before 1600, proving it is a new phenomenon. He uses precise coordinate data (culmination and altitude) to distinguish the New Star from other nearby faint stars.</summary>

<meta>Kepler continues his analysis of Tycho Brahe’s observations, specifically addressing why certain bright stars—near where the New Star of 1600 eventually appeared—might have been omitted from earlier records.
