## ON THE STAR OF THE SWAN.
->162<-

There remains one difficulty concerning star 12, which is located in the breast <note>This refers to the star Gamma Cygni, also known as Sadr. The "New Star" P Cygni appeared in the sky very close to this existing star.</note>, and whose vicinity this new star has occupied. Tycho recorded this in his Catalog as being of the third magnitude <note>Brightness was measured on a scale of 1 to 6; a "third magnitude" star is moderately bright and easily visible to the naked eye.</note>; and in the specific observation we are discussing, most of the other stars are referenced in relation to it. Yet, on February 23, 1593, this same star was omitted—even though it ought to have been seen before others, given its third-magnitude brightness and its position near the meridian <note>The imaginary north-south line in the sky. Measuring a star as it crosses the meridian allows for the most precise calculation of its position.</note> at that time.

I reply that the observation in question was focused only on stars of the sixth magnitude <note>The faintest stars visible to the human eye, requiring clear conditions to see.</note> in those regions that were then crossing the meridian, or were very close to it, such as stars 1, 2, 3, 4, and 5 in the neck. Star 12, however, had already passed the meridian and was sinking toward the west. Even though it is a third-magnitude star, there was no requirement to include it if it served no purpose for that night’s specific study—especially since its position was outside the boundary <note>original: "limbus," referring here to the edge or the graduated limb of the astronomical instrument being used.</note> in which Tycho was working at that time.

This concludes the discussion of Tycho’s observations, which have demonstrated that this new star was not visible <note>Kepler is proving that the "New Star" (P Cygni) was truly a new phenomenon, as it was not seen by Tycho Brahe in the 1590s despite his careful mapping of the area.</note> during that period.

Now let us proceed to the actual observations of the New Star itself.

It appears that Willem Janszoon Blaeu <note>Willem Janszoon Blaeu (1571–1638) was a prominent Dutch cartographer and globe-maker who had studied under Tycho Brahe at his observatory on the island of Hven.</note>, formerly a pupil of Tycho, was the first to notice it around the end of the year 1600. He initially believed it to be one of the stars already listed in Tycho’s catalog, but noted that at that time it was glowing more redly <note>original: "rutilaret," suggesting a fiery, reddish, or shimmering golden light.</note> than usual.

<summary>Kepler concludes his defense of Tycho Brahe’s records, explaining that the New Star was absent in 1593 and its later omission was merely a result of narrow observational focus. He then identifies Willem Janszoon Blaeu as the first to discover the star in 1600, noting its unusual reddish appearance.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Willem Janszoon Blaeu, P Cygni, Sadr, Gamma Cygni, stellar magnitude, meridian, 1600 Nova, Cygnus</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>Kepler continues his argument that the New Star in Cygnus was not present in earlier years by examining Tycho Brahe’s records from 1597 and 1580. He points out that even when Tycho was desperately searching for stars to fill his catalog or using Cygnus for navigation, this specific star was never mentioned.</meta>

**ON THE STAR OF THE SWAN.**          **163**

Likewise, four stars are mentioned between the mouth and foot of Pegasus, where mention is made of the "middle wing of the Swan." They carelessly neglected only this one in the breast of the Swan, to be sure. I will say nothing of the many others added beyond the usual number, which are further removed from the Swan.

In the year 1597, they said sixty stars were lacking to complete a full thousand in their catalog. To fill this gap, they gathered four between Capella and the Pole Star in a straight line; four between the side of Perseus and the Pole in a straight line; fourteen between Capella and the Pole at the sides, divided into two trapeziums and two triangles; three between the last stars of the tail of the Great Bear <note>Ursa Major</note> at the side; nine in a winding line under the "unformed" stars <note>Stars not yet assigned to a specific constellation figure</note> between the tails of the Bear and the Lion; and six on either side of the Pole Star. Behold this poverty of stars, which did not abstain from such contemptible, faint ones! It is a wonder, then, if a search so hungry for stars could not catch the scent of one so large in the breast of the Swan.

And in this final part of the observations, an eighth argument is born for us. <margin>**VIII.** By temporary vertical lines.</margin> For on October 27, 1597, times were being noted by the stars of the Swan coinciding with the Pole Star on the same vertical line. This method was prompted by the absence of instruments, for Tycho Brahe had departed from Denmark shortly before. Even then, there was no mention of a second star in the breast. And on October 30, at about 5:45, they say: *Nothing could be taken on the plumb line because of the clouds.* You see how diligently they were intent on the opportunity. Immediately, when their clock indicated 6:21, the breast [of the Swan] lined up; and at 6:15, the tail was in position. <margin>*Note.*</margin> If this star of ours had been there then, it would have been on this vertical line shortly before the breast star.

Ninth, let us return from the end to the beginning of the observations. <margin>**IX.** By the tail of the Comet.</margin> For of all the arguments that can be prepared from them, this one seems most manifest to me: that in the description of the Comet of the year 1580, on November 25 or December 2 or 9, these things are noted:

*On Friday, around 6 o'clock in the evening, when I was in a boat between Scania and Hven <note>The island where Tycho's observatory, Uraniborg, was located</note>, with the stars already shining to some extent after Sunset: I saw the Comet still appearing quite clearly and shining, with a magnitude like a star of the second magnitude, though the tail was hardly visible. It seemed, however, as if certain longer rays extended toward the Zenith overhead, as far as the eye could see. The Comet itself was near the Head of Hercules, lower than it, toward the West and North: and it was distant from that star in the Head of Hercules by three diameters of the Sun at most, as far as the eye could see—that is, by nearly one and a half degrees. For this distance was notably smaller than the distance between the two closer stars in the body of the Vulture <note>original: "Vulturis." The constellation Aquila (the Eagle) was frequently referred to as the Vulture in the 16th century.</note>.* <margin>*Note.*</margin> But why did he not say that this distance was nearly equal to the space between the two neighboring stars in the body of the Swan, if there were two there then? For both constellations are equally close to Hercules.

->164 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

He continues: "That line seemed to extend through the Comet toward the aforementioned [star] in the head of Hercules (where it would be produced) into the one which is in the middle of the Swan's body, as far as the sight <note>original: "quoad visum"</note> [goes]." If there had been two stars there at that time, he would have distinguished them, because he adds the phrase, "as far as the sight." Thus far his account goes. A picture is also added, where the middle star in the body is depicted alone: but from this, it appears that he took the left shoulder of the Serpent-bearer <note>Ophiuchus</note> for the head of Hercules. For this line, from the breast of the Swan to the star he named the Head of Hercules, separates the head of the Serpent-bearer to one side slightly, and the tail of the Swan to the other.

<margin>10. Witnesses of the Newness.</margin>
Tenthly, therefore, let the authority of speaking and living witnesses be added to these silent witnesses; and this authority is all the more powerful because, as said from the beginning, they have no one who can contradict them from their own memory.

<margin>Jansonius</margin>
The first is Willem Jansonius <note>Willem Janszoon Blaeu (1571–1638), a renowned Dutch cartographer and globe maker</note>, who declares that this new star was first seen by him in the year 1600, according to an inscription made on a celestial globe published in 1601. I have no reason to doubt this man's integrity: his noble geographical and astronomical pursuits testify to it. This kind of study is so composed that it does not tolerate a lover unless he is faithful and honest. That he possesses knowledge of the stars is sufficiently proven by the fact that he was a disciple and for some time a companion in observations to Tycho Brahe. Once I saw the origin of this rumor traced back to him, I did not cease—especially with the Emperor’s approval—to question him by letter about the circumstances in which he first happened upon the sight and knowledge of this New Star. However, what happened to my letters, I do not know; I have certainly received no response from him so far.

<margin>Byrgius</margin>
The other whom I know is Jost Bürgi <note>Justus Byrgius (1552–1632), a Swiss clockmaker and mathematician who served Emperor Rudolf II</note>, the Clockmaker to His Imperial Majesty. Although he is unskilled in languages <note>meaning he did not know Latin, the academic language of the time</note>, he easily surpasses many professors in the knowledge and speculation of mathematical matters. Indeed, he possesses practical skill so uniquely that a future age will celebrate him as a leader in this field, no less than Dürer in painting, whose fame grows through the hidden passage of time, like a tree. But not to wander too far afield <note>original: "ne extra oleas," a classical idiom meaning to stay within the boundaries of the course</note>; our Bürgi did not discover this star himself at the start. Instead, when word was sent from Holland—written by the architect of Prince Maurice to the Count of Lippe, and from him to Bürgi in the year Jansonius’s globe appeared—that a New Star was seen in the Swan, he had a place to turn, even though this man, being self-restrained and far removed from vanity, doubted the readiness of his own memory. He had previously been in the service of the most illustrious William, Landgrave of Hesse <note>William IV (1532–1592), a prince famous for his astronomical accuracy</note>, whose zeal and diligence in sidereal science was greater than is common in a Prince...

<summary>Kepler introduces human testimony to confirm the star's novelty, citing the Dutch cartographer Willem Blaeu and the Imperial clockmaker Jost Bürgi. He highlights their expertise and professional connections to prove the star was a recent discovery rather than a previously overlooked constant.</summary>

<meta>Kepler continues his list of witnesses to the new star's appearance, moving from Willem Blaeu to the renowned instrument maker Jost Bürgi.</meta>

**ON THE STAR OF CYGNUS. 165**

...often required, and his most excellent inventions spurred Tycho Brahe to emulation, as can be seen throughout that man’s works, especially in his book of *Letters*.) This man of whom I speak, Bürgi <note>Jost Bürgi (1552–1632), a Swiss clockmaker and mathematician of immense skill</note>, while preparing a celestial automaton, had added a celestial globe made of silver. This was at the time when Rothmann, the Landgrave’s astronomer, was devoting his greatest effort to the fixed stars. Since many errors were being discovered in Ptolemy's catalog <note>original: "abaco Ptolemaei"</note>, and many stars slightly brighter than others had been omitted by Ptolemy outside the traditional constellations, it pleased the Prince <note>Landgrave Wilhelm IV of Hesse-Kassel</note> that whatever the craftsman was to engrave should be continuously compared with the sky itself; this was diligently performed by Bürgi. That completed globe, truly a royal gift, was sent by Landgrave Wilhelm to Emperor Rudolf, and it remains to this day among His Majesty’s treasures. Another similar globe was then under Bürgi’s hands, and was given a little later to Archduke Maximilian by Landgrave Maurice while he was passing through Kassel.

Therefore, Bürgi, who did not find this star in the Swan (celebrated as New) in his own memory, turned to this globe of his with the utmost confidence: not finding it in the old engraving—of whose diligence he was so certain—he marked it in its proper place with ink. It happened a little later that, having obtained a leave of absence from the Landgrave, he moved to the Emperor’s service, and here he devoted his care to the repair of many admirable works, including that first globe he had made. Neither did this globe show any star in the breast of the Swan except for the ancient Ptolemaic one. Thus, Bürgi pronounces most confidently regarding the novelty of this star.

Shall I also add a third witness, Johann Bayer? I shall certainly do [Marginal note: **Bayer.**] so. For although I previously grouped him among those who withhold judgment <note>original: "ἐπέχουσι" (epekhousi); a Greek term for those who suspend belief or stay neutral</note>, he himself provided the reason in his *Uranometria* <note>Bayer’s famous star atlas, published in 1603</note>. He was much more open in letters written to a certain great man, copies of which were provided to me without any conditions; thus, I do not fear that the author will be angry with me. For they contain no secrets, nothing that would harm his reputation, and nothing that is not in the public interest to know. Add to this that I can, in a way, claim them for myself, as I provided the occasion for them. At the beginning of the year 1602, the noble Lord Franciscus Gansneb, called Tengnagel, the son-in-law of Tycho Brahe and now a Councilor of Appeals to His Imperial Majesty, had come to Bürgi at Kassel, bringing with him Joannes Eriksen, who was formerly an astronomical assistant to Brahe. Having been informed by Bürgi about the star, and while they were traveling to East Frisia, they reported the matter to David Fabricius. Fabricius, by way of 
**X 3 Ham-**

The account also traces the communication of the discovery between key astronomers like Tengnagel and David Fabricius.</summary>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>On this page, Kepler continues discussing the testimony regarding the "New Star" in the constellation Cygnus (the Swan), specifically citing the correspondence and observations of Johannes Bayer, the author of the famous star atlas, Uranometria.</meta>

166 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;JOANNES KEPLER

Someone from Hamburg wrote of these same things to Jæstelius <note>Justus Astelius, a professor of mathematics at Wittenberg</note>, Professor of Mathematics at Wittenberg; Jæstelius wrote to me in Prague; I wrote to Bavaria. Thus, with the rumor communicated to Munich and then to Augsburg, Bayer’s <note>Johannes Bayer (1572–1625), whose star atlas *Uranometria* revolutionized celestial mapping</note> opinion was also requested, as they knew he then had under his hands that most sharp-eyed work of cataloging the stars, the *Uranometria*; which was published for the public immediately the following year, 1603. He responded, therefore, in this manner:

*Concerning the new star of the Hen or Swan, etc., having been timely informed, as much because of the suspicion <note>original: "invidiam," often meaning the ill-will or professional jealousy that follows a claim of "novelty" in the heavens</note> of novelty as the absence of MY (note) DESCRIPTIONS, and being about to approach the matter somewhat more hesitantly, I also recalled Pliny, who requires a man engaged in studies to be less bold and more timid. On the seventh of June, and for several days following, having contemplated the preliminary sketch <note>original: σχεδιογραφίαν (*skediographian*)</note> of this constellation (which he certainly had ready long ago, and as he says in his Celestial Map, prepared with long-term labor), I noted right from the start a certain foreign little star that seemed suspicious to me, about which I hesitated in doubt until the summer solstice. Therefore, I delineated to a hair's breadth the position of the Swan or Bird, and the placement of its stars, according to the classical Doctors of Astronomy, with my own (I think, older) observation added on top. I found (that is, having compared the sky with this drawing) according to the common count 17 stars, with two unformed ones; but besides these, I detected another star at the end of the neck and the beginning of the Breast, no longer merely suspicious to me, but truly a star never seen until now, of roughly the third magnitude, etc.*

Thus far the letter. To whom, indeed, should one believe, if not the craftsman about his art, and the student about his occupation? For the fact speaks for itself—as does the massive preparation for the *Uranometria* and the expensive copper plates—that this was then that man’s occupation, and not for one year alone: to transfer the celestial arrangements onto paper, and not to overlook those omitted by Ptolemy, and thus to correct the charts of Piccolomini <note>Alessandro Piccolomini (1508–1578), author of an earlier influential star atlas</note>.

But as to why he became more cautious in the *Uranometria*, and appealed to the silent consensus of others rather than his own experience: I believe he was moved by the example of others, and especially my own. For shortly after the rumor had spread, I had written those things mentioned at the beginning of this Narrative, and I had communicated them to Bavaria. The numbers which he transferred from my writing into the *Uranometria* prove that he read them. In that writing, having confessed at the start that I was being failed by my own memory, I made this conclusion:

*Whatever this star may be, old or new: it is established by geometric reasoning that it is certainly elevated most high among the fixed stars themselves. Once this is understood, when one considers that so many months have passed while it is always seen in the same magnitude, never equaling its neighbor in the breast, never...*

<summary>Kepler quotes a letter from Johannes Bayer describing his discovery and mapping of the new star in Cygnus. Kepler notes how Bayer’s work corrects earlier astronomers and explains the collaborative exchange of observations across Europe.</summary>

Kepler is discussing the testimony of Johannes Bayer, who had observed the star but hesitated to publish his findings due to the star's fluctuating brightness and the controversial nature of "new" celestial objects at the time.</meta>

-> ON THE STAR OF THE SWAN. 167 <-

...diminished to the smallness of the one in the beak <note>The star Albireo (Beta Cygni), located at the head or "beak" of the Swan constellation.</note>, and was further thinned out, possessing a constant and pale color. Indeed, it is no wonder if the delay itself <note>The long period during which the star remained visible but faint.</note>, though very well confirmed by the previous arguments, nonetheless leaves one anxious as to what will become of it. Because of this doubt of mine, I think Bayer was somewhat frightened and rendered doubtful of his own experience—as commonly happens when many people are confidently speaking against a thing—and he ceased from bringing the star forward as testimony. Although I have presented it here in its plain form, it is not solitary, but relies on a legitimate band of several witnesses, so that it might easily withstand any challenge.

I think the **Novelty** of this star has been sufficiently attested; for if these evidences do not suffice for someone, it is believable that such a person would not keep faith even if entire nations were affirming it. It remains for me to review what I myself observed in this star, using the aforementioned sextant instrument.

In the year 1602, on the 18th/28th day <note>This fraction represents the date in both the Julian (18th) and Gregorian (28th) calendars, a common practice in Kepler's time during the transition between systems.</note>

From these distances, the followers of Tycho Brahe <note>original: "Braheani"; the astronomers trained by or using the methods of Tycho Brahe, whose data was the gold standard of the era.</note> have established, after considering all factors, the location of the star at 16° 18' of Aquarius, with a latitude of 55° 30' North. From this, the right ascension of the New Star is found to be 300° 46', and its declination 36° 52' North. It therefore culminates <note>Reaches its highest point in the sky.</note> along with 28° 37' of Capricorn. In the Andalusian part of Spain, in Sicily, the Peloponnese, Ionia, Cilicia, Syria, and other places on Earth situated under this same parallel, it passes daily through the Zenith <note>Directly overhead.</note>. For those located at a polar altitude <note>Latitude.</note> of 53° 8', it grazes the horizon in the North; such as in England, Holland, Brunswick, the March <note>The Margraviate of Brandenburg.</note>, Livonia <note>Modern-day Estonia and Latvia.</note>, and Moscow. Further toward the North, it does not set at all <note>It becomes "circumpolar," meaning it stays above the horizon 24 hours a day.</note>.

<note>An illustration depicts the constellation Cygnus as a swan in flight, diving downward. Several stars are marked on its body and wings. A specific star on the neck is labeled with the letter 'N' to indicate the "Nova" or New Star. The swan is framed by two stylized clouds, a common artistic convention in 17th-century star charts.</note>

*YOU HAVE HERE, Illustrious and Noble BARON, a short narrative written by your client, supported by the use of your own instruments, and promised to you three years ago. It is therefore yours by many titles. You shall read it so that, considering the long duration during which this New Star has remained in view, you may either defend the position that nothing like it has ever existed—and from that, deliberate with yourself on its most grave significance and the intent of Him who created it—or, if you prefer, you may conclude that many such stars are born in the course of centuries. In that case, let it serve as a conjecture for how much ignorance of Nature holds the human race in its grip. To most, this star, now six years old, creates so much shock and so much wonder that you will find it very difficult to find anyone who would trust the astronomers regarding its novelty. For the rest, I reverently pray that Your Illustrious Nobility, in view of the smallness of the work, might instead accept my goodwill, and continue to hold me in your favor.*

END OF THE NARRATION CONCERNING THE STAR OF THE SWAN.

JOHANNES—

*On the natural effects of this new star in*
*sublunary nature.* <note>The "sublunary" realm refers to the Earth and its atmosphere—everything "below the moon"—which in Aristotelian physics was subject to change and decay, unlike the "unchanging" heavens.</note>

CHAPTER XXVIII.

**S**i I were to make the primary goal of this little book the untying of the star's meanings, I could rightly say that everything said, concluded, investigated, and doubted thus far is directed toward this art of prophecy. <note>original: "Manticen"; from the Greek "manteia," referring to divination or the prophetic arts.</note> For different circumstances of a star's appearance carry different meanings, and the methods of divination are drawn from the conditions of its essence. Thus, for one who has explained nothing of those conditions, it is inevitable that he will wander like a stranger among the interpretations.

In consideration of this, if I were to refrain entirely from any explanation of meanings, there would be excuses enough. For I have demonstrated thus far that I hesitate in explaining the physical causes of the star and its coincidence with the Great Conjunction; <note>The "Great Conjunction" is the meeting of Jupiter and Saturn, which occurred in 1604 in the fiery signs of the zodiac, an event Kepler viewed as highly significant.</note> I have been unable to pronounce anything except with stumbling. Therefore, a fear takes hold of me regarding the same uncertainty in unfolding the significations—an uncertainty that is all the more notable because the common crowd of men seeks knowledge of specific future events more greedily than they seek Philosophy.

But indeed, because every description of this kind is thought imperfect if it does not also deal with significations, this plan can be adopted: first, I shall explain those things which are connected by a natural bond to the new star, so that the star was either the physical cause or a sign for them; second, those things which can be said with probability through the art of conjecture, proven by other examples beyond the appearance of a natural connection, and assuming the providence of an intelligent nature. Meanwhile, I shall also examine certain obvious things—whether those which astrologers predicted would happen, or those which time itself has brought forth—by the standard of truth, to see if they were legitimately predicted and if they occurred because of the star's appearance.

<margin>
*How the*
*heavens in-*
*fluence sub-*
*lunary affairs.*
</margin>

Thus, if there is any natural signification of this star, it must be explained in the same way I used previously in chapters 8 and 9 to demonstrate the efficacy of the "aspects." <note>In astrology, "aspects" are specific angular distances between planets (like 90 or 180 degrees) that Kepler believed physically resonated with the "soul" of the Earth.</note> For every form of celestial influence is established not in the action of the heaven (except for that of light for the purpose of warming—in which respect this star, as long as it shone, was perhaps as powerful as two or three fixed stars at the beginning, and later less; in quality, however, it was compared to Sirius, <note>original: "Caniculae," the Dog Star.</note> or the shoulder of Orion, <note>Betelgeuse.</note> or Arcturus, or among the planets to Jupiter and Mars, as indicated by its color): but in the rece- <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("receptione" or "receiving") and continues on the next page.</note>

**On the New Star in the Serpent-bearer** **171**

<margin>Kinship of the heavens with sublunary things:</margin>
reception, and thus in the passion <note>In 17th-century philosophy, "passion" refers to the capacity of a thing to be acted upon or to receive an impression from an external force.</note> of sublunary nature, which is either sensitive itself, or very much like the sensitive faculty. For a kinship exists between the souls and faculties of sublunary things and the nature of the heavens; and this kinship is threefold: of heat, of motion, and of reason. 

First, for the light of the heavens is the producer of heat: and the souls and faculties of <margin>Of heat.</margin> vegetative things each stir up their own heat in the body which they inform, <margin>Of motion.</margin> and they protect that heat according to their strength. Likewise, faculties have been added to the stars which move them—for the most part indeed material, or shared with the body, both innate and external (such as the magnetic faculty we have here among us)—but also including animal <note>Here "animal" is used in its Latin sense, *animalis*, meaning "pertaining to the soul" (*anima*) or life-force, rather than just referring to non-human creatures.</note> faculties: and here on Earth, similarly, the souls and faculties of vegetative things exist within their bodies for the sake of life and motion. Finally, <margin>Of reason.</margin> just as those moving faculties of the stars are in some way participants in Mind—so that they understand, imagine, and affect their own path, as it were; not indeed by logical reasoning as we humans do, but by an innate power instilled in them at the first creation—so too the animal faculties of natural things possess a certain understanding of their own end (though without logical reasoning) toward which they direct all their actions. Since there is such a great kinship, there is no further reason for you to wonder at that sympathy <note>original: "συμπάθειαν"; the Greek concept of *sympatheia*, a cosmic resonance where parts of the universe react to one another.</note> between the motions of the heavens and the animal faculties, which I confirmed above with manifest experiments in chapters 8 and 10. Therefore, let those things which were specifically proven there now be assumed; and let a general pronouncement finally be made by probable reason, as if collected from induction:

*That nothing exists or happens in the visible heaven whose influence is not extended by some hidden reason into the Earths, and into all the faculties of natural things: and that these animal faculties here on earth are affected in the same way that the heaven itself is affected. Thus it happens that whenever something new and unusual appears in the heaven, sublunary Nature (over which some animal faculty presides) in some way shudders.* <note>Kepler argues that the Earth is a living being that "feels" changes in the sky.</note> *For I shall openly flee to the Poets, and to my first address* <note>original: "προσφώνησιν"; a formal salutation or greeting.</note> *which I used toward this star; because among those who hold power in Philosophy today, I despair of gaining a hearing for the cause of a new doctrine. Therefore: "Far off, O unholy ones! God, behold, God!"* <note>original: "Procul ô prophani, Deus, en Deus." Kepler is quoting Virgil’s Aeneid (6.258) to signal he is entering a sacred or divinely inspired mode of speech.</note>

*Creeping star, omen of the troubled Divine Mind,*
*You who burn like a carbuncle with glowing fire!*

*Your divine births? For not even at that time when the powerful*

<meta>This page continues Chapter 28 from page 171. Kepler is expanding on his theory that the "soul of the Earth" (the sublunary nature) responds to celestial light and patterns, using this to explain the physical influence of eclipses and the new star of 1604.</meta>

172 **JOHANNES KEPLER**

<margin>From where eclipses derive their effects.</margin>
This same principle seems to me the basis for asserting that eclipses <note>original: *defectibus luminum*, literally "failures of light"</note> also have a real influence. For it is discordant <note>original: *ἀνάρμοστον* (anarmoston), meaning out of harmony or jarring</note> (qualitatively, though not geometrically or quantitatively) and in a certain way contrary to sublunary nature for the moon to be suddenly extinguished, or for the sun’s light to be turned away from its measured half of the terrestrial globe, where it usually and perpetually remains. That sight strikes the animal nature, which is kin to light, causing it to conceive a long-lasting and harmful state of distress. Therefore, let this same principle be used to assert the influence of these new phenomena as well.

<margin>How the effects of three natural and supernatural heavenly bodies are mixed together.</margin>
By a doubling of effects, therefore: since the great conjunction <note>A "Great Conjunction" refers to the rare alignment of the outer planets—Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars—which occurred in 1604.</note> of the three superior planets itself added immense <note>original: *decumanos*, literally "of the tenth wave," a Latin idiom for something unusually large or powerful.</note> stimuli to sublunary natures to move themselves, and since this new star fell into the same place and time as that great conjunction, it follows that it added enormous strength to the ordinary disturbance. This was true both because of its conjunction with each of the joined planets and because of the novelty of the star itself, causing a most memorable astonishment <note>original: *ἔκπληξιν* (ekplēxin), a Greek term for being struck with wonder or shock.</note>. However, a necessary doubt remains: whether we should argue by addition <note>original: *κατὰ πρόσθεσιν* (kata prosthesin)</note> or by subtraction <note>original: *κατ' ἀφαίρεσιν* (kat' aphairesin)</note>. That is, whether nature, having turned its attention toward the new star, turned somewhat away from the great conjunction—so that the imagination of the more unusual thing overwhelmed and erased the memory of the accustomed thing—or whether nature felt the combined forces of both. One might also doubt whether the commotion the star caused was more like joy or sadness; whether it hindered Nature in her work, or incited and aided her. What seems more likely to me among these options, I shall explain by reviewing the sublunary natures themselves.

<margin>The star’s effects on weather.</margin>
The first and most conspicuous is that animal faculty which, presiding over the globe of the Earth, produces all kinds of meteors <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomena, including rain, wind, and snow, not just falling stars.</note>. And since the entire winter that passed between the years 1604 and 1605 was exceedingly humid, especially on those days when the planets encountered or were aligned with the new fixed star—and since it was a warm winter on this side of the Alps in upper and lower Germany, but the coldest of all in human memory across the Alps in Italy and Spain—I argue from the effect that the forces of both celestial causes were applied against one another.

<margin>Why the winter was different in different regions.</margin>
I attribute the aforementioned difference in the winter to the Alps themselves. It is as if, during this winter, the Alps were in a state of perpetual exhalation; the material of the winds, struck back by the cold of the air, was spread in a circle—part toward the north, part toward the south. That which went south over Italy, coming from a northern region, was naturally cold. But that which was carried north over Germany, coming from the south, retained the warm nature of that region, by no great miracle. But what if someone considers this whole...

<summary>Kepler argues that the Earth possesses a soul-like faculty that responds to celestial light, explaining why eclipses and the new star of 1604 caused significant weather patterns and "animal" distress. He notes that the winter of 1604-1605 was uncommonly humid and varied wildly in temperature between Germany and Italy due to these celestial influences acting upon the Earth's atmosphere.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 28 from page 172. Kepler is elaborating on his theory that the Earth possesses a "soul" or "nature" that responds to celestial events, explaining how the 1604 New Star and the Great Conjunction of planets influenced the weather and atmospheric phenomena.</meta>

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER 173<-

...place this region or climate where warmth prevailed within the exhalation <note>original: *expiratione*, referring to vapors or "breaths" released by the Earth's interior</note>. Italy and that climate were outside the exhalation, so that the winds blowing there were carried toward us and expired here. For anyone who says this will rely on the likelihood that every exhalation is warm when it first emerges, but as it is carried far away, it is stirred into motion and grows colder and colder.

Something similar must be imagined regarding this very summer, which was excessively humid through Germany and the Gallic lands <note>modern-day France</note>, and therefore pestilential; yet across the Pyrenees in the Spanish lands, it was dry and sterile. Why is it surprising if nations disagree in their pursuits when such contrary conditions come upon them from the heavens? Nor was the winter notable only for its moisture; it was much more so for the nocturnal fires in the sky, very frequent blood-red appearances, and openings or "chasms" <note>original: *hiatibus seu chasmatis*, likely referring to the Aurora Borealis or similar atmospheric lights</note>; and, what is equally prodigious for winter, rainbows appearing in the regions of both the Sun and the Moon. Add to this the somewhat more frequent parhelia <note>sundogs</note>, paraselenae <note>moondogs</note>, and Halos.

Therefore, it is likely that this winter humidity and the frequency of celestial prodigies were the effect of the new star. Let us distribute these effects between the new star and the great conjunction with sound reasoning in this way:

In the globe of the Earth, there is a twin faculty (insofar as it pertains to meteors <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomena, including rain, wind, and rainbows, not just falling stars</note>). The first is for <margin>Foundations of meteorology and a description of the Earth’s visceral faculties.</margin> attracting sea waters into hidden seats of concoction; the other is for expelling concocted vapors through a kind of sweat, so to speak; or if you prefer, compare this expulsion to the seminal excretion of living creatures. It is most certain that the second of these faculties—the expulsive one—is stimulated by the harmonic <margin>The expulsive faculty.</margin> aspects of the planets. By this stimulation, it is brought about that the expulsion is indeed continuous (as in the generation of rivers) but not perpetually uniform; rather, its intensity is interrupted by many intervals, just as the aspects fall according to the astronomical order. And by Hercules! <note>A common Latin exclamation, *me hercule*, used to emphasize a point</note> One might not absurdly attribute a certain pleasure to this excretion: so many things about the Earth correspond to living creatures. Indeed, physicians <margin>Whether it is accompanied by pleasure.</margin> teach that whenever the veins are swollen with genital fluid, an excretion easily occurs—even during sleep when a pleasant image is presented—without any physical contact. What of this matter...

<meta>This page continues Chapter 28, where Kepler explores his theory of the Earth as a living entity with an "anima" (soul) or vital faculty. He is comparing the Earth's internal processes of moisture and "breathing" to the biological functions of animals, specifically in response to the Great Conjunction of 1603 and the New Star of 1604.</meta>

174 [tab] JOHANNES KEPLER

[centered] *He descends into the lap of his joyful spouse, and great*
[centered] *In the mingling of his great body, he nourishes all offspring.*
[centered] <note>original: "Coniugis in gremium latæ descendit, & omnes / Magnus alit magno commixtus corpore fœtus." This is a quote from Virgil’s *Georgics*, traditionally describing the sky (Aether) fertilizing the earth with rain.</note>

That poet, indeed luxuriating in fiction, attributed rains to the Heavens as a substitute for seed, which he might pour into the earth. I, however, ascribe nothing to the heavens except for that <term>Venus-like quality</term> <note>original: *speciem illam Veneream*, referring to the beauty or attractiveness of celestial configurations</note> (by which I mean the astrological **aspects**) that provokes the Earth to pour forth this "seed" from within itself—provided that its veins have swelled with an abundance of humors. This, then, is how the **expulsive faculty** of the Earth operates.

<margin>The attractive faculty</margin>
At what intervals the Earth’s **attractive faculty** acts is indeed obscure. In living creatures, the intervals of this faculty—which <term>concocts</term> <note>a historical term for the internal "cooking" or digestion of fluids</note> the reproductive humor within and inserts it into the veins—vary: youth, spring, the fullness of food, and a <term>plethoric</term> <note>overfull or congested</note> body promote it; whereas old age, winter, hunger, and disease hinder it. Yet these intervals cannot be compared with those of excretion. For the latter are sudden, headlong, and brief; while the former are slow, more sluggish, and in a way more continuous. Let philosophers look to see what might be found similar to this in the globe of the Earth. It is certain that sometimes the Earth lacks that which it might excrete, even if it is most fervently stimulated by the planetary aspects. For then, as experience teaches, instead of rains, there usually arise certain burning, unpleasant, sulfurous, and biting vapors; from which come dry spells, parched conditions, and <gloss>droughts</gloss> <note>original Greek: αὐχμοί (*auchmoi*)</note>. Compare these to the sterile, futile, and laborious couplings of animals; so, by Hercules, it is so, for sterility follows these conditions of the Earth. And Aristotle celebrates the effects of Comets in this regard, noting that they signify sterility and parched lands, a view which experience supports.

<margin>From where it is stimulated.</margin>
Indeed, if Comets signify as causes, since they are for the most part above the Moon, they will be causes in no other way than has been said and maintained thus far: namely, as objects that move the senses. Therefore, Comets and Eclipses of the Sun will have this in common: to terrify and dismay the **vegetative faculty** of the Earth, and thus to hinder its work—which is, among other things, to attract the nourishment and matter for rains and rivers, and, if you please, even to "concoct" it.

Is it therefore the case that, except for when there are Comets and Eclipses, the work of attracting and concocting humors in the Earth continues fervently? Or are there also here stimulating causes by which this fervor is sometimes rendered sharper? I do not know; except for what is already well-celebrated among astrologers: that fertile years follow the **great conjunctions**. If this is true, then the conjunction of the superior planets <note>Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars</note> will stimulate the Earth to attract seawater and will aid its "concoction."

<margin>The natural effect of the Great Conjunction.</margin>
Two pieces of evidence from the present situation support this dogma: first, that fertile years, by the grace of God, followed the **great conjunction** that occurred in the month of December in the year 1603. Indeed, the low price of wine near the <term>Neckar</term> <note>original: *Nicrum*, the Neckar River in Kepler's home region of Württemberg</note> was almost the same as in the years 1584 and 1585 after that conjunction in Pisces—except for the extent to which money itself becomes more devalued every day. The second is that both the previous winter after the autumn of the year 1603, and this very winter about which we have said so much, showed the earth [overflowing] with humors...

<summary>Kepler argues that the Earth has a biological-like "soul" that responds to astrological events (like the 1603 Great Conjunction) by regulating weather and fertility through its own internal "faculties." He correlates the abundance of wine and rain to these celestial triggers, viewing the Earth as an organism that "digests" and "excretes" moisture.</summary>
<keywords>Earth's soul, attractive faculty, expulsive faculty, Great Conjunction of 1603, Aristotle, weather theory, wine prices, Neckar River, astrology, biological metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 28 of Kepler's treatise. Having previously discussed the Earth’s "expulsive faculty" (its ability to release moisture or energy), Kepler now examines the "attractive faculty" (the ability to absorb or draw in) and how the Great Conjunction and the New Star of 1604 influenced these natural, biological-style processes of the terrestrial globe.</meta>

ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER 175

...the Earth had been most swollen; therefore, it had previously absorbed this much moisture; and indeed, there was also a **Great Conjunction** <note>The rare alignment of Jupiter and Saturn, which occurred in 1603, just before the New Star appeared in 1604.</note>. Thus, it can be believed that this conjunction served as a stimulus for the Earth to drink in moisture. Is the stimulus for "drinking in" and for "excreting" the same? I do not know, except that even in a living creature, the mere sight of a fellow creature is believed both to generate <term>reproductive humor</term> <note>original: *humorem genitalem*; in 17th-century science, "humors" were the vital fluids of the body</note> and sometimes to excrete it. If this similarity is granted, we shall also apply this distinction: just as in living creatures the <term>concoction</term> <note>original: *coctio*; the process of "cooking" or maturing fluids within the body</note> of humor takes a span of time, while the excretion is momentary; so too in the Earth, the cause stimulating it to attract, drink in, and concoct becomes more prolonged than the cause stimulating it to excrete. And since the definition of an **aspect** <note>An astrological angle between planets</note> depends on the highest precision, which is momentary: the **Conjunction**, or the proximity of Saturn and Jupiter, will be called a stimulus for the Earth to attract—not merely as an aspect, but by a more general principle, namely because of the very proximity of their lights—so that while the cause lasts a long time, the effect can also be more long-lasting. Since the **New Star** arose at that very time when the hidden depths of the earth were beginning to overflow, then after it joined the planets, it increased this stimulus for attraction and continued that attraction, so that the Earth’s moisture could not be quickly exhausted. Meanwhile, as often as it formed configurations with the planets, it gave a stimulus to the **expulsive faculty**; and thus it can be believed to have worked in both ways.

And so much for that general faculty of the terrestrial globe and its commotion caused by the new star: let us speak of the rest. Setting aside plants and other living things, whose workings are more obscure, let us come to the prince of living creatures, **Man**.

<margin>On the natural effects of the new star in Man.</margin>

There is a twofold consideration of Man: first, as a rational creature; second, as one endowed with that natural faculty which he shares with the globe of the Earth itself—perceiving celestial things without discourse, without teaching, without progress, and even without knowledge of himself. For it was said above in Chapter 10 that it is common to the **Stag** <note>Kepler often uses the myth of Actaeon, who was turned into a stag, to discuss how creatures respond instinctively to natural laws.</note> along with **Actaeon** and **Melthaeus**, with **Ptolemy**, and with **Aristoxenus** <note>Ptolemy and Aristoxenus were ancient authorities on music and harmony.</note>, to be sweetly affected when listening to that **geometric proportion** which exists in sounds, and which in this subject is called **harmony**; even though he himself may be ignorant that the thing affecting him is a geometric matter, nor has he ever learned geometry, nor could he learn it. It has also been said that in the rays of the planets there exists the same proportion that is in sounds; and by these, the emotions are similarly moved, as is whatever is natural in the human soul, which **Aristotle** pronounced "brute and mortal."

<margin>The star's effect on human reason is accidental to it...</margin>

Regarding these different aspects of Man, I also speak distinctly. And first, concerning **Reason** itself, which the best Creator bestowed upon us in His own image: the matter requires no explanation. The minds of mortals were vehemently struck with consternation at the first sight of this star. **Natural history** bears witness to this.

<summary>Kepler argues that the Earth acts like a living organism, absorbing and releasing moisture in response to planetary alignments. He then shifts his focus to the human response to the New Star, distinguishing between our rational minds and our innate, "brute" instinct to respond to celestial harmonies.</summary>
<keywords>Earth as living entity, Great Conjunction, humors, attractive faculty, expulsive faculty, human reason, celestial harmony, geometric proportion, Aristotle, Ptolemy, psychological impact of supernovas</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler's discussion on the "signification" or portents of the New Star. While the previous page focused on the physical stimulus of the Great Conjunction on the Earth's "humors," Kepler now shifts to the social and intellectual effects of the New Star, specifically how it stimulated human activity, publishing, and his own research.</meta>

176 JOHANNES KEPLER

...history of the first chapter. Under this name, the star signified various rumors, various <term>ominations</term> <note>Prophetic signs or omens</note> regarding future events, the consternation and amazement of the common people, and many hindrances to many businesses, as well as many new preoccupations. As for what specifically happened to individuals, let them say so themselves; as for what concerns me, I have reviewed it in the preface.

I had to look toward Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, and Mercury; but having set those aside, and having delayed the labor of investigating their perennial motions from the observations of Tycho Brahe, I turned my eyes toward the **New Star**, and I remained struck dumb by it. You may ask, what did the **New Star** portend? To be sure, it portended many writings by many men about the new star, and much business for printers, who were led on by profit and by the curiosity of men's consciences. Whether these writings were fewer in number than I had foretold in my small pamphlets written in the <note>original: *vernaculo idiomate*; referring to the German language</note> common tongue, I do not know. This much I do know: I myself took care that my own prophecy would not be fulfilled by my own pamphlets, for I printed no more than two hundred copies, adding the prohibition **LET NO ONE IMITATE** <note>original: *NE QVIS IMITETVR*; a legal warning against pirated editions</note>. Yet another man sitting in Amberg, having caught this phrase just as an echo usually does, returned it thus: <margin>The Amberg / Echo.</margin> **THE AMBERGERS HAVE IMITATED**. Indeed, both these men and those in <note>original: *Argentinenses*; the people of Strasbourg</note> Strasbourg have imitated it. Therefore, I am grateful to them, for they fulfilled my prophecy by their own little effort, without any work of my own.

Is there indeed anything more that this star portended for my studies? Why not? Certainly, I would not have produced a description of that part of the sky unless this new star had arisen in it; nor would I have devised so many things about the secrets of nature in this very book which I am writing and which I here publish, unless this new star had come forth from the secrets of Nature. Therefore, if to some philosophers this new philosophy of mine seems absurd, let them hold this <term>portent</term> <note>original: *portentum*; meaning both a prophetic sign and a "monstrous" or strange thing</note> of a commentary as something for which they can say that portentous star was kindled—lest perhaps it should seem to them to have been in vain.

The star was able to do none of these things through itself; it sent down nothing to the earth except its brilliance. But when its appearance was received into the eyes of men, and it was recognized as new by astronomers (who had acquired this science through use, practice, or study), and when those men were either placed in such a position that they were spurred to write by reason of their duty, or they practiced the art of printing and lived by it and knew of men's curiosity—when these causes, I say, concurred on earth, these effects were connected to the **New Star**. Thus, in the case of the star, there is nothing but the *occasion*; whereas in the perception of the star and in men themselves, the *governance of the entire cause* resides. You will find that this fits beautifully both with many things said above and with those that will follow.

In the same way also, if there could be any other perception or estimation of the star—be it astrological or common—it could lead men to various other things.

<summary>Kepler argues that the "portents" of the New Star were not caused by the star itself, but by the human reaction to its light, which spurred astronomers to study and printers to pirate books. He wittily notes that his own prophecy of "many writings" was fulfilled by the very people who pirated his work in Amberg and Strasbourg.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, printing, piracy, Amberg, Strasbourg, causality vs. occasion, human perception, new philosophy, Tycho Brahe</keywords>

to introduce. For what if the common people, stirred by the celebration of the **New Star** into an expectation of **New** things, more easily admitted something new; being persuaded that it was fated, and promoted by a certain necessity, since even a miraculous star was kindled for its sake? What if certain private individuals throughout Hungary and Britain, led by astrological argument, interpreted this star as a good omen (for very many people praised it for the most pleasant splendor of its light) and, because it had shone in the house of Jupiter, they judged it to be a patron of the true religion (which each side estimated according to their own judgment or that of their party) and dared to attempt those things of which all posterity will speak? What if there existed one who would devise articles of a new religion or a new Peace in religion, confirmed by the consideration of this star—not an astrological one, but a simple and common one—because it stood above the conjoined Planets? If such a person lurks anywhere, who either arrogates the gift of prophecy to himself or is conscious of his own power to achieve his designs: I would consider him prefigured by that monster which was born this year, 1606, on the <note>original: *Ianuarij die 2/12*; Kepler provides dates in both Old Style and New Style calendars</note> 2nd/12th day of January at Strasbourg. <margin>Monster</margin> Two female infants, whose lower bellies were distinct, with two livers, and the veins of each directed into their own arms and their own feet, for the sake of which there was a double spine <margin>artfully</margin> and a double cerebellum in a doubled back of the head, so that the motor nerves might go into the doubled arms and feet; at the same time, there were two pairs of <margin>made.</margin> ears, because the auditory nerves arise from the cerebellum, which here was doubled. These two females, I say, were joined above the navel facing each other into one Thorax, but with their heads following after one face; they received one pair of eyes, one nose, one mouth, and for that reason one throat, one lung, one heart, likewise one esophagus, and one stomach. And although the Father of the monster is said to have been a joiner <note>original: *arcularius*; a cabinet-maker or chest-maker</note>, whose daily work in his trade is to glue together well-planed and smoothed boards—from which arises the suspicion of an imaginative impression <note>Kepler refers to the contemporary theory of "maternal imagination," where a mother's thoughts or sights during pregnancy were believed to physically shape the fetus</note> made upon the fetus of the pregnant wife—nevertheless, He <margin>Cause of</margin> who glued these females together, that wondrous Artisan, employed an <margin>the monster.</margin> incredibly greater wisdom (as the Anatomists will confess) than joiners are accustomed to; for a way was found by which two might live from the same principle. Indeed, the world has long been sated with contentions regarding matters of faith; yet hope is not near that, while the remaining parts are dying off, one single part will prevail in the world. For what if the same thing happens to those parties which fight today as happened to the ancient sects of Christians, so that they endure for a very long time in the World? Just as alongside the Latins <note>The Roman Catholic Church</note>, there endured throughout Asia and Africa the Greeks, the Judaizing Syrians, the Jacobite Eutychians, and the Nestorian Tatars—of whom the former possessed most ample kingdoms throughout Africa and the latter throughout Northern Asia—besides the Maronite Monothelites, the Armenians, and the Georgians. Therefore, he who strives to find a way by which two who feel differently...

->**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER**<- **179**

Those, however, who consider these matters too small to recognize in them a certain <margin>There are great disturbances today.</margin> hidden stimulation of human ardor—one which is not undertaken by design due to obvious and customary objects, but which flows from some internal bitterness of Natures—I think that the same thing happens to them as to those walking in a **fog**. For as long as they see one another, they do not estimate the great thickness of the fog by which they are surrounded; but when they look upon the fog from a distance, standing themselves in the clear sky, only then do they marvel at the density that meets their eyes. Thus, those who wish may minimize today's disturbances; yet it is certain that once some time has passed and all that is being done today has been recorded, readers will judge that these times were altogether more tumultuous than others.

Indeed, in the year 1572, after that star in **Cassiopeia** shone forth, the provinces were for the most part more peaceful than others; yet in **Belgium** and **France**, since affairs <margin>What followed the star in the year 1572.</margin> were already disturbed around that time, such a great commotion followed anew from that year—not by a single blow, but through a long-woven thread—that entire illustrious families and entire Republics were utterly overturned, and new ones arose. There is not yet an end to these disturbances, even though very few now survive who were present at the first beginnings; both sides have been most miserably punished, and even those who withdrew early in flight and settled in other regions have been wretchedly harassed by the envy of the inhabitants and by poverty.

By the introduction of this one example, whether I argue well or fruitlessly, I certainly infer this one thing without harm: I believe that the governors of Republics, and even the Monarchs themselves, ought to have their eyes and minds intent upon this example and provide diligently, lest today’s exacerbation of the whole of humanity be given too much trust; and lest even now the seeds are being sown of the longest-lasting calamities both within **Germany** and in the neighboring lands; and lest these seeds, where they can be seen to have been sown, finally grow through negligence and the refusal of even the smallest labor or inconvenience, and strike roots so deep that they cannot afterwards be uprooted without the ruin of the entire field.

For to finally conclude that which seems to me possible to say according to the order of nature concerning the **New Star**: this also must be considered, that since this star stood very near <margin>Effect of the new star on those born under it.</margin> the **Ecliptic**, joined to three planets, and obtained a celestial light similar to the fixed stars, it is certainly consistent that its appearance—like that of the other Planets and fixed stars—is also received into the Natures of those being born. Therefore, those who were born around the 9th and 10th of December in the **New Style** had the **Sun** joined to the new star; not to mention those who, during the entire time of its duration, came forth at those hours in which the star culminated or rose. And it is consistent that all these have been allotted a <term>revolutionary nature<note>original Greek: *νεωτερίζουσαν* (neoterizousan); literally "desiring new things" or "tending toward innovation," often used in a political context to describe those who seek to overthrow the status quo</note></term>. It is not advisable to pass down modern disturbances to them as if by hand, so that they themselves might be mixed up in the same when they have grown up. Great is the power of imagination in pregnant...

**On the natural effects of the Fiery Trigon.**

**CHAPTER XXIX.**

**I**t is necessary to insert this separate explanation, not only because the description of the **Fiery Trigon** <note>In astrology, a "Trigon" or "triplicity" is a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element. The Fiery Trigon consists of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. A "Great Conjunction" of Saturn and Jupiter occurs in a new element roughly every 200 years.</note> will seem incomplete unless a few responses are given to the infinite predictions of astrologers drawn from it, but also because in the following pages, the circumstances of the Fiery Trigon will be entangled with the star itself. This is so that it may clearly appear in what respect this happens—what the Fiery Trigon can achieve by itself, and how it might be assisted by the star. Finally, I wish to create a separation between those things which, according to the decrees of astrologers, are ascribed to the Great Conjunction, and those things which hope, fear, and various prejudices have suggested to astrologers (and specifically to my friend **Fabricius** <note>David Fabricius (1564–1617), a German astronomer and divine who corresponded frequently with Kepler.</note>). For according to the principles employed in Chapters 26 and 27, it can be believed that God Himself takes some account of those things which are drawn with a sincere mind from astrological precepts; thus, it is most profitable to have them thoroughly purged of errors.

And indeed, it has been stated in the recent chapters, and scattered throughout the earlier ones, what effect this conjunction of **Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars**—which in the years 1603 and 1604 began the Fiery Trigon—could naturally have upon the faculty of the terrestrial globe, specifically that which governs <term>Meteors</term> <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomena, including weather, lightning, and comets, not just falling stars.</note>. Indeed, the decrees of astrologers are so constructed that they could instill a fear of the world’s conflagration in a <term>Melancholy man</term> <note>According to the theory of the four humors, a "melancholy" person was thought to be prone to gloom and fearful introspection.</note>. Some hot periods in the following years were already beginning to confirm this persuasion; however, this very summer of the year 1606, which exceeds all measure in its humidity, has completely overturned that theory once more. 

Thus long ago, in the year 1524, when astrologers (based on the opinion of a **Watery Trigon**) had predicted mournful and horrific floods, <margin>The effect of the Fiery Trigon on weather.</margin> some gullible people abandoned the plains and retreated to the mountains. Yet they perished not by water, but shortly thereafter by the flooding blood of the **Peasants** <note>This refers to the German Peasants' War of 1524–1525, a massive popular revolt in the Holy Roman Empire that ended in bloody suppression.</note>. Therefore, the expectation of parched earth and droughts based solely on the name "Fiery Trigon" is vain. For there will be no fewer rains, snows, and floods during these two hundred years than there were fires, lightning bolts, and droughts during the completed Watery Trigon. For it was stated in Chapter 6 that the designations from the **Elements**, and thus also from fire, came to the trigons not from their own Nature, and much less from their effect, but from the mere whim of astrologers. O, the infinite vanity of astrologers, who never begin to be wise, and never cease to build their prognostications upon these most futile foundations of playful names!

What? Have not some progressed to such a point of madness that, because we Christians hold it as certain that the heavens and the earth which now exist are reserved for **Fire** until the Day of Judgment, they think this <margin>The Fiery Trigon is not...</margin>

<summary>Kepler critiques the superstitious belief that the "Fiery Trigon" (a 200-year astrological cycle) causes literal fires or world-ending conflagrations, arguing that weather patterns remain variable regardless of astrological names.</summary>

...ion of comparing historical events and religious eras with Astronomy. Some of these were presented before your eyes in a table in the seventh chapter. Nevertheless, anyone who carefully considers all the evidence will discover that a new state of affairs did not suddenly emerge exactly at the specific moments when the **Fiery Trigon** <note>The "Fiery Trigon" refers to the alignment of Jupiter and Saturn within the "fire" signs of the zodiac (Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius), an event occurring in a full cycle roughly every 800 years. Many of Kepler's contemporaries viewed this as a herald of the apocalypse.</note> returned. For whatever experience can naturally attribute to these shifts of the Trigons—or rather, to these **Great Conjunctions** <term>Great Conjunction: The meeting of the planets Saturn and Jupiter in the same degree of the zodiac. Early modern thinkers believed these alignments drove the rise and fall of empires.</term>—is captured by this general phrase: *The disturbance of things that are naturally prone to change.* 

<margin>Unrest.</margin>

Indeed, in those affairs and those Republics <note>original: "Rebuſpub." (Respublicis). In the 17th century, this referred generally to any organized state or political body, not necessarily a "republic" in the modern sense.</note> which had already been simmering with gentle unrest for a long time, new and significant upheavals were stirred up. I was able to safely predict three years ago that this would happen on this occasion as well. 

<margin>But not the overturning of Empires.</margin>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 183**

...is a fable of a hundred years: or if something entirely new is brought forth, before it grows to such a size that it catches the eye, most of those who are alive today will have left this life. Therefore, their curiosity is useless and in vain. Let men cease, therefore—on the occasion of this beginning of the **Fiery Trigon** <note>The "Fiery Trigon" refers to the alignment of Jupiter and Saturn within the "fire" signs of the zodiac (Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius), an event occurring in a full cycle roughly every 800 years. Many of Kepler's contemporaries viewed this as a herald of the apocalypse.</note>, considered purely and naturally—to dream magnificent dreams for themselves: the Turks about crushing the Christians; the Christians about subverting the tyranny of the Ottoman house, or about converting the vast kingdoms of Asia, subject to the King of the Persians, to the Christian faith—supposedly because the Fiery Trigon favors the Christian religion. Or the factions struggling across Europe, one side seeking the extirpation or recovery of the other; things which can hardly happen without a massive shaking of the world and unheard-of tumults. For whatever of this kind is piously hoped for or feared, the seeds of those things certainly do not yet appear sufficiently for an astrologer to be able to form a likely conjecture from them, as long as he looks only at the **Great Conjunctions** <term>Great Conjunction: The meeting of the planets Saturn and Jupiter in the same degree of the zodiac. Early modern thinkers believed these alignments drove the rise and fall of empires.</term>. This is what he does who decides that even the star itself is a natural and astrological effect of the Great Conjunction, as Fabricius <note>David Fabricius (1564–1617) was a German astronomer and theologian who corresponded with Kepler and was among the first to observe the New Star (SN 1604).</note> does quite clearly, and as other astrologers did who wrote before the New Star appeared. For as to the fact that the new star appeared at the conjunction beyond the usual course of nature, I have not yet begun to speak of the *supernatural* significance of this matter; that will happen a little later. For the present, I emphasize what I said a little above: namely, that those things which are not signified through a certain natural mode by the configurations of the planets are simply not pre-signified at all.

<margin>Which of the Great Conjunctions is the most important?</margin>

What I have said, I have confirmed by a natural argument drawn from the Great Conjunctions themselves: there is no reason why, because of this recent conjunction, we should expect anything special or greater than in the previous two centuries by the power of Nature. For it makes no difference to the movement of human affairs whether the Trigon is named from "fire" or from "water." But this matters a great deal: whether many or few planets meet, and whether they do so closely or loosely and at a distance.

For since that human faculty which is capable of harmonies between the celestial radiations <note>Kepler believed the human soul possessed an innate "harmonic" faculty that allowed it to perceive and respond to the mathematical proportions created by planetary light.</note> also grants this to man—that he be a social animal—it is surely the foundation and principle of political states, scattered through individuals. (For the gathering into one is an instinct among men rather than a calculated plan, with God providing for the weakness of our reasonings and anticipating them with this instinct.) Thus it happens that a **joining** <note>original Greek: ἁρμογὴ (*harmogē*), meaning a fitting together or alignment.</note> of rays causes a **joining** of operations; and the minds of many more easily unite in the same work when the rays of many stars are coupled in the sky. And the more closely the stars unite, the more closely men embrace and help one another; and thus more subtle and sharp (that is, harmonic) geniuses are born. Thus in the years 1444, 1504, 1563, and 1623, Saturn and Jupiter are joined most closely;

<summary>Kepler dismisses grand prophecies of immediate political or religious revolution, arguing that Great Conjunctions influence human history only by stimulating the natural human instinct for social cooperation and intellectual harmony.</summary>

Having dismissed the apocalyptic fears of the "Fiery Trigon" on the previous page, he now uses precise astronomical data to evaluate whether the current alignment in Sagittarius (1603) is truly as unique as others have claimed.</meta>

184 JOHANNES KEPLER

...because the place of the conjunctions <note>original: "coitionum" — refers to the meeting or "coming together" of planets in the same zodiacal sign.</note> is Cancer and Leo, where the ascending nodes of Jupiter and Saturn are located; and the node of Saturn, which runs further out to the North, follows. Thus, around the beginning of Leo, a mutual intersection of the paths of Saturn and Jupiter occurs. Calculation indeed shows that in the years 1444 and 1504, Saturn was further south than Jupiter; but in the years 1563 and 1623, it was already further North. Therefore, within 400 years they scarcely come closer together than happened in the year 1563 (<note>original: "in Opticis meis fol. 305. sphalma unius centenarij tolle." — Kepler is referencing his own 1604 book on optics, *Ad Vitellionem Paralipomena*, asking the reader to correct a hundred-year error in his previous calculations.</note>) and since very few minutes of a degree <note>original: "scrupula"</note> intervene between the latitudes of Saturn and Jupiter, I do not indeed grant so much to calculation that I would dare to deny that that conjunction was corporeal <note>A "corporeal" or "bodily" conjunction means the planets appeared so close that they seemed to touch or merge into one body.</note>. For that reason, that conjunction was far to be preferred over the modern one of the year 1603 and its like in the years 1544 and 1484, which show Saturn to be one degree further north than Jupiter. It is also to be preferred over the conjunctions in the sign of Pisces in the years 1464, 1524, 1583, and 1643, which depress Saturn just as much to the south beneath Jupiter. The altitude of the sign in this Northern hemisphere also adds force to those in Cancer; since those which occur in Pisces and Aries are low, and much lower than in Sagittarius like the modern one. Finally, in Sagittarius, Pisces, and the beginning of Aries, Saturn and Jupiter descend from their **apsides** <term>Apsides: The points in a planet's orbit where it is either at its maximum or minimum distance from the Sun.</term>; in Cancer and Leo they ascend. Therefore, as far as astronomical causes are concerned, the meetings in Cancer and Leo are much more powerful than this modern one in Sagittarius.

      Conversely, regarding the multitude of the planets, I do not know if any of the great conjunctions that have occurred since the founding of the world can be compared with that which was in the year 1524, in the month of February: when Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars were joined as if on the same day in the 10th degree of Pisces, while Venus stood a few degrees before them, the Sun and Mercury at the end of Aquarius were held just before the group, and being joined and in direct motion themselves, the Moon passed through them all in the space of thirty hours. But in the year 1603, when Saturn and Jupiter were joined, Mars was absent in Libra; and after it reached the location in the year 1604, Jupiter had already passed Saturn. Hither, therefore, O Astrologers, hither Astronomy commands you to turn your eyes: where there are no empty bugbears of names <note>Kepler is mocking the superstitious "frightening names" often given to planetary alignments by popular astrologers.</note>, but where there are things which are made great by Nature—a conspiracy of motions and the tightest coupling of rays.

      It must also be noted in this conjunction of ours that the Sun, a few days before the conjunction, was also joined to those coming together; which Venus and Mercury, as frequently happens, accompanied. But this conjunction has this in common with all conjunctions of the nearest centuries, in which the Sun is always found nearby; so that the conjunction of the year 1563 alone out of all those that surround us in the nearest 250 years,

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER 185**

...stand, it would be conspicuous, namely at the morning rising of the Planets; and yet even here the sun is not very far away. Thus, it is completely denied to these conjunctions of our times what was able to happen in the time of Christ; namely, that conjunctions happen in opposition to the sun, when the planets are slow because of retrogradation; and so they meet three times in one go <note>A "triple conjunction" occurs when two planets meet, appear to move backward (retrograde) due to Earth's orbit, meet again, and then meet a third time as they move forward. This happened in 7 BC and is a candidate for the Star of Bethlehem.</note>.

Therefore, whether astrologers defend nature or their own custom—believing that the revolutions of Commonwealths and religions are depicted in this most beautiful administration of celestial motions—they had an evident argument from this why they should expect nothing further that could be compared in magnitude with those things which had already happened for 150 years, as long as they looked only at this great conjunction of the year 1603, before the new star had yet arrived.

The things which I then discussed in this manner from astronomy will be found to be so, if it is pleasing to stray a little into histories and into the comparison of past things with the present. Truly, in these 150 years, so many and such great things have happened that greater things could hardly have been possible; and these are such that, in their own respect to natural causes and the consensus of sublunary nature with celestial harmonies, they can be most beautifully related. For who is so sluggish <note>original: "oscitans" — literally 'yawning' or 'drowsy,' describing someone inattentive to history.</note> that he does not perceive this from the reading of histories; <margin>Comparison of the centuries slightly preceding with the present.</margin> that the World, from the time of the overturned former Roman Empire, from the incursion of barbarian nations, as if seized by some deepest lethargy <note>original: "veterno"</note>, slept for a thousand years; but from the year 1450, having been awakened, it returned to its former alacrity? Before, with Commonwealths overturned and salutary rituals trampled underfoot, barbarian customs had grown, some traces of which we laugh at and mock today: Justice was cast down, force prevailed; the exercises of the Nobility were acts of robbery. Christian doctrine having been uprooted, a great part of the world had consented to the most foul and absurd dogmas of Muhammad. Among Christians, there was much barbarism of speech, much ignorance, and other things which would be long to pursue: the Kings of the Saracens were created and moved from their place by Caliphs. How great, by the immortal God, was the foulness of the Kingdom of the Sultans in Egypt? where no one could rule unless he had first been a slave, had first condemned the nation he was about to rule for impiety, had hated it worse than a dog or a snake, had defected from his own opinion, and had become a perjurer against Christians <note>Kepler is describing the Mamluk Sultanate, where the ruling class was composed of manumitted military slaves of foreign origin.</note>. I do not deny that many and great things were done by many; but if you compare those with ours, you will find they were achieved by multitude and many hundreds of thousands rather than by counsel; nor were they long-lasting. Most of the arts known to the ancients were extinct; the study of letters, reduced within the narrow confines of monasteries, lived there also miserably; the courts of princes, especially in Germany, were presided over by Ecclesiastics: to whom alone it was not given as a reproach to know letters...

He contrasts the modern era's focus on "counsel" and the revival of arts with the "barbarism" and ignorance of the preceding millennium.</summary>
