<meta>This page continues Kepler's providential interpretation of the New Star. Having previously discussed the Turks, he now applies the same logic to the potential conversion of the Jewish people and the "Indians" of the New World, suggesting that the current religious conflicts within Christendom might be a divine prelude to a global unification of faith.</meta>

206               JOHANNES KEPLER

If there is any interpreter who perhaps understood the passage of St. Paul cited above differently, he nevertheless would not wish to detract anything from future experience, being mindful of the general rule which establishes the actual event as the genuine interpreter of prophecies. Let the reader compare the phrase of St. Paul: *Blindness in part has happened to Israel, until the fullness of the nations has entered <note>original Greek: εἰσέλθῃ (eiselthē)</note>, and so all Israel will be saved <note>original Greek: σωθήσεται (sōthēsetai)</note>.* <note>Kepler is quoting Romans 11:25-26. He uses the original Greek for key verbs while writing in Latin.</note> I say, compare these things with the phrase of Christ in Luke 21, verse 24: *And Jerusalem will be trodden down by the nations, until the times of the nations are fulfilled.*

The same likelihoods lead us in the cause of the Jews as previously in the cause of the Turks. A certain learned Hebrew said to me, exclaiming loudly: *"Today you have come many thousands of miles closer to us than your ancestors did."* <note>original: *Hodie vos multis millibus miliarium propius ad nos accessistis, quam majores vestri.*</note> To be sure, as they are a people of bitter spirit and tongue, and eager for slander, they attribute many blasphemies to Christians. Therefore, I think this should also be ascribed to the hidden providence of God, that today, with such great fervor, opportunities for slander are everywhere being cut off from the Jews. Another Jew told me that it was common knowledge among them that for eighty years they have been treated more tolerably by the nations than formerly. And they celebrate the decree of Suleiman, the Emperor of the Turks, who in the year 1540 ordered the walls of Jerusalem to be restored and water to be brought into the city through pipes. <note>Suleiman the Magnificent, the Ottoman Sultan, rebuilt the walls of Jerusalem between 1537 and 1541; this was often viewed with messianic or prophetic significance by contemporaries.</note>

In general, I am of the opinion that all the modern contentions used among Christians and the difference of cults <note>Kepler refers here to the divisions between Catholics, Lutherans, and Calvinists.</note> tend toward this goal: that from mutual ruin there may be an opportunity for converting the aforementioned nations—on one hand the Indians, on the other the Jews and Turks. To this you may not inconveniently refer what José de Acosta wrote concerning the idolatry of the Indians and its remedies in Book 5 of *On Procuring the Salvation of the Indians* in this manner. <note>José de Acosta (1540–1600) was a Spanish Jesuit missionary whose writings on the Americas were widely read in Europe.</note>

Chapter IX. *Indeed, I do not know in what words to signify it: the minds of those people are not so much imbued with as they are thoroughly transformed into idolatry, so that they perform nothing in leisure, nor in business, nor in public, nor in private, without first applying the superstition of their idols. They do not rejoice at weddings, nor mourn at a funeral, nor provide or receive a banquet, nor finally step foot out of the house or set a hand to work without pagan sacrilege. To such an extent does the Devil hold their senses oppressed in the most wretched servitude. With what great artifice they hide and disguise all these things when they perceive that they are not free to act publicly—and again, with what great impudence they go mad when they think it is permitted to them—I can only marvel at rather than sufficiently describe.* And after reviewing almost infinite species of idolatry used among them, he adds: *With how many victims, how many libations, and with what an order of ceremonies they pursued the worship of all these things...*
                                                                                                            pursued <note>catchword: *rentur*</note>

<summary>Kepler argues that the appearance of the New Star coincides with a period of potential global conversion, suggesting that Jewish and Turkish perceptions of Christianity are shifting and quoting José de Acosta on the deep-seated idolatry of the American Indians.</summary>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER** [Page number] **107**

the chiefs of the Incas, it would be infinite to describe, etc. And in reality, and through certain observation, it has been noted that those nations of Indians held many and more serious superstitions of a diabolical nature, in which the power and skill of their Kings and Commonwealths especially excelled, etc. Afterward, when in chapters 10 and 11 he had taken up the remedies against Idolatry to be explained, he adds this at the end: *But that must be cared for above all, that for harmful rites, healthy ones be introduced, and ceremonies be obliterated by ceremonies. Therefore, priests should persuade themselves that the use of holy water, and of Images and Rosaries and beads, and candles, and palms, and the rest, which the holy Church approves and frequents, is very opportune for the neophytes <note>A term for newly converted Christians.</note>, and they should follow all these things with much praise in popular sermons, so that instead of the old superstition, they may accustom those imbued with new and religious signs. Thus it will happen that, occupied with better and more elegant things, they may set down from their hands and minds the obsolete superstition of their sect.* Thus says Acosta <note>original: *Acostus*. Referencing José de Acosta (1540–1600), a Spanish Jesuit missionary and naturalist who wrote extensively about the Americas.</note>. But if anyone now weighs the pursuits of the Muslims, and especially the [Marginal note: Rabbi Joseph Albo, around the year of Christ 1425, composed the book *Ikkarim* <note>Hebrew for 'Principles' or 'Roots'.</note>, which led many to Arianism <note>A theological position that denies the full divinity of Jesus, which Kepler suggests was a "stepping stone" between Judaism and mainstream Christianity.</note>.]
Jews, which are diametrically opposite to those of the Indians: 
he will easily conclude that they will be invited by opposite remedies; and therefore, following the example of the Goths mentioned above, they could most easily of all be led to the Anti-trinitarians, then to the Arians, then to healthier gatherings of Christians—especially if, through us, they could retain the ceremonies of their own law (1 Corinthians 7, verses 17 to 24)—but they will scarcely ever reach those remedies proposed to the Indians. What if God, therefore, where we Europeans cannot agree among ourselves from factions so greatly in conflict, sends some to the Indians and others to the Turks? Or perhaps even by a certain plan, he has already preserved both and placed some to the West and others more to the East, so that, divided by locations, they may cease to bite and consume each other in turn; and that each faction may benefit, if not themselves, at least nations entirely alien to the Church?

Now as pertains to the factions of Christians among themselves: it would be a long task to bring forth from the Apocalypse of Saint John, from the Prophet Daniel, and from the riddle of the life of Christ the Savior, what each one disputes for themselves: how they apply the numbers expressed in these Prophecies; how some still expect the Antichrist, predicted by Christ and the Apostles, while others contend he has already come. Certainly, some suspicion could arise for both from this star: for the former, of the Antichrist; for the latter, of the Judge of the living and the dead himself being imminent. And because Christian poets have transferred the image of the Serpent-bearer <note>The constellation Ophiuchus.</note> to that "seed of the Woman" crushing the head of the Serpent <note>A reference to the messianic prophecy in Genesis 3:15.</note>: let those whose profession this is <note>i.e., theologians</note> see, therefore, how this new star, added to his back foot as a stimulus <note>original: *stimulo*, also meaning a goad or spur.</note>, can be beautifully explained by the citation of the words of Scripture, especially Apocalypse 12 and 20; it suffices to have given the warning.

### 208 JOHANNES KEPLER

Therefore, as it concerns that saving advent of our Lord Jesus Christ for Judgment and the consummation of the age <note>A theological term referring to the end of the world or the "end of the era."</note>—which is most desired and certain for all true Christians—just as some have said that the returning **Fiery Trigon** <term>Fiery Trigon: In astrology, this refers to the period when the great conjunctions of Jupiter and Saturn occur in the "fiery" signs of the zodiac (Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius). This happens roughly every 800 years and was viewed by Kepler as a significant marker of historical epochs.</term> was its sign, as was stated above: so there will be no lack of people who make this star a herald of that same event. They are encouraged in this by that **Star of the Magi**, which had preceded the first advent of Christ. 

I certainly rebuke the religious sentiment of these people less than the curiosity of those earlier ones, who were adapting a matter—which is beyond controversy a natural phenomenon—to this supernatural work. For why should I rebuke those who imitate Christ the Lord? For He commanded His listeners to keep watch, because they do not know at what hour He is to come; those who, at every sign in the heavens or on the earth, which [men...] <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the original Latin "homi-" likely completes to "homines" (men/humanity).</note>

### ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER 209

...changes of the people, and perhaps even a New Sect, as other astrologers have suggested, it signifies: and because the star precedes them <note>Referring to the planets Jupiter and Mars, mentioned later.</note> in time and place, in both longitude and latitude, it therefore promises peace after strife. And because the star stood near the **royal path of the planets** <term>Royal path of the planets: The ecliptic, the apparent path that the Sun and planets follow through the sky.</term>; this suggests the abolition of confusion and deviation, and the restoration of Order; which some will interpret as a return to the Catholic Church, while others will see it as a return to the simplicity of Apostolic worship. 

And because it was then the beginning of the **Fiery Trigon** <term>Fiery Trigon: A rare astrological period occurring every 800 years when the great conjunctions of Jupiter and Saturn happen in the fire signs (Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius).</term> which has been made famous by the most significant chapters of history, such as the Creation of the World and the Birth of Christ (see chapter 7, and Tycho Brahe in the conclusion of his *Progymnasmata* <note>original: *Progymnasmata*, Tycho Brahe's "Introductory Exercises" to the new astronomy.</note>, page 805). Therefore, some recall to the principles of Christianity is signified. 

And because the star was higher than Jupiter and Mars, and yet appeared with them—of which the former <note>Jupiter</note> signifies pomp, pride, and authority; while the latter <note>Mars</note> signifies restlessness, battles, quarrels, seditions, the fury of the common people, and reckless impulses—therefore it is signified

<meta>This page continues Kepler's analysis of the Supernova of 1604 (the "New Star"), moving from the specific physical conjunctions of Jupiter and Mars to the broader, more ominous astrological implications involving Saturn and the current state of European politics.</meta>

### 210 JOHANNES KEPLER

Although as far as this determination of time is concerned, we have exceeded the limits of our own principles <note>Kepler is referring to the logical rules he established for interpreting celestial omens without descending into baseless superstition.</note>. For if the place and time of the star in the 18th degree of Sagittarius were chosen by God because of the meeting of Jupiter and Mars; then that meeting of Saturn with the New Star after the sixtieth day was a necessary consequence of natural motion, not something specifically sought by God. Either God disposed the entire meaning of the matter according to a foreseen specialty of celestial motions—which is absurd, (for He sends nothing to men for the sake of the heavens; rather, the heavens are used to signify those things which He is about to send to men)—or, in the matter which God signified through the conjunction of the star with Jupiter and Mars, Saturn’s meeting with the New Star will naturally be able to signify those things we said concerning the sixtieth year: which is again most absurd, to decree concerning a matter to be exhibited divinely, beyond the manner of nature: or, because neither of these is true, we will not be able to use this meeting of Saturn with the New Star to form our conjectures.

It is, however, not difficult to gather this: because the star endured for more than a whole year, a very long-lasting event is signified; for none of the comets have been so long-enduring.

Although I do not predict these things concerning a change for the better with as much confidence as Roeslin <note>Helisaeus Roeslin (1545–1616), a physician and astrologer who predicted that the New Star heralded a massive global revolution and the return of a golden age.</note> uses. For what if that Saturnine religion, or that Saturnine Prophet <note>In the astrological language of the time, "Saturnine" often referred to Judaism, or more broadly to austere, traditionalist, or even melancholic and deceptive religious movements.</note>, who is signified by the New Star and its agreement with Saturn in latitude, is to be interpreted far differently? 

<margin>
"When the Son of Man comes, do you think he will find faith on the earth?" <note>original: "Ubi venerit filius hominis, putas quod fide invenurus sit in terris?" — A quote from Luke 18:8, reflecting Kepler's pessimism about the religious climate of his day.</note>
</margin>

My mind shudders to explain what omens the present face of things and the examples met with among all nations supply to me. But Harpocrates <note>The Greek god of silence and secrecy.</note> intervenes opportunely. To be sure, Saturn is cold, Saturn is a despiser, Saturn carries everything upside down and indifferently. Saturn, when pressed and forced, simulates and dissimulates everything, investigates everything, scrutinizes everything, suspects everything, makes everything suspicious, hardens the soul, and puts on stubbornness. This is the one who could associate even the Turks with "bad Christians" in a single treaty—but one that is hidden and unacknowledged. Nor is any punishment so great as that which we have not deserved.

<margin>
Epilogue.
</margin>

I have set forth all the ways by which I think one can arrive at the meaning of the star by some methodical reasoning: which I ask the reader, again and again, to judge based on the assumed principles. For if you deny the things I have assumed in each place, those things which I have built upon them will also collapse. That one thing repeated often above, I now also emphasize: that very little room is left in this business for the subtlety of disputation (which I will seem to many to have used), in which, as experience witnesses, **Enthusiasms** <term>Enthusiasms: In the 17th-century sense, this refers to divine inspiration or a state of being "possessed" by a spirit, rather than mere excitement.</term> have such power: since the whole matter is divine, and the interpretation of the matter is divine, as was said above.

Moreover, I ask those readers who know me more closely not to turn it into a fault against me if I have made my theological and political discourses shorter than they perhaps expected concerning...

<summary>Kepler argues that the timing of the New Star's appearance follows natural laws rather than a scripted divine play, while expressing deep anxiety that the "Saturnine" qualities of the era—deceit, coldness, and suspicion—portend a dark future for Christendom.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Helisaeus Roeslin, Harpocrates, astrological logic, 1604 Supernova, divine signs, religious conflict, omen</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s cautious approach to astrology. Having previously compared his views to Roeslin’s, he now defends his professional role as Imperial Mathematician and explains why he avoids using the New Star to make specific political or religious attacks.</meta>

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. [space] 211<-

regarding the conflicts of this time, and their catastrophe, let them weigh my condition: I have been hired by the Emperor, not to be a public prophet <term>vates: a divinely inspired seer or poet</term>, but so that I might complete, according to my strength, the science of astronomy which was left destitute of its genuine Master, Tycho [Brahe] <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601) was Kepler's predecessor as Imperial Mathematician; Kepler spent years finishing Tycho's "Rudolphine Tables."</note>. In that office, I must take account of all men, for whom the Great Emperor strives to win this favor of a cultivated and perfected Astronomy. I should not write such side-tasks <term>parerga: secondary or ornamental works</term> for the sake of private individuals, by which others might be deterred or alienated, and perhaps the whole of the main work <note>original: τὸ ἔργον (to ergon)</note> which I handle might be neglected by everyone. I consider this restriction to be a very great benefit: for I am certain that if I had been allowed to express the feelings of my mind freely in print—and to run out beyond the limits of my profession into Politics and the state of the Church due to the kinship of these matters—there would be no warring parties today that I would not have offended with my writing. Truly, *sin is committed both within and without the walls of Troy.* <note>original: "Iliacos intra Muros peccatur & extra." A quote from Horace's Epistles (1.2.16) implying that both sides in a conflict are at fault.</note>

Nor was there any lack of fear that the same thing would happen to me as happened to others; such as to Fabricius <note>David Fabricius (1564–1617), an astronomer and pastor who corresponded with Kepler.</note>, whose writing on the significations of this star contains nothing other than complaints about his neighbors, his opinion on the state of the Empire, and a desire for vengeance and reform. Thus he shapes his oracles as if he were an orator hired by that Magistrate under whom he lives, partly to terrify rebels and wicked neighbors, and partly to conciliate and rouse the Emperor. In the same way, Crabbus <note>Johannes Crabbus, a contemporary prognosticator.</note> acts plainly as a counselor, showing that Christian affairs are perishing through sloth and sleepiness, and that the Turkish enemy <note>The Ottoman Empire was the primary external threat to the Holy Roman Empire at this time.</note> grows strong in this way. Nor does he weigh that whatever he narrates, whether true or false, he narrates in vain; for these things were not to be proposed in a prognostic, but in a secret Council, or else kept silent. For the heavens tell us no such thing; the indicators of diseases are always more numerous than the doctors. And by these things, these men expose themselves to the ridicule of many. 

Therefore, both my condition and God Himself have supplied me with the most wholesome counsel by His own example: He exhibited a general sign to the eyes of all parts [of the world] to be beheld, so that He might warn individuals to descend into themselves and examine their own vices, and having recognized their errors and their crimes, be converted to true repentance. Tycho Brahe also for the most part imitated this; and what reason is there why I should not follow closely in his footsteps?

Doubtless, the vanity of the common people is great, and their custom most perverse: when some portent is exhibited by divine power, they immediately turn their eyes and ears from it to an interpreter, regarding this very man as a new portent. They are anxious not for what reasons he uses, but entirely for what he might say will happen—a most true portent itself! These people think that for the sake of a single petty astrologer, and to commend his sudden opinions, a star was lit by God in the heavens. Nor do I attribute these things to the common people through slander or exaggeration; the matter is exactly as I say. Weigh, I pray, whoever is a little more religious, a little more inclined toward humanity

                                                                    Dd 2                      and [humanity]

<meta>This page concludes Kepler’s treatise. He continues his argument from the previous page, where he was discussing the "easy" or credulous disposition of certain readers who demand specific political prophecies from astronomers.</meta>

212	**JOHANNES KEPLER ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER**

<note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing the thought from page 211.</note> —more inclined toward [credulity], such a person does not pay attention to every word of the astrologer as if they were so many oracles; he thinks entirely that all things are said and done by the hidden disposition of God, however much he may understand the flaw in the arguments the astrologer uses. For men of this kind, no explanation of a matter ever suffices that stays within the boundaries of the art <term>ars: here referring to the technical discipline of astrology/astronomy</term>; instead, they demand from the astrologer that he lay bare before people’s eyes the innermost feelings of the mind through the circumstances of places, times, persons, religions, and public affairs. Unless the astrologer complies with them—that is, unless he becomes the most foolish of mortals—he immediately runs into various criticisms: they deny he is acting candidly, they accuse him of timidity, flattery, desertion, and a thousand crimes. Nor do they distinguish between astronomy and astrology; they think there would be no use for astronomy unless it is so mixed, through the art of divination, with these external circumstances involved in public life, that all of life and even the slightest actions of men are tied to the heavens and pre-determined to happen. 

Therefore, by way of conclusion, I want this said to them: let them go away with their curiosity to the prophecies of Abbot Joachim, St. Bridget, Merlin, and the rest <note>Kepler lists famous mystical and legendary prophets: Joachim of Fiore (12th-century mystic), St. Bridget of Sweden (14th-century visionary), and the Arthurian Merlin.</note>, and to the prognostications of Antonio Torquato, Carion, Theophrastus, Jakob Hartmann, Misocacus, Sibald Brandinus, and Herlicius <note>These were contemporary or near-contemporary makers of popular almanacs and astrological predictions. "Theophrastus" refers to Paracelsus.</note>—men who "place their mouths in the heaven" <note>A reference to Psalm 73:9, implying these men speak with arrogant authority as if they were God.</note> and recognize no judge but the Outcome; and finally let them go to those who have treated these men according to their merits, like Johann Rasch, Paul Messing, and others, and let them delight themselves there. But from this little book, which I have dedicated entirely to astronomical and natural speculations, let them keep away their eyes, which are most hostile to this subject matter.

To the others, who on the contrary praise moderation, I hope I have proved myself a good and peaceful German. If they notice anything here that seems excessive, they should attribute it to the astrologers, whose more general prognostications had to be examined; but if they find things in which I have not satisfied them, let them consider that this is the condition of mortality. Indeed:

->*It has always been permitted for good men to feel differently*<-
->*about the same things without harming their friendship.*<-
<note>original: "Non eadem fentire bonos de rebus ijfdem / Incolumi licuit femper amicitia."</note>

Nor can anyone be so quick to criticize that I would not surpass him in the ease of my compliance, should he advise me rightly.
